## 导读

> 本导读整理自 Socratopia 网站本书介绍页：https://www.socratopia.app/library/close-listening-beethoven-en

This is Volume 2 of Close Listening — and the volume's central claim is that you can walk through Ludwig van Beethoven's full 56-year creative life in 25 chapters, with about 50 anchor works held up to the ear one at a time. The book is organized by the order Beethoven lived: Bonn boy → Vienna prodigy → Heiligenstadt 1802 hinge → heroic middle period → 1815–1820 crisis years → late inward turn → final string quartets → 1827 death. Three threads run across the volume — a biographical arc, a catalogue of nine compositional signatures (the C-minor character, the dramatic slow introduction, the subito piano , the variation-form finale, the long buildup-to-climax, and four others) that Beethoven returned to across forty-five years, and six listening habits the book asks you to unlearn.

The book is honest about what is documented and what is Schindler embroidery. The "fate knocks at the door" line is flagged where it appears; the Schubert-after-Op. 131 anecdote is treated as Holz's later memoir; the Ries account of the torn-up Napoleon dedication is presented as plausible-but-unverifiable. Each anchor work ships with one named primary recording — Kleiber 1974 for the Fifth, Furtwängler 1951 Bayreuth for the Ninth, Pollini 1977 for the Hammerklavier and Op. 111, Quartetto Italiano 1969 for Op. 131 — plus two or three alternates with one-line interpretive descriptions. By the end of the volume, you will know about fifty Beethoven works by ear and can place any recording you encounter into one of the four major conducting lineages (Furtwängler, Toscanini, Karajan, Kleiber) or the HIP tradition.

What makes this book different from a textbook is the closing chapter's instruction: live with him for a year . The book offers a ten-listen starter sequence (Symphony 5 / Symphony 9 / Pathétique / Moonlight / Appassionata / Op. 111 / Op. 131 / Op. 132 Heiliger Dankgesang / Diabelli Variations / Missa Solemnis Kyrie + Benedictus) and asks you to revisit each anchor twice — once for the prose, once for the music. The companion is built to walk with you through that year, returning to specific moments (the false horn entry at 10:00 in the Eroica ; the attacca transition at 22:30 in the Fifth's third-to-fourth movement; the boogie-woogie of Op. 111 Variation 3) one moment at a time.

This is not a Beethoven biography (Lockwood, Solomon, Swafford have written the great ones; the book directs you to them). It is not a "best of Beethoven" anthology. It is the friend who picks up the call when you say I want to go deep on Beethoven — walk me through the fifty-six years .

**Target audience**：Adult amateur listeners who have read or could read Volume 1 of Close Listening . They met Beethoven there through the Fifth Symphony, the Eroica , the Ninth, and the Op. 132 Heiliger Dankgesang — and want the full walk. They know Für Elise , Moonlight , Pathétique . They cannot read sheet music. They want a knowledgeable friend who will take them through Beethoven's full creative life in the order he lived it.

**Prerequisites**：None strictly. Readers who have worked through Volume 1 will catch the cross-references most easily, but Chapter 1 includes a Volume-1-onboarding subsection that defines motivic development, sonata form, and the Dies Irae spotting analogy for readers arriving without that background. No music-reading required. Ability to find a recording on YouTube, Bilibili, or a streaming service.

**Study hours**：80–120 hours. Each chapter pairs 5–40 minutes of listening with 30–60 minutes of reading and another 20–30 minutes of conversation with the Socratopia companion. A reader pacing one chapter per week …

## Introduction

You have already met Beethoven.

If you have read Volume 1 of _Close Listening: Classical_ , you met him through the four-note hammer of the Fifth Symphony, through the _Eroica_ 's revolution, through the Ninth's choral finale, and through the Op. 132 _Heiliger Dankgesang_. Four chapters in the survey volume; four landmark works. Each chapter pointed forward, to a further conversation that the survey could not have inside its own covers.

This volume is that further conversation.

The reader of this volume is a person who finished _Close Listening: Classical_ — or who arrived here from elsewhere with documented love for the four works above — and called a friend to say: _I want to go deep on Beethoven. Walk me through the fifty-six years._ This book is the friend who picks up the call.

Fifty-six years. Beethoven was born in Bonn in December 1770 and died in Vienna in March 1827. His earliest surviving compositions date from when he was about twelve. His last completed works — the String Quartets Op. 131, Op. 135, the replacement finale to Op. 130 — date from the autumn of 1826, six months before his death. From first sketches to last bar, his creative life runs from roughly 1782 to 1827. Forty-five years of composition. Across those forty-five years he wrote roughly 138 works with opus numbers, plus another 200-odd without — symphonies, sonatas, concertos, quartets, trios, songs, one opera, one Mass of the late period, the Diabelli Variations, the Grosse Fuge.

This volume anchors on about fifty of them.

#### How the Volume Is Organized

Twenty-five chapters across seven parts.

**Part I — Why & How to Listen to Beethoven (Chapters 1–2).** Orientation; installation of the four-section listening loop on the _Pathétique_ first movement.

**Part II — The Bonn Boy and the Young Vienna Lion (Chapters 3–5).** Bonn 1770–1792, early Vienna 1792–1798, the early piano sonatas, the early symphonies, and the Heiligenstadt Testament.

**Part III — The Heroic Middle Period (Chapters 6–13).** The _Eroica_ , the middle-period piano sonatas, the two great middle-period concertos, Symphonies 5 and 6 (premiered together on the famous December 22, 1808 four-hour concert), Symphonies 7 and 8, the middle-period chamber music, and _Fidelio_ (the only opera).

**Part IV — The Crisis Years (Chapter 14).** 1815–1820: the Karl custody battle, the financial pressures, the documented compositional slowdown, the transitional Op. 90 sonata.

**Part V — The Late Period (Chapters 15–20).** The late piano sonatas (Op. 101 through Op. 111), the Diabelli Variations, the Ninth Symphony, the _Missa Solemnis_ , and the late string quartets.

**Part VI — Synthesis (Chapters 21–24).** The documented compositional signatures, the three-period framework's defenders and critics, the recording history, and the cultural reception 1827–2026.

**Part VII — Your Beethoven Listening Life From Here (Chapter 25).** Reading list, recording list, concert advice, the next single-composer volumes in the Close Listening series.

#### Each Chapter Has the Same Shape

The four-section listening loop, inherited from Volume 1:

  1. **Before You Listen** — the setup. Hook anecdote, what-to-notice agenda, one-sentence background, search hints for finding the recording.
  2. **The Listening Window** — second-pass listen with timestamps into a primary recording.
  3. **What's Happening in the Music** — the structural-and-analytical map.
  4. **The Stories** — the documented biography and reception context.

Each chapter then closes with three sections (per the Close Listening series convention):

* **Conversation Seeds** — specific prompts for chatting with the Socratopia companion.
* **Things to Listen For on Re-Listen** — concrete observables for the next listen.
* **Going Deeper** — one further-listening or further-reading recommendation.

A final **Chapter Summary** consolidates what the chapter has covered.

#### Three Threads Run Across the Volume

**Thread A — Beethoven's 56-Year Biographical Arc.** Bonn boy → Vienna prodigy → Heiligenstadt 1802 hinge → heroic middle period → 1815–1820 crisis years → late inward turn → final string quartets → 1827 death. The documented Lockwood-Solomon-Swafford framework throughout. Each chapter's Hook Anecdote draws on this documented biography.

**Thread B — Beethoven's Compositional Signatures.** The C-minor character, the dramatic slow introduction, the subito piano, the variation-form finale, the long buildup-to-climax, the unison-orchestra openings, the heroic key-change at the climax, the fugal finale, the extended sustained trill. Each signature is introduced where it first appears documented, called back when it recurs. Chapter 21 is the convergence-and-payoff chapter.

**Thread C — Six Listening Habits to Unlearn.** That Beethoven is grim (he isn't, all the time); that famous works must come first (they don't have to); that he invented everything (he extended); that the deafness is the story (it isn't); that the early works are warm-ups (they aren't); that the late quartets are inaccessible (they aren't). Each habit is named in Chapter 1 and worked against throughout.

#### Pace and Use

A reader pacing one chapter per week finishes the volume in roughly six months. A reader who reads each chapter twice — once for the prose, once after listening through the anchor — will absorb the volume better and take longer. The book is built for either pace.

The chapters can also be read non-sequentially. If you want to start with the late quartets (Chapter 20), you can. If you want to read the symphonies straight through (Chapters 5, 6, 9, 10, 11, 18), you can. The cross-references in each chapter point you to the related chapters.

#### The Companion

This book is the knowledge base for the Socratopia companion. The companion reads the chapters, identifies what to discuss with you, and asks questions that guide you toward understanding. The Conversation Seeds at the end of each chapter are explicit prompts for engaging the companion in dialogue about the work just heard.

The companion does not read the book to you. The companion reads ~10 pages of the book at a time, identifies what to teach in the current session, and asks questions whose answers the book contains. The book's job is to give the companion enough substance for substantive Socratic dialogue. The companion's job is to bring that substance to life through conversation.

#### What You Will Know at the End

About fifty Beethoven works by ear, with their opus numbers and approximate dates available to recall.

The three-period framework — what it captures and what it hides.

How an Op. 1 No. 3 (1795) C-minor argument grows into a Symphony 5 (1808) C-minor argument and finally a Sonata Op. 111 (1822) C-minor argument: three points in a recurring compositional preoccupation, each one different.

What the Heiligenstadt Testament is and what it isn't.

What a Beethoven sonata-form first movement does, what a Beethoven scherzo does, what a Beethoven variation finale does, what a Beethoven fugue does — heard not as abstract forms but as devices Beethoven used at specific moments to specific effects.

A set of documented Beethoven signatures (the C-minor character, the dramatic slow introduction, the subito piano, the variation-form finale, the long buildup-to-climax) that you can spot in any Beethoven piece you encounter next.

Three or four named recordings of each major work, with one-line interpretive descriptions that let you say to a friend: _I like the Kleiber Fifth more than the Karajan Fifth because of the urgency in the first movement, but I'll switch to Karajan when I want the long phrases._

And — most important — an opinion. Beethoven is the kind of composer who rewards the listener's developing opinion. You may end this book preferring the _Eroica_ to the Fifth as the real revolution. You may end it loving the _Pastoral_ more than either of them. You may decide Op. 131 is the deepest work Beethoven wrote, or you may decide _Missa Solemnis_ is. The book is not in the business of telling you which Beethoven to love. It is in the business of giving you enough Beethoven to make the love specific.

That is the walk. Twenty-five chapters. Fifty anchored works. Fifty-six years.

The next chapter starts with the question: _why Beethoven, and why now?_ From there, every chapter runs the loop.

— SocraLab

## Chapter 1: Why Beethoven, and Why Now

In _Close Listening: Classical_ , you met Beethoven four times.

You met him through the four-note hammer of the Fifth Symphony — the rhythmic question that turns into a seven-minute workshop. You met him through the _Eroica_ , the symphony that broke open what a symphony could be. You met him through the Ninth, the choral finale that gave Europe its anthem. And you met him through the last great slow movement of his life — the _Heiliger Dankgesang_ from the String Quartet in A minor, Op. 132, the prayer of a recovered invalid in the Lydian mode.

Four works. Four chapters in the prior volume. Each chapter pointed forward, to a further conversation that the volume could not have inside its own covers. This book is that further conversation.

The reader of this volume is a person who finished _Close Listening: Classical_ and called a friend to say: _I want to go deep on Beethoven. Walk me through the fifty-six years._ This book is the friend who picks up the call.

Fifty-six years. Beethoven was born in Bonn in December 1770 and died in Vienna in March 1827. His earliest surviving compositions date from when he was about twelve. His last completed works — the String Quartets Op. 131, Op. 135, the replacement finale to Op. 130 — date from the autumn of 1826, six months before his death. From first sketches to last bar, his creative life runs from roughly 1782 to 1827. Forty-five years of composition. Across those forty-five years he wrote roughly 138 works with opus numbers, plus another 200-odd without — symphonies, sonatas, concertos, quartets, trios, songs, one opera, one Mass-of-the-late-period, the Diabelli Variations, the Grosse Fuge.

The volume you are holding does not cover all of them. It anchors on about fifty.

### 1.1 The Volume's Relationship to Volume 1

The prior volume, _Close Listening: Classical_ , was a comprehensive walk through Western classical music in twenty-seven chapters. Beethoven occupied three of those chapters. That is the right proportion in a survey volume: Beethoven sits between Mozart and Schubert, in a line that goes back to Bach and forward to Mahler, and the survey volume's job is to keep all the composers in the same field of view.

This volume's job is different. It zooms in. It walks through one composer's full creative life _in the order he lived it_. Bonn boy → Vienna prodigy → middle-period revolutionary → crisis years → late inward turn → final string quartets. The walk has fifty-six years of weather, and the chapters reflect the weather as it changes.

The cross-references to Volume 1 are explicit where they exist. Chapter 6 of this volume revisits the _Eroica_ (Volume 1 Chapter 13). Chapter 9 revisits the Fifth Symphony (Volume 1 Chapter 3), this time all four movements. Chapter 18 revisits the Ninth (Volume 1 Chapter 14). Chapter 20 revisits the _Heiliger Dankgesang_ (Volume 1 Chapter 14 again, now in the context of all six late quartets). In each case, this volume goes deeper. You met the work once; now you meet it embedded in its place in Beethoven's life, with documented sketch history and reception history that Volume 1 did not have room for.

If you have not read Volume 1, you can still read this volume. The four-section listening loop is re-installed in Chapter 2. The form vocabulary (sonata form, exposition, development, recapitulation, motif) is built up again from the first listen on. The book is designed to stand alone.

#### A quick orientation for readers who skipped Volume 1

If you arrive here without Volume 1 in your background, three concepts get cross-referenced often and are worth knowing before you start:

* **Motivic development** : building a long musical structure out of a tiny repeated cell (three to six notes) that is put through every possible variation, fragmentation, reorchestration, and reharmonization without losing its identity. The four-note hammer of Beethoven 5, _short-short-short-LONG_ , is the most-famous example; the whole first movement is built from that cell. Beethoven was already doing this in 1798 with the _Pathétique_ (Chapter 2 starts here). When the book points back to "motivic development as in Volume 1 Chapter 3," it means _this technique_.

* **Sonata form** : the standard large-scale architectural plan for first movements of symphonies, concertos, piano sonatas, and string quartets from about 1770 through about 1900. Three main sections plus a coda: **exposition** (state two contrasting themes in different keys), **development** (transform and fragment the themes, modulate restlessly), **recapitulation** (return the themes, now both in the home key), **coda** (a closing wrap-up). Most chapters will assume you can map these four sections onto the timestamps the Listening Window provides. Chapter 2 will walk through sonata form again on the _Pathétique_ first movement.

* **The Dies Irae spotting exercise** (Volume 1's running game): Volume 1 traced a recurring medieval Catholic _Day of Wrath_ chant melody that composers from Berlioz to Rachmaninoff to John Williams have quoted. The point was to give the listener _one thing to listen for_ across many works. This volume's Chapter 21 builds the same spotting exercise for Beethoven — except the recurring elements are not external quotations but internal Beethoven habits (the C-minor character, the dramatic slow introduction, etc.). When the book invokes the Dies Irae analogy in Chapter 21, it means "Volume 1 had you spotting one thing across many works; we are now going to spot signatures the same way."

That is enough background to start. Anything else gets defined as it comes up.

### 1.2 The Three-Period Framework

You will see, throughout this book, the words _early period_ , _middle period_ , and _late period_ applied to Beethoven's compositional life.

Early: roughly 1782–1802. Bonn-to-Vienna, the first Vienna decade, ending at the Heiligenstadt crisis. Op. 1 through about Op. 35.

Middle: roughly 1803–1814. The heroic period. The Eroica through the Seventh and Eighth Symphonies. Op. 55 through about Op. 92.

Late: roughly 1815–1827. The inward turn. Op. 90 transitional; the great Late period proper from Op. 101 onward — the late piano sonatas, the Diabelli Variations, the Missa Solemnis, the Ninth, the late string quartets.

This three-period framework is not this book's invention. It was introduced by Wilhelm von Lenz in 1852 in _Beethoven et ses trois styles_ , and every major modern Beethoven biographer — Lewis Lockwood (2003), Maynard Solomon (1998), Jan Swafford (2014), William Kinderman (2009) — uses some version of it. It is the standard scaffolding of Beethoven scholarship.

The framework is also imperfect. Some works straddle the lines: the _Archduke_ Trio (1811) is composed in the heroic-middle stretch but already has late-style features. Op. 90 (1814) is one foot in middle and one foot in late. The Op. 95 quartet (1810) has a slow movement that already looks ahead to the late style. The framework hides as much as it illuminates. Chapter 22 of this volume is dedicated to exactly this honesty.

But the framework is useful. Beethoven's compositional voice did change across the forty-five years. The young man writing the Op. 1 trios in 1795 is a different composer from the man writing the _Diabelli Variations_ in 1823 — different in technique, in ambition, in what he expected of his listeners. The three-period frame is the shorthand for that change. We will use it, while remembering it is shorthand.

### 1.3 Six Listening Habits to Unlearn (Beethoven Edition)

Volume 1 introduced six listening habits to unlearn for classical music in general. Beethoven gets his own six. They overlap with the general six but are calibrated for what specifically goes wrong when amateur listeners come to Beethoven with preconceptions.

**Habit 1: "Beethoven is grim."** Beethoven has a public image as the brooding, fist-shaking, deaf, ravaged genius. Storming through the Fifth Symphony's destiny-knock. Hurling thunderbolts in the _Hammerklavier_ fugue. The image is partial. He also wrote some of the wittiest music in the Classical-Romantic canon: the second movement of Symphony 8, which is a giant joke about the metronome; the second movement of the _Pastoral_ , with the cuckoo-and-quail tags pinned to specific bars in the score with deadpan musical seriousness; the finale of the _Razumovsky_ Op. 59 No. 2, with its mock-Russian theme that Beethoven turned upside down; the _Diabelli_ variation that imitates the cobbler's-patch theme back at Diabelli with affectionate mockery. The grim-Beethoven image is real. So is the playful-Beethoven, and the playful-Beethoven is documented in the same Lockwood-Solomon-Swafford literature.

**Habit 2: "Famous works first, rare works later."** A reasonable starting strategy in many genres turns into an obstacle in Beethoven. The reader who insists on working her way through the Fifth, the Ninth, the _Moonlight_ , and the _Eroica_ before approaching the late quartets ends up with the late quartets framed as a graduation reward, and then never approaches them, because the prerequisites become indefinite. Op. 131 is not a graduation reward. It rewards direct attention from anyone who can sit still through Symphony 5. This book accordingly walks the reader to Op. 131 in Chapter 20, with vocabulary built up across the prior nineteen chapters, without making her earn the right to be there.

**Habit 3: "Beethoven invented everything."** The biographical novels and the documentary voiceovers like to present Beethoven as the pure originator — the lone genius who created sonata form, who invented the symphony as a vehicle for personal expression, who broke open the variation set. He did none of those things in isolation. He inherited sonata form from Haydn (whose teaching he documented as resented, even as he learned from it). He inherited the symphony as personal-expression vehicle from late Haydn and early Mozart. He inherited the variation set from a tradition that goes back at least to the Renaissance and that he engaged most seriously through his study of Bach's keyboard variations. What Beethoven did was take inherited forms and push them to their structural limits. That is not invention from nothing. It is invention as relentless extension. The distinction matters because the "lone genius" myth flattens the historical conversation Beethoven was actually having with his predecessors.

**Habit 4: "The deafness is the story."** Beethoven's progressive deafness is a documented fact. From around 1796 his hearing began to fail; by the Heiligenstadt Testament of 1802 he was contemplating suicide because of it; by 1818 he had begun the conversation books that his visitors used to communicate with him; by the last years he was substantially deaf. These are documented in Wegeler-Ries, in the conversation books themselves, in the medical record. The deafness was a real and central fact of his life.

But the deafness is _a_ story, not _the_ story. The late style is creative invention, not pathology. Lewis Lockwood and Charles Rosen are both explicit about this. The Op. 132 _Heiliger Dankgesang_ , the late quartets, the _Missa Solemnis_ , the _Diabelli Variations_ — these are not deafness leaking through the music. They are the products of a composer who had spent forty years training, who had assimilated Bach and Palestrina and Handel deeply in the late years, and who was working at the absolute upper edge of what the available forms could do. The deafness shaped the _circumstances_ of the work (no more public performance after 1815, more time for composition, more isolation, more dependence on inner hearing). It did not produce the work. The work is the composer's lifelong development at maturity. We will say this once here and then mostly stop talking about the deafness.

**Habit 5: "The early works are warm-ups for the great middle period."** Op. 1 No. 3 (1795) is a major work. The Pathétique (1798) is a major work. The Op. 18 string quartets (1798–1800) are major works. The _Moonlight_ (1801) is a major work. To read Beethoven's first decade in Vienna as preparation-for-Eroica is to miss what the first decade actually achieved. Beethoven arrived in Vienna in 1792 as a twenty-one-year-old prodigy who had already published works in Bonn; by 1795 he had announced himself with the Op. 1 trios in a Lichnowsky soirée; by 1800 he was the most-promising young composer in Vienna, with the First Symphony premiered and the Op. 18 quartets in print. This is a young composer at the top of his immediate generation. It is not warm-up.

**Habit 6: "I don't get the late quartets."** The late quartets — Op. 127, Op. 130, Op. 131, Op. 132, Op. 135, and the _Grosse Fuge_ Op. 133 — are the deepest territory in Beethoven and arguably in the chamber repertoire. Op. 131 in particular is a work that opens slowly. The amateur listener encountering it for the first time may feel that the music is bypassing her. The bypassing is not in the music; it is in the not-yet-installed listening vocabulary. The reader who arrives at Chapter 20 of this volume will have heard, by then, the Op. 1 No. 3 (Ch 3), the early piano sonatas (Ch 2, 4), the Razumovsky (Ch 12), the _Archduke_ (Ch 12), the _Hammerklavier_ Adagio (Ch 15), and Op. 111 (Ch 16). The vocabulary will be in place. Op. 131 will be available.

### 1.4 The Shape of the Walk

Twenty-five chapters across seven parts. Part I (this chapter + Chapter 2) installs the loop. Part II (Chapters 3–5) covers the Bonn boy and early Vienna lion. Part III (Chapters 6–13) is the heroic middle period: Symphonies 3, 5, 6, 7, 8; the great middle-period sonatas; the two great middle-period concertos; the chamber music; _Fidelio_. Part IV (Chapter 14) covers the crisis years 1815–1820. Part V (Chapters 15–20) is the late period proper: the late piano sonatas in two chapters, the Diabelli Variations, Symphony 9, the _Missa Solemnis_ , and the late quartets. Part VI (Chapters 21–24) is synthesis: the compositional signatures, the three-period framework, the recording history, and the cultural reception. Part VII (Chapter 25) is the closing: where to go next.

Each chapter follows the same four-section structure you learned in Volume 1: _Before You Listen_ (the setup), _The Listening Window_ (a second-pass listen with timestamps), _What's Happening in the Music_ (the structural map), _The Stories_ (the documented biography and reception). Each chapter ends with three sections in the Close Listening series convention: _Conversation Seeds_ for talking with the Socratopia companion, _Things to Listen For on Re-Listen_ for the next time you put the recording on, and _Going Deeper_ for the curious reader who wants one further pointer.

Length follows material. Chapters 6, 9, 12, 15, 18, 19, 20 are the long ones — Eroica, Symphony 5 (all four movements), the chamber music spread, the _Hammerklavier_ , Symphony 9, the _Missa Solemnis_ , the late quartets all need real space. Chapters 1, 14, 22, 23, 24 are tighter — orientation, transition, and synthesis don't need as many pages.

A reader pacing one chapter per week finishes the volume in roughly six months. A reader who reads each chapter twice, once for the prose, once after listening through the anchor, will absorb the volume better and take longer. The book is built for either pace.

### 1.5 What You Will Know at the End

About fifty Beethoven works by ear, with their opus numbers and approximate dates available to recall.

The three-period framework — what it captures and what it hides.

How an Op. 1 No. 3 (1795) C-minor argument grows into a Symphony 5 (1808) C-minor argument and finally a Sonata Op. 111 (1822) C-minor argument: three points in a recurring compositional preoccupation, each one different.

What the Heiligenstadt Testament is and what it isn't.

What a Beethoven sonata-form first movement does, what a Beethoven scherzo does, what a Beethoven variation finale does, what a Beethoven fugue does — heard not as abstract forms but as devices Beethoven used at specific moments to specific effects.

A set of documented Beethoven signatures (the C-minor character, the dramatic slow introduction, the subito piano, the variation-form finale, the long buildup-to-climax) that you can spot in any Beethoven piece you encounter next.

Three or four named recordings of each major work, with one-line interpretive descriptions that let you say to a friend: _I like the Kleiber Fifth more than the Karajan Fifth because of the urgency in the first movement, but I'll switch to Karajan when I want the long phrases._

And, most important, an opinion. Beethoven is the kind of composer who rewards the listener's developing opinion. You may end this book preferring the _Eroica_ to the Fifth as the real revolution. You may end it loving the _Pastoral_ more than either of them. You may decide Op. 131 is the deepest work Beethoven wrote, or you may decide _Missa Solemnis_ is. The book is not in the business of telling you which Beethoven to love. It is in the business of giving you enough Beethoven to make the love specific.

That is the walk. Twenty-five chapters. Fifty anchored works. Fifty-six years.

The next chapter starts the loop with the _Pathétique_.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion which of the four Beethoven works from Volume 1 (Fifth Symphony, Eroica, Ninth, Op. 132 Heiliger Dankgesang) she would recommend returning to first as preparation for this volume, and why that one._
* _Tell the companion which of the Six Habits you most recognize in your own listening so far, and ask her how to work specifically against it. The conversation will be more useful if you can name one Beethoven piece you have skipped or bounced off, and let her help you re-enter it._
* _Ask the companion about the documented Wilhelm von Lenz origin of the three-period framework, and why scholars from Lockwood to Kinderman have kept using it despite its imperfections._
* _Have the companion name three "rare-Beethoven" works (ones not in the famous-eight list) that she thinks reward early attention, and to say why each one._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The opening of the Fifth Symphony's first movement, if you have a copy ready: a fast taste, just thirty seconds, to remind yourself what the four-note cell sounds like. We will return to all four movements in Chapter 9.
* The opening of the _Heiliger Dankgesang_ (Op. 132, third movement), if you have it: a slow, hymnal F-Lydian-mode chorale. This is what late Beethoven sounds like. We will return to it in Chapter 20.
* The opening Adagio of the _Pathétique_ (Op. 13, first movement), if you have it: a slow, dramatic introduction in C minor that bursts into a fast Allegro. This is what early Beethoven sounds like. We will start the next chapter here.

### Going Deeper

If you read one book alongside this volume, read **Lewis Lockwood,_Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (W. W. Norton, 2003)**. It is the contemporary single-volume gold standard — balanced biographical-and-musical voice, careful with sources, accessible to the amateur reader. Every chapter of this volume draws on Lockwood; every Beethoven student in 2026 starts there.

### Chapter Summary

This volume picks up from the four Beethoven encounters in Volume 1 (Symphony 5, Eroica, Symphony 9, Op. 132) and walks the full fifty-six years of his creative life in the order he lived it. Twenty-five chapters across seven parts; about fifty anchored works.

The three-period framework, early (1782–1802), middle (1803–1814), late (1815–1827), is the scaffolding, introduced by Wilhelm von Lenz in 1852 and used by every modern Beethoven biographer (Lockwood, Solomon, Swafford, Kinderman). The framework is useful and imperfect; Chapter 22 will be honest about both.

Six listening habits to unlearn, calibrated for Beethoven: that he is grim (he isn't, all the time); that famous works must come first (they don't have to); that he invented everything (he extended); that the deafness is the story (it isn't); that the early works are warm-ups (they aren't); that the late quartets are inaccessible (they aren't).

The next chapter installs the four-section listening loop on the _Pathétique_ first movement. From there, Part II walks into Bonn and the Op. 1 trios, and the biographical spine begins.

* * *

---

## Chapter 2: The Listening Basics for Beethoven — _Pathétique_ , First Movement

A slow chord. Then silence. Then the same chord again, a half-step higher. Then a long held note in the bass, with the upper voices speaking against it in tense, suspended harmonies.

You are listening to ten seconds of the _Pathétique_ and Beethoven has already done something that none of his teachers in Vienna would have done in the same place.

Haydn would not have opened a piano sonata this way. Mozart would not have opened a piano sonata this way. The standard model for a piano sonata first movement in 1798 was: state the first theme directly, briskly, in the home key, in the expected tempo. The slow introduction was a _symphonic_ device, used occasionally by Haydn at the start of a symphony. Putting one in front of a piano sonata's first movement was an importation of the symphonic ambition into the more intimate keyboard genre. Beethoven did it twice on the way to his middle period — here in Op. 13 (1798) and again in Op. 81a _Les Adieux_ (1809–10). The Op. 13 version became famous; the Op. 81a version became the bridge into the late style. We are starting where the device first arrived in his catalogue.

The _Pathétique_ is the right work to run the loop on. It is structurally clear (slow introduction, then sonata-form Allegro, with the slow introduction returning at two crucial moments later in the movement). It is famous enough that you may already know the opening even if you have never sat with it before. It is documented in Charles Rosen's _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas_ (2002), which is the source-of-record for piano-sonata analysis in this volume. And it is the launching pad for **Thread B** , Beethoven's documented compositional signatures, because _the dramatic slow introduction_ is the first one we will name.

This chapter rehearses the loop you will run on every chapter afterwards. Read the _Before You Listen_. Listen once. Read _The Listening Window_ with the recording in hand and listen again, this time with timestamps. Read _What's Happening in the Music_ for the structural map. Read _The Stories_ for the documented context and the recording lineages. Then the three closing sections — Conversation Seeds, Things to Listen For on Re-Listen, Going Deeper — point you toward your next move.

### 2.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — the title that wasn't Beethoven's, except this time it was

Most of the famous Beethoven sonata titles — _Moonlight_ , _Appassionata_ , _Hammerklavier_ , _Waldstein_ , _Les Adieux_ — were not given by Beethoven himself. _Moonlight_ was Ludwig Rellstab's, invented in 1832, five years after Beethoven's death. _Appassionata_ was the publisher Cranz's, in a Hamburg edition of 1838. _Hammerklavier_ was a German word Beethoven used for the instrument on the title page of Op. 101 and Op. 106, and it stuck to Op. 106 specifically because of the work's scale.

The _Pathétique_ is the exception. Beethoven himself authorized the title, _Grande Sonate Pathétique_ , for the first edition published by Joseph Eder in Vienna in December 1799. The word _pathétique_ in late-18th-century French (and in the German cultural context where Beethoven worked) did not mean what _pathetic_ means in modern English. It carried something closer to _passionate_ or _moving in a tragic register_ — the kind of register that comes from the Greek _pathos_ , the suffering and grandeur of high-classical drama. The title is the composer signalling, on the title page, what kind of object the listener is about to encounter: not an entertainment, not a salon piece, but a high-dramatic statement.

In 1799 this was unusual for a piano sonata. Sonatas were domestic music — published for amateurs to play at home, in front of dinner guests, on instruments that ranged from middling to good. The genre was not the place where composers staked dramatic claims. The dramatic claims were made in symphonies and operas. Beethoven, with the _Grande Sonate Pathétique_ , was effectively saying: _I am going to make a dramatic statement in the domestic genre, and I am going to label it as such on the title page so you cannot misread it._ It was an early move in what would become a lifelong project — pushing the piano sonata to do work the symphony usually did. By the time he reached Op. 106 _Hammerklavier_ (1818), the piano sonata in his hands was as ambitious as any symphony of its day.

The _Pathétique_ is the document where this project announces itself. Listen with that announcement in your ear.

#### What to notice

Three things, no more.

  1. **The slow introduction.** The first ten or eleven measures of the movement, about 1:25 in the Gilels recording, are a slow, heavy, chord-laden introduction marked _Grave_ (very slow, very serious). Notice how _static_ it feels: long chords, slow harmonic motion, long silences. Notice how, near the end, it accelerates — descending runs that get faster and faster and crash into the fast movement that follows. The introduction is _not_ in the same tempo as the rest of the movement; it is the dramatic frame.

  2. **The fast first theme — and the cell it is built from.** When the Allegro starts (around 1:32 in Gilels), notice the first theme: a fast, ascending, urgent figure in the right hand, with a hammering left-hand accompaniment of repeated octaves. Notice that the theme has a recognizable shape, a short, two-note rising cell, that gets repeated and developed across the next several measures. The whole _exposition_ is built from this cell. This is the same _motivic development_ device you met in Volume 1's Beethoven 5 chapter. Beethoven was already using it here, ten years before the Fifth Symphony.

  3. **The return of the slow introduction.** Two more times in the movement — once at the start of the development (around 5:00 in Gilels) and once near the end (around 8:00) — the slow _Grave_ introduction comes back, in altered form. Beethoven _threads the slow introduction through the entire movement_. It is not a one-time entrance device. It is a recurring frame. Listen for it at those two later moments — you will hear it come back, slower, more weighted, and you will recognize it.

That is the listening assignment. Slow introduction, the cell of the fast movement, and the two later returns of the slow introduction. Three things.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the _Pathétique_ in 1798 and published it in December 1799, dedicating it to Prince Karl von Lichnowsky — his most important early Vienna patron, in whose Friday-morning soirées Beethoven had been performing his new piano works since at least 1795.

### 2.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Now the second listen, with timestamps into the Gilels 1980 recording.

* **0:00–0:08.** The opening Grave chord. A heavy C-minor chord in the left hand, with a high-voice suspension that resolves downward. Notice the _heaviness_ : Gilels takes the chord slowly, lets it ring, before the next move. The dynamic is _fortissimo piano_ : a loud strike that drops immediately to softness. This is one of Beethoven's documented signatures, the _subito piano_ (sudden quiet), which we will meet again in Symphony 5 (Chapter 9) and Op. 132 (Chapter 20).

* **0:08–0:35.** The Grave continues: a series of heavy chords, each followed by a softer, suspended-harmony response from the upper voices. The _dotted rhythm_ : long-short, long-short — is the signature shape of the Grave throughout. Notice that the harmonic motion is slow: chords held, resolved, held again. Beethoven is establishing the dramatic weight before he allows the movement to start.

* **0:35–1:10.** A more lyrical passage, still in the Grave tempo. The dotted rhythm continues but now there are longer phrases: almost arioso, almost like a vocal lament. Charles Rosen describes this passage in _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas_ as "the slow movement's _song_ hidden inside the introduction's _frame_ " — Beethoven is showing you, in advance, that the movement contains a lyrical voice as well as a dramatic one.

* **1:10–1:32.** The acceleration. The Grave begins to break apart. Fast descending runs in the right hand, increasing in speed and volume, finally crashing down to a low note in the bass that opens directly into the fast Allegro. The transition is _attacca_ : no pause, no breath; the slow introduction collapses into the fast movement under its own momentum. Listen for the moment the music _can no longer hold itself slow_ and has to pour forward.

* **1:32–1:50.** The first theme of the Allegro. The tempo marking is _Allegro di molto e con brio_ : very fast, with brilliance. The first theme has two parts: a fast ascending two-note cell in the right hand (do-RE, do-RE, do-RE), and a hammering octave accompaniment in the left hand. Notice the _cell_ : it is short, distinctive, and Beethoven will use it everywhere. This is the motivic-development discipline you met in Volume 1's Beethoven 5 — except now you can see Beethoven was already doing it in his first decade in Vienna.

* **1:50–2:20.** Development of the first theme. The cell is taken through different keys, fragmented, reassembled. Beethoven is _not_ moving on to a second theme yet; he is sustaining the first theme's energy through transformation. This is the _cell as workshop_ from Volume 1 Chapter 3, in early form.

* **2:20–2:50.** The transition to the second theme. Listen for a _change of register_ : the music suddenly settles, the texture lightens, the music modulates toward a new key (E♭ minor, which is unusual; the standard would be E♭ major, the relative major of C minor; Beethoven goes minor instead, keeping the dramatic temperature high).

* **2:50–3:30.** The second theme. A more lyrical, descending melody in the right hand, with a flowing accompaniment in the left. The second theme is _also_ tense and minor-key: not consoling, as the standard sonata-form second theme would be. Beethoven uses the second theme to _continue the dramatic argument_ , not to relax from it. Charles Rosen notes this as a signature of Beethoven's C-minor character: even the second theme refuses to relax.

* **3:30–4:30.** The closing material of the exposition. Fast figuration, returns of the cell, building energy toward the close of the exposition. Around 4:25 the exposition ends and Gilels takes the repeat: the entire exposition (back to 1:32) plays again. Most modern pianists take this repeat; Kempff in his 1965 reading does not. The repeat lets you hear the exposition twice with everything you now know about the cell.

* **5:00–5:15.** **The Grave returns.** Just before the development section, the slow introduction comes back: about four measures of it, in a new key, but unmistakably the same music as the opening. This is the first of the two later returns. Listen for it: a sudden slow-down, the heavy dotted-rhythm chords, the same harmonic shape. Beethoven is _threading the introduction through the structure_.

* **5:15–6:15.** The development section. The cell from the first theme is now put through the full workshop: fragmented, modulated, taken into distant keys, brought back. The motion is restless, urgent, mostly minor.

* **6:15–6:35.** The recapitulation begins. The first theme returns in the home key of C minor. Notice that the return _means_ something it could not have meant the first time, because you now know the cell has been put through development. The cell coming back intact, after the workshop, is the structural payoff.

* **6:35–7:30.** The recapitulation continues. Second theme returns, now in the home key: though Beethoven keeps it minor, not relaxing into major.

* **8:00–8:15.** **The Grave returns one last time.** Just before the coda, the slow introduction comes back again: even more weighted, even slower in Gilels. This is the second of the two later returns, and it is the most dramatic. Listen for the way Beethoven brings the dramatic frame back at the end, having now earned every weight of it.

* **8:15–8:50.** The coda. Fast, driving, built on the cell, closing the movement decisively in C minor. The slow introduction does _not_ return one more time: the dramatic frame has done its work; the cell finishes the movement on its own terms.

About nine minutes total in Gilels. The slow introduction appears three times, at the opening, before the development, and before the coda, and frames the entire dramatic structure. The fast Allegro is the body; the Grave is the frame.

### 2.3 What's Happening in the Music

Three concepts, all of which the listening just illustrated: _the dramatic slow introduction_ , _sonata form_ (which Volume 1 introduced and we now apply on a new piece), and _motivic development_ (which Volume 1 also introduced and which we now see at an earlier point in Beethoven's career than Symphony 5).

#### Definition: Dramatic Slow Introduction

A dramatic slow introduction is a slow, weighted opening section that precedes the main fast first movement of a work. It is in a slower tempo than the body of the movement, often in a heavier dynamic, and often establishes a dramatic register that the fast movement then proceeds against.

In plain language: a dramatic slow introduction is a _prologue_. Think of it as the curtain rising slowly on a tragedy, before the action begins. The introduction sets the mood, names the stakes, and tells the listener: _what follows is not light entertainment_. Beethoven inherited the device from late Haydn (whose Symphony 104 "London," the _Drumroll_ 103, and others all open with slow introductions), but he extended it. Where Haydn's introductions usually appear once, at the start of a symphony, Beethoven sometimes lets them _return_ later in the movement, threading the introduction through the body. The _Pathétique_ is the clearest early example of this: the Grave returns twice after its initial appearance, at structurally crucial moments (before the development and before the coda). The introduction is no longer a one-time entrance device; it is a recurring frame.

The dramatic slow introduction is the first compositional signature we will name in **Thread B**. We will meet it again at:

* **Symphony No. 1, Op. 21 (1800)** : Adagio molto introduction to the first movement (Chapter 5).
* **Symphony No. 7, Op. 92 (1812)** : Poco sostenuto introduction to the first movement (Chapter 11), one of the longest slow introductions Beethoven wrote.
* **Symphony No. 4, Op. 60 (1806)** : Adagio introduction.
* And later still in **Op. 81a _Les Adieux_ (1809–10)**: the slow piano-sonata introduction once again, this time portraying the patron Archduke Rudolph's farewell.

The signature accumulates across the catalogue. By Chapter 11 you will recognize it on sight.

#### Definition: Sonata Form (review)

Sonata form is the most important large-scale architectural plan in classical music from about 1770 to about 1900. The first movement of almost every symphony, concerto, piano sonata, and string quartet from this period is in sonata form. It has three main sections, often plus a coda:

  1. **Exposition.** The composer states the main themes — usually two contrasting themes in different keys. First theme in the home key (often dramatic), second theme in a contrasting key (often more lyrical). In the _Pathétique_ first movement, the exposition runs from 1:32 (where the Allegro starts) to 4:30 (in the Gilels recording, with the repeat included). The first theme is the fast ascending cell with hammering octaves; the second theme is the more lyrical descending melody in E♭ minor.

  2. **Development.** The composer takes the themes from the exposition and _develops_ them — transforms them, fragments them, moves them through different keys. In the _Pathétique_ first movement, the development runs from 5:15 to about 6:15. The Grave returns at 5:00, _before_ the development proper begins — a Beethoven extension of the standard form.

  3. **Recapitulation.** The themes return, now both in the home key. In the _Pathétique_ first movement, the recapitulation runs from about 6:15 to 8:00.

  4. **Coda.** A closing passage that wraps up the movement. In the _Pathétique_ , the coda runs from 8:15 to the end, with the Grave returning one last time at 8:00 just before the coda begins.

The _Pathétique_ 's sonata-form structure is unusually clear — which is one reason it is the right work to install the loop on. By the time you have heard it twice, you can map the four sections (plus the three appearances of the Grave) onto the timestamps.

#### Definition: Motivic Development (preview)

You met motivic development in Volume 1, Chapter 3 (Beethoven 5, first movement). The same technique is at work here. The two-note cell of the _Pathétique_ Allegro's first theme is the seed; the entire movement's energy comes from putting this seed through transformations.

In plain language: motivic development is _building a large structure from a small repeated idea, putting the idea through every possible variation, fragmentation, reorchestration, and reharmonization, without losing its essential identity_. Beethoven was already doing this in 1798. He would continue to do it through the _Eroica_ in 1804, through Symphony 5 in 1808, and into the late quartets in 1825–26. It is the through-line of his compositional technique.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes think the slow Grave introduction is _the_ first theme of the movement, and that the Allegro starts with a second theme. This is wrong. The first theme of the movement is the fast ascending cell with hammering octaves that starts at 1:32. The Grave is the introduction; it is _in front of_ the sonata form, not part of the exposition's first-theme group.

The distinction matters because if you confuse the introduction with the first theme, you will lose track of the sonata-form architecture. The exposition is the Allegro; the Grave is the frame. Beethoven _threads_ the frame through the body (the two later returns), but the frame is not the body. Hold the distinction.

#### A note on key — C minor again

The _Pathétique_ is in C minor. The Op. 1 No. 3 (1795) was in C minor. The Fifth Symphony (1808) is in C minor. The _Coriolan_ Overture (1807) is in C minor. The last piano sonata, Op. 111 (1822), is in C minor. C minor is Beethoven's documented key of struggle-with-stakes — what scholars call his _C-minor character_ , after Lewis Lockwood's discussion of the recurring tonal preoccupation. We will return to this signature in Chapter 3 (the Op. 1 No. 3), Chapter 9 (Symphony 5), and Chapter 16 (Op. 111). The _Pathétique_ is the early stop on this thread.

### 2.4 The Stories

#### How a 28-year-old established himself on a title page

Beethoven was twenty-eight years old when he composed the _Pathétique_ in 1798. He had been in Vienna for six years. He had published the Op. 1 piano trios in 1795 (Chapter 3 of this volume) and the Op. 2 piano sonatas in 1796. He was the most-promising young virtuoso-composer in Vienna, regularly performing in the salons of the Lichnowskys, the Razumovskys, the Lobkowiczes, the Esterházys-and-friends. He was also, even then, difficult: documented stories from this period include the moment when he stormed out of a Lichnowsky soirée because a French officer asked him to play, the moment when he refused to play for a noble visitor at all, the moment when Haydn (with whom he had been studying since 1792) complimented an early work and Beethoven retorted that Haydn had not actually understood it. The composer's public face in 1798 was a young man who had decided his music would not be servant to its audience — the audience would meet the music on the music's terms.

Publishing a piano sonata titled _Grande Sonate Pathétique_ in 1799 was consistent with this public face. The title was a statement about the work's seriousness. The slow introduction — a device imported from symphonic ambition into the keyboard genre — was a structural statement about the same thing. Beethoven was saying: _this is the high-dramatic register, in the domestic medium, and I am claiming both at once._

The work was an immediate popular success. The Eder edition sold quickly. Within a few years the _Pathétique_ was a standard repertoire piece for Vienna's amateur pianists, and by 1810 it had been published by multiple firms in Paris, London, and Leipzig. Beethoven had successfully claimed the dramatic register in the domestic genre. The path was now open to _Moonlight_ (1801), _Waldstein_ (1804), _Appassionata_ (1805), and eventually _Hammerklavier_ (1818) and Op. 111 (1822). The _Pathétique_ opened the gate.

#### Charles Rosen on the slow introduction

Charles Rosen, in _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas: A Short Companion_ (Yale University Press, 2002) — the source-of-record for piano-sonata analysis in this volume — devotes several pages to the _Pathétique_ 's slow introduction. His central observation: Beethoven uses the Grave not as a _prologue_ to the Allegro but as an _interrogation_ of it. The introduction's slow heavy chords, returning at the start of the development and again before the coda, _interrupt_ the Allegro's forward motion. The Allegro is the body of the work, but the body keeps getting checked by the slow introduction's weighted return — as if the music's own gravity keeps reasserting itself.

Rosen contrasts this with Haydn's use of slow introductions, where the introduction appears once at the start and is forgotten thereafter. Beethoven's innovation, Rosen argues, was to keep the introduction _present_ as a recurring counterweight to the fast movement's drive. The dramatic frame is not abandoned once the body begins; it returns, and each return reshapes what the body means.

This is the kind of structural observation the book will use Rosen for throughout. When we get to Op. 106 _Hammerklavier_ in Chapter 15 and to Op. 111 in Chapter 16, Rosen will be the anchor voice for the structural analyses.

#### Three readings of the same opening

**Emil Gilels, 1980 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Gilels was a Russian pianist of the Soviet golden age — Sviatoslav Richter's contemporary and rival, a Heinrich Neuhaus student, a pianist whose recorded Beethoven sonata cycle (largely Deutsche Grammophon, 1972–1984) remained incomplete at his death in 1985 but is widely regarded as one of the great cycles of the recording era. His _Pathétique_ is the model 1980s reading: heavy, dignified, structurally clear, with the Grave taken slowly and weighted, the Allegro fast but never rushed, every voice in the texture audible. The recording sound is the late-DG analog Berlin sound — present, full, slightly warm. If you are going to keep one _Pathétique_ in your collection, this is the safest first choice.

**Wilhelm Kempff, 1965 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Kempff was a German pianist of an earlier generation — born 1895, eighty-nine years older than Gilels at the time of his death. His Beethoven sonata cycles (two complete: 1951–56 mono, 1964–65 stereo) are among the most-loved interpretations of the postwar era. Kempff's _Pathétique_ is lighter, faster (about eight and a half minutes versus Gilels's nine), more singing. The Grave is dramatic but less _heavy_ ; the Allegro is propulsive but more _speaking_ than _driving_. Kempff does not take the exposition repeat, which is part of why his reading is shorter. Some listeners prefer Kempff for the _clarity_ of the lines; some prefer Gilels for the _weight_ of the drama. The two readings are different aesthetic answers to the same question.

**Maria João Pires, 2002 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Pires is a Portuguese pianist whose Beethoven sonata cycle (selected recordings, DG, 1990s–2000s) emerged later in her career as a complement to her Mozart and Schubert work. Her _Pathétique_ is the most _intimate_ of the three — Pires plays the Allegro slightly slower than Gilels, lets the second theme breathe more, brings out the lyrical voice inside the dramatic frame. Her Grave returns at 5:00 and 8:00 are particularly weighted; she lingers on the slowness. Some listeners find Pires's reading too gentle for a work titled _Pathétique_ ; others find it the most musical of the three because she refuses to mistake drama for force. The reading is unambiguously different from both Gilels and Kempff.

The three pianists give you the work-vs-interpretation triangle in early form. You will be holding more readings of more works as the book proceeds — by Chapter 16 you will be holding four or five readings of Op. 111. The triangle starts here.

#### Cross-piece links

* If the dramatic slow introduction interests you, the natural next stop is **Symphony No. 1 in C major, Op. 21 (1800)** — Beethoven's first symphony, which also opens with a slow introduction (Adagio molto) before the Allegro. We will hear it in Chapter 5.
* If the C-minor character interests you, the natural next stop is the **Piano Trio in C minor, Op. 1 No. 3 (1795)** — the work that announced Beethoven's C-minor preoccupation, composed three years before the _Pathétique_. We will hear it in Chapter 3.
* If the motivic-development technique interests you, the natural next stop is the **Fifth Symphony (1808)** — where the same technique is taken to its most extreme. You have already heard it in Volume 1; we will return to all four movements in Chapter 9.
* If you want the next-most-famous "named" early sonata, listen to the **Moonlight (Op. 27 No. 2, 1801)** — also in C minor in the first movement, but operating in a completely different register (Adagio sostenuto throughout the first movement, where the _Pathétique_ alternates slow and fast). We will hear it in Chapter 4.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion to compare Gilels 1980, Kempff 1965, and Pires 2002 in the opening Grave — specifically how slowly each one takes the first chord, and what that opening choice says about how each pianist hears the rest of the movement._
* _Tell the companion which of the two later Grave returns (at 5:00 before the development, or at 8:00 before the coda) felt more dramatic to you on the second listen, and ask her about Charles Rosen's reading of why both returns matter structurally._
* _Ask the companion about the difference between dedications and titles in Beethoven's published sonatas — why the_ Pathétique _title was Beethoven's own but_ Moonlight _was Rellstab's invention. The conversation can run from Lichnowsky-the-dedicatee into the documented Beethoven-publisher relationships of the 1790s._
* _Tell the companion that this is your first close listen of the Beethoven Volume and ask her which of the next three chapters' anchor works (Op. 1 No. 3 trio, Moonlight, Symphony 2 Larghetto) she would recommend you sample first as a sanity check before continuing._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The _subito piano_ moment at 0:00–0:08 — the loud strike that drops immediately to softness. Once you can hear it once, you will hear it everywhere in Beethoven.
* The hammering left-hand octaves under the first theme of the Allegro, starting at 1:32. The octaves are not accompaniment in the genteel sense; they are _drum-stroke_ accompaniment, pushing the right hand's cell forward.
* The second theme at 2:50–3:30 — note that it is in E♭ minor (rather than the standard E♭ major), and notice how the _minor-key second theme_ keeps the dramatic temperature high rather than relaxing.
* The acceleration at 1:10–1:32 leading into the Allegro — the way the slow introduction breaks down under its own momentum rather than ending and being replaced.

### Going Deeper

If you read one piece of scholarship alongside this chapter, read **Charles Rosen's discussion of the _Pathétique_ in _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas: A Short Companion_ (Yale University Press, 2002), the chapter "The Early Sonatas."** Rosen's analyses are the source-of-record for piano-sonata structure in this volume; he is concise, technical without being forbidding, and consistently illuminating on the question of why Beethoven's compositional decisions work.

### Chapter Summary

The first movement of the _Pathétique_ is a sonata-form Allegro with a slow Grave introduction that returns twice in the body of the movement — once before the development (at 5:00 in Gilels) and once before the coda (at 8:00). The introduction is not a one-time entrance device but a recurring dramatic frame. The Allegro is built on a short two-note cell, developed across the exposition in the motivic-development style you met in Volume 1.

You learned that the title _Grande Sonate Pathétique_ was Beethoven's own (unlike most of the famous sonata nicknames, which came from publishers or later writers), and that publishing a piano sonata under such a dramatic title in 1799 was a statement of ambition — the high-dramatic register imported into the domestic genre.

You launched **Thread B** with the _dramatic slow introduction_ as the first documented Beethoven compositional signature. We will meet it again in Symphony 1 (Chapter 5), Symphony 7 (Chapter 11), and Op. 81a _Les Adieux_ (briefly in Chapter 7).

You launched the **C-minor character thread** with the _Pathétique_ 's home key. We will return to it in Chapter 3 (Op. 1 No. 3), Chapter 9 (Symphony 5), and Chapter 16 (Op. 111).

You worked the four-section listening loop — _Before You Listen_ , _The Listening Window_ , _What's Happening in the Music_ , _The Stories_ — on a single piece. From here, every chapter will run the same loop.

The next chapter takes you back in time, to Bonn 1770–1792 and early Vienna 1792–1798. The anchor is the Op. 1 No. 3 trio, the work that announced Beethoven's arrival in Vienna's professional musical life. After this you will know how Beethoven got to the Pathétique.

* * *

---

## Chapter 3: Bonn 1770–1792, Early Vienna 1792–1798, and the Op. 1 No. 3 Trio

In November 1792 a twenty-one-year-old Beethoven arrived in Vienna, with about a hundred ducats in his pocket from the Elector of Cologne, a letter of recommendation to Haydn, and a private resolve recorded in his pocket diary the day before he left Bonn. _"Receive Mozart's spirit from Haydn's hands,"_ the resolve ran in part, in the handwriting of Count Waldstein (the future dedicatee of Op. 53), who had inscribed the line into Beethoven's autograph book to send him off.

Three years later, in the autumn of 1795, that twenty-four-year-old Beethoven held a private performance at Prince Karl von Lichnowsky's Vienna palace, in front of an invited audience that included Haydn himself. The program was the three Piano Trios that would shortly be published as Beethoven's Op. 1. The third of the three, the Trio in C minor, was, by documented contemporary accounts (including Ferdinand Ries's later _Biographische Notizen_), the most ambitious of the set. Haydn, after the performance, reportedly recommended that Beethoven _not_ publish the C-minor trio yet, on the grounds that the public was not ready for it.

The advice has been discussed by every Beethoven biographer since Wegeler-Ries. Lewis Lockwood, Maynard Solomon, and Jan Swafford all read the moment differently. Lockwood treats Haydn's advice as professional caution — the older composer protecting a younger composer from a piece the Vienna public might find harsh. Solomon treats it as the first documented friction between Beethoven and his teacher, the moment when Beethoven begins to suspect Haydn does not understand what he is doing. Swafford gives the moment its full dramatic weight: this is the work where Beethoven first announces what later scholars will call his _C-minor character_ , and Haydn, hearing it, recognizes that something new is in the room and counsels prudence — possibly out of genuine care, possibly out of unease.

Beethoven published the trio anyway. In 1795. All three trios at once. By 1796 the set was in print at Artaria and selling well. The C-minor trio's reputation as the boldest of the three only grew. Robert Schumann would later call the Op. 1 No. 3 _the first true Beethoven_. The work has been argued ever since.

This chapter is the work, and the man behind it. We will start with the Bonn years, walk through the move to Vienna and the early Vienna life, and then sit with the C-minor trio's first movement. The trio is the anchor; the biography is the frame.

### 3.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — what the Lichnowsky soirée actually was

In 1795 the Lichnowsky palace at Krugerstrasse 1067 in Vienna held a Friday-morning concert series for invited guests. Prince Karl von Lichnowsky was a Silesian-born nobleman, married to Maria Christiane von Thun (whose mother had been a patron of Mozart), a passionate amateur musician who played the piano well and the violin adequately. He had taken Beethoven into his home in 1794, given him an annuity of 600 florins, and made him the resident composer-virtuoso of the Friday concerts.

The Friday-morning concerts were not public. They were drawing-room performances for an audience of perhaps twenty to forty invited guests — Viennese nobility, visiting musicians, members of the Imperial court. They were the place where new chamber music was tested before publication. The composer-virtuoso would play, often with named guest instrumentalists; the audience would listen attentively, drink coffee, give immediate verbal feedback. This was the system through which Vienna's chamber-music repertoire was made.

Haydn was at one of these Friday mornings in autumn 1795, when Beethoven played through the three Op. 1 trios. Haydn was sixty-three years old, the most famous living composer in Europe, recently returned from the second London visit, the author of more than a hundred symphonies, dozens of string quartets, and the new oratorio that would shortly become _The Creation_. He had been teaching Beethoven counterpoint since 1793 and had, by documented Beethoven correspondence, found the experience difficult — the younger composer was talented, ambitious, and not always grateful for corrections.

Haydn's advice that the C-minor trio not be published yet has been transmitted through Ferdinand Ries's _Biographische Notizen_ (1838), co-authored with Franz Wegeler. Ries was Beethoven's pupil from about 1801 onward and was reporting an anecdote he had been told. It is a single-source claim, traced through a sympathetic informant whose later memoir is broadly reliable but who has his own perspective. Modern scholars (Lockwood, Solomon) treat the anecdote as probably accurate in substance but caution against over-reading the specific words. What is certain: the trio was performed, Haydn was present, Beethoven published the work anyway, and the relationship with Haydn cooled measurably afterward. By 1796 Beethoven was no longer studying with Haydn.

Listen to the C-minor trio's first movement with this scene in your imagination: a Friday morning in a Vienna drawing room in 1795, twenty or thirty guests, a piano-violin-cello trio at the front of the room, the most famous composer in Europe in the audience, and a twenty-four-year-old Beethoven at the keyboard playing a work that was about to start an argument that would last two centuries.

#### What to notice

Three things.

  1. **The opening unison.** The trio opens with all three instruments, piano, violin, cello, playing the same C-minor scale figure in unison. Not in harmony. _Unison._ This was unusual for an Op. 1 piano trio in 1795. Trios in Haydn's hands typically opened with a piano statement that the strings then answered or supported. Beethoven opens with all three voices saying the same thing at the same time, in C minor, with a dramatic dynamic and a pointed rhythm. Listen for the _gravity_ of the opening: three instruments speaking as one.

  2. **The aggressive piano writing.** The piano part throughout this movement is unusually demanding. Beethoven was establishing himself as a piano virtuoso, and the piano writing in Op. 1 No. 3 is the showcase. Listen for the _density_ of the piano part: fast figuration, big chords, full octaves, no resting moments. The piano is not the polite accompaniment to a string duet; it is the lead voice, sometimes against the strings, sometimes with them, but always demanding.

  3. **The dramatic argument.** This is a sonata-form first movement, exposition, development, recapitulation, but the entire movement is in a _dramatic register_ unusual for a piano trio in 1795. Listen for the _seriousness_ : this is not entertainment music. The work has the ambition of a symphony, in the medium of a chamber trio. This is what would later be called the _C-minor character_ arriving for the first time in Beethoven's catalogue.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the Op. 1 piano trios between 1793 and 1795, premiered them at the Lichnowsky palace in autumn 1795, and published them through Artaria in Vienna in 1795 — the first published works in his official catalogue, dedicated to Prince Lichnowsky.
