### 3.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into the Beaux Arts 1965 recording.

* **0:00–0:05.** The opening unison. All three instruments play the same C-minor figure together. Notice the _force_ : this is not a delicate Friday-morning opening; it is a declaration. The figure itself is short, ascending, with a sharp dotted rhythm. Beethoven is announcing the key and the temperament in five seconds.

* **0:05–0:25.** The piano takes the lead, developing the opening figure. The strings answer. Notice that the texture is _thick_ : Beethoven is not letting any of the three voices rest. This is a chamber-music density unusual for 1795.

* **0:25–0:50.** The first theme proper. A more sustained melodic line emerges in the piano, accompanied by the strings. But notice that the _dramatic temperature_ does not drop. The first theme is built on the opening figure's contour; the cell is becoming a theme.

* **0:50–1:35.** The transition to the second theme. The music modulates restlessly, the texture stays thick, the dynamic level varies but does not drop into ease. Notice the _forward drive_ : there is no point in the first 90 seconds where the music settles into placidity.

* **1:35–2:30.** The second theme arrives, in E♭ major (the relative major of C minor: the standard sonata-form key relationship). This is a more lyrical theme, sung by the violin, with the cello and piano supporting. But notice: this is a Beethoven C-minor work; that even the second theme has _backbone_. It is lyrical but not gentle; it sings but with weight.

* **2:30–3:10.** Development of the second theme, leading toward the close of the exposition. Listen for the way Beethoven keeps the C-minor temperament present even in the major-key contrasting section: there are dark inflections, minor-mode shadows, moments where the music slides briefly back into C-minor territory before pulling back to E♭ major.

* **3:10–3:30.** The closing material of the exposition. Returns of the opening cell, fast piano figuration, the exposition closing decisively in E♭ major.

* **3:30.** The exposition ends. Beaux Arts takes the repeat: the entire exposition (back to 0:00) plays again. The repeat is important: you hear the opening unison twice, and the second hearing is different because you now know what follows.

* **6:25.** **The development section begins.** The opening figure is now taken apart. Listen for fragments of the cell in different keys, in different instruments, in different rhythmic positions. The development is short but intense: about ninety seconds of restless transformation.

* **6:25–8:00.** Development continues. The music passes through several keys (G minor, F minor, D♭ major briefly), with the cell never leaving the texture for more than a few seconds. Notice how _unified_ the development is: this is not a workshop on multiple themes, but a workshop on the single opening figure.

* **8:00.** **The recapitulation begins.** The opening unison returns, in C minor, with the full force of the original opening. Notice that the return is _meaningful_ in a way the opening could not be: you have now heard the cell put through ninety seconds of development, and its return intact is the structural payoff.

* **8:00–9:30.** The recapitulation walks through the same material as the exposition, but now everything in C minor (or relative areas around C minor: the second theme returns in C major rather than E♭ major, the standard sonata-form move). Notice that the C-major second-theme return is the _one_ place where the dramatic temperature drops slightly. Beethoven allows a brief major-key consolation before the coda.

* **9:30–10:00.** The coda. A short closing passage built on the cell, returning the movement to C minor for its decisive close. Listen for the way the coda _re-asserts_ the dramatic frame after the brief major-key consolation: the work ends in C minor, not consoled.

About ten minutes total in Beaux Arts. The cell appears in nearly every measure. The dramatic temperature stays high throughout, with only one brief consolation in the recapitulation's second theme.

### 3.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Definition: The C-minor character (Thread B launch)

The _C-minor character_ is the term modern Beethoven scholarship uses for a recurring set of compositional features that Beethoven brings to bear on his C-minor works specifically. The features include:

* A _dramatic urgency_ in the opening material (often unison openings, sharp rhythmic figures, dotted patterns)
* A _refusal of easy consolation_ in the secondary material (the second themes stay tense rather than relaxing)
* A _long buildup of tension_ that resolves only at structural moments (development climaxes, recapitulation returns, coda)
* A _concluding ambivalence_ — some C-minor works end in C minor (Op. 1 No. 3, Coriolan), some end in C major as a hard-won triumph (Symphony 5, Op. 111 second movement), but the C-major triumph is never easy
* A particular attention to _texture_ — heavy, full, often unison or octave-doubled writing

The features are attested across multiple works. Lewis Lockwood discusses the C-minor character explicitly in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_. Charles Rosen discusses it in _The Classical Style_ (1971) and in _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas_ (2002), particularly in the analyses of Op. 13 _Pathétique_ , Op. 37 Piano Concerto No. 3, and Op. 111.

In plain language: when Beethoven wrote in C minor, he was reaching for a specific dramatic register — what nineteenth-century critics would have called _tragic urgency_. Three of his most-loved instrumental works share this register: Op. 1 No. 3 (1795), the _Pathétique_ (1798, Chapter 2), and the Fifth Symphony (1808, Chapter 9). Two more are at the peaks of his career: the Piano Concerto No. 3 in C minor (1800, briefly mentioned in Chapter 5), and the Sonata Op. 111 in C minor (1822, Chapter 16). The C-minor character is one of the most consistent compositional preoccupations across his forty-five-year creative life.

This chapter is the first time we name it. Each subsequent C-minor anchor will name it again with its current context. By Chapter 16 the thread will be complete; by Chapter 21 we will summarize the full set of documented Beethoven signatures, with the C-minor character at the head of the list.

#### What was new in 1795

The Op. 1 No. 3 trio is not the first piano trio in C minor. Haydn wrote some; Mozart wrote some. What was new in 1795 was the _dramatic register_ and the _piano-virtuoso ambition_ combined into one work.

Haydn's piano trios, which Beethoven studied closely under Haydn's tuition, were sophisticated chamber works, but they were not dramatic _statements_. They were drawing-room art, made for the salon, with the keyboard often as a polite accompanist to the string voices. Mozart's piano trios were similar in scale and register. Beethoven took the genre and _symphonized_ it: bigger gestures, more piano virtuosity, more dramatic temperature, more structural ambition. The Op. 1 trios were, in effect, the first piano trios to insist on being heard as _major works_. This is what Haydn, by the documented reporting, was hearing when he recommended that the C-minor trio not be published yet. The work was outside the genre's expectations.

The next twenty years of piano-trio writing would respond to this. Schubert's trios (the great D. 898 of 1827 and D. 929 of 1827) inherit Beethoven's symphonic ambition. The Mendelssohn trios, the Brahms trios, the Tchaikovsky trio — all of them are descendants of what Beethoven did in Op. 1.

#### Definition: Unison opening (Thread B preview)

You met a Beethoven unison opening in Volume 1 Chapter 3 — the four-note hammer of Symphony 5, where all the strings and clarinets play the same notes together with no harmonic accompaniment. The opening of Op. 1 No. 3 is an earlier example of the same device. The unison opening is a recurring Beethoven signature. We will name it again in Chapter 9 (Symphony 5) and Chapter 18 (the _Schreckensfanfare_ opening of the Ninth Symphony's finale).

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume Beethoven's _early period_ means works that sound like polite Haydn-Mozart with occasional Beethovenian edges. Op. 1 No. 3 corrects this. The work is already, in 1795, _unmistakably Beethoven_ — the C-minor temperament, the dense piano writing, the symphonic ambition in a chamber genre. The label _early_ applies to the period (before 1802 by Lenz's framework) but not to the artistic register; the artistic register is already mature.

This is one of the listening habits Chapter 1 promised to unlearn: _the early works are warm-ups_. They are not. The _Pathétique_ you heard in Chapter 2 is a major work. Op. 1 No. 3 is a major work. The Op. 18 quartets that we will mention in Chapter 12 are major works. The "early" tag captures the _chronological position_ of the works, not their stature.

### 3.4 The Stories

#### Bonn 1770–1792

Beethoven was born in Bonn in December 1770, the second-born but eldest-surviving child of Johann van Beethoven and Maria Magdalena Keverich. His baptismal record dates from 17 December 1770; the actual birthdate (probably 15 or 16 December) was not recorded.

Bonn was the residency of the Electorate of Cologne, a small German principality with a court that maintained a professional musical establishment. Beethoven's grandfather Ludwig (after whom he was named) had been the Kapellmeister of the Bonn court — the most prestigious musical position in the city. Beethoven's father Johann was a tenor in the court chapel and a music teacher, but a less successful figure: progressively alcoholic, financially unstable, increasingly difficult as a husband and father. The documented stories from Beethoven's childhood include episodes of Johann waking the young Ludwig in the middle of the night to practice, of Johann pushing Ludwig to perform publicly as a Mozart-style child prodigy (with mixed results), and of Johann's gradually worsening alcohol problem that by the mid-1780s had effectively made the teenage Ludwig the family's primary breadwinner.

The Bonn court provided the structure within which Beethoven's musical education actually happened. From 1779 he studied with Christian Gottlob Neefe, the court organist — a serious, well-trained musician who introduced Beethoven to Bach's _Well-Tempered Clavier_ (which Beethoven would still be studying in 1820). Neefe wrote about his pupil in the _Magazin der Musik_ of 1783, predicting that Ludwig would become "a second Mozart if he progresses as he began." By 1784 Beethoven was the assistant court organist at fourteen; by 1789 he was earning enough at the court to support the family after his mother's death in 1787 and his father's effective incapacitation.

In 1787 Beethoven traveled to Vienna for a short visit — the documented Mozart meeting. The evidence for what happened at this meeting is thin. Otto Jahn's _W. A. Mozart_ (1856) reports a story (told to Jahn by a Bohemian musician, Johann Schenk, who claimed to have heard it from Beethoven himself) that Beethoven played for Mozart, Mozart was not at first impressed, Beethoven asked for a theme on which to improvise, Mozart gave one, Beethoven improvised, and Mozart said to friends afterward: _"Watch him; this young man will make a name for himself in the world."_ The story is widely repeated and is treated with caution by modern Beethoven biographers — Lockwood notes that the chain of transmission (Beethoven → Schenk → Jahn → us) is long enough that the exact words cannot be relied on. What is certain: Beethoven did visit Vienna briefly in spring 1787, before his mother's terminal illness called him back to Bonn. He may have met Mozart. He probably did not study with Mozart in any extended sense.

The 1792 move to Vienna was permanent. Funded by the Elector Maximilian Franz (the youngest brother of Marie Antoinette and Emperor Joseph II, who had inherited the Cologne electorate in 1784 and was a patron of the arts), Beethoven left Bonn in November 1792, arriving in Vienna with the Waldstein autograph-book inscription and the letter of introduction to Haydn. He never returned to Bonn — the French Revolutionary armies overran the Rhineland in 1794, and Maximilian Franz fled into exile shortly afterward. Beethoven would correspond with old Bonn friends for the rest of his life but would not see Bonn again.

#### The Vienna patronage system 1792–1798

Beethoven arrived in Vienna at a particular moment in the history of the city's musical patronage. The traditional system, court patronage of a resident _Kapellmeister_ , had been the standard for the previous century. Haydn had spent thirty years at the Esterházy court. Mozart had tried to make this system work and had largely failed; he had been forced to support himself through public concerts, teaching, and publication. By 1792 the Habsburg court's musical patronage was in decline (the chapel was being reorganized; the imperial musical establishment was contracting). The new system, emerging in Vienna in the 1790s, was _aristocratic-private_ : a network of music-loving noble families who maintained private orchestras or chamber establishments, hosted concerts in their palaces, and supported individual composers through dedications, annuities, and commissions.

Beethoven became, in his first six years in Vienna, the most-prominent beneficiary of this new system. His major patrons in this period:

* **Prince Karl von Lichnowsky (1761–1814)** : the chief patron, hosted Beethoven in his palace from 1794, gave him a 600-florin annuity, dedicatees of Op. 1 (trios), Op. 13 (_Pathétique_), and Op. 26 (sonata).
* **Count Andreas Razumovsky (1752–1836)** : the Russian ambassador to Vienna, an amateur violinist who maintained a string quartet, eventual dedicatee of the Op. 59 quartets (Chapter 12).
* **Prince Joseph Franz Maximilian Lobkowicz (1772–1816)** : younger than Beethoven by two years, an enormously wealthy nobleman with a private orchestra, dedicatee of Op. 18 (string quartets), Op. 55 (_Eroica_), Op. 67 (Symphony 5), and many others.
* **Baron Gottfried van Swieten (1733–1803)** : older patron, Imperial librarian, the man who had revived Bach and Handel for the Vienna audience in the 1780s, hosted Sunday Bach-and-Handel concerts that Beethoven attended.

This network was the structure within which Beethoven's first decade in Vienna unfolded. The Lichnowsky Friday-morning concerts were the place where new chamber works were premiered. The Lobkowicz palace was the place where new orchestral works were tested. The van Swieten Sunday meetings were the place where Beethoven was exposed to Bach and Handel as living musical resources (rather than historical curiosities). By 1798, when the _Pathétique_ was composed, Beethoven had a full network of patrons supporting his work, a regular performing schedule, and a publication relationship with Artaria. He was the most-promising young composer in Vienna's most active period.

#### The Haydn studies and their cooling

Beethoven was nominally Haydn's pupil from late 1792 through about 1794. The lessons were in counterpoint — the technical discipline of writing multiple independent musical lines that fit together — and used the standard textbook of the period, Johann Joseph Fux's _Gradus ad Parnassum_ (1725). The documented Haydn correspondence shows that the lessons were not happy. Haydn was a careful but not relentlessly engaged teacher; Beethoven was a talented but not patient pupil. Haydn would mark exercises, return them with corrections, and Beethoven would sometimes ignore the corrections, sometimes argue with them. By early 1794 Beethoven was secretly taking parallel lessons with Johann Schenk, who would correct exercises before Beethoven submitted them to Haydn — the documented story is from Schenk's later memoir and is not fully verifiable, but Lockwood treats it as plausible.

By the time Haydn departed for his second London visit in January 1794, the formal teacher-pupil relationship was effectively over. Beethoven continued counterpoint studies with Johann Georg Albrechtsberger (a respected theorist at St. Stephen's Cathedral) and with Antonio Salieri (specifically for Italian vocal writing). But Haydn remained, professionally, the older composer Beethoven had to position himself in relation to. The dedication of Op. 2 (1796) — three piano sonatas, dedicated _To Joseph Haydn, Doctor of Music, by his pupil Ludwig van Beethoven_ — was the most explicit public framing of the relationship.

What is important for the Op. 1 No. 3 reading: by autumn 1795, when Haydn heard the C-minor trio at Lichnowsky's, Haydn and Beethoven had been through a difficult two years. Haydn's advice to delay publication — whatever it was exactly — was professional counsel delivered in the context of a teaching relationship that had not gone well. Beethoven heard the advice, considered it, and published the trio anyway. The pattern (an older authority offers counsel; Beethoven listens; Beethoven proceeds with his own judgment) would repeat many times across his career.

#### Three readings of the Op. 1 No. 3

**Beaux Arts Trio, 1965 (Philips).** The Beaux Arts Trio was an American chamber ensemble — pianist Menahem Pressler with a rotating lineup of violinists and cellists — that was, from its founding in 1955 through Pressler's last performances in 2008, one of the great piano trios of the recording era. Their first complete Beethoven trio cycle (Philips, 1964–65) is the standard reference recording for these works. The Op. 1 No. 3 in Beaux Arts is dramatic, structurally clear, with all three voices in balance. Pressler at the piano gives the work its weight; the strings (Daniel Guilet on violin, Bernard Greenhouse on cello) play with the steady, classical Beaux Arts sound. This is the model Beethoven trio reading of the 1960s — sober, structurally serious, perfectly balanced. If you keep one _Op. 1 No. 3_ in your collection, start here.

**Florestan Trio, 2004 (Hyperion).** The Florestan Trio was a British ensemble — pianist Susan Tomes with violinist Anthony Marwood and cellist Richard Lester — active from 1995 to 2012. Their Beethoven trio cycle (Hyperion, 2002–2007) is one of the most-praised modern readings of the works. The Op. 1 No. 3 in Florestan is faster, more articulate, with sharper rhythmic profile than Beaux Arts. The opening unison is more _bitten_ ; the development section is more _driven_. Florestan is the more contemporary reading — more aware of the period-performance discussions that have shaped early-music thinking since the 1980s, even as the ensemble plays modern instruments.

**Trio Wanderer, 2012 (Harmonia Mundi).** Trio Wanderer is a French ensemble — Vincent Coq (piano), Jean-Marc Phillips-Varjabédian (violin), Raphaël Pidoux (cello) — active since 1987. Their Beethoven trio cycle (Harmonia Mundi, 2009–2012) is a contemporary reading with a French sense of color and lightness that contrasts with the German weight of Beaux Arts. The Op. 1 No. 3 in Wanderer is the most _transparent_ of the three — the textures are opened up, the inner voices are more audible, the dramatic temperature is high but never thickened. Wanderer makes Beethoven sound _Mozartean-with-edge_ , which is in some ways closer to the 1795 Vienna context than the heavier German tradition.

The three readings give you the work-vs-interpretation register that the volume will use throughout. Beaux Arts is the model 1960s reading. Florestan is the model 2000s reading. Wanderer is the model contemporary French reading. Together they give you the range of how the work has been heard across the recording era.

#### Cross-piece links

* If the C-minor character interests you, the natural next stop is the **Pathétique** (Op. 13, 1798, Chapter 2 of this volume — already heard). Beyond that, **the Piano Concerto No. 3 in C minor, Op. 37 (1800–1803)** — Beethoven's only C-minor piano concerto, the work that bridges the early-Vienna Op. 1 and the heroic-middle Op. 55. Mentioned briefly in Chapter 5; not anchored in this volume but rewarding direct attention.
* If the dense piano writing interests you, the natural next stop is the **Piano Sonata Op. 2 No. 3 in C major (1796)** — also dedicated to Haydn, also packed with piano virtuosity, the sonata where Beethoven was demonstrating to his teacher what the next generation of piano writing would look like.
* If the chamber-symphonic ambition interests you, the natural next stop is the **Op. 18 string quartets (1798–1800)** — six works that did for the string quartet what Op. 1 did for the piano trio. We will hear Op. 18 in paragraph-level overview in Chapter 12.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the Haydn-Beethoven counterpoint lessons of 1792–1794 and what the documented record (Beethoven's exercise books, marked by Haydn) actually tells us about how the teaching went. The companion can walk you through the Lockwood account, which is closer to careful and complicated than the heroic-versus-jealous-older-teacher caricature._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the Op. 1 No. 3 first movement felt most_ unmistakably Beethoven _to you, and ask her where else in his catalogue she hears the same gesture. The conversation can run from the opening unison into Symphony 5, or from the dense piano writing into Op. 2 No. 3, depending on what you point at._
* _Ask the companion about the 1787 Mozart meeting in Vienna — what the actual evidence is, what is reliably documented, what the "watch him" quotation depends on, and why modern biographers (Lockwood, Solomon, Swafford) treat the story with caution._
* _Have the companion recommend one or two further early-period Beethoven chamber works (not the trios, not the sonatas, not yet the quartets) for sampling. Good candidates include the Cello Sonatas Op. 5 (1796) and the Violin Sonatas Op. 12 (1798)._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The opening unison at 0:00–0:05 — all three instruments speaking together in C minor. Compare this with the opening of Symphony 5's first movement, which uses the same unison-orchestra device on a much larger scale.
* The second theme at 1:35–2:30 — in E♭ major but never relaxing. Notice how the C-minor temperament keeps surfacing in the major-key contrasting section.
* The development section at 6:25–8:00 — the cell from the opening passes through several keys without ever leaving the texture. Try to track which instrument has the cell at each moment.
* The recapitulation's second theme around 8:30 — now in C major rather than E♭ major. This is the one place in the movement where the dramatic temperature drops briefly.

### Going Deeper

If you read one chapter of scholarship alongside this one, read **Jan Swafford,_Beethoven: Anguish and Triumph_ (Houghton Mifflin Harcourt, 2014), the chapters on Bonn and on the first Vienna years (roughly chapters 1–10).** Swafford is the narrative-voice biographer of contemporary Beethoven scholarship; his prose is gripping and his Bonn chapters give you the childhood and education in detail that few other modern biographies match. For the early Vienna years specifically, Swafford pairs well with Lockwood's _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (2003), which is more analytical but less novelistic.

### Chapter Summary

The Piano Trio in C minor, Op. 1 No. 3, composed in 1793–1795 and published in 1795, was Beethoven's announcement of arrival in Vienna's professional chamber-music scene. The first movement is a sonata-form Allegro that opens with a unison statement from all three instruments, piano, violin, cello, in C minor, then proceeds through dense piano writing and a dramatic argument that maintains its temperature across the full ten minutes.

The work launched **the C-minor character** — Beethoven's compositional preoccupation with a specific dramatic register that he would revisit through the _Pathétique_ (Chapter 2), the Piano Concerto No. 3, the Fifth Symphony (Chapter 9), the _Coriolan_ Overture, and the final Sonata Op. 111 (Chapter 16). The Op. 1 No. 3 is the first stop on this thread.

You met the Bonn 1770–1792 biography — court childhood with an alcoholic father, education with Christian Neefe at the court, the 1787 Vienna trip, the 1792 permanent move funded by Maximilian Franz. You met the Vienna patronage system that supported Beethoven's first decade: Lichnowsky, Razumovsky, Lobkowicz, van Swieten. You met the cooling of the Haydn studies, which had ended by 1794, and the autumn-1795 Lichnowsky soirée where Haydn heard the C-minor trio and counseled against publication.

You met three readings of the work, Beaux Arts 1965, Florestan 2004, Trio Wanderer 2012, and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter walks back to the piano sonata and meets two more famous early-period sonatas, the _Moonlight_ and the _Waldstein_ , alongside the _Pathétique_ you already know. Three Beethoven sonatas; three different title-attributions; one composer's first-decade piano-virtuoso ambition at its peak.

## Chapter 4: Early Piano Sonatas — _Moonlight_ , _Waldstein_ , with the _Pathétique_ in Memory

In 1801 Beethoven published two piano sonatas under the joint title _Sonata quasi una fantasia_ (Sonata in the manner of a fantasy), Op. 27. The second of the two — in C♯ minor, with a slow first movement of unusual contour — was dedicated to Countess Giulietta Guicciardi, a young pupil and apparent romantic interest of Beethoven's at the time. The work was an immediate hit. It was reprinted many times in the next decade. It was the work Beethoven was most likely to be asked to play in the salons.

In 1832, five years after Beethoven's death, the German poet and music critic Ludwig Rellstab published a fictionalized account of the work in which the first movement evoked moonlight on the waters of Lake Lucerne. The image was vivid; it stuck. By the 1850s the sonata was being printed in editions as the _Mondscheinsonate_ — the _Moonlight Sonata_. It has been _Moonlight_ ever since, in every language.

Beethoven never used the title. He died never knowing the sonata would carry it. He would, if the documented Beethoven temperament is any guide, have disliked the name — the Adagio sostenuto is a slow, intense, harmonically restless movement, and the moonlight-on-Lake-Lucerne image is, by the standards of the music itself, sentimental. But the title is what the world calls the work, and we are not going to fight it. We are going to listen to the work with the title in our ear and with awareness that the title is not Beethoven's.

This chapter pairs the _Moonlight_ with the _Waldstein_ (Op. 53, 1804), the sonata that marks the bridge from Beethoven's first decade in Vienna into the heroic middle period. The two works are stylistically miles apart — _Moonlight_ 's opening Adagio is one of the most introspective Beethoven movements; _Waldstein_ 's opening Allegro is one of the most extroverted. Heard together, they show the range of what Beethoven was doing in the first half-decade of the new century. The _Pathétique_ (1798, Chapter 2) is the precedent for both.

### 4.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — the title that wasn't Beethoven's

Ludwig Rellstab (1799–1860) was a Berlin music critic, poet, and friend of Schubert and Mendelssohn. By the 1830s he had become one of the most-read music writers in German-speaking Europe. In 1832 he published, in his journal _Iris im Gebiete der Tonkunst_ , a fictional vignette in which a young man rows his beloved out onto Lake Lucerne by moonlight, the moonlight reflects off the water, and the music in his head, the music that fits the scene, is the first movement of Beethoven's Op. 27 No. 2. Rellstab did not say Beethoven had this scene in mind. He invented the scene himself, and offered the music as its imaginative match.

The vignette was reprinted in collected editions of Rellstab's writing. By the 1850s the _Mondscheinsonate_ title was being used in published editions and in concert programs. By 1870 it was universal. The original publication title, _Sonata quasi una fantasia_ , survived only on the title page of the first edition and in catalogues.

What is the listener to do with this? The honest move is to know that the title is Rellstab's, not Beethoven's, and to listen to the music as music rather than as illustration of a romantic image. The Adagio sostenuto is _not_ moonlight-on-the-water music. It is C♯ minor, slow, with a constant triplet figuration in the right hand, a deep low-bass line, and a chorale-like melody in the upper voice that emerges gradually from the triplet texture. It is intense, harmonically restless, formally unusual (it is not in sonata form), and emotionally hard to name — _meditative_ may be the closest single word, but the meditation has weight, not lightness. The moonlight is Rellstab's projection. The music is more difficult than the projection allows.

The same warning applies to several other Beethoven sonata titles. _Pathétique_ (Op. 13) was Beethoven's own. _Moonlight_ (Op. 27 No. 2) was Rellstab's, 1832. _Appassionata_ (Op. 57) was the publisher Cranz's, 1838. _Waldstein_ (Op. 53) was Beethoven's _dedication_ to Count Waldstein, applied as a title by later editors. _Hammerklavier_ (Op. 106) was a German word Beethoven used on the title pages of Op. 101 and Op. 106 that stuck to Op. 106 specifically because of the work's scale. The titles are useful as identifiers but should not be confused with composer-authorized programs. Op. 27 No. 2 is not _about_ moonlight. It is a sonata in C♯ minor with an unusual three-movement structure. The title is a marketing convenience that gained the force of habit.

#### What to notice

Three things.

  1. **The triplet figuration.** The first movement is built on a continuous flow of triplet eighth notes in the right hand — three notes per beat, four beats per measure, twelve notes per measure, almost without pause through the entire movement. Listen for the _constancy_ of the triplet flow; it is the carpet on which everything else happens.

  2. **The bass line.** Underneath the triplets, the left hand plays a deep, slow bass line — usually one note per measure, sometimes two. The bass moves slowly and deliberately, and the harmonic motion of the movement is carried by the bass. Listen for the _weight_ of the bass; it is the gravity of the movement.

  3. **The chorale-like upper melody.** The top voice, usually the topmost note of each triplet group, outlines a slow, dignified melody that the listener gradually recognizes as the _tune_ of the movement. The melody is not stated separately; it emerges from inside the triplet texture. Listen for the _melody hidden inside the texture_ ; finding it is the listening assignment of the movement.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the _Moonlight_ sonata in 1801, published it in 1802 (the joint Op. 27 with _Sonata quasi una fantasia_ as the official title), and dedicated it to his then-pupil Giulietta Guicciardi (later Countess Gallenberg) — a relationship that was at least intense and probably romantic, though documentary evidence is limited.

### 4.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into Kempff 1965.

* **0:00–0:10.** The opening triplets in the right hand, the deep bass note in the left hand, no melody yet. Kempff takes the opening at a measured Adagio pace: not slow-slow-slow, but deliberate. The triplet pattern is established: G♯-C♯-E, G♯-C♯-E, the triplet rocking back and forth across three notes. The bass moves: a low octave C♯ at the bottom, sustained, then resolving to a higher pitch as the harmonies shift.

* **0:10–0:40.** The melody emerges. Listen for the topmost note of the triplet groups: a slow, descending line that starts on G♯ and gradually walks downward. The melody is _inside_ the triplet figuration; it does not announce itself as a separate voice. Kempff plays the melody with slight emphasis on the top notes: voicing the chorale out of the texture without breaking the triplet flow.

* **0:40–1:30.** The melody continues to unfold. Notice the _harmonic restlessness_ : the chord under the triplets is constantly changing. We are in C♯ minor, but Beethoven moves through related keys (E major, B major, F♯ minor) before circling back. The triplet pattern stays constant; only the bass and the harmony shift underneath.

* **1:30–2:10.** A more dissonant passage. Listen for the _tension_ : the harmonies darken, the bass descends into lower territory, the upper melody outlines a more chromatic line. This is the closest the movement comes to a _development_ section, but the form is not sonata form; the music is more _through-composed_ , with phases of intensity that come and go.

* **2:10–3:00.** A return to the opening texture. The triplet pattern is back, the bass is back at the low C♯, the harmony settles back into C♯ minor. Listen for the _feel of return_ : even though the movement is not in sonata form, Beethoven uses the opening material as a structural anchor that the music returns to after periods of harmonic wandering.

* **3:00–4:00.** The longest sustained passage of the movement. The upper melody is now more pronounced; the harmony walks through several remote keys before returning to C♯ minor. Notice the _quietness_ : the dynamic level stays very soft (_sotto voce_ , _sempre pianissimo_) throughout: Beethoven specifies this in the score. The movement is one of his most consistently quiet pieces; Kempff respects this absolutely.

* **4:00–5:00.** A more spacious section. The triplet pattern continues but the bass becomes more active, the harmony moves more rapidly. Listen for the _acceleration of harmonic motion_ even as the surface tempo stays the same: Beethoven is increasing the underlying density of the music without changing its outward pace.

* **5:00–6:00.** The closing passage. The triplet pattern continues; the bass returns to the low C♯; the harmony settles back into the home key. The movement ends quietly, with a final restatement of the opening texture and a soft cadence. Kempff lets the final chord ring before lifting the pedal.

About six minutes total in Kempff. The triplet pattern runs almost without break throughout. The dynamic level stays soft. The form is _through-composed_ with periodic returns to the opening texture — not sonata form, not rondo, not any of the standard formal patterns. Beethoven has invented the form for this particular piece.

### 4.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The architectural inversion of _Moonlight_

The standard Classical sonata layout — inherited from Haydn and Mozart, used by Beethoven in most of his sonatas — is _fast → slow → fast_. A dramatic Allegro first movement; a lyrical or songful slow movement; a brisk and often dance-like finale. The _Pathétique_ (Op. 13, Chapter 2) followed this pattern: dramatic Allegro with slow introduction, then a famous Adagio cantabile, then a fast Rondo.

The _Moonlight_ (Op. 27 No. 2) does something different. Its three movements are: _slow → middle → fast_. An Adagio sostenuto opening, a brisk Allegretto middle, then a fierce Presto agitato finale. The opening movement is the slow one. The closing movement is the fast one. The Classical layout has been _inverted_.

The inversion is structural. The first movement does not establish a tonic, develop tension, and dramatically resolve — the standard sonata-form work. It establishes an atmosphere and sustains it. The third movement (Presto agitato) carries the dramatic weight that the first movement would normally carry; it is the most fierce of the three, the one with the sonata-form energy. The slow movement has migrated from the middle position (its standard Classical place) to the opening position, and what was once at the opening, the dramatic Allegro, has migrated to the closing position.

This inversion is what the title page's _quasi una fantasia_ signals. A fantasy is a piece in which the formal expectations are loosened. Beethoven is telling the listener: _I am not writing a standard sonata; I am writing a sonata that behaves like a fantasy_. The work is still recognizably a sonata (three movements, the right tonal relationships, the standard chamber medium), but the layout has been reconfigured.

Why did Beethoven do this? Charles Rosen's _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas_ gives the technical answer: the Op. 27 sonatas (both of them — Op. 27 No. 1 is also a four-movement work with non-standard layout) are experiments in _connected form_ , in which the movements run together more freely than in the standard sonata. Op. 27 No. 1 has movements that segue without break. Op. 27 No. 2 keeps the movements separated but rearranges their function. The two sonatas are Beethoven's first serious attempts to _redesign the multi-movement sonata_. The redesign would continue in later works — Op. 53 _Waldstein_ would join its first and second movements by an attacca; Op. 101 would join several movements; Op. 110 would meditate freely between fugue and arioso. The Op. 27 works are the start.

#### The mood of the Adagio sostenuto

The first movement's _constant triplet texture_ is one of the most-discussed features in Beethoven's piano writing. The texture creates a _floating_ quality — the triplets do not have strong downbeats; they roll continuously, neither marching nor dancing. The bass line provides a weighted foundation; the melody emerges from the top voice without separating itself; the harmony walks through a series of minor-mode and related keys without ever settling.

What is the mood? Hard to name. Rellstab's _moonlight_ is one projection. Hector Berlioz wrote about the movement in 1860 and called it a _grief that consumes itself_. Edward Cone, the 20th-century music theorist, called it _meditation on a single rhythmic-textural idea_. Charles Rosen avoids one-word mood labels and writes simply that the movement is _contemplative without being placid_ — there is constant motion (the triplets), constant subtle harmonic change, constant pull on the listener's attention, but the surface is quiet and the dynamic is uniformly soft.

For the amateur listener, the most useful approach is to _not_ try to name the mood, but to follow the movement of the inner voices. The melody that emerges from the top notes of the triplets is the work's main material. The harmonic motion underneath is the work's structural skeleton. The triplet pattern is the work's medium of presentation. Listen to these three elements and the mood will take care of itself.

#### Definition: Through-composed form (preview)

Most of the multi-movement works in this book, symphonies, sonatas, quartets, use _sectional_ forms in which the movement is divided into clearly demarcated parts (exposition / development / recapitulation in sonata form; theme / variations in variation form; chorus / verse / chorus in song form). The _Moonlight_ first movement does not. The movement is _through-composed_ : the music flows continuously, with no sharp section boundaries, no repeats, no recapitulation in the strict sense. There are periodic returns to the opening material, but the returns are gestural rather than structural.

Through-composed form is rare in Beethoven's instrumental music but common in late Beethoven (the slow movements of Op. 132 and Op. 131 are essentially through-composed). The _Moonlight_ Adagio is an early example, in a sonata where the formal experimentation is the point.

We will not name through-composed form again until Chapter 20, when we meet the late quartets. For now, it is enough to notice that the movement does not follow standard formal patterns and that this is intentional.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes describe the _Moonlight_ Adagio as "the slow movement" of the sonata. It is not. It is the _first_ movement. The slow movement of a Classical sonata is the middle movement, and in Op. 27 No. 2 the middle movement is the Allegretto — a brisk dance-like piece, not slow at all. The Adagio sostenuto is in the _opening_ position, where you would expect a dramatic Allegro. This is the architectural inversion we discussed above.

The misconception matters because if you think of the Adagio as "the slow movement," you are reading the work according to the standard layout and missing what Beethoven actually did. The work _opens_ with the Adagio. The Allegretto is the _middle_. The Presto agitato is the _finale_ and the most dramatic of the three. The inversion is the whole point.

### 4.4 The Stories

#### Giulietta Guicciardi and the dedication

The Countess Giulietta Guicciardi (1782–1856) was the dedicatee of Op. 27 No. 2. She was a young Italian-Austrian noblewoman, a pupil of Beethoven's from about 1800, with whom Beethoven appears to have been romantically involved. The documented evidence comes from a letter Beethoven wrote in November 1801 to Franz Wegeler, his Bonn friend, in which Beethoven describes having fallen in love with a young woman who has changed his life: _"I am living somewhat more pleasantly now... a charming, fascinating girl who loves me and whom I love. For two years past I have again had a few happy moments, and this is the first time that I feel — that marriage could make me happy. Unfortunately, she is not of my rank — and now indeed I should not be able to marry."_

The young woman is widely identified by modern Beethoven scholars as Guicciardi, though Beethoven does not name her in the letter. The relationship did not lead to marriage. Guicciardi married Count Wenzel Robert von Gallenberg in 1803 and moved to Naples shortly after. Beethoven's reaction to the marriage is not directly documented, but the dedication of Op. 27 No. 2 (1802, the year before her marriage) and the famous _Immortal Beloved_ letter of July 1812 (still unaddressed but probably written to a different woman) suggest that Beethoven's romantic life in this period was difficult and unresolved.

What is important for the Op. 27 No. 2 reading: the work is dedicated to a young woman whom Beethoven loved, who married someone else within a year of the publication, and whose memory survives in the music's title even though the title itself is Rellstab's later invention. The work is, by chance of cultural transmission, the Beethoven sonata that has accumulated the most romantic-biographical projection, and the projections are mostly not historical. The historical core is one documented relationship, one dedication, and one publication. The rest is later commentary.

#### The Heiligenstadt Testament context

The _Moonlight_ was composed in 1801. The Heiligenstadt Testament — Beethoven's documented document of October 1802, in which he describes his despair over his deafness and his decision not to take his own life — was written the following year. We will hear the Testament in full in Chapter 5, alongside the early symphonies. For now, the relevant fact is that the _Moonlight_ sonata was composed in the period immediately before the hinge between early and middle Beethoven, and that the work's introspective opening movement is sometimes read (as in Solomon's _Beethoven_) as a foreshadowing of the inward turn that Heiligenstadt would crystallize. The reading is plausible but speculative; Lockwood treats it with caution. The work stands on its own without the biographical projection.

#### The _Waldstein_ — Op. 53, 1804

The _Waldstein_ sonata (Op. 53, 1804) was composed three years after the _Moonlight_ , dedicated to Count Ferdinand von Waldstein (the same patron who had inscribed the _receive Mozart's spirit from Haydn's hands_ line in Beethoven's autograph book in 1792), and stands at the threshold of the heroic middle period.

The work is the opposite of the _Moonlight_ in almost every respect. Where _Moonlight_ is C♯ minor, _Waldstein_ is C major. Where _Moonlight_ is slow, _Waldstein_ is fast. Where _Moonlight_ 's first movement is one continuous quiet triplet flow, _Waldstein_ 's first movement (Allegro con brio) is one of the most demanding Beethoven Allegros — full of fast repeated chords, brilliant figuration, abrupt dynamic contrasts, and a dramatic harmonic argument that ranges far from the home key.

The _Waldstein_ 's first movement (about eleven minutes in Pollini's 1997 recording) opens with a rapid pulsation of repeated chords in the right hand — _eight repeated eighth-note chords_ on the home C major, immediately answered by the same figure a step lower — and proceeds through a sonata-form argument in which the development section moves through a long sequence of distant keys before the recapitulation arrives. The second theme is famously in E major rather than the expected G major — a chromatic-third relationship that Beethoven was beginning to use in this period and would use much more boldly later (Op. 106 is full of it).

The Waldstein is _Beethoven's Hammerklavier in early form_. It is a piano sonata that demands a virtuoso pianist, that fills out a full Classical layout (three movements, fast-slow-fast), and that pushes the piano to its limits. After the _Waldstein_ (1804) and the _Appassionata_ (1805), the piano sonata in Beethoven's hands had become as ambitious a vehicle as the symphony. The transformation that began with the Op. 1 trios and continued through the _Pathétique_ was now complete.

We will return to the _Waldstein_ in Chapter 7, alongside the _Appassionata_ and _Les Adieux_ , in the context of the middle-period piano sonatas as a group. For now, listen to its first movement (Pollini 1997 is the recommended recording — fast, brilliantly accurate, structurally lucid) and notice the _distance_ between the _Moonlight_ 's Adagio and the _Waldstein_ 's Allegro. Three years separate them. They are different worlds.

#### Three readings of the _Moonlight_

**Wilhelm Kempff, 1965 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Kempff's _Moonlight_ is the standard postwar German reading — measured, dignified, with the Adagio at a deliberate tempo and the triplet flow respected as a continuous medium rather than dissolved into rubato. The chorale-melody is voiced clearly without being separated from the texture. The dynamic stays soft throughout, as Beethoven specified. The recording (part of Kempff's 1964–65 stereo cycle) is one of the most-loved Beethoven sonata readings of the 20th century.

**Daniel Barenboim, 1966 (EMI).** Barenboim's _Moonlight_ (recorded when he was twenty-four, the same age Beethoven was when he wrote the Op. 1 trios) is the precocious-young-virtuoso reading. The Adagio is taken with more rubato than Kempff, the melody is voiced more romantically, the dynamic varies more. Some listeners find this approach more _feeling_ ; others find it overinterpreted. The reading is one of the great early-career Barenboim documents; his later readings (he has recorded the cycle three times) are more sober.

**Maria João Pires, 2002 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Pires's _Moonlight_ is the most _introspective_ of the three. She takes the Adagio at the slowest tempo, lets the harmonic motion register fully, voices the chorale-melody with extreme care. The reading is unambiguously her own, Portuguese subtlety applied to German weight, and is the most-praised modern recording of the work.

These three give you the work-vs-interpretation triangle for the _Moonlight_. Each pianist takes a different stance on the central question: _how slow is the Adagio sostenuto, and how should the triplet flow be voiced?_ The answers diverge enough that the readings sound like three different pieces.

#### Cross-piece links

* If the architectural inversion interests you, the natural next stop is **Op. 27 No. 1** — the _other_ Op. 27 sonata, paired with the _Moonlight_ in publication, with an even more experimental layout (the movements run continuously into one another). Less famous than its sibling but rewards direct attention.
* If the through-composed form interests you, the natural next stop is the **slow movement of Op. 132 (Heiliger Dankgesang, 1825)** — also through-composed, also harmonically wandering, but on a much grander scale. We will hear it in Chapter 20.
* If the C-major brilliance of the _Waldstein_ interests you, the natural next stop is the **Piano Concerto No. 1 in C major, Op. 15 (1795–1800)** — Beethoven's first piano concerto, which has similar virtuoso piano writing in a lighter context.
* If the late-period continuation of these ambitions interests you, the natural next stop is the **Op. 109 in E major (1820)** — late Beethoven's reply to the early-period architectural experiments. We will hear it in Chapter 16.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the Rellstab moonlight invention of 1832 — when it appeared, in what publication, and how it gradually displaced Beethoven's original title_ Sonata quasi una fantasia. _The conversation is a good introduction to the history of how Beethoven's reception has shaped the way the music gets received._
* _Tell the companion which of the three readings (Kempff, Barenboim, Pires) felt most "right" to you for the Adagio sostenuto, and ask her to compare each pianist's voicing of the chorale-melody hidden inside the triplets._
* _Ask the companion about Giulietta Guicciardi and the documented Beethoven letter of November 1801 to Wegeler — what is reliably known about the relationship, and how modern biographers treat the romantic biography of this period._
* _Tell the companion you would like to sample the_ Waldstein _first movement after the_ Moonlight _Adagio — ask her to recommend a recording (Pollini 1997 is the chapter's pick) and to point out one moment in the_ Waldstein _that contrasts most directly with the_ Moonlight's _opening._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The triplet pattern from 0:00 onward — count the triplets per measure and notice how rare the pauses in the flow are.
* The chorale-melody hidden in the top voice of the triplets — try to hum the melody after listening, separating it from the triplet accompaniment in your inner hearing.
* The harmonic motion at 1:30–2:10 — listen for the way the bass moves into more chromatic territory while the triplet pattern stays constant.
* The dynamic level throughout — Beethoven specifies _sempre pianissimo_ , _senza sordino_. Notice that Kempff never breaks the soft dynamic across the full six minutes.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further-listening recommendation, listen to **Op. 27 No. 1** alongside Op. 27 No. 2 — the two Op. 27 sonatas were published together as "Sonata quasi una fantasia, Nos. 1 and 2," and hearing them together makes Beethoven's formal experiment more legible. Andras Schiff's 2008 ECM cycle includes both; or Maurizio Pollini's late 2000s DG releases. Schiff's lecture-recital videos on the Beethoven sonatas (available on the Wigmore Hall and DG channels) include an excellent talk on the Op. 27 pair.

### Chapter Summary

The first movement of the _Moonlight_ sonata (Op. 27 No. 2, 1801) is a through-composed Adagio sostenuto in C♯ minor, built on a continuous triplet texture in the right hand, a slow deep bass line, and a chorale-like melody that emerges from inside the triplet texture. The dynamic stays soft throughout. The form is not sonata form but a more freely structured arc with periodic returns to the opening material.

The title _Moonlight_ was invented by Ludwig Rellstab in 1832 — five years after Beethoven's death. Beethoven's original title was _Sonata quasi una fantasia_ (Sonata in the manner of a fantasy), and the work belongs to a pair (with Op. 27 No. 1) in which Beethoven was experimenting with non-standard sonata layouts. The architectural inversion (slow first movement instead of fast) is the work's structural innovation.

You met the Op. 53 _Waldstein_ (1804) in preview as the bridge into the middle-period piano sonatas. Where _Moonlight_ is slow and introspective, _Waldstein_ is fast and extroverted; together they show the range of what Beethoven was doing in this period.

You met Giulietta Guicciardi, the dedicatee, and the documented November 1801 Beethoven letter to Wegeler — the closest the documentary record gets to the Beethoven-Guicciardi romantic biography.

You met three readings of the _Moonlight_ — Kempff 1965 (the model German reading), Barenboim 1966 (the precocious young-virtuoso reading), and Pires 2002 (the modern introspective reading) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter brings the hinge between early and middle Beethoven into focus: Symphonies 1 and 2 framed by the Heiligenstadt Testament. After this you will know what made the late Beethoven of the _Moonlight_ and _Waldstein_ turn into the Beethoven of the _Eroica_.

## Chapter 5: Symphonies 1 and 2, and the Heiligenstadt Testament

On 6 October 1802, in the village of Heiligenstadt outside Vienna where his doctor had sent him to rest his ears, Ludwig van Beethoven sat down and wrote a letter to his brothers Carl and Johann that he never sent.

The letter is now called the Heiligenstadt Testament. It is one of the most-studied documents in the history of Western music. It was found among Beethoven's papers after his death in 1827; his brother Carl had died in 1815 and never saw it; his brother Johann survived Beethoven and apparently encountered the letter for the first time when it was discovered. The original manuscript is preserved in the Hamburg State and University Library. The text is in German, in Beethoven's own handwriting, with corrections and second thoughts visible on the page.

The Testament is the hinge of Beethoven's biography. It is the document where Beethoven confronts, in writing, the deafness that had been progressing since at least 1796 and that by 1802 was severe. It is the document where Beethoven describes his despair, his isolation, his anger at being misunderstood as cold and unfriendly when in fact he was withdrawing because he could not hear well enough to participate in society. It is the document where Beethoven describes contemplating suicide and deciding against it — _only art held me back_. And it is the document immediately preceding the _Eroica_ (composed 1803–1804). After the Testament, Beethoven returns to Vienna and writes the symphony that breaks open what a symphony can be.

This chapter places the two early symphonies (Op. 21, 1800 and Op. 36, 1802) on either side of the Testament. Symphony 1 was composed and premiered in Vienna in spring 1800 — a confident, well-made symphony in C major in the tradition of late Haydn. Symphony 2 was composed mostly in 1802, in the spring and summer at Heiligenstadt itself, in the months when Beethoven was deciding whether to live. The work was finished after the Testament was written and premiered in April 1803, on a benefit concert in which Beethoven also premiered his Piano Concerto No. 3 (in C minor — the C-minor character returning, see Chapter 3) and _Christus am Ölberge_ (his early oratorio).

The Testament is the chapter's anchor text. The anchor _listening_ is Symphony 2's second movement Larghetto — the lyrical, songful slow movement Beethoven was composing in the spring and summer of 1802 at Heiligenstadt, in the months immediately before he wrote the document that decided his life would continue. We are going to listen to a slow movement of unmistakable beauty written by a composer in the deepest crisis of his life. The contrast between the music and the biographical context is one of the most-studied phenomena in Western music history.

### 5.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — what the Heiligenstadt Testament actually says

The Heiligenstadt Testament opens:

> _For my brothers Carl and Johann Beethoven,_ _O you men who think or say that I am hostile, peevish, or misanthropic, how greatly do you wrong me. You do not know the secret cause which makes me seem that way to you. From childhood my heart and mind were disposed to the gentle feelings of good will; I was even ever eager to accomplish great deeds. But, only think, that for six years now I have been hopelessly afflicted, made worse by senseless physicians, from year to year deceived with hopes of improvement, finally compelled to face the prospect of a lasting malady (whose cure will take years or, perhaps, be impossible)._

The text continues for about a thousand words. Beethoven describes the isolation forced on him by the deafness — _I was compelled early to isolate myself, to live alone_ — and the misunderstanding of his withdrawal as misanthropy. He describes the desperate measures he has tried (the stay in Heiligenstadt was an attempt at country-air recuperation). He describes the moments at which he has come close to giving up. And then, near the end of the document, he writes:

> _I would have ended my life. — Only art it was that withheld me. Ah, it seemed impossible to leave the world until I had brought forth all that I felt was within me._

The line — _only art it was that withheld me_ — is the document's center. Beethoven is saying: I considered ending my life because of the deafness, and what stopped me was the work I had not yet done. The compositions still inside me kept me alive.

He concludes the document with addresses to his brothers (asking them to share his property after his death), to the doctor Schmidt who treated his hearing (asking him to record the nature of the illness so that posterity will understand him), and to _Divinity_ (asking for the strength to bear the remaining years). The signature is dated 6 October 1802. Beethoven added a postscript four days later, on 10 October.

Beethoven then placed the document among his papers and left it. He did not send it to his brothers. He did not show it to anyone. He went back to Vienna and resumed work. The autumn of 1802 was, by the compositional chronology, when he completed the orchestration of the Symphony No. 2 in D major — the work whose second movement Larghetto we are about to listen to. Then, in 1803, he began composing the _Eroica_. The Testament had been written and put away. The decision to live had been made on the page. The work was the proof.

What is the listener to do with this? The honest move is to listen to the Symphony 2 Larghetto without forcing a biographical reading onto it. The movement is not _about_ the Heiligenstadt despair. It is not a _response_ to the Testament. It is a piece of music composed in the same months when the Testament was being written, by a composer who had made a decision to continue, and whose continuation produced, among other things, this slow movement. The biographical context is the _frame_ within which the work was made. The work is not the biographical message; it is the _what the composer made_ during that particular season.

Lewis Lockwood, in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ , makes this point explicitly. The Testament and the music are _contemporaneous_ ; they are not _commentary on each other_. The Larghetto is what Beethoven was composing while the inner life that produced the Testament was happening. The two are different kinds of objects. Read the Testament for what it tells us about the inner life; listen to the Larghetto for what it tells us about the compositional life. Both belong to the same person; neither explains the other.

#### What to notice

Three things in Symphony 2's Larghetto.

  1. **The opening melody.** The movement opens with one of the loveliest melodies Beethoven ever wrote — long-breathed, songful, in D major, played by the violins at moderate-soft dynamic. Listen for the _singing_ quality of the melody; this is Beethoven writing in the style that contemporaries called _cantabile_ , singing-style, as if for a human voice, but in the instrumental medium. The melody is fourteen measures long, slow, and shaped like a long human breath.

  2. **The dialogue between voices.** As the movement unfolds, the melody passes from violins to woodwinds and back, with the lower strings providing a slow walking bass. Listen for the _conversation_ between the instrumental sections: the melody belongs to no single voice but moves through the orchestra as if being passed hand to hand.

  3. **The development of the song.** The movement has a sonata-form structure (exposition, development, recapitulation) but the form is much subtler than in a fast first movement. Listen for the way the song-melody is _varied_ across the movement — taken into different keys, given different harmonic colorings, expanded with new countermelodies — without ever losing its identity as a song.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed Symphony No. 2 in D major, Op. 36 between 1801 and the autumn of 1802 (the orchestration completed at or near the time of the Heiligenstadt Testament), and premiered the work on 5 April 1803 at the Theater an der Wien in Vienna on a benefit concert that also included the premieres of Piano Concerto No. 3 and _Christus am Ölberge_.
