### 5.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into Klemperer 1957.

* **0:00–0:30.** The opening melody, played by the violins, with woodwinds and lower strings providing a soft accompaniment. The tempo is _Larghetto_ : slow but not slow-slow, more like _moderately slow_. Klemperer takes it on the slow side of the standard range. Notice the _long phrase_ : the melody breathes across four-bar and eight-bar arcs without breaking into shorter motivic cells. This is _cantabile_ writing, singing-style, applied to the symphony orchestra.

* **0:30–1:30.** The first phrase is extended. The melody returns with subtle variations, then passes to the woodwinds (oboe and clarinet, mainly). Listen for the _conversation_ between strings and winds. The lower strings provide a quiet walking bass underneath.

* **1:30–2:30.** A more lyrical development of the first theme. The melody is taken through related keys (A major, the dominant). Listen for the way Beethoven _opens_ the harmonic territory: the music is moving but never destabilizing; the temperament stays serene.

* **2:30–3:30.** The second theme arrives. Listen for a slightly more _intricate_ texture: the second theme is more conversational, with shorter melodic units passed between instruments. The dynamic stays soft.

* **3:30–4:30.** The closing material of the exposition. The first theme returns briefly to close the exposition section. About 4:30 the exposition ends; in some recordings the orchestra takes a repeat (back to 0:00). Klemperer does not take the repeat; Kleiber does in his 1979 recording.

* **4:30–6:00.** **The development section begins.** Beethoven now takes the first-theme melody through more distant keys (G major, B minor briefly, E minor) and lets it appear in unexpected places: in the cellos, in the woodwinds in inverted form, in the violins fragmented. Listen for the _gentleness_ of the development: this is not a stormy middle section. The melody is being _explored_ , not put under stress.

* **6:00–7:00.** A more sustained passage of development, with the melody appearing in the woodwinds against a string accompaniment. Notice the _peace_ of the texture even as the harmonies move freely. Beethoven is doing something hard: he is _developing_ the material without breaking the meditative atmosphere.

* **7:00–7:30.** The retransition. The harmonies move toward the home key of D major in preparation for the recapitulation. Listen for the _expectation_ : the music does not announce the return loudly, but you can feel the home key approaching.

* **7:30–8:30.** **The recapitulation begins.** The opening melody returns in the home key, played by the violins with the same instrumentation as the opening. Notice that the return is gentle, not triumphant: the song-melody is restored, not transformed.

* **8:30–10:00.** The recapitulation continues, with the second theme now in the home key. The melodies return in the order of the exposition but with subtle variations of orchestration. Listen for the _enrichment_ : Beethoven has added small details (a flute countermelody here, a horn pedal-note there) that were not in the exposition.

* **10:00–11:30.** The coda. The opening melody returns once more, now with a fuller orchestration. Beethoven extends the melody with new arching phrases, brings the woodwinds into more prominent roles, and lets the movement come to its peaceful conclusion. The final cadence is quiet, on a soft D-major chord.

* **11:30–12:00.** The final measures. The orchestra dies away into silence. Klemperer holds the final chord and lets it fade.

About twelve minutes total in Klemperer. The melody dominates the entire movement; no passage is built on contrast or conflict. The temperament stays gentle throughout. Beethoven was composing this music, by the chronology, in the spring and summer of 1802 at Heiligenstadt, in the months when the Testament was being inwardly composed. The Larghetto is what came out of those months.

### 5.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Symphony 1 in C major, Op. 21 (1800)

Beethoven's First Symphony was composed in 1799 and premiered on 2 April 1800 at the Burgtheater in Vienna. The work is in four movements — Adagio molto–Allegro con brio / Andante cantabile con moto / Menuetto / Adagio–Allegro molto e vivace — and runs about twenty-five minutes total.

The First is, by widespread critical consensus, a late-Haydn symphony with the temperament of an early Beethoven. The orchestration is conservative (no trombones, no piccolo, the standard Classical pairings). The harmonic language is grounded in Mozart-and-Haydn norms. The form is conventional — sonata-form first movement (with a slow introduction; the dramatic slow introduction signature from Chapter 2's _Pathétique_ is here as well), slow second movement, dance-like third movement (called _Menuetto_ but, by tempo and character, already much closer to a scherzo), and rondo finale.

What is _new_ in the First Symphony is mostly in the details. The slow introduction to the first movement opens on a dominant-seventh chord _not in the home key_ — Beethoven begins the symphony on what sounds like the wrong chord, in F major rather than C, and only by the third or fourth measure does he resolve home. This was startling in 1800 (contemporary reviewers commented on it) but it is a small startling — the work as a whole remains within Classical norms.

The First Symphony is the work where Beethoven announces that he can write a symphony, on the model of his teacher Haydn, at the standard expected of a Vienna composer in 1800. It is not yet the work where Beethoven announces what _his_ symphony will become. That announcement waits until the _Eroica_ (Chapter 6).

#### Symphony 2 in D major, Op. 36 (1801–1802)

The Second Symphony is the step forward. Composed mostly in 1802 (the year of the Heiligenstadt Testament), premiered in April 1803, the work expands the scale of the symphony in every dimension — longer movements, more developed transitions, more harmonic adventure, a finale that anticipates the _Eroica_ 's scale.

The four movements: Adagio molto–Allegro con brio (first movement, with a slow introduction nearly twice the length of Symphony 1's) / Larghetto (the anchor of this chapter) / Scherzo: Allegro (Beethoven now calls the third movement _Scherzo_ explicitly — no longer _Menuetto_ ; the document of the genre shift is in this work) / Allegro molto.

The first movement's slow introduction is one of the longest in any Beethoven symphony — about three minutes of harmonically restless slow music before the Allegro begins. The Allegro itself has an unusually long development section. The Larghetto, as we have just heard, is the most expansive slow movement Beethoven had written for orchestra. The Scherzo is short and witty (its trio section is notably rustic). The finale opens with an unusual _appoggiatura_ , a sharply dissonant ornament, that contemporaries found startling.

Hector Berlioz, writing about Symphony 2 in 1862, gave the work one of its earliest critical defenses: _"the Second is the symphony of joyful adolescence, the symphony where Beethoven has not yet abandoned the spirit of Haydn but is preparing the heart of the Eroica."_ Berlioz's reading has held up reasonably well; modern scholars (Lockwood, Solomon, Swafford) generally treat Symphony 2 as the transitional work between the late-Haydn-style First and the genuinely new _Eroica_.

#### Definition: The dramatic slow introduction (Thread B return)

Both Symphony 1 and Symphony 2 open with slow introductions. We met this device in Chapter 2 (the _Pathétique_ Grave). The signature is documented in Lockwood (chapter on the early symphonies) and in Tovey's _Essays in Musical Analysis_ (the essays on each symphony). Beethoven inherited the device from late Haydn (whose Symphonies 92, 100, 102, 103, 104 all open with slow introductions) but extended it in two ways: by making the introductions longer (Symphony 2's slow introduction is about three minutes; Haydn's longest is usually about one minute), and by sometimes letting the introduction _return_ later in the movement (the _Pathétique_ model; the Symphony 7 model, Chapter 11).

In Symphony 1 the introduction is short and conventional (Adagio molto, about a minute, with the harmonic surprise of opening on the wrong chord). In Symphony 2 the introduction is expanded to nearly three minutes — a long, harmonically wandering passage that establishes the dramatic frame of the entire work. The two symphonies show the signature evolving: from minor extension of Haydn (Symphony 1) to substantial structural element (Symphony 2). The next step in the evolution is the _Eroica_ , which dispenses with a slow introduction altogether — the opening is two massive chords, then directly into the Allegro. Beethoven would use slow introductions again (Symphony 4, Symphony 7) but Symphony 3 marked the moment when he could choose to or not.

#### Definition: The Scherzo (Beethoven's genre claim)

The standard Classical third movement of a four-movement symphony was a _Menuetto_ — a moderate-tempo court dance with a contrasting trio section. Haydn used this form throughout his symphonies; Mozart used it throughout his.

Beethoven's Symphony 1 calls its third movement _Menuetto_ but in tempo and character is already a scherzo — fast, light, more rhythmic than danced. Symphony 2 makes the genre shift explicit: the third movement is labeled _Scherzo: Allegro_. From this point onward, Beethoven's symphonic third movements are scherzi, not minuets. The minuet, as a symphonic dance type, is effectively retired from Beethoven's catalogue after this work.

The genre shift had consequences. The scherzo is faster, lighter, often more humorous than the minuet. It also opens space for more rhythmic experimentation — Beethoven's scherzos can be in unexpected meters (the Symphony 9 scherzo uses _ritmo di tre battute_ , three-bar groupings, alongside the standard four-bar phrasing), unexpected tempi (some of the late-quartet scherzos are at _Presto_), unexpected lengths (the Symphony 7 scherzo, also at Presto, repeats its trio twice).

Symphony 2 is the moment when Beethoven made the genre shift. The minuet, in his hands, became the scherzo.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume that because the Heiligenstadt Testament is dated October 1802, the music Beethoven was composing in 1802 must somehow _reflect_ the Testament's despair. The Symphony 2 Larghetto is the easiest test case: it was being composed in the same months, in the same village, and it is one of the most serene movements Beethoven ever wrote. The Testament and the Larghetto are not in the _same emotional register_. They cannot both be the _real_ Beethoven of 1802 if you assume that the music must mirror the biographical mood.

The correction: the music does not mirror the biographical mood. It does not have to. A composer can write a serene movement during a period of personal crisis, and the serenity of the music does not falsify the reality of the crisis. The Testament and the Larghetto are different objects, produced by the same person, in the same season, and the relationship between them is not transparent. This is one reason the Lockwood-Rosen-Kerman tradition of late-style discipline (Chapter 14 onward) insists on not reading the music as biographical mood-music: it does not work as an interpretive principle, even on the earlier works where it is sometimes attempted.

### 5.4 The Stories

#### The deafness — what is documented

Beethoven's hearing began to deteriorate around 1796, when he was twenty-five years old. The earliest documented references are in letters to friends in Bonn (the Wegeler-Ries correspondence). By 1801 he was telling his old friend Wegeler that he could no longer hear high notes clearly, that he was suffering from a constant ringing in his ears (tinnitus), and that he was afraid of being discovered as deaf in social situations. By 1802 he was withdrawing from public musical life increasingly. The Heiligenstadt summer was an attempt at recuperation; the doctor Schmidt had recommended country air.

The cause of the deafness has been debated for two centuries. Modern medical historians have proposed several diagnoses: otosclerosis (a hardening of the bones of the inner ear), syphilis (the 19th-century supposition, largely discounted now), lead poisoning (some forensic evidence from Beethoven's hair samples in the early 2000s suggested elevated lead levels), Paget's disease, sarcoidosis. No diagnosis is universally accepted. What is clear: the deafness was progressive, was bilateral, and got slowly worse through the rest of Beethoven's life. By 1812 it was severe. By 1818 it was such that he could no longer carry on a normal spoken conversation (the documented start of the _conversation books_ , in which visitors wrote their side of the conversation while Beethoven spoke or wrote his). By 1824, at the premiere of the Ninth Symphony, the documented account is that Beethoven, conducting from the keyboard, could not hear the applause and had to be turned around by the contralto Caroline Unger to see the audience standing — a story we will revisit in Chapter 18.

The deafness's progression is the _medical fact_. The deafness's _meaning_ in Beethoven's life is a more complicated matter. The Heiligenstadt Testament gives one perspective: a young composer in his early thirties, contemplating the loss of the sense most essential to his profession, deciding to live only because the work was not finished. Later perspectives (the conversation books, Beethoven's late correspondence, the isolation of the 1820s) show the deafness becoming a _structural condition_ of his life — no longer the crisis but the background. The Heiligenstadt moment is when the crisis was most acute. After 1802 Beethoven appears to have accepted the deafness as a permanent condition and built his life around it.

For our purposes, what matters is the chronology: the deafness was already substantial in 1802, the Testament is the document where Beethoven confronted it in writing, and the music of 1802–1803 (Symphony 2, the _Eroica_ sketches, the Op. 30 violin sonatas, the Op. 31 piano sonatas, the Piano Concerto No. 3) was composed by a man who had just made the decision to continue working despite the deafness. The _Eroica_ (Chapter 6) is the first major fruit of that decision.

#### The April 1803 premiere

Symphony 2 was premiered on 5 April 1803 at the Theater an der Wien in Vienna, on a benefit concert organized by Beethoven. The program included:

* Symphony No. 1 (composed 1799, premiered 1800; here re-performed)
* Symphony No. 2 (world premiere)
* Piano Concerto No. 3 in C minor (world premiere, Beethoven as soloist)
* _Christus am Ölberge_ , Op. 85 (oratorio, world premiere)

The concert was the format Beethoven would use repeatedly across his career: a benefit performance in a rented theater, with multiple new works premiered by the composer himself, with proceeds going to Beethoven directly. The April 1803 concert was financially successful. The Symphony 2 was reviewed positively, though contemporary reviewers found it less immediately accessible than the First. The Piano Concerto No. 3 — Beethoven's only C-minor piano concerto, returning to the C-minor character of Op. 1 No. 3 (Chapter 3) and the _Pathétique_ (Chapter 2) — was particularly admired.

The April 1803 concert is also the moment when Beethoven's professional life shifts. The Heiligenstadt summer of 1802 had ended; the Testament had been written and put away; the composer was back in Vienna, performing, premiering, and on the cusp of composing the _Eroica_. The transition from early to middle Beethoven was underway.

#### Three readings of the Larghetto

**Otto Klemperer / Philharmonia, 1957 (EMI).** Klemperer was a German conductor of the older generation (born 1885), a Mahler pupil, a survivor of the Nazi years (he conducted the Berlin Philharmonic before being forced into exile in 1933, returned to Europe after the war), and one of the great Beethoven interpreters of the postwar era. His Philharmonia Beethoven cycle (1955–61) is famously slow, weighted, dignified, structurally clear. The Symphony 2 Larghetto in Klemperer is at the slow end of the standard range — about twelve minutes, where the standard is closer to nine or ten — and the slow pace allows the melody to breathe at full length. Some listeners find Klemperer too slow; others find his slowness the only way to hear the movement's architecture fully. The recording sound is the late 1950s EMI Kingsway Hall sound: warm, present, with full bass.

**Carlos Kleiber / Vienna Philharmonic, 1979 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Kleiber recorded a Symphony 4 / Symphony 7 disc and a Symphony 5 / Symphony 7 disc with the Vienna Philharmonic in the 1970s, but his Symphony 2 / Symphony 7 coupling (1979 DG) is one of the great Beethoven Second recordings of the era. Kleiber's tempi are faster than Klemperer's, the Larghetto runs about ten and a half minutes, and the playing has Kleiber's characteristic electric energy. The melody is sung with a slightly more _forward_ motion; the dialogue between sections is more incisive. Some listeners prefer Kleiber's energy; some prefer Klemperer's weight. Both readings are correct in different ways.

**John Eliot Gardiner / Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique, 1992 (Archiv).** Gardiner's complete Beethoven symphony cycle with the Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique (period instruments, recorded 1992–94, released on Archiv) is one of the foundational documents of historically-informed Beethoven. The Larghetto is taken faster than Klemperer or Kleiber, about nine and a half minutes, and the orchestral sound is much _drier_ , with period strings (gut strings, no vibrato), period winds (with their distinct timbres), and period brass (natural horns and trumpets, audibly different from modern). The result is a Symphony 2 that sounds much closer to what 1803 Vienna might have heard — though we cannot know exactly. Gardiner's reading is the model HIP interpretation of the work.

The three readings give you the range of Beethoven 2 across the recording era. Klemperer is the model classical-modernist reading. Kleiber is the model expressive-modernist reading. Gardiner is the model HIP reading. Each is internally consistent; together they form the triangle that the recording-history chapter (Chapter 23) will sketch more broadly.

#### Cross-piece links

* If the Symphony 2 Larghetto's lyrical sustenance interests you, the natural next stop is the **slow movement of Symphony 9 (the Adagio molto e cantabile in B♭ major, third movement, 1824)** — another long, songful, variations-form slow movement in the same lyrical tradition. We will hear Symphony 9 in Chapter 18.
* If the dramatic slow introductions interest you, the natural next stop is the **slow introduction of Symphony 7 (Poco sostenuto, 1812)** — the longest slow introduction in Beethoven's symphonies and the most harmonically adventurous. We will hear Symphony 7 in Chapter 11.
* If the Heiligenstadt biographical context interests you, the natural next stop is the **Piano Concerto No. 3 in C minor, Op. 37 (1800–1803)** — premiered on the same April 1803 concert, in the C-minor character that Op. 1 No. 3 (Chapter 3) launched.
* If the Beethoven-deafness chronology interests you, the natural reading is the **Wegeler-Ries correspondence (1801) and the _Biographische Notizen_ (1838)** — Beethoven's own letters about the deafness to his Bonn friends, plus the later memoir by Wegeler and Ries that records the documented progression.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion to walk through the Heiligenstadt Testament's text — what Beethoven actually says, in what order, and where the famous "only art" line appears. The conversation gives you the document's structure and lets you see how it has been read by Solomon, Lockwood, and Swafford differently._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the Symphony 2 Larghetto felt most_ serene _to you, and ask her about the Berlioz reading of the Second as "joyful adolescence preparing the heart of the Eroica."_
* _Ask the companion about the chronology of the deafness — when Beethoven first reported it, when he tried recuperation in Heiligenstadt, when the conversation books begin in 1818, and how the medical historians of the 21st century have approached the diagnosis (the lead-poisoning hypothesis from the 2000s, etc.)._
* _Have the companion compare Klemperer 1957, Kleiber 1979, and Gardiner 1992 in the Larghetto — what is the right tempo for this movement, and what is each conductor hearing in the music that justifies the tempo choice?_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The opening melody at 0:00–0:30 — try to hum it after listening. It is one of the lovelier Beethoven melodies and worth knowing by heart.
* The development section at 4:30–7:00 — listen for the way the song-melody appears in unexpected places (woodwinds, lower strings) without ever leaving the texture.
* The retransition at 7:00–7:30 — the harmonies move home without announcing themselves. Beethoven brings you back to D major almost invisibly.
* The coda from 10:00 — the melody returns once more with a fuller orchestration. Listen for the small details Beethoven adds (the flute countermelody, the horn pedal-notes) that were not in the exposition.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **the Heiligenstadt Testament itself** — the text is short (about a thousand words), public domain, and reproduced in every Beethoven biography. The Wikipedia article on the Testament includes the full English translation; Maynard Solomon's _Beethoven_ (1998) includes both the German original and a careful English translation with notes. The text is the most-direct documentary access we have to Beethoven's inner life in this period, and reading it after listening to the Symphony 2 Larghetto produces an effect that no biographer's commentary quite captures.

### Chapter Summary

Symphony No. 2 in D major, Op. 36, composed mostly in 1802 at Heiligenstadt and premiered in April 1803, is the hinge between Beethoven's first decade in Vienna and the heroic middle period that the _Eroica_ will open. The four-movement work expands the scale of the symphony in every dimension; the second movement Larghetto is one of the most expansive slow movements Beethoven had written for orchestra.

The Heiligenstadt Testament, dated 6 October 1802, is the letter Beethoven wrote to his brothers, and never sent, in which he describes his despair over his deafness and his decision to continue working because the compositions still inside him kept him alive. The Symphony 2 Larghetto was being composed in the same months. The relationship between the Testament's despair and the Larghetto's serenity is not transparent: the music does not mirror the biographical mood, and the lesson is the Lockwood-Rosen-Kerman principle that the music must be read as music, not as biographical commentary.

You met Symphony 1 (1800) as the model late-Haydn-style Beethoven symphony — confident, well-made, conservative. You met Symphony 2 (1802) as the transitional work in which Beethoven expands the symphony's scale, changes the third movement from Menuetto to Scherzo (the genre claim), and prepares the ground for the _Eroica_.

You learned that the dramatic slow introduction (Thread B) is now a recurring Beethoven signature — present in both early symphonies, present in the _Pathétique_ , and on its way to becoming a standard tool that Beethoven will deploy and withhold strategically across the rest of his career.

You met three readings of the Larghetto — Klemperer 1957 (model classical-modernist), Kleiber 1979 (model expressive-modernist), Gardiner 1992 (model HIP) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter walks into the _Eroica_ , the symphony that begins the heroic middle period and breaks open what a symphony can be. The composer who decided in Heiligenstadt to continue is about to make the most ambitious work of his career to that point.

## Chapter 6: Symphony No. 3 "Eroica" — First Movement

Two massive chords. Then a quiet cello-and-bass figure that outlines an arpeggio of the home key — three notes up, then a stepwise descent that turns chromatic, twists down to an unexpected low note, then climbs back up. The figure repeats. The second time it climbs higher. A flute and oboe take it up. The orchestra builds. Within a minute the four-bar opening figure has become a long, sustained, harmonically restless paragraph that does not stop expanding for the next fourteen minutes.

You are listening to the first movement of Beethoven's Third Symphony — the _Eroica_ , composed 1803–1804, premiered privately in summer 1804 and publicly in April 1805.

You met this movement in Volume 1 of _Close Listening_ , in Chapter 13. The Volume 1 chapter framed the _Eroica_ as the hinge work between Classical and Romantic — the symphony that broke open what a symphony could be. The framing is accurate but, in the survey context of Volume 1, necessarily compressed. This chapter has the room Volume 1 did not have. We will hear the same first movement, this time with the compositional history (the sketches at the Beethoven-Haus Bonn that Lewis Lockwood has studied in depth), the Napoleon dedication and its withdrawal, the private and public premieres, and the long argument that this single fourteen-minute first movement is one of the most consequential pieces of music in the history of Western art.

The basic facts are stable. Beethoven composed the _Eroica_ between mid-1803 and early 1804, in the months immediately following the Heiligenstadt Testament (Chapter 5). The work was originally dedicated to Napoleon Bonaparte; the dedication was withdrawn (the story involves Beethoven tearing the dedication page of the autograph, after hearing that Napoleon had declared himself Emperor on 18 May 1804); the work was published in October 1806 with the more general dedication _Sinfonia eroica, composta per festeggiare il sovvenire di un grand'Uomo_ — "Heroic Symphony, composed to celebrate the memory of a great man." The first private performance was at the Lobkowicz palace in summer 1804; the first public premiere was on 7 April 1805 at the Theater an der Wien in Vienna.

What is _not_ stable is the work's interpretation. Is the _Eroica_ about Napoleon? About the Heiligenstadt-and-back-from-Heiligenstadt biographical arc? About the heroic in some more general sense? About nothing in particular — a piece of music that, like other pieces of music, refers primarily to its own internal logic? Each reading has its defenders. We are going to listen to the first movement with all of these readings in our ear and decide, at the end of the chapter, which we prefer. The book takes no position; it presents the cases.

### 6.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — the torn dedication

In May 1804, Ferdinand Ries (Beethoven's pupil, later co-author of the _Biographische Notizen_) arrived at Beethoven's lodgings to find Beethoven in a fury. The news had just come from Paris: on 18 May, Napoleon had declared himself Emperor of the French, replacing the Republican framework that had been in place since the Revolution. By Ries's account (published in 1838), Beethoven exclaimed: _"Is then he, too, nothing more than an ordinary human being? Now he will trample on the rights of man and indulge only his ambition. He will exalt himself above all others, become a tyrant!"_ Beethoven then took the manuscript of the new symphony — the title page of which bore the inscription _Sinfonia grande intitolata Bonaparte_ — and _tore the title page in two_. He flung the torn pages on the floor. Later, Beethoven changed the dedication to a more general one.

The story is famous. It is also a single-source story, transmitted through Ries in a memoir published thirty-four years later. Modern scholars (Lockwood, Solomon, Swafford) treat the substance as broadly accurate — Beethoven did remove the Bonaparte dedication; he was angry about Napoleon's Imperial declaration; the autograph manuscript does show a scratched-out title — but caution against believing the exact words and the exact gesture. What is documented: a copy of the _Eroica_ score in the archive of Prince Lobkowicz has the original title page partly preserved, with the _Buonaparte_ inscription visible but scratched through, with the words _geschrieben auf Bonaparte_ ("written on Bonaparte") still legible at the bottom. The autograph manuscript itself does not survive intact, so the _torn in two_ part of Ries's story cannot be verified directly. The dedication change is documented; the torn-page gesture is plausible but unprovable.

What is also documented: Beethoven's relationship with Napoleon was complicated for many years afterward. He did not simply replace contempt for admiration. The 1810 _Mass in C_ he composed for Prince Esterházy was, by one account, originally considered as a possible Napoleon-dedication piece. As late as 1820 Beethoven would speak ambivalently about Napoleon to visitors (the conversation-book records). The _Eroica_ dedication was withdrawn but the underlying political fascination with what Napoleon had been (a man who had risen from soldier to ruler on the strength of military and political achievement, embodying the Revolutionary promise of the _career open to talent_) did not disappear from Beethoven's intellectual world. The torn dedication is a _moment_ , not a final position.

For the listener, what matters: the _Eroica_ was conceived in 1803 in a political and biographical context (post-Heiligenstadt; Napoleon's pre-Imperial republicanism still apparently intact) that was different from the context in which it was finished and premiered (post-Imperial declaration; Beethoven's open disillusionment). The music does not change between these contexts. But the _meaning the listener attaches to the music_ can change. We will return to this question at the end of the chapter.

#### What to notice

Three things in the _Eroica_ 's first movement.

  1. **The two opening chords.** The movement opens with two massive, full-orchestra E♭-major chords on the downbeats of two consecutive measures. Not a slow introduction. Not a melodic statement. Two declarative chords, separated by a single beat of silence, that announce the home key and the orchestral weight before the actual musical material begins. Listen for the _announcement_ : this is the start of a symphony in the way that a curtain rising is the start of a tragedy.

  2. **The opening cello-and-bass figure.** After the two chords, the cellos and basses play a four-measure figure: a rising arpeggio of E♭-major (the home triad), then a stepwise descent that turns chromatic, twists down to a low C♯ (a note _outside_ the home key), then climbs back up. This figure is the _first theme_. Listen for the _strangeness_ of the chromatic twist at the bottom of the figure: in 1803 this was an unusual move at the very start of a symphony, dropping out of the home key in the second measure of the first phrase. Beethoven is signalling immediately that the symphony will not stay where it begins.

  3. **The unprecedented scale.** The first movement is about fourteen minutes long in most recordings — twice the length of any previous symphonic first movement. The exposition alone is longer than the entire first movement of a typical Haydn or Mozart symphony. Listen for the _expansion_ of every section: the first theme is longer, the transitions are longer, the second theme is longer, the development is much longer (the longest development in any symphonic first movement up to that date), the recapitulation is longer, the coda is so long that some commentators have called it a _second development_. The work's first move is to claim symphonic space that no previous composer had claimed.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the _Eroica_ between mid-1803 and early 1804, premiered it privately at the Lobkowicz palace in August 1804 and publicly on 7 April 1805 at the Theater an der Wien in Vienna, originally dedicated the work to Napoleon Bonaparte, withdrew the dedication in May 1804 after Napoleon declared himself Emperor, and published the work in October 1806 with the general dedication _Sinfonia eroica, composta per festeggiare il sovvenire di un grand'Uomo_.

### 6.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into Klemperer 1959.

* **0:00–0:05.** The two opening chords. Massive E♭-major. Notice the _unison_ quality: the entire orchestra striking together, no harmonic decoration, just the chord on the downbeat. The two chords are separated by a beat of silence. Klemperer takes them at full weight, slightly slower than the basic Allegro con brio.

* **0:05–0:25.** The cellos and basses play the four-measure first-theme figure: rising E♭-major arpeggio, then a stepwise descent that twists chromatically downward to a low C♯, then climbs back. Listen for the chromatic twist around 0:15: the move out of the home key, then back. The figure repeats with subtle variation.

* **0:25–0:55.** The first theme is taken up by the upper strings, then by the woodwinds. The orchestration thickens. Beethoven is _extending_ the theme: not yet developing it, just letting it unfurl across a larger texture.

* **0:55–1:40.** Continued extension. Listen for the _forward drive_ : the music does not pause, does not catch its breath. The transition to the second theme has already begun, but you cannot pinpoint a single moment of transition; the music is one continuous building gesture.

* **1:40–2:30.** The second-theme group arrives. There are actually _several_ secondary themes in the _Eroica_ exposition (this is one of the work's structural innovations: Beethoven gives himself multiple lyrical episodes rather than the standard single second theme). Listen for the _new melodic ideas_ that emerge from the woodwinds and upper strings: a lyrical descending figure in the oboes around 1:50; a more pastoral phrase in the woodwinds around 2:10.

* **2:30–3:30.** Development of the second-theme group, leading toward the close of the exposition. Multiple themes are now in play: the opening cell, the chromatic twist, the lyrical descending figure, the pastoral phrase. Beethoven is treating the exposition as an _exposition of multiple materials_ rather than two contrasting themes.

* **3:30–4:30.** The closing material of the exposition. The opening cell returns, the orchestral texture thickens, the exposition closes decisively in B♭ major (the standard dominant key). About 4:15 the exposition ends; Klemperer takes the repeat in some performances, omits it in others: in the 1959 recording, the repeat is taken.

* **4:30–8:00.** Around this time, after the repeat, **the development section begins** (in Klemperer's 1959 reading, around 7:30 after the repeat). Listen for the _unprecedented scale_ : the development is about six minutes long, the longest in any symphonic first movement up to 1804. Beethoven takes the opening theme through a long sequence of keys, fragments it, develops it against new countermelodies.

* **9:00–10:30.** **The most-discussed passage in the symphony** : the development reaches its climax. At about 9:30 (Klemperer) Beethoven introduces a _new theme_ in the middle of the development: a stark, dissonant, descending figure in the upper strings, against repeated chords in the rest of the orchestra. The dissonance is harsh by 1803 standards; contemporary listeners found it disturbing. The figure is sometimes called _the dissonance episode_ or _the catastrophe_. After about thirty seconds of this material, the music _collapses_ into a long sustained dominant chord: and then, at about 10:00, the famous _false horn entry_ : a solo horn plays the opening of the first theme _in the home key_ (E♭ major), while the strings are still on the dominant. The two keys clash. The first hearing at the 1805 premiere reportedly produced confusion among the orchestral players themselves; documented anecdotes describe the violinist Anton Krumpholz complaining that the horn player had come in early.

* **10:30–11:30.** The recapitulation begins. The opening two chords, the cello-and-bass figure, the chromatic twist: all return, in the home key. Listen for the _return as resolution_ : after six minutes of restless development, including the dissonance episode and the false horn entry, the original material comes back intact. This is what Lockwood calls the _structural moment_ of the _Eroica_ first movement.

* **11:30–13:30.** The recapitulation walks through the exposition material with subtle variations of orchestration and harmony. The second-theme group returns in the home key (E♭ major); the multiple lyrical episodes return in adapted forms.

* **13:30–15:30.** **The coda: the most extraordinary closing section in any Beethoven symphony.** Most Classical sonata-form first movements close with a brief coda (a few measures, maybe a minute). The _Eroica_ 's coda is about two minutes long and functions almost as a second development. Beethoven takes the opening theme through one more cycle of transformation: new keys, new countermelodies, a dramatic horn-call moment around 14:30, and a final closing buildup that arrives at the home key with full orchestral force. Listen for the _length_ of the coda: this is not a closing tag; this is a second structural argument.

* **15:30–16:00.** The final measures. Hammering E♭-major chords, the full orchestra striking together, the movement closing in the same massive register in which it opened. Klemperer's final chord is held; the orchestra rests.

About sixteen minutes total in Klemperer (the slow reading). About fourteen minutes in Gardiner (the fast reading). The structure: two opening chords, exposition (multiple themes), exposition repeat, development (with the dissonance episode and the false horn entry as climaxes), recapitulation, and an extraordinary coda. Every section is expanded; the work claims space no previous symphony had claimed.

### 6.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The structural innovation

The _Eroica_ first movement is in sonata form. So is the first movement of Beethoven's Symphony 1 (Chapter 5). What is new in the _Eroica_ is not the form but the _scale_ and the _internal density_. Specifically:

**Multiple themes in the exposition.** Where Haydn and Mozart typically used two themes (first and second), Beethoven uses several. The first theme group includes the opening cello-and-bass figure, the chromatic-twist variant, and the extension that follows. The second theme group includes the lyrical descending figure in the oboes, the pastoral phrase in the woodwinds, and additional secondary materials. The exposition is built from more material than the standard Classical layout.

**An expanded development.** The development is about six minutes — six times longer than the development of Symphony 1 first movement (which is about a minute). This is not merely a longer section; it is a development that functions as a _separate argument_ , with its own internal sections (the working-through of the opening theme; the dissonance episode; the false horn entry; the return to the home dominant).

**A long coda.** The coda is two minutes — substantial enough to function as a second development. Beethoven takes material that has already been recapitulated and develops it further, arriving at the close only after a second round of structural work.

**A genuinely new theme in the development.** The dissonance episode introduces material that was not in the exposition — a new descending dissonant figure that appears for the first time in the development and is not heard again afterward. This is unusual: most Classical sonata-form developments work exclusively with material from the exposition. Beethoven introduces _new material_ in the middle of the development, then drops it. The dissonance episode is structurally a kind of _expressive interlude_ in the working-through.

These four innovations — multiple themes, expanded development, long coda, new development material — are in every modern Beethoven biography. Lewis Lockwood's _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ devotes a full chapter to the _Eroica_ and identifies these features explicitly. The innovations were not unknown in Beethoven's previous work (Symphony 2 already had a longer-than-standard development; Op. 1 No. 3 had multiple themes in the exposition), but the _Eroica_ combines them at unprecedented scale.

#### The dissonance episode and the false horn entry

Two moments in the development have been particularly discussed.

The **dissonance episode** (around 9:30 in Klemperer) is a passage in which Beethoven introduces sharp dissonances against repeated orchestral chords. The harmonies clash; the music sounds _wrong_ by 1803 standards. The episode is brief (about thirty seconds) but it stands out from anything in the previous symphonies. Contemporary reviewers of the 1805 premiere noted the harshness; some praised it (Berlioz, retrospectively, called it _audacious_); some criticized it (some Vienna reviewers found it ugly). The episode has become a landmark in the analysis of the _Eroica_ : Beethoven is doing something the standard Classical language does not permit, in the middle of a symphony, and refusing to apologize.

The **false horn entry** (around 10:00 in Klemperer) is a passage in which a solo horn, against the orchestra sustaining a dominant-seventh chord, plays the opening of the first theme _in the home key_. The two are not compatible — the dominant-seventh implies a key, the theme implies a different key, and the horn entering with the home-key theme while the orchestra is on the dominant produces a clash that lasts for a few measures before the orchestra resolves to the home key for the recapitulation. The clash sounds, on first hearing, like a mistake — and the documented anecdote (from Ferdinand Ries) is that at the first rehearsals one of the violinists believed the horn player had come in early. Beethoven's response (also Ries) was that the horn player was right; the _clash was the point_.

The two moments are connected. The dissonance episode prepares the false horn entry: both involve harmonic clashes within the orchestral texture, both push at the boundaries of what Classical practice would have permitted. They are the two pressure points of the development. After the false horn entry, the recapitulation arrives and the harmonies settle. But the dissonances have happened and the music does not pretend they did not.

These are the moments that earned the _Eroica_ its early reputation for boldness, and the moments that some early reviewers found ugly. They are also, by modern critical consensus, the moments that establish Beethoven's middle-period approach: the willingness to push harmonic and structural boundaries when the expressive content demands it.

#### Definition: The long buildup-to-climax (Thread B return)

The _Eroica_ first movement establishes the _long buildup-to-climax_ signature. We have not formally named this signature before, but it is in Lockwood and Tovey as one of Beethoven's recurring compositional strategies. The signature: take a thematic figure, set up a tension, sustain the tension across an extended span, build the orchestration and dynamics toward a structural climax (typically the recapitulation or the coda), let the climax arrive with the full weight of the previous buildup.

In the _Eroica_ first movement, the long buildup is the development section. Six minutes of restless transformation, the dissonance episode, the false horn entry, the long approach to the home dominant — all preparation for the recapitulation's arrival at 10:30. The climax is not a single bar; it is the _moment of return_ of the home key and the original theme, earned by the six-minute buildup.

We will meet this signature again in:

* **The Ninth Symphony finale** (Chapter 18): the long instrumental recitatives, the introduction of the Ode to Joy melody, the buildup to the choral entry.
* **The _Grosse Fuge_** (Chapter 20): the long contrapuntal development, the buildup to the central climaxes.
* **The _Hammerklavier_ finale** (Chapter 15) — the fugue's relentless extension toward its closing statement.

Each of these works applies the signature on a different scale, in a different medium. The _Eroica_ is the early prototype.

#### Definition: The two opening chords (an unusual opening device)

Most Classical symphonies open with either a slow introduction (Haydn's late symphonies, Beethoven's First and Second) or a thematic statement at tempo (Mozart's symphonies, Haydn's earlier symphonies). The _Eroica_ opens with neither. It opens with two declarative chords, no introduction, then directly into the cello-and-bass figure. This is the first time in symphonic history (by general scholarly agreement) that a major symphony opens with _bare chordal announcement followed by direct thematic entrance_.

The device would be widely imitated. Schumann's First and Fourth Symphonies open with related gestures; Mahler's First Symphony opens with sustained chords leading into a thematic figure; Brahms's First, Second, and Fourth all use opening-statement gestures derived from this Beethoven model. The _Eroica_ 's opening is a _form_ that subsequent composers learned from. Two chords; then directly into the work.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume that because the _Eroica_ is the "heroic" symphony, the music must sound _unambiguously triumphant_ throughout. It does not. The first movement is restless, harmonically adventurous, full of dissonance and clash; the second movement (the famous Funeral March in C minor) is one of the most-mourning pieces in the orchestral repertoire; the third movement (the Scherzo) is light and dancing rather than heroic; only the fourth movement (the finale, a variation set on a theme from Beethoven's earlier _Prometheus_ ballet) is unambiguously celebratory.

The misconception matters because if you expect the _Eroica_ to be a four-movement march of heroic triumph, the actual music will disappoint you. The work is heroic in the older sense, _grand_ and _high-stakes_ , but not in the popular sense of unrelieved triumph. It contains, within its four movements, dissonance, mourning, dancing, and celebration. The "hero" of the title (whoever the hero is — Napoleon was the original answer, but Beethoven's later thinking opened the question) goes through all four of these registers in turn. The work is a _life_ in the heroic mode, not a parade.

### 6.4 The Stories

#### The compositional history

The earliest sketches for the _Eroica_ are in the Wielhorsky Sketchbook, beginning in late 1802 — within weeks of the Heiligenstadt Testament. Lewis Lockwood's work on the sketchbooks (his _Beethoven: Studies in the Creative Process_ , 1992, is the standard study) reconstructs the compositional process in considerable detail. Beethoven worked on the _Eroica_ through most of 1803 and into early 1804. The autograph manuscript (or what survives of it; the manuscript is incomplete) shows extensive revisions, particularly in the development section of the first movement and in the Funeral March.

The work was not commissioned. Beethoven composed it for himself, with the eventual plan of dedicating it to Napoleon Bonaparte — at the time still First Consul of the French Republic, still embodying for Beethoven the revolutionary ideal of _meritocracy made political reality_. Beethoven had become interested in Napoleon's career in the late 1790s and through correspondence with the French ambassador Bernadotte (who was briefly in Vienna in 1798) had absorbed the Republican-French case for Napoleon. The plan for the dedication was in place by early 1804; the autograph title page (or the copy of it preserved in the Lobkowicz archive) bore the inscription _intitolata Bonaparte_.

The dedication was withdrawn in May 1804 (the Ries account; see the Hook Anecdote). The first private performance was in August 1804 at the Lobkowicz palace, with Prince Lobkowicz's private orchestra; the Beethoven was conducting from the keyboard, as was his standard practice. The work was performed several more times privately over the next year. The public premiere was on 7 April 1805 at the Theater an der Wien in Vienna, on a concert that also included other works.

The public premiere's reception was mixed. Some Vienna reviewers found the work too long, too harmonically harsh, structurally confusing. Others (notably the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_) recognized the work as new and important. Within a few years, the _Eroica_ had established itself as the most-ambitious symphonic work in the Vienna repertoire. By 1810 Hoffmann's review of the Fifth Symphony (Chapter 9) could already reference the _Eroica_ as the canonical Beethoven symphonic landmark.

#### The Lobkowicz patronage and the private premiere

Prince Joseph Franz Maximilian Lobkowicz (1772–1816) was Beethoven's second-most-important early patron after Lichnowsky. The Lobkowicz palace in Vienna's Burggasse maintained a private orchestra throughout the early 19th century — twenty to thirty musicians on full retainer, plus extras hired as needed for major performances. Lobkowicz used the orchestra for private concerts in his palace and for testing new works before public premiere.

The _Eroica_ 's first private performance, in August 1804, was at the Lobkowicz palace. The chronology suggests that Beethoven performed parts of the symphony to Lobkowicz on the keyboard during the spring of 1804 as the work was being completed, then conducted the first full orchestral reading at the palace in August. Lobkowicz subsequently held the rights to first performance for several months — a arrangement in which Beethoven took a payment of 500 florins for the privilege of private performances before the public premiere. The public premiere in April 1805 occurred after Lobkowicz's exclusive period had expired.

The Lobkowicz-Beethoven relationship was the model for noble patronage in the new system that had emerged in 1790s Vienna (Chapter 3). Beethoven wrote regularly for Lobkowicz's orchestra and dedicated multiple works to Lobkowicz, including (in addition to the _Eroica_) the Op. 18 quartets, Symphony 5, Symphony 6, and the Triple Concerto Op. 56. Lobkowicz's bankruptcy in 1813 (one of the financial catastrophes that contributed to Beethoven's 1815–1820 crisis years, Chapter 14) ended this support.

#### What the work is "about" — the four positions

The _Eroica_ 's subject matter has been debated for more than two hundred years. The four positions, in summary:

**Position 1: The Napoleon position.** The work was originally dedicated to Napoleon and intended to portray Napoleon's heroic career. The opening movement is the hero's life-action; the Funeral March mourns the heroic dead (perhaps a general fallen in battle, perhaps the Republican ideal that Napoleon had crushed); the Scherzo is the heroic energy in dance form; the finale is the hero's victory. Defenders: many 19th-century critics. Modern: the position is generally not held in its strong form by current scholars, who treat the Napoleon connection as but the program-music-of-Napoleon reading as overdetermined.

**Position 2: The Heiligenstadt position.** The work is Beethoven's musical response to the Heiligenstadt Testament — the _Eroica_ is the heroic self that emerges from the despair of the Testament; the first movement is the assertion of life-against-deafness; the Funeral March mourns the self that almost died at Heiligenstadt; the Scherzo is the recovered energy; the finale is the new life. Defenders: Maynard Solomon (in _Beethoven_ , 1998), partially. Modern: holds some currency; Solomon's reading is widely cited.

**Position 3: The Promethean position.** The work is about the Promethean ideal — the hero as the bringer of fire to humanity, the figure who suffers for the gift he brings. Beethoven had recently composed the _Creatures of Prometheus_ ballet (1801), and the _Eroica_ 's finale uses a theme from that ballet (the variation finale's main theme is the _Prometheus_ theme). The hero of the _Eroica_ is, on this reading, the Promethean creator-and-sufferer. Defenders: some scholarly readings (Scott Burnham's _Beethoven Hero_ 1995 makes a related case, though Burnham is more focused on the music itself than on Prometheus specifically). Modern: well-regarded as a productive interpretive frame.

**Position 4: The music-as-music position.** The _Eroica_ is not _about_ anything in particular. It is a symphony in the heroic mode, _grand and high-stakes_ , but the music does not refer to specific external events or persons. The title is general (_Sinfonia eroica_ — "Heroic Symphony"), the Napoleon dedication was withdrawn, and the music can be heard fully on its internal logic. Defenders: Donald Tovey (in his _Essays in Musical Analysis_ , vol. 1), Charles Rosen partially. Modern: the most-common position among current professional musicologists.

The four positions are not mutually exclusive. The Napoleon dedication is documented; the Heiligenstadt context is documented; the Prometheus reference in the finale is documented; the music's internal logic is what we hear. A reasonable position is to hold all four in mind and let the music play against them. The _Eroica_ is large enough to support all four readings without being captured by any one of them.

#### Three readings of the first movement

**Otto Klemperer / Philharmonia, 1959 (EMI).** Klemperer's _Eroica_ is the model 1950s reading: slow, weighted, structurally clear, with the dissonance episode and the false horn entry given full weight. The first movement runs about sixteen minutes — slow by current standards. The orchestra is the Philharmonia at its postwar peak. The recording sound is rich and present. If you are going to keep one _Eroica_ in your collection, Klemperer is the safest first choice — the work in its full classical-modernist register.

**Carlos Kleiber / Royal Concertgebouw, broadcast 1991 (privately circulated).** Kleiber conducted the _Eroica_ in the 1980s and 1990s in several concerts but never made a commercial studio recording of it. The 1991 Concertgebouw broadcast is the most-circulated of his _Eroica_ documents (it appears on various private and quasi-private labels). The reading is electric, faster than Klemperer (about fifteen minutes), with the dissonance episode taken at unrelieved intensity and the false horn entry given Kleiber's characteristic urgency. The recording sound is broadcast-quality (not studio); the playing is at the absolute upper edge of what the Concertgebouw could produce in 1991. For listeners who want the _Eroica_ as kinetic event rather than monument, Kleiber is the recording.

**John Eliot Gardiner / Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique, 1993 (Archiv).** Gardiner's HIP reading uses period instruments (gut strings, natural horns, natural trumpets, period winds) and faster tempi based on Beethoven's metronome markings (the markings are but controversial; some are considered impossibly fast). The first movement runs about fourteen minutes. The orchestral sound is much drier than Klemperer's or Kleiber's, with the natural horns producing the horn-call moments at their characteristic period timbre. The dissonance episode is taken with sharper articulation than the modern-instrument readings. For listeners who want to hear what the 1805 audience might have heard (within the limits of what we can know), Gardiner is the recording.

#### Cross-piece links

* Volume 1 Chapter 13 of _Close Listening: Classical_ introduced the _Eroica_ in survey form, focused on the work's role as the Classical-to-Romantic hinge. This volume's chapter is the deeper pass on the same work.
* The dissonance episode and the false horn entry have direct descendants in Beethoven's later catalogue. The dissonance language reappears in the development of Symphony 9's first movement (Chapter 18) and in the development of the _Grosse Fuge_ (Chapter 20). The false-key-entry device — a thematic statement in the home key against an orchestra still on a different key — recurs in transformed form in Symphony 6 (the Storm movement) and the late string quartets.
* The variation-form finale of the _Eroica_ (built on the theme from _Creatures of Prometheus_) is the precedent for several later Beethoven variation finales — Op. 109, Op. 111 second movement, the Ninth Symphony's choral finale, and the _Diabelli Variations_ (Chapter 17). We will treat the variation-form finale as a Beethoven signature in Chapter 21.
* The work that _Eroica_ immediately led to is Symphony 4 in B♭ major, Op. 60 (1806) — a more relaxed, more conventional symphony that has been called the _bridge between Eroica and Fifth_. Robert Schumann famously called Symphony 4 a _slim Greek maiden between two Norse giants_. Not anchored in this volume; rewards direct attention.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion to walk through the four interpretive positions on what the_ Eroica _is "about" (Napoleon / Heiligenstadt / Prometheus / music-as-music) and to say which she finds most useful when listening to the first movement. The conversation should not produce a single answer; it should produce articulated reasons for preferring or holding the four positions in tension._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the first movement felt most_ unprecedented _to you on the second listen — the two opening chords, the chromatic twist of the cello-and-bass figure, the dissonance episode, the false horn entry, or the coda's second-development. Ask her how Beethoven scholarship has treated each moment._
* _Ask the companion about the documented Ries account of the torn dedication — what is reliably known, what depends on Ries's later memoir, and how modern biographers (Lockwood, Solomon, Swafford) treat the various claims._
* _Have the companion compare Klemperer 1959, Kleiber 1991 (broadcast), and Gardiner 1993 in the dissonance episode and the false horn entry — what each conductor hears in those moments, and how the period-instrument approach changes the listener's experience of the harmonic clashes._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The two opening chords at 0:00 — try to hear them as the _announcement_ the symphony makes before any actual music begins. The two chords are not a slow introduction; they are a different kind of opening gesture.
* The chromatic twist at 0:15 in the cello-and-bass figure — the moment the music moves outside the home key in the second measure of the first phrase. This is the symphony saying it will not stay where it begins.
* The dissonance episode at 9:30 — listen for the sharp dissonant figure in the upper strings against the repeated orchestral chords. The episode is brief but stands outside the Classical harmonic language.
* The false horn entry at 10:00 — the solo horn enters with the opening theme in the home key while the orchestra is still on the dominant. The clash lasts a few measures.
* The coda from 13:30 — the longest closing section in any Beethoven symphony. Listen for the way it functions as a second development.

### Going Deeper

If you read one chapter of scholarship alongside this one, read **Lewis Lockwood's chapter on the _Eroica_ in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (W. W. Norton, 2003), chapter 8 "Heroic Symphonies."** Lockwood is the contemporary anchor scholar for the _Eroica_. His chapter reconstructs the compositional process from the surviving sketches, treats the Napoleon-and-Heiligenstadt contexts carefully, and gives the structural analysis with a clarity matched only by Donald Tovey's older essay (still useful, in Tovey's _Essays in Musical Analysis_ , vol. 1). If you read both Lockwood and Tovey on the _Eroica_ , you have the core of the work's modern interpretation.

### Chapter Summary

The first movement of Beethoven's Third Symphony — the _Eroica_ , composed 1803–1804 — is a sonata-form Allegro at unprecedented scale, with two opening chords, a cello-and-bass first-theme figure with a chromatic twist, multiple themes in the exposition, an expanded development that includes the dissonance episode and the famous false horn entry, a long recapitulation, and a coda that functions as a second development.

The work was originally dedicated to Napoleon Bonaparte. The dedication was withdrawn in May 1804 after Napoleon declared himself Emperor; the documented Ries anecdote (the torn title page) is broadly accurate in substance though the exact gesture is unverifiable. The work was published in October 1806 with the general dedication _Sinfonia eroica_. The first private premiere was in August 1804 at the Lobkowicz palace; the first public premiere was on 7 April 1805 at the Theater an der Wien.

You learned the four interpretive positions on what the _Eroica_ is "about" (Napoleon / Heiligenstadt / Prometheus / music-as-music) and the case for each. The four are not mutually exclusive; the work is large enough to support all four without being captured by any one.

You met the **long buildup-to-climax** signature (Thread B), with the _Eroica_ first-movement development as the early prototype. We will meet the signature again in the Ninth Symphony finale (Chapter 18), the _Grosse Fuge_ (Chapter 20), and the _Hammerklavier_ finale (Chapter 15).

You met three readings — Klemperer 1959 (the model classical-modernist), Kleiber 1991 broadcast (the model expressive-electric), Gardiner 1993 (the model HIP) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter brings the middle-period piano sonatas, _Waldstein_ , _Appassionata_ , _Les Adieux_ , into focus. The keyboard parallel to the heroic symphonies is about to come into view.

* * *

---

## Chapter 7: Middle-Period Piano Sonatas — _Waldstein_ , _Appassionata_ , _Les Adieux_

In a five-year stretch from 1803 to 1809, Beethoven composed three piano sonatas that became — once they had picked up their nicknames from publishers and later editors — three of the most-famous works in the piano repertoire. _Waldstein_ (Op. 53, 1804), _Appassionata_ (Op. 57, 1804–05), and _Les Adieux_ (Op. 81a, 1809–10). Together they are the keyboard parallel to the great heroic symphonies — the _Eroica_ (1803–04), the Fourth (1806), the Fifth (1808), the Sixth (1808), the Seventh (1812), the Eighth (1812).

The three sonatas are not stylistically uniform. _Waldstein_ is bright, brilliant, extroverted in C major. _Appassionata_ is dark, dense, structurally extreme in F minor. _Les Adieux_ is a programmatic three-movement narrative in E♭ major. What unites them is the _symphonic ambition_ : each work treats the piano sonata as a vehicle for major dramatic statement, in the way that the prior generation (Mozart, late Haydn) reserved for symphonic and operatic works.

This chapter's anchor is _Appassionata_ 's first movement. It is the most-violent of the three; Charles Rosen has called it the most-extreme middle-period sonata. It is also the work where Beethoven's _C-minor character_ (Chapter 3; tracked across the catalogue in Chapter 21) meets the heroic-symphonic expansion (Chapter 6) in a single instrumental statement. The result is a movement that pushed the piano of 1805 to the absolute upper edge of what it could do, and pushed the listener to the absolute upper edge of what a piano sonata could deliver.

We will also walk _Waldstein_ and _Les Adieux_ in paragraph-level overview, because they are the necessary context. The three sonatas together are the middle-period keyboard summit.

### 7.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — Cranz's nickname and the publisher's instinct

In 1838, the Hamburg music publisher August Cranz issued a new edition of Beethoven's Op. 57 piano sonata. On the title page, in addition to the standard opus number, Cranz added a nickname: _Sonate appassionata_. The Italian word _appassionata_ means _passionate, ardent, impassioned_. Cranz had not consulted Beethoven (Beethoven was eleven years dead). He had not consulted any of Beethoven's surviving friends or pupils. The nickname was a marketing choice. Cranz's instinct: the F-minor sonata had a register, driven, dark, intense, structurally extreme, that the buying public would recognize, and a one-word title would help the public find it.

The nickname stuck. By the 1850s the Op. 57 was being printed in editions as _Sonate Appassionata_ in German-, French-, and English-speaking markets. By 1900 the nickname was universal. Beethoven's own title — _Sonata für das Pianoforte, Op. 57, dem Grafen Franz von Brunswick gewidmet_ — survived only in catalogues.

The case is analogous to the _Moonlight_ (Chapter 4): a publisher or critic gives a nickname after Beethoven's death; the nickname becomes the title; Beethoven never knew. The honest move for the modern listener is to know the provenance — that _Appassionata_ is Cranz 1838, not Beethoven — while continuing to use the convenient nickname.

What is _interesting_ about Cranz's choice is that the nickname fits. The Op. 57 is, by general agreement, one of the most-impassioned works in Beethoven's catalogue. Charles Rosen, in _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas: A Short Companion_ , writes that the _Appassionata_ is _the most violent of the middle-period sonatas_ and discusses the work's structural features — the alternating loud-and-soft dynamics, the very wide pitch range, the relentless forward drive — as deliberate compositional choices designed to push the piano to its expressive limits. Cranz, in 1838, was responding to features of the work that were and remain real. The nickname is not Beethoven's, but it is not wrong.

#### What to notice

Three things in the _Appassionata_ first movement.

  1. **The opening figure.** The movement opens with a quiet, dark, descending arpeggio in F minor — the right hand outlining the F-minor triad in low octaves, the left hand answering with a similar figure two octaves lower. The opening is _subdued_ — pianissimo, in the deep bass register of the piano, almost subterranean. Listen for the _low_ sound: the _Appassionata_ opens in the keyboard's lowest territory, very different from the bright opening of _Waldstein_ (which opens in mid-range with repeated chords). The dark opening is the work's announcement.

  2. **The subito explosions.** Throughout the movement, Beethoven uses sudden dynamic contrasts — a passage will be quiet, almost whispered, and then _abruptly_ erupt into fortissimo for one or two measures, then drop back to quiet. This is the _subito piano_ signature (Thread B), or its inverse — _subito forte_. Listen for the _suddenness_ of the dynamic changes; they are not gradual crescendos. The piano is being pushed between extremes.

  3. **The relentless forward drive.** The first movement has very few moments of repose. The energy is sustained at high pressure throughout, with developmental work happening almost continuously rather than being confined to a designated development section. Listen for the _lack of breath_ : the movement does not stop to catch itself; it drives forward from beginning to end, with the moments of dynamic contrast (the subito explosions) as the only varieties of texture within the relentless drive.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the _Appassionata_ in 1804–1805, dedicated it to his friend and patron Count Franz von Brunsvik (the older brother of Therese von Brunsvik, who has been one of the candidates for the _Immortal Beloved_ of 1812), and published it in 1807 — at the same time he was composing the Fifth Symphony.
