### 7.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into Richter 1960.

* **0:00–0:15.** The opening descending F-minor arpeggio in the right hand, answered by the left hand two octaves below. Richter takes the opening very quietly, in the deep bass register. Notice the _low sound_ : the piano is being used in its bottom octave, where the harmonics are darkest and the sound is most weighted. The pace is moderate; the Allegro assai marking has not yet kicked in fully.

* **0:15–0:35.** A second statement of the figure, slightly varied. Then a sudden eruption, _fortissimo_ , for a few measures, before dropping back to piano. Listen for the first of the _subito explosions_ : the abrupt dynamic shift that is characteristic of the movement.

* **0:35–1:00.** The first theme proper. The opening figure is taken into more developmental territory; the harmonies move; the texture thickens. Notice the _anxious quality_ : the music is moving but not arriving anywhere stable.

* **1:00–1:30.** A more lyrical episode: a brief moment of relative repose. The second theme, in A♭ major (the relative major). Listen for the _singing_ quality of this moment; it is the closest the first movement comes to a moment of song.

* **1:30–2:00.** The lyrical episode is taken up by more agitated material. The dynamic increases. The forward drive resumes.

* **2:00–2:45.** Continued development of the first-theme material. The texture becomes denser; the piano writing becomes more demanding (fast figuration in both hands, wide leaps, full chord voicings).

* **2:45–3:15.** The closing material of the exposition. A return to the opening figure, now louder, and a closing buildup. About 3:00 the exposition ends. **Richter does not take the exposition repeat.** Most modern pianists do not, in the _Appassionata_ : the movement's relentless drive makes the repeat structurally redundant.

* **3:15–4:30.** **The development section begins.** Beethoven takes the opening figure through a series of remote keys (C♯ minor, G♭ major, B minor) and through dynamic extremes. Listen for the _intensification_ : the development is not a workshop of leisurely exploration but a _pressing_ of the material through every harmonic territory at high speed.

* **4:30–5:30.** Continued development. The texture becomes denser still. The piano writing is at the upper edge of what the 1805 fortepiano could deliver; modern pianists on modern instruments have more dynamic range available, which Richter and others use freely.

* **5:30–6:30.** The retransition to the recapitulation. Listen for the _long preparation_ : Beethoven builds tension through several measures of repeated figuration before the recapitulation finally arrives. The arrival is not announced with a fanfare; the recapitulation begins quietly, with the opening figure returning in the home key. The contrast: long buildup, quiet return — is one of the movement's structural signatures.

* **6:30–8:00.** The recapitulation. The exposition material returns, walked through in adapted form (the second theme now in F major rather than A♭ major). The texture remains dense; the forward drive remains intense.

* **8:00–10:00.** **The coda.** Like the _Eroica_ 's coda, this is more than a closing tag. Beethoven extends the first-theme material through one more cycle of development and arrives at the closing measures with a final eruption: fast figuration, fortissimo chords, the descending opening figure transformed into a closing cadence. The final measures are _not_ triumphant: the work ends in F minor, with the dark register restored, the energy spent but not victorious.

* **10:00–10:15.** The final chords. Richter holds the closing F-minor chord and lets it fade.

About ten minutes total in Richter. The movement maintains its high-pressure forward drive across the whole span. The subito dynamic shifts punctuate the texture. The harmonic argument is restless throughout. The closing in F minor (rather than F major) is one of the few middle-period Beethoven works that does _not_ end in the major mode: the _Appassionata_ refuses the heroic-resolution arc that the Fifth Symphony will provide.

### 7.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Why the _Appassionata_ is the "most extreme" middle-period sonata

Charles Rosen's _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas: A Short Companion_ makes the case that the _Appassionata_ is structurally the most extreme of Beethoven's middle-period sonatas. The argument rests on three features:

**The pitch range.** The _Appassionata_ uses a wider pitch range than any previous Beethoven sonata. The lowest notes are at the bottom of the 1805 fortepiano's compass (low F in the bass, almost the lowest available); the highest notes are at the top. Beethoven uses the full available range repeatedly, with sudden leaps between extremes. The pianist's hands must cover almost the entire keyboard within single passages.

**The dynamic range.** The score is marked with extreme dynamic contrasts — pianissimo to fortissimo within a few measures, sometimes within a single measure. The contrasts are not gradual crescendos but _subito_ shifts. The 1805 fortepiano had a more limited dynamic range than the modern concert grand, which means that on the period instrument the contrasts were even more _brutal_ than they sound on a modern piano. Beethoven was pushing the instrument's available dynamics to their limits.

**The harmonic compression.** The movement's harmonic motion is faster and more chromatic than in the _Waldstein_ or the earlier sonatas. The exposition moves through more keys; the development passes through still more; the coda extends the harmonic argument further. The result is a movement in which the listener has fewer harmonic resting places than in any previous Beethoven first movement.

Rosen's reading is the modern critical consensus. The _Appassionata_ is recognized, by general agreement, as the upper limit of what Beethoven attempted in the middle-period piano sonata, and as one of the landmarks where the piano sonata becomes a vehicle as ambitious as the symphony.

#### The _Waldstein_ (Op. 53, 1804)

Composed in summer 1804, just after the _Eroica_ 's completion, the _Waldstein_ sonata is the bright counterpart to the _Appassionata_ 's darkness. The work is in three movements: Allegro con brio (the dramatic first movement) / Introduzione: Adagio molto (a brief slow connector, originally planned as a substantial Andante that Beethoven eventually replaced with the shorter _Introduzione_ — the original Andante was published separately as _Andante favori_ , WoO 57) / Rondo: Allegretto moderato (the brilliant variation-and-rondo finale).

The first movement opens with eight repeated chords on C major in the right hand, answered by the same figure a step lower in B♭. The opening is famously _direct_ — no slow introduction, no harmonic complexity, just chordal announcement of the home key followed immediately by motion. This is similar to the _Eroica_ 's two opening chords, but in keyboard scale. The figure becomes the basis of the entire first movement, which is one of the most-demanding piano Allegros in the repertoire.

The recommended recording for the _Waldstein_ is **Maurizio Pollini, 1997 (Deutsche Grammophon)**. Pollini's reading is fast (about eleven minutes for the first movement), structurally clear, technically impeccable. Alternates: Sviatoslav Richter (a Live 1955 performance; the recording quality is rougher but the playing is fierce); Daniel Barenboim (the 1980s EMI cycle).

The _Waldstein_ dedication to Count Ferdinand von Waldstein (the same Bonn patron who had inscribed the _receive Mozart's spirit from Haydn's hands_ line in Beethoven's autograph book in 1792, Chapter 3) is one of the most-Beethoven dedications: Waldstein had been a constant presence in Beethoven's life from the Bonn years through the early Vienna years, and the dedication is a return-gift across the decade.

#### _Les Adieux_ (Op. 81a, 1809–1810)

_Les Adieux_ (Op. 81a) is the programmatic exception in Beethoven's piano sonatas. The work is in three movements: _Das Lebewohl_ (The Farewell, Adagio–Allegro) / _Abwesenheit_ (Absence, Andante espressivo) / _Das Wiedersehen_ (The Return, Vivacissimamente). The three-movement narrative depicts the departure, absence, and return of someone close, and the case is that the someone close is Archduke Rudolph, Beethoven's most important late-period patron, who left Vienna in May 1809 when the French army (under Napoleon, again) occupied the city, and returned in January 1810.

The work is the most-explicit programmatic statement in the Beethoven piano sonatas. The first-movement Adagio opens with three notes, G–F–E♭, set under the syllables _Le-be-wohl_ (German for "farewell"), with the three-note motif then becoming the building block of the entire first movement. The second movement (Absence) is in C minor and depicts the absence as a kind of empty waiting. The third movement (Return) is a fast and joyful Vivacissimamente that brings the work back to E♭ major.

The recommended recording is **Wilhelm Kempff, 1965 (Deutsche Grammophon)** — the same Kempff who recorded the _Moonlight_ and _Pathétique_ , here in the late-Beethoven keyboard register. Alternates: Alfred Brendel 1996 (Philips), Maurizio Pollini's late 2000s reading (DG).

_Les Adieux_ sits at the threshold of the late period. The work was composed in 1809–1810, the final years of the heroic middle period. The structural features — the three-note motif developed obsessively across the first movement, the harmonic restraint, the programmatic three-movement narrative — already point toward the introspective compositional style of Op. 90 (1814) and the late piano sonatas (Op. 101 onward, from 1816 — Chapter 15).

#### Definition: The _subito piano_ and _subito forte_ (Thread B)

The _subito piano_ (sudden quiet) and its inverse _subito forte_ (sudden loud) are Beethoven signatures. We met the _subito piano_ briefly in Chapter 2 (the opening of the _Pathétique_ , where Beethoven specifies _fortissimo piano_ — a loud strike that drops immediately to softness). The _Appassionata_ uses both _subito piano_ and _subito forte_ repeatedly across the first movement: passages will be at high dynamic and then _abruptly_ drop to whisper, or be at whisper and then _abruptly_ erupt.

The signature is in Rosen and in Lockwood (the chapter on the middle-period sonatas in _The Music and the Life_). The technique was unusual in the Classical-era piano repertoire — Haydn and Mozart used it occasionally for dramatic effect; Beethoven used it as a _constitutive feature_ of certain movements. The _Appassionata_ first movement is the showpiece. We will meet the device again in the development of Symphony 5 (Chapter 9) and in the late string quartets (Chapter 20); the full _subito_ arc across Beethoven's catalogue is mapped in Chapter 21, §21.3.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the _Appassionata_ 's ending in F minor (rather than the heroic F major) is a failure of the work to reach the per aspera ad astra arc that the Fifth Symphony provides. The misreading is understandable but wrong. Beethoven did not always _resolve_ his minor-key works into the major mode. The _Appassionata_ is one of several Beethoven works that ends in the minor mode — Op. 1 No. 3 ends in C minor (Chapter 3), the _Pathétique_ ends in C minor, the _Coriolan_ Overture ends in C minor, the F-minor Quartet Op. 95 ends in F minor.

The C-minor character thread is sometimes the per aspera ad astra arc (Symphony 5, ending in C major triumph). Sometimes it is the work that ends in the minor mode (Op. 1 No. 3, _Pathétique_ , _Coriolan_). The distinction matters: Beethoven _chose_ whether to resolve into the major mode based on the expressive content of the specific work, not as a uniform pattern. The Symphony 5 arc is famous because it is one model. The _Appassionata_ 's refusal to resolve is another. (Chapter 21 lays out the full C-minor catalogue and shows which works take the per-aspera-ad-astra arc and which end in the minor mode — _Appassionata_ and Op. 95 belong to the latter family.)

### 7.4 The Stories

#### Count Brunsvik and the dedication

Count Franz von Brunsvik (1777–1849) was a Hungarian aristocrat and amateur cellist with whom Beethoven was on friendly terms throughout the 1800s. He was the older brother of Therese von Brunsvik (one of the candidates for the _Immortal Beloved_ of 1812) and Josephine von Brunsvik (another candidate, with whom Beethoven had what was probably his most intense relationship in the 1804–07 period). The Brunsvik family — particularly Therese, Josephine, and their cousin Giulietta Guicciardi (the _Moonlight_ dedicatee, Chapter 4) — was at the center of Beethoven's romantic biography in the first decade of the 19th century.

The _Appassionata_ 's dedication to Franz von Brunsvik is one of the few Beethoven dedications that can be read as _friendship-and-family_ rather than patron-and-client. Brunsvik was not Beethoven's financial patron in the way Lichnowsky, Lobkowicz, or Razumovsky were. He was a friend, a fellow musician, a member of a family Beethoven was emotionally close to. The dedication is the gesture across that friendship.

#### Solomon's _Immortal Beloved_ thesis

Maynard Solomon's _Beethoven_ (1977, 2nd ed 1998) made the most-cited argument in modern Beethoven scholarship about the _Immortal Beloved_. The Immortal Beloved is the addressee of the letter Beethoven wrote in July 1812, found unsent among his papers after his death — a letter to a woman whom Beethoven addresses as "My angel, my all, my very self" and to whom he expresses passionate but conflicted love. The letter is dated only with day and month ("July 6 and 7, morning") and place ("K.", which Solomon and others identified as Karlsbad), not year. The identification of the year as 1812 was made through the matching of the dated travel records.

The identity of the Immortal Beloved has been argued for nearly two centuries. The leading 19th-century candidates were Giulietta Guicciardi, Therese von Brunsvik, and Amalie Sebald. Solomon, in his 1972 article and his 1977 book, made the case for **Antonie Brentano** (1780–1869, married to Franz Brentano of Frankfurt, mother of four, a friend of Beethoven's in the 1810–12 period). Solomon's case rested on the matching of travel records (Antonie Brentano was documentably in Karlsbad in July 1812; the other candidates were not), the matching of social circumstances (Antonie's husband Franz had handled some of Beethoven's financial affairs in 1810), and other documentary alignments.

The Solomon thesis has not been universally accepted but is widely cited; Lewis Lockwood treats it as the most-plausible single identification. Other modern scholars have argued for Josephine von Brunsvik (Beethoven had been romantically involved with her in 1804–07; the case rests on the surviving Beethoven-to-Josephine letters of that period) or for the open question.

For our purposes, the relevant point: the _Appassionata_ 's dedicatee, Franz von Brunsvik, was the brother of one of the leading Immortal Beloved candidates (Josephine). The Brunsvik family was at the center of Beethoven's romantic biography in the _Appassionata_ years. The work emerges from a emotional matrix that the dedication itself only hints at.

#### The 1807 publication and the piano of the period

The _Appassionata_ was published in February 1807 by the Bureau d'Arts et d'Industrie in Vienna. The publication came two years after the work's composition — Beethoven typically had his works performed privately for some months before publication, allowing the publisher to coordinate with the dedicatee and the dedicatee to receive the printed score first.

The piano of 1807 was the Viennese fortepiano — an instrument with a five-and-a-half-octave range (Beethoven would push to six octaves in the _Hammerklavier_ of 1818, when fortepianos had been extended), with a much lighter action than the modern concert grand, with less sustain, with a clearer but less powerful sound. The _Appassionata_ was composed for this instrument and is at the upper limit of what the instrument could deliver. On a 1805 Streicher or Walter fortepiano, the work's dynamic extremes are _more brutal_ — the soft passages are quieter, the loud passages are at the absolute limit of the instrument's power, the contrast feels more physical.

On a modern concert grand (Steinway D, Yamaha CF, Bösendorfer Imperial), the _Appassionata_ has more dynamic range available than Beethoven had to work with. Modern pianists must choose how to translate the work. Richter's 1960 Carnegie Hall reading uses the modern grand's full dynamic range; the soft passages are barely audible at the back of the hall, the loud passages are at the modern grand's limit. Period-instrument readings (e.g., Andreas Staier on fortepiano, or Steven Lubin on early piano) bring the work closer to the 1805 sound but at the cost of less dynamic range overall. Each choice is a defensible reading.

#### Three readings of the _Appassionata_

**Sviatoslav Richter, 1960 (Live at Carnegie Hall, Sony / RCA).** Richter's 1960 American tour included a famous Carnegie Hall recital of which the recordings (the _Appassionata_ , the Schubert _Wanderer Fantasy_ , the Prokofiev sonatas) are among the most-loved live piano documents of the recording era. The _Appassionata_ is taken at extreme intensity: very fast tempi in the fast sections, very quiet in the soft sections, with the _subito_ dynamic contrasts at maximum impact. The playing has the Richter combination of structural rigor and emotional incandescence. The recording sound is concert-hall live (with audience presence audible); the playing is the model of mid-20th-century Russian Beethoven.

**Emil Gilels, 1972 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Gilels's _Appassionata_ is the model German-sound reading. Slightly slower than Richter, with the dynamic range slightly compressed (Gilels was not as extreme as Richter on the soft end), with the structural clarity at the absolute upper edge. The reading is part of Gilels's eventually-incomplete Beethoven sonata cycle and is one of the cycle's most-loved entries. The recording sound is the late-DG Berlin sound — warm, present, full.

**Maurizio Pollini, 2002 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Pollini's _Appassionata_ (re-recording; his earlier 1976 reading is also widely admired) is the modern reading. The tempo is moderate, the structural argument is taken at full weight, the dynamic range is wide but slightly less extreme than Richter's. Pollini's voicing of the inner harmonies is unusually clear; the harmonic motion of the development is more legible in Pollini than in either Richter or Gilels. The reading is the contemporary reference recording.

#### Cross-piece links

* If the heroic-symphonic ambition of the _Appassionata_ interests you, the natural next stop is the **Fifth Symphony first movement** (Chapter 9) — the symphonic parallel composed in the same 1804–08 stretch.
* If the _Waldstein_ particularly engaged you, the natural next stop is the **Piano Concerto No. 4 in G major, Op. 58** (Chapter 8) — composed in the same productive 1806 stretch, in a related register of keyboard virtuosity.
* If _Les Adieux_ 's three-movement narrative interests you, the natural next stop is the **late piano sonata Op. 101** (Chapter 15) — also in a multi-movement narrative form, the threshold of the late style.
* If the structural extremity interests you, the natural next stop is the **Op. 106 _Hammerklavier_** , the great late-style structural extreme (Chapter 15) — the work that the _Appassionata_ anticipates by thirteen years.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the Brunsvik-and-Guicciardi network of Beethoven's romantic biography in the 1801–1812 period — Therese, Josephine, Giulietta, Franz, and the way the_ Moonlight _(Ch 4) and_ Appassionata _(this chapter) dedications fit into it. The conversation will run into the_ Immortal Beloved _question; let it._
* _Tell the companion which of the_ subito _dynamic shifts in the_ Appassionata _first movement felt most physical to you on the second listen, and ask her about Charles Rosen's analysis of the dynamic-as-structural-element in the middle-period sonatas._
* _Ask the companion to compare Richter 1960, Gilels 1972, and Pollini 2002 — specifically how each pianist handles the dynamic extremes and the structural argument of the development section. The three pianists are working with different traditions; she can place each in context._
* _Bring up the _Appassionata_ 's ending in F minor (rather than F major) with the companion — what Beethoven was choosing and what it tells us about the C-minor-character thread, where Op. 1 No. 3 also ends in the minor and Op. 111 ends in major._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The deep-bass opening at 0:00 — try to focus on the _register_ of the piano being used. Beethoven is in the bottom octave; the sound is dark and weighted.
* The first _subito_ dynamic shift around 0:15–0:20 — listen for the suddenness, not the volume itself. The shift is not a crescendo; it is an abrupt break.
* The retransition at 5:30–6:30 — Beethoven builds tension across several measures of repeated figuration before the recapitulation arrives. The arrival is quiet, not announced.
* The coda from 8:00 — like the _Eroica_ 's coda, this functions as a second development before the closing measures.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Charles Rosen's chapter on the _Appassionata_ in _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas: A Short Companion_ (Yale University Press, 2002).** Rosen's analysis of the work's structural extremity, the role of dynamic contrast as a constitutive feature, and the place of the _Appassionata_ in the middle-period sonata catalogue is the modern reference. Pair with Lewis Lockwood's chapter on the middle-period piano sonatas in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (2003) for the biographical-and-compositional context.

### Chapter Summary

The _Appassionata_ (Op. 57, 1804–05) is the structural extreme of Beethoven's middle-period piano sonatas. The first movement uses the full pitch range of the 1805 fortepiano, the full dynamic range available (with _subito_ shifts between extremes as a constitutive feature), and a harmonic argument that maintains its high-pressure forward drive across the entire ten minutes. The movement ends in F minor — refusing the heroic-resolution arc that the Fifth Symphony will provide three years later.

The nickname _Appassionata_ was given by the publisher August Cranz in 1838, eleven years after Beethoven's death. Beethoven dedicated the work to Count Franz von Brunsvik, brother of two of the Immortal Beloved candidates (Therese and Josephine von Brunsvik). Solomon's _Brentano_ identification of the Immortal Beloved is the modern reference; the _Appassionata_ 's emotional context overlaps with this network.

You met the _Waldstein_ (Op. 53, 1804) in paragraph-level overview — the bright C-major counterpart, dedicated to the old Bonn patron Count Waldstein, with one of the most-demanding piano Allegros in the repertoire. The recommended _Waldstein_ recording is **Pollini, 1997 (DG)**.

You met _Les Adieux_ (Op. 81a, 1809–10) in paragraph-level overview — the explicit three-movement programmatic narrative (Farewell, Absence, Return) composed for Archduke Rudolph's departure from and return to Vienna during the 1809 French occupation. The recommended recording is **Kempff, 1965 (DG)**.

You met the **subito piano** signature (Thread B) — the abrupt dynamic shift between extremes, a Beethoven device used as a _constitutive feature_ of certain movements. We met it briefly in Chapter 2; we have now met it as a main feature; we will meet it again in Symphony 5 (Chapter 9) and the late quartets (Chapter 20).

You met three readings of the _Appassionata_ — Richter 1960 (model Russian incandescent), Gilels 1972 (model German structural), Pollini 2002 (model modern reference) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you to the two great middle-period concertos — the Violin Concerto Op. 61 and the Piano Concerto No. 4, both composed in 1806. After the _Appassionata_ 's violent keyboard solitude, the concertos offer the opposite: the soloist in dialogue with the orchestra, at the heart of the heroic-middle stretch.

* * *

---

## Chapter 8: The Two Great Middle-Period Concertos — Violin Op. 61 and Piano No. 4

In a single productive stretch of 1806, between the _Eroica_ and the Fifth Symphony, Beethoven composed his only Violin Concerto, his Fourth Piano Concerto, and the three Op. 59 _Razumovsky_ string quartets. The stretch is one of the most fertile in any composer's life. We are going to spend this chapter on the two concertos.

The Violin Concerto in D major, Op. 61 (premiered 23 December 1806 at the Theater an der Wien, Franz Clement as soloist). The Piano Concerto No. 4 in G major, Op. 58 (composed in the same stretch, premiered privately at Lobkowicz in March 1807 with Beethoven as soloist; the public premiere followed at the December 22, 1808 four-hour concert that we will return to in Chapter 9). Both works are among the most-loved Beethoven concertos. Both opened territory in the concerto genre that subsequent composers responded to.

The Violin Concerto is the chapter's anchor. The Piano Concerto No. 4's second movement, Andante con moto, is the secondary anchor, in the brief dialogue between solo piano and orchestral strings that Wilhelm Kempff called _Orpheus taming the Furies_. Both anchors are first-movement-or-second-movement focused; the full concertos take fifty to sixty minutes between them and we are listening to the most-discussed individual movements.

### 8.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — Franz Clement's premiere

The Violin Concerto's premiere on 23 December 1806 was given by Franz Clement (1780–1842), the violinist for whom Beethoven composed the work. Clement was a Vienna native, a prodigy who had performed publicly from age eight, by 1806 the concertmaster of the Theater an der Wien orchestra and one of the most-respected violinists in the city. Beethoven and Clement were friends; the autograph score of the Violin Concerto bears, on its title page, the inscription _Concerto par Clemenza pour Clement_ (a pun: _Concerto par Clemenza_ , Concerto by Clement; _pour Clement_ , for Clement).

The premiere did not go entirely well. The contemporary reviews (the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_ of 8 February 1807, the _Wiener Zeitung_ of 24 December 1806) describe a mixed reception. Some critics found the work too long, too sprawling, with insufficient virtuoso display for the violinist. One Vienna review reportedly remarked that the work's structural ambition was "interesting but uneconomical." The audience response was respectful but not enthusiastic.

What appears to have particularly puzzled the 1806 audience: **the opening**. The concerto begins with five repeated kettledrum notes on a D, alone, without any orchestral support, for two measures. Then the woodwinds enter, then the strings, then the full orchestra builds. The kettledrum-alone opening was unprecedented in a concerto. Concertos opened with orchestral statements (the tutti exposition, in the Classical concerto-form convention). The Beethoven Violin Concerto opens with a _single percussion instrument_ establishing the rhythmic and tonal frame, before any melodic material appears.

Clement himself played the work apparently well, by the account, though contemporary reports mention that he played one of his own concertos in the second half of the concert in between the Beethoven and (apparently) some other works — a typical Vienna concert program of the era, which often combined the new and the old in a single evening. Clement's role in the work's reception was respectful but did not become a long advocacy; he played the work occasionally over the next years but did not make it the centerpiece of his repertoire.

The Beethoven Violin Concerto then languished for forty years. It was rarely performed between 1806 and 1844. The work was rescued from neglect by the young **Joseph Joachim** (1831–1907), who in May 1844, at the age of twelve, played it in London under Mendelssohn's direction. The performance was a sensation. Joachim made the work part of his standard repertoire and (with the cadenza he composed for the first movement) effectively re-established the work in the violin canon. By 1860 the Beethoven Violin Concerto was one of the central works of the violin repertoire; it has been ever since.

The 1806 premiere was, in the sense, a failed event. The 1844 revival was the moment the work entered the canon. The story is a useful reminder that the canonization of works is often slow and depends on advocacy — Joachim was the Mendelssohn-influenced figure who chose to keep playing the concerto in the 1840s; without that advocacy, the work might have remained in the historical footnotes of the symphonic literature.

#### What to notice

Three things in the Violin Concerto's first movement.

  1. **The five repeated kettledrum notes.** The movement opens with the timpani playing five identical D's, alone, with no other instruments. The notes are short, soft, even, and rhythmically distinctive — _one-two-three-four-five_ , with the fifth slightly stretched. Listen for the _isolation_ of the opening: a single percussion voice establishing the work's rhythmic identity before any harmony or melody.

  2. **The woodwind entrance.** After the kettledrum notes, the woodwinds (oboes, clarinets, bassoons, with horns) enter with the first theme — a calm, lyrical descending figure in D major. Listen for the _transition_ from the percussion-alone opening into the woodwind statement; Beethoven is establishing the work's character one layer at a time.

  3. **The eventual entrance of the solo violin.** The solo violinist does not enter immediately. The orchestral exposition (the _tutti_ exposition, in the Classical double-exposition concerto form) runs for about three minutes before the soloist plays a single note. When the violin enters, it does so with a slow ascending scale that climbs into the violin's upper register, then settles into the first theme. Listen for the _delayed entrance_ : this is one of the longest orchestral expositions in the Classical concerto repertoire before the soloist arrives.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the Violin Concerto in D major, Op. 61 in late 1806 for the violinist Franz Clement, who premiered it on 23 December 1806 at the Theater an der Wien in Vienna; the work was little-performed for the next four decades and was effectively revived by the young Joseph Joachim in May 1844 in London.

### 8.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into Mutter / Karajan 1979.

* **0:00–0:08.** The five repeated kettledrum notes, alone. Notice the _softness_ : not a fanfare; not a percussion explosion. The timpani notes are at moderate dynamic, evenly spaced, with the fifth note slightly stretched. The opening establishes the rhythmic identity of the work without announcing dramatic intensity.

* **0:08–0:30.** The woodwinds enter with the first theme. The melody is calm, descending, in D major. Listen for the _serenity_ : the work opens in a pastoral-classical register, very different from the dramatic _Appassionata_ or the urgent _Eroica_. The Violin Concerto is one of Beethoven's most-lyrical works; the opening establishes the lyrical voice immediately.

* **0:30–1:30.** The first theme is taken up by the full orchestra. Notice the _kettledrum rhythm_ recurring throughout the texture: the five-note rhythm from the opening keeps returning, sometimes in the timpani, sometimes in the strings, sometimes embedded in the woodwinds. The five repeated notes are not a one-time opening device: they are a _rhythmic cell_ that runs through the entire movement.

* **1:30–2:30.** The second theme arrives. Listen for the _lyrical extension_ : the second theme is even more lyrical than the first, with a long sustained melody in the oboes that the strings then take up. The mood is unhurried; the harmonic motion is gentle.

* **2:30–3:15.** The closing material of the orchestral exposition. The first theme returns; the orchestra builds to a closing cadence in the dominant. About 3:15 the orchestral exposition ends.

* **3:15–3:45.** **The solo violin enters.** Listen for the slow ascending scale that climbs into the violin's upper register, then settles into the first theme. The entrance is _not_ heroic; the soloist enters in dialogue with the orchestra, not in dramatic announcement.

* **3:45–6:00.** The solo violin takes the first theme through more elaborate figuration, with the orchestra now in accompanying role. Notice the _virtuoso writing_ : fast scales, double stops (two notes played simultaneously), arpeggios in the violin's upper register. The writing is demanding but never overpowering; Beethoven keeps the violin within a singing register even at virtuoso pace.

* **6:00–8:00.** The solo violin moves to the second theme, then through transitions and variations. The orchestra continues to provide the kettledrum-rhythm pulse in the background. Listen for the _integration_ : the soloist is not a separate voice contending with the orchestra; the soloist is integrated into the orchestral fabric, sometimes leading, sometimes supporting.

* **8:00–10:30.** The closing material of the solo exposition. The first theme returns in the solo violin; the orchestra builds; the exposition closes in the dominant. In some performances Mutter's recording takes the standard repeat of the exposition; the 1979 reading does not. The recording moves directly into the development.

* **10:30–14:00.** **The development section.** Beethoven takes the first theme through related keys, with the soloist now leading and the orchestra reacting. Listen for the _dialogue_ : the solo violin proposes a new direction; the orchestra responds; the violin takes the response further. The development is the dialogue between solo and tutti, more than in any earlier Beethoven concerto.

* **14:00–17:00.** Continued development, building toward the recapitulation. The kettledrum rhythm returns more prominently. The harmonic motion drives toward the home key.

* **17:00–17:30.** **The recapitulation begins.** The five repeated kettledrum notes return: alone, as at the opening — for two measures, then the woodwinds enter with the first theme. Listen for the _symmetry_ : the recapitulation echoes the opening's structure, the percussion notes returning before the melodic material.

* **17:30–22:00.** The recapitulation walks through the exposition material with the solo violin now leading. The themes return; the textures elaborate; the orchestra and soloist remain in dialogue.

* **22:00–23:30.** **The cadenza.** Beethoven did not write a cadenza for the violin version of the concerto (he did write one for his own piano arrangement of the work, which we will discuss in §8.3: that cadenza has been adapted for violin by various players, notably Wolfgang Schneiderhan and Isabelle Faust). The cadenza is the soloist's showpiece — an unaccompanied virtuoso passage where the soloist demonstrates technical command, traditionally improvised but in practice usually pre-composed.

Mutter plays Fritz Kreisler's cadenza (composed in 1907) — the most-commonly-played cadenza in modern performance. The Kreisler cadenza is lyrical, technically demanding, and integrates the kettledrum-rhythm material into its development. Listen for the _connection to the movement's material_ : Kreisler is not introducing new themes; he is elaborating on what Beethoven has already presented.

* **23:30–24:00.** After the cadenza, the orchestra returns for the closing measures. The first theme returns once more; the kettledrum rhythm appears for the last time; the movement closes quietly in D major.

About twenty-four minutes total in Mutter. The kettledrum rhythm runs through the entire movement. The dialogue between soloist and orchestra is the work's central structural device. The lyrical character is maintained throughout.

### 8.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The double-exposition concerto form

The Beethoven Violin Concerto is in the _double-exposition concerto form_ : the standard Classical concerto layout inherited from Mozart and Haydn. The form: an orchestral exposition (the _tutti exposition_) that introduces the themes, followed by a _solo exposition_ in which the soloist enters with the themes (typically with elaborate decorations and additional virtuoso material). Then a development section in which soloist and orchestra work the material together. Then a recapitulation in which the themes return. Then a cadenza for the soloist (typically just before the closing tutti). Then a closing tutti.

The form was the standard concerto layout from about 1770 through about 1820. Mozart's twenty-three piano concertos use it. Haydn's concertos use it. Beethoven's first three piano concertos use it. The Violin Concerto extends it in two ways: by making the orchestral exposition longer than usual (about three minutes, where Mozart's typical orchestral exposition is closer to two), and by giving the soloist a more _integrated_ role in the development (where Mozart's concertos often have the soloist alternate with the orchestra in clear blocks; Beethoven blurs the boundaries).

The form would be modified in later concertos. The Piano Concerto No. 4 (Op. 58, composed in the same stretch as the Violin Concerto) opens with the soloist entering first, _before_ the orchestra — an innovation that breaks the double-exposition convention. The Piano Concerto No. 5 (the _Emperor_ , Op. 73, 1809) opens with a more elaborate cadenza-like soloist entrance before the orchestral exposition. By the time Beethoven finished his concerto work in 1809, the double-exposition form had been substantially modified.

#### The kettledrum rhythm as structural device

The five repeated kettledrum notes at the opening are the rhythmic cell of the entire first movement. Beethoven uses the _short-short-short-short-long_ pattern as a recurring motif that surfaces throughout the orchestra — sometimes in the timpani, sometimes in the strings playing pizzicato, sometimes in the woodwinds, sometimes in the cellos and basses. The cell is rhythmic rather than melodic; what recurs is the _rhythm_ (four short notes followed by a long), not a specific pitch sequence.

This is motivic-development discipline (Chapter 3, Chapter 6) extended to the _rhythmic_ domain. Beethoven had used motivic development of pitch material in his prior works (the four-note cell of the Fifth Symphony, the cell of the Op. 1 No. 3, the cell of the _Pathétique_). In the Violin Concerto he applies the same discipline to a _rhythmic_ cell. The five-note rhythm is the equivalent of a thematic cell, used as the building block of the movement.

Tovey's _Essays in Musical Analysis_ (vol. 3) discusses the kettledrum rhythm at length and identifies it as one of the work's central structural innovations. Lockwood and other modern scholars extend Tovey's analysis. The technique would influence later composers' use of rhythmic cells (Brahms's first symphony, Mahler's second, Sibelius's symphonies all show related uses).

#### The cadenza question

Beethoven left the cadenza in the Violin Concerto _unwritten_. The standard Classical convention was that the cadenza was the soloist's improvisation — the soloist's chance to demonstrate not only technical mastery but compositional invention. Beethoven himself improvised cadenzas in his own piano-concerto performances (the documented anecdotes from Czerny and Ries record extensive improvised cadenzas in the late 1790s and early 1800s). The Violin Concerto's cadenza was, by the convention, expected to be improvised by the violinist or supplied by the violinist as a written-out solo passage.

Most violinists in the 19th and 20th centuries have used pre-composed cadenzas rather than improvising. The three most-played:

**The Joseph Joachim cadenza (1880s).** Joachim, the violinist who effectively revived the work in 1844, composed cadenzas for the Beethoven Violin Concerto's three movements in the 1880s. The Joachim cadenza for the first movement is the most-played 19th-century cadenza, characterized by virtuoso double-stops, complex chordal writing, and integration of the kettledrum-rhythm material. **Heifetz's 1955 recording uses the Joachim cadenza.**

**The Fritz Kreisler cadenza (1907).** Kreisler — Austrian violinist, composer, and one of the great violinists of the early 20th century — composed his own cadenza for the work in 1907. The Kreisler cadenza is more _lyrical_ than Joachim's, with more singing-line writing and less heavy double-stopping. It has become the most-played 20th-century cadenza. **Mutter's 1979 recording uses the Kreisler cadenza.**

**Beethoven's own piano-arrangement cadenza adapted for violin.** When Beethoven made his own piano arrangement of the Violin Concerto (Op. 61a, completed in 1807), he wrote cadenzas for the piano version. The first-movement cadenza is famous for including a _timpani part_ — the soloist is accompanied by timpani throughout, playing the five-note rhythmic cell. The cadenza has been _adapted for violin_ by several modern players (notably Wolfgang Schneiderhan in the 1960s, then Isabelle Faust in her 2010 reading with Abbado, and others). The adaptation requires the violinist to play the figuration that Beethoven wrote for piano, often re-distributed across the four strings, with a timpani player accompanying. **Faust's 2010 recording uses the Beethoven-piano-cadenza adaptation.**

The three cadenzas give three different readings of what the cadenza moment should be. Joachim is virtuoso, Kreisler is lyrical, Beethoven's own (adapted) is structural (it integrates the timpani cell into the cadenza itself). Modern violinists choose among them — sometimes mix cadenzas across movements, sometimes write their own — and the choice tells you something about how the soloist hears the work.

#### The Piano Concerto No. 4, second movement

We are anchoring the secondary listening on the second movement of the Piano Concerto No. 4 — Andante con moto, in E minor, about five minutes long.

The movement is one of the most-discussed individual movements in the Beethoven concerto catalogue. The structure: the orchestra (strings only, no winds) plays a sequence of stern, austere, unison phrases in E minor; the solo piano answers each phrase with a contrasting lyrical, consoling response in major. The dialogue continues across the movement, with the orchestra's phrases gradually softening and the piano's responses gradually leading the way, until at the end the orchestra has been _tamed_ into agreement with the piano's lyrical voice.

The interpretation, that the movement depicts Orpheus taming the Furies, was articulated by **Wilhelm Kempff** in his writings on Beethoven and has been widely adopted. The Orpheus myth: the legendary singer descends to the underworld to retrieve his dead wife Eurydice, and is allowed to do so only if he can charm the rulers of the underworld with his music. The Furies, the avenging spirits, oppose him; he sings; they are eventually persuaded. Liszt had composed a _Orpheus_ symphonic poem (1854) using related material; the connection to the Beethoven movement is interpretive rather than as Beethoven's own intent. Beethoven himself left no programmatic notes for the movement.

Whether the Orpheus reading is _the_ reading or _a_ reading is open. What is documented: the movement is one of the most-strikingly constructed slow movements in the concerto literature, with the explicit dialogue-as-structure (strings versus piano, in alternating units) as the central device. The recommended recording is **Wilhelm Kempff / Ferdinand Leitner / Berlin Philharmonic, 1962 (DG)** — Kempff playing the work he had interpreted in writing as the Orpheus-taming-the-Furies movement.

#### Definition: The double-exposition concerto form (continued)

The double-exposition concerto form was the standard Classical layout. We have seen how Beethoven extends and modifies it. The form's central feature: the orchestral exposition introduces themes that the soloist will then re-introduce with elaboration. The structural device: the _delay_ of the soloist's entrance allows the orchestra to establish a frame within which the soloist's eventual entrance can be heard as _response_ or _answer_.

The Beethoven Violin Concerto's three-minute orchestral exposition is at the upper end of what the form allowed. Mozart's typical orchestral exposition is two minutes or less. Beethoven extends the proportion, giving the orchestra more time to establish the lyrical character and the kettledrum rhythm before the soloist arrives. The expansion is part of the same pattern we saw in the _Eroica_ 's expanded form: every section is given more space.

After Beethoven's late concertos (Piano Concerto No. 4 with its opening-soloist innovation, the _Emperor_ No. 5 with its cadenza-like opening soloist), the double-exposition form would be increasingly modified by 19th-century composers. Chopin's two piano concertos (1830) keep the form. Schumann's Piano Concerto (1845) modifies it. Brahms's two piano concertos (1859 and 1881) substantially redesign it. By Tchaikovsky and Rachmaninoff, the double-exposition is no longer the default. The Beethoven concertos are at the end of the form's classical period.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the Beethoven Violin Concerto is a _virtuoso showcase_ like the Romantic-era concertos of Paganini, Wieniawski, or even Tchaikovsky. It is not. The work is one of Beethoven's most-lyrical compositions, with the violin writing emphasizing singing-line beauty over technical display. The fast passages exist but serve the lyrical content rather than displacing it. The cadenzas are the only stretches of pure virtuoso writing, and those are _added_ by later violinists.

The misconception matters because if you expect the Violin Concerto to be like Tchaikovsky's, you will find it slow and undramatic. If you expect it to be like Mozart's, you may find it sprawling. The work is its own thing — a long-breathed lyrical concerto in which the violinist is asked primarily to sing, with virtuoso display as a secondary requirement. The 1806 Vienna audience's mixed reception probably reflected, in part, the work's failure to deliver the virtuoso spectacle that early-19th-century audiences expected from a concerto premiere.

### 8.4 The Stories

#### Franz Clement and the inscription

The autograph score of the Violin Concerto bears the inscription on the title page: _Concerto par Clemenza pour Clement primo Violino e Direttore al theatro a Vienna_ — a friendly pun playing on Clement's name with the Italian word _clemenza_ (clemency, mercy). Beethoven and Clement were on good enough terms for the inscription to be a joke between friends.

Clement was thirty-six at the time of the premiere — six years older than Beethoven. He had been a child prodigy whose father had toured him through Europe in the 1790s (he played for Haydn in London at age twelve; Haydn signed his autograph book with a friendly note). By 1806 Clement was an established Vienna musician, concertmaster of the Theater an der Wien, regularly performing solo recitals in the city. The relationship with Beethoven appears to have been collegial and friendly — Clement had probably played in some of the _Eroica_ 's private rehearsals; he was present in the Vienna musical community Beethoven was part of.

The story of Clement's premiere has another detail. Clement reportedly sight-read the work at the premiere; that is, played it for the first time at the actual concert. The story comes from the _Wiener Theaterzeitung_ of 25 December 1806 and is repeated in many later sources. Whether literally true or somewhat exaggerated (Clement may have rehearsed portions of the work), it is consistent with the Vienna performance culture of the period, in which new works were often premiered with minimal rehearsal. Beethoven's frustration with the premieres of his works (the December 1808 four-hour concert that we will discuss in Chapter 9 includes complaints from Beethoven about under-rehearsed performances) is part of the same pattern.

#### Joachim and the 1844 revival

Joseph Joachim (1831–1907) was twelve years old when he played the Beethoven Violin Concerto in London in May 1844, with Mendelssohn conducting at the Philharmonic Society. The performance was the turning point in the work's reception history.

Joachim had been a child prodigy in Pest (now Budapest) and Vienna, then in Leipzig, where he had become Mendelssohn's pupil. Mendelssohn arranged for Joachim's London debut. The Beethoven Violin Concerto was Mendelssohn's choice for the program — a work that had been neglected for nearly forty years, that Mendelssohn admired as one of the great concertos, and that he believed deserved more performance.

The 1844 London performance was a critical success. _The Musical World_ and other London papers carried enthusiastic reviews. Joachim subsequently made the work a centerpiece of his repertoire, performing it frequently across Europe over the next fifty years. By the 1860s — when Joachim was in his thirties and the most-respected violinist in Europe — the Beethoven Violin Concerto was firmly in the violin repertoire. Joachim's cadenza for the work (composed in the 1880s) became the standard pre-composed cadenza of the 19th century.

The revival story is a useful historical case. Works enter the canon through advocacy by individual performers — Mendelssohn's choice to program the work, Joachim's choice to keep performing it, the critical reception that followed. Without that advocacy, the work might have remained in the neglected category. The case is parallel to Bach's revival (which Mendelssohn also drove, with the 1829 _St. Matthew Passion_ performance) and to other revival stories. Canons are made, not given.

#### The Piano Concerto No. 4 — composition and premiere

The Piano Concerto No. 4 in G major, Op. 58, was composed in 1805–1806 — concurrent with the _Razumovsky_ quartets and the Violin Concerto. The work was dedicated to Archduke Rudolph (Beethoven's most-important late-period patron from this point forward; see Chapter 12, Chapter 19) and premiered privately in March 1807 at Lobkowicz with Beethoven as soloist.

The public premiere was at the famous December 22, 1808 four-hour concert at the Theater an der Wien — the same concert that premiered the Fifth and Sixth Symphonies (Chapter 9). Beethoven was the soloist; the orchestra was under-rehearsed; the account is that Beethoven, who was by then beginning to lose his hearing in the higher register, had difficulty hearing the soloist's part in the dialogue with the orchestra. The performance was reportedly the last public concert at which Beethoven appeared as a piano soloist; his public solo career effectively ended in late 1808 because of the deafness.

The Piano Concerto No. 4 is structurally one of Beethoven's most innovative concertos. It opens with the _piano alone_ — five measures of solo piano statement before the orchestra enters. This is the inversion of the standard double-exposition form: instead of the orchestra introducing the themes and the soloist responding, the soloist introduces and the orchestra responds. The innovation was widely noted at the time and has been credited as the start of the _integrated concerto_ tradition that Schumann, Brahms, and later 19th-century composers would extend.

The second movement (the Orpheus-taming-the-Furies movement we are anchoring on) is followed by a Vivace third movement that runs without break from the second — an _attacca_ transition. This too is innovative for the concerto genre; most Classical concertos have clear gaps between movements. Beethoven would use the attacca device again in the _Emperor_ (No. 5) and in many later works.

#### Three readings of the Violin Concerto

**Anne-Sophie Mutter / Herbert von Karajan / Berlin Philharmonic, 1979 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Mutter was fifteen years old when she recorded this Violin Concerto with Karajan and the Berlin Philharmonic. The reading is one of the most-loved Mutter documents — lyrical, technically clean, with the Karajan-Berlin sound at its late-1970s peak. Mutter plays the Kreisler cadenza, which fits her lyrical approach. The recording sound is the late DG Berlin sound: warm, present, slightly mannered in the EMI-like way Karajan favored.

**Jascha Heifetz / Charles Munch / Boston Symphony Orchestra, 1955 (RCA).** Heifetz was the most-celebrated violinist of the mid-20th century — Russian-born American virtuoso whose tone was, by general agreement, one of the most-distinctive in violin history. His 1955 Beethoven Concerto with Munch is one of his great recordings. The tempos are slightly faster than Mutter's; the playing is at the absolute upper edge of technical command. Heifetz plays the Joachim cadenza, which suits his approach (the heavier double-stopping, the more virtuoso writing). The recording sound is mid-1950s RCA Boston — clean, articulate, with the Boston Symphony's golden-age sound.

**Isabelle Faust / Claudio Abbado / Orchestra Mozart, 2010 (Harmonia Mundi).** Faust is a German violinist of the contemporary generation, known for her HIP-informed readings (though she plays a modern Stradivarius, not a period instrument). Her 2010 Beethoven Concerto with Abbado and the Orchestra Mozart (a chamber-orchestra-sized ensemble Abbado founded in Bologna) is one of the most-praised modern readings. Faust plays the Beethoven-piano-arrangement cadenza adapted for violin, the one with the timpani accompaniment, making this the rare modern recording that includes the timpani-and-violin cadenza dialogue. The orchestral sound is more transparent than Mutter's full-Berlin sound; the playing is more inflected with period-performance awareness even on modern instruments.

The three readings give the work-vs-interpretation triangle: Mutter the classical-romantic, Heifetz the virtuoso-rigorous, Faust the contemporary-historically-informed. Each is correct in its own framework.

#### Cross-piece links

* If the kettledrum-rhythm-as-structural-cell interests you, the natural next stop is **Symphony 7's second movement Allegretto** (Chapter 11) — also built on a recurring rhythmic cell (a dotted-rhythm ostinato) at unprecedented length.
* If the Piano Concerto No. 4's Orpheus-and-Furies dialogue interests you, the natural next stop is the **slow movement of the _Hammerklavier_** (Chapter 15) — also built around piano-as-singer in dialogue with surrounding texture, though at much greater scale.
* If the cadenza question interests you, the natural next stop is **comparing the three cadenzas in three recordings** — the Kreisler in Mutter, the Joachim in Heifetz, the Beethoven-piano-adapted in Faust. Each cadenza is a different reading of what the moment should be.
* If you want the _Emperor_ concerto (No. 5, 1809), the natural recommended recording is **Krystian Zimerman / Leonard Bernstein / Vienna Philharmonic, 1989 (DG)** — one of the great _Emperor_ readings of the recording era. The work is not anchored in this volume; the _Emperor_ is briefly discussed in Chapter 14 as one of the last pre-crisis pieces.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the Joachim revival of 1844 — what made Mendelssohn and Joachim choose the work in that year, how the London Philharmonic Society received it, and how the advocacy of an individual performer can rescue a work from forty years of neglect._
* _Tell the companion which of the three cadenzas (Joachim in Heifetz, Kreisler in Mutter, Beethoven-piano-adapted in Faust) felt right to you, and ask her about the case for each as the modern soloist's choice._
* _Ask the companion about the December 22, 1808 four-hour concert that included the Piano Concerto No. 4's public premiere, and how Beethoven's hearing loss by that point affected his ability to perform as soloist._
* _Have the companion compare the Kempff "Orpheus taming the Furies" reading of the Piano Concerto No. 4 second movement with what the music itself does — does the Orpheus narrative fit, or does it project a story onto material that supports other readings as well?_

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The five repeated kettledrum notes at 0:00; they will return many times throughout the movement. Try to count how many times you can hear the _short-short-short-short-long_ rhythm in the orchestra in the first three minutes.
* The orchestral exposition before the soloist enters (0:00–3:15) — try to follow the kettledrum-rhythm transformations as it passes through different instruments.
* The soloist's first entrance at 3:15 — listen for the slow ascending scale that climbs to the upper register before settling into the first theme.
* The cadenza around 22:00 — try to identify which Beethoven themes Kreisler is elaborating in his cadenza writing.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further-listening recommendation, listen to the **Piano Concerto No. 5 "Emperor," Op. 73 (1809)** — Beethoven's last completed piano concerto, the work composed during the 1809 French occupation of Vienna and dedicated to Archduke Rudolph. The recommended recording is **Krystian Zimerman / Leonard Bernstein / Vienna Philharmonic, 1989 (DG)**. The _Emperor_ is the heroic-period summit of the concerto literature and pairs well with the lyrical Violin Concerto we have just heard.

### Chapter Summary

The Violin Concerto in D major, Op. 61 (1806) is the lyrical center of Beethoven's middle-period concerto writing. The work opens with five repeated kettledrum notes alone, a rhythmic cell that recurs throughout the first movement, followed by a long orchestral exposition before the soloist enters. The first movement is in the standard double-exposition concerto form, expanded at every section. The cadenzas are not written by Beethoven and are typically supplied by Joachim (1880s), Kreisler (1907), or by adaptation of Beethoven's own piano-arrangement cadenza.

The work was premiered by Franz Clement on 23 December 1806 at the Theater an der Wien. The 1806 reception was mixed; the work was neglected for forty years; the revival was Joseph Joachim's London performance under Mendelssohn in May 1844. The Joachim advocacy effectively established the work in the violin canon.

You met the Piano Concerto No. 4 in G major, Op. 58 (1805–06) as the innovative companion to the Violin Concerto — opening with piano alone (inverting the double-exposition convention), with the second-movement Andante con moto as the "Orpheus taming the Furies" dialogue between solo piano and orchestral strings. The recommended recording is **Kempff / Leitner / Berlin Philharmonic, 1962 (DG)**.

You met three readings of the Violin Concerto — Mutter / Karajan 1979 (classical-romantic with Kreisler cadenza), Heifetz / Munch 1955 (virtuoso-rigorous with Joachim cadenza), Faust / Abbado 2010 (contemporary with Beethoven-piano-adapted cadenza) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you to the Fifth Symphony — all four movements this time, in fuller context than Volume 1 had room for. The hinge work of the heroic middle period.

## Chapter 9: Symphony No. 5 — All Four Movements

In Volume 1 of _Close Listening_ , you met the first movement of Beethoven's Fifth Symphony. The Volume 1 chapter ran through the four-note motif, the workshop method, and the 1808 premiere; it ended by pointing forward to a fuller treatment of the whole symphony in a later volume. This is that later volume.

We are going to listen to all four movements, in the same Carlos Kleiber 1974 recording that Volume 1 used. The first movement you have already heard twice (once with the Volume 1 chapter, once for yourself). The second, third, and fourth movements are the new territory. We will move through them in sequence, with the compositional history and the December 22, 1808 premiere context in the background.

The Fifth Symphony is, by general agreement, the most-famous work of orchestral music in the Western tradition. The four-note opening is one of the most-recognized sounds in human culture — quoted in films, advertised in commercials, used as ringtones, paraphrased in every context where Western culture wants to gesture toward _seriousness_ or _destiny_. The cultural icon status is so complete that the _actual music_ often becomes hard to hear.

The four movements together are about thirty-two minutes of music. The first movement is the seven-minute workshop you already know. The second is a long, lyrical slow movement in A♭ major in variation form. The third is a scherzo-and-trio in C minor that introduces the famous attacca transition into the fourth movement. The fourth is the triumphant C-major finale that completes the per aspera ad astra arc, minor-key struggle to major-key triumph, that Beethoven made canonical with this work. We will walk through them in that order.

### 9.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — what Schindler invented and Hoffmann did not

The most-famous Beethoven quotation about the Fifth Symphony is, by professional consensus, something Beethoven never said.

According to Anton Schindler's 1840 _Biographie von Ludwig van Beethoven_ , when asked what the four-note opening meant, Beethoven replied: _So pocht das Schicksal an die Pforte!_ — "Thus fate knocks at the door." The line has been quoted in every program note for two hundred years. It has shaped the way generations have heard the opening. It has given the symphony a story.

Modern Beethoven scholarship has systematically dismantled Schindler's biography. Schindler — Beethoven's friend and self-appointed secretary in the late 1820s — spent the years after Beethoven's death curating and inventing Beethoven's legacy. He forged entries in Beethoven's conversation books (Lockwood's _Beethoven and His Conversation Books_ , 2018, traces the forgeries in detail). He invented incidents that did not happen. He supplied quotations Beethoven had not delivered. The "fate knocks" line has been examined critically for forty years and is, by general scholarly agreement, a Schindler embroidery — possibly inspired by something Beethoven actually did say, possibly invented from nothing, but not Beethoven's words.

This volume's discipline (Chapter 1, the topic prompt's §5.B2) requires that Schindler-sourced anecdotes be flagged. The "fate knocks" line is the most famous example. Modern Beethoven scholars do not include it in critical biographies without the caveat. The line continues to appear in popular program notes; we are not going to repeat it here as fact. The opening four notes are something else, on their own terms, and we will listen to them as music rather than as illustration of an invented quotation.

What is _real_ , and in actual contemporary sources, is **E. T. A. Hoffmann's 1810 review** of the Fifth Symphony in the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_. Hoffmann's review is one of the founding documents of Romantic music criticism. We will return to it in §9.4. The review's opening line — _"Beethoven's instrumental music opens to us the realm of the colossal and immeasurable"_ — was the first time a major critic argued that instrumental music could be the highest of the arts, the medium that spoke directly to the spirit without the mediation of words. Hoffmann's 1810 review is the act that gave the Fifth Symphony its cultural status as _the_ Beethoven symphony.

The Schindler line gave the symphony a story. The Hoffmann review gave the symphony a place in the history of how Europeans wrote about music. The story is doubtful; the place is documented. Listen to the Fifth with both in your ear.

#### What to notice

Three things across all four movements.

  1. **The four-note motif across the whole symphony.** The four-note cell (_short-short-short-LONG_) is the seed of the first movement, as Volume 1 explored. Listen for its _return_ in the third movement (where it becomes the scherzo's rhythmic foundation) and in the fourth movement (where it appears in transformed form in the trombone-and-piccolo finale). The motif is the structural unifier across all four movements.

  2. **The C-minor-to-C-major arc.** The first movement is in C minor; the second movement is in A♭ major (a related key); the third movement is in C minor; the fourth movement is in C major. The arc — minor-key struggle, lyrical interlude, more struggle, major-key triumph — is the _per aspera ad astra_ (through hardships to the stars) arc that Beethoven made canonical with this work. Listen for the _key change_ from third to fourth movement: the C-minor scherzo dies away into a quiet sustained chord, and then the C-major finale erupts into full orchestral fortissimo. This is the structural payoff of the whole symphony.

  3. **The famous transition from third to fourth movement.** The third movement does not end. It segues directly into the fourth via a long sustained chord and a quiet timpani roll, with the strings and woodwinds gradually adding voices, until the moment when the full orchestra erupts into the fourth movement's C-major fanfare. The transition takes about ninety seconds and is one of the most-discussed passages in the symphonic literature. Listen for the _attacca_ — no break between movements, the music flowing continuously from the C-minor scherzo's quiet darkness into the C-major finale's blazing brightness.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed Symphony No. 5 in C minor, Op. 67 between roughly 1804 and 1808, premiered it on 22 December 1808 at the Theater an der Wien in Vienna on a four-hour concert program that also included the Sixth Symphony, the Piano Concerto No. 4, the Choral Fantasy, and other works; the published score (1809) was dedicated jointly to Prince Lobkowicz and Count Razumovsky.
