### 9.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the symphony at least once (and the first movement twice, between Volume 1 and now). Second sustained listen, with timestamps into Kleiber 1974.

#### Movement I: Allegro con brio (you know this — 0:00–7:25 in Kleiber)

The first movement is the seven-minute workshop on the four-note cell. We covered it in detail in Volume 1, Chapter 3. The key landmarks: the four-note opening (0:00); the contrasting horn-fanfare-to-second-theme transition (0:43); the second theme (0:46); the exposition repeat that Kleiber takes; the development beginning around 4:32 after the repeat; the recapitulation at 5:48; the oboe cadenza moment at 6:00; the coda from 6:55 to 7:25.

On this re-listen, focus on the four-note rhythm in the lower strings under the lyrical second theme (0:46–1:14) — Volume 1 mentioned it but did not dwell. Beethoven is _not_ letting the cell leave. Even during the lyrical interlude, the cell is whispering in the cellos and basses. The first movement is genuinely a workshop on a single rhythmic idea; the cell appears in nearly every measure.

#### Movement II: Andante con moto (7:30–17:30 in Kleiber)

The second movement is the lyrical slow movement, in A♭ major, in variation form.

* **7:30–8:30.** The opening theme. Listen for the _singing_ quality: a long flowing melody in the cellos and violas, gentle, songful, in A♭ major. The theme is more lyrical than anything in the first movement.

* **8:30–9:30.** A second theme. Listen for the _contrast_ : a slightly more martial, slightly more emphatic phrase in the brass and woodwinds, in C major (the _home key_ of the symphony): a _premonition_ of the C-major triumph that the fourth movement will deliver. The C-major moment in the second movement is the foreshadowing of the symphonic arc.

* **9:30–11:30.** A variation on the first theme. The melody is now in the upper strings with more elaborate decoration. The texture builds.

* **11:30–13:30.** Another variation. The melody is now in the woodwinds; the strings provide a flowing accompaniment.

* **13:30–15:30.** A more dramatic variation. The orchestra builds; the brass enters in fuller force; the C-major-foreshadowing phrase returns and is extended.

* **15:30–17:00.** A return to the gentler first theme. The variation moves toward the close.

* **17:00–17:30.** The closing measures. The movement ends quietly in A♭ major, with the melody dying away into pizzicato strings.

About ten minutes total for the second movement in Kleiber. The variation form is the structural device. The C-major foreshadowing is the bridge to the symphonic arc.

#### Movement III: Allegro (Scherzo) (17:30–22:30 in Kleiber)

The third movement is the C-minor scherzo. It is not labeled _Scherzo_ in the score (Beethoven calls it simply _Allegro_) but in tempo and character it is a scherzo in everything but name.

* **17:30–18:00.** The opening. Listen for the _quiet_ opening: the cellos and basses play an ascending unison figure in C minor, soft, mysterious. The mood is very different from the first movement's pounding opening.

* **18:00–18:30.** The first phrase is taken up by the upper strings. The texture builds.

* **18:30–19:00.** **The horns enter with a recurrence of the four-note rhythm from the first movement.** This is the motif return: the _short-short-short-LONG_ rhythm reappears in the horns, transformed but recognizable. The motif unifies the first and third movements.

* **19:00–20:30.** The trio section (the contrasting middle section of the scherzo). Listen for the _fugal_ writing: the lower strings begin a fugue subject, the upper strings answer, the woodwinds enter. The trio is a brief exercise in contrapuntal texture.

* **20:30–21:30.** The scherzo returns. The horns' motif returns. The texture builds.

* **21:30–22:00.** **The famous transition begins.** The scherzo dies away into a long sustained note in the strings. The timpani begins a quiet roll. The dynamics drop to pianissimo.

* **22:00–22:30.** **The transition continues.** The strings sustain. The timpani rolls. The dynamics build very slowly. Listen for the _anticipation_ : the music is going somewhere, and somewhere very dramatic, but the buildup is slow and quiet. This is one of the longest and most-discussed transitions in the symphonic literature.

#### Movement IV: Allegro (Finale, attacca from III) (22:30–32:00 in Kleiber)

The fourth movement is the C-major triumph.

* **22:30.** **The fourth movement erupts.** The full orchestra strikes a C-major fanfare in fortissimo. Listen for the _brightness_ : after the C-minor darkness of the third movement, the C-major arrival is sonically overwhelming. Beethoven adds three new instruments at this exact moment that have not appeared in the first three movements: the **trombones** (three of them), the **piccolo** , and the **contrabassoon**. These are the first appearances of these instruments in Beethoven's symphonic writing. The orchestra has been _enlarged_ for the finale.

* **22:30–23:30.** The first theme. Listen for the _triumphant character_ : an ascending arpeggio of C major, full orchestral force, with the trombones giving the texture a depth not heard before in the symphony.

* **23:30–25:00.** Development of the first theme. The texture is dense; the brass is prominent; the rhythmic energy is high.

* **25:00–26:00.** A second theme arrives. The horns and woodwinds carry a lyrical melody in G major (the dominant). The first-movement's four-note rhythm appears embedded in the texture (listen for it in the lower strings).

* **26:00–28:00.** Continued development of the multiple themes. The exposition reaches its close around 28:00.

* **28:00–30:00.** **The return of the third-movement scherzo material.** Beethoven does something unusual here: in the middle of the fourth movement, after the development has been underway, he _reintroduces material from the third movement_ : the quiet scherzo passage briefly returns, before being banished by the C-major triumph again. Listen for the _brief shadow_ of the third movement in the middle of the fourth: it is short (about thirty seconds) but it is and structurally important. The shadow makes the C-major triumph's return all the more decisive.

* **30:00–31:30.** The recapitulation and approach to the coda. The first-movement four-note rhythm returns in the trombones. The C-major texture is sustained.

* **31:30–32:00.** **The coda: the longest closing section in any Beethoven symphony up to that date.** Beethoven sustains the C-major triumph for what feels like an excessive duration: dozens of bars of repeated C-major chords, the entire orchestra hammering the home key, the trombones blazing, the piccolo at the top. The coda has been compared (variously, by Berlioz onward) to _Beethoven not wanting to let go of C major after the long C-minor struggle_ : the dramatic logic requires the triumph to be sustained at length, not curtailed. The closing measures are a final series of orchestral fortissimo chords on C major, ending the symphony in absolute brightness.

About thirty-two minutes total for the symphony in Kleiber. The four-note motif appears across all four movements. The C-minor-to-C-major arc is the structural backbone. The third-to-fourth movement attacca transition and the trombone-piccolo-contrabassoon orchestration shift at 22:30 are the structural-and-orchestrational signatures of the work.

### 9.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The four-note motif as cross-movement unifier

Volume 1 introduced the four-note motif as the seed of the first movement. The Volume 1 chapter focused on motivic development within one movement. The fuller picture, available now with all four movements in hand, is that the motif unifies the whole symphony.

The motif appears in transformed form across the four movements:

* **First movement.** The motif is the seed of every measure, in its original _short-short-short-LONG_ rhythm in C minor, with the descending third (G-G-G-E♭).
* **Second movement.** The motif's rhythm appears embedded in the orchestration of the variation theme (the dotted-rhythm figures in the brass), though it is harder to spot here. The C-major foreshadowing phrase (9:00) uses the dotted rhythm extensively.
* **Third movement.** The motif appears in the horns at 18:30 in transformed harmonic context (now in C minor, transformed pitch). The rhythm is unmistakable.
* **Fourth movement.** The motif appears in the lower strings under the second theme (around 25:00) and in the trombones during the coda (around 31:00). The rhythm is the _thread_ that binds the four movements together.

This cross-movement unification is one of Beethoven's compositional innovations. Symphony 3 (the _Eroica_) had used a recurring theme across the second-movement Funeral March and the fourth-movement variations, but the _Eroica_ 's connections were thematic (the _Prometheus_ theme returning). The Fifth Symphony's connections are _motivic_ : the four-note rhythm, the smallest possible unit, threaded across all four movements. This is the advance of cyclic-symphonic technique that subsequent 19th-century composers (Berlioz, Schumann, Liszt, Brahms, Mahler) would develop further. The _idée fixe_ of Berlioz's _Symphonie fantastique_ (1830) is the descendant of Beethoven's four-note thread.

#### The per aspera ad astra arc

The Fifth Symphony's C-minor-to-C-major arc is the narrative architecture that Beethoven made canonical with this work. The Latin phrase _per aspera ad astra_ ("through hardships to the stars") was not used by Beethoven himself (it has classical origins, used by various Latin authors), but the _arc the phrase describes_ is the compositional design of the Fifth Symphony.

The arc:

  1. **Struggle (movement 1, C minor).** The work opens in C minor with the four-note workshop. The struggle is intense, restless, unresolved at the close of the first movement.
  2. **Interlude / lyrical center (movement 2, A♭ major, with C-major foreshadowing).** The slow movement provides a lyrical respite from the struggle, with the C-major foreshadowing (at 9:00) hinting at the eventual resolution.
  3. **Renewed struggle (movement 3, C minor, with thinning of texture).** The scherzo brings back the C-minor temperament, with the four-note motif now in the horns. The mood is darker than the first movement's _workshop_ — more _spectral_ , more _anxious_.
  4. **Triumph (movement 4, C major).** The C-major finale, prepared by the long attacca transition, delivers the major-key resolution. The shadow of the third movement (at 28:00) briefly returns before being banished.

The arc is one of the most-influential narrative structures in 19th-century music. Brahms's First Symphony (1876) follows the same arc explicitly. Mahler's Second Symphony (1894) does. Tchaikovsky's Fifth Symphony (1888) does. Sibelius's Fifth (1915) does. The Beethoven Fifth is the _prototype_ of the symphonic narrative that the 19th and early 20th centuries used repeatedly.

The _per aspera ad astra_ arc is not the _only_ model Beethoven worked with. The _Appassionata_ (Chapter 7) ends in F minor, not F major — the struggle without the triumph. The _Pathétique_ (Chapter 2) ends in C minor. Op. 1 No. 3 (Chapter 3) ends in C minor. Beethoven _chose_ whether to end in the major mode based on the expressive content of the specific work. The Fifth Symphony is the canonical major-mode-triumph case. The other works are the refusals of the same arc.

#### The orchestration shift in movement four

The fourth movement adds three instruments that did not appear in the first three movements: trombones (three), piccolo, and contrabassoon. These instruments enter at 22:30, at the moment of the C-major eruption.

The trombones are particularly important. In 1808 Vienna, trombones were not standard in the symphonic orchestra. They had been used in opera (Mozart's _Don Giovanni_ uses three trombones in the Commendatore scene) and in church music (the trombone choir was a sacred-music tradition), but they were rarely brought into symphonic writing. Beethoven uses them in the Fifth's finale (and again in the Sixth's Storm movement, in the Ninth's finale, and in some other late works). The decision to bring trombones into the symphony is one of the orchestrational innovations of the Fifth.

The piccolo similarly was not standard in the symphony; it was a military-band and opera instrument. The contrabassoon (the bass extension of the bassoon family) was unusual. Beethoven's choice to add all three for the finale is the expansion of the symphonic orchestra. The new instruments give the finale a sonic _brightness_ (piccolo on top), a sonic _depth_ (contrabassoon at the bottom), and a sonic _weight_ (trombones in the middle) that the first three movements do not have. The orchestration mirrors the dramatic arc: the finale is _bigger_ sounding than the previous movements because it is the moment of triumph.

#### The attacca transition

The transition from third to fourth movement is one of the most-discussed passages in the symphonic literature.

The third movement's scherzo dies away to pianissimo. The strings hold a sustained C note. The timpani begins a quiet roll on C. The dynamics stay at pianissimo. Over the course of about ninety seconds the orchestra slowly adds voices, first violins, then violas, then more, while the dynamics very gradually build. The tension accumulates. Then, at 22:30, the full orchestra explodes into the C-major fanfare with the trombones, piccolo, and contrabassoon entering for the first time.

The transition is one of Beethoven's most-celebrated compositional moments. Hector Berlioz, in his 1862 _À travers chants_ , called the passage _the moment of revelation_. Donald Tovey, in _Essays in Musical Analysis_ vol. 1, devotes several paragraphs to it. The passage is the model of the _long buildup to climax_ signature (Thread B; introduced in Chapter 6 for the _Eroica_ 's development). Here the signature operates at the structural level of _between movements_ , not within a movement.

The passage's effect depends on the conductor's pacing. Furtwängler (1947 broadcast) draws out the buildup to seem to last forever; the eruption is the more shattering. Karajan (1963) takes the buildup at a brisker pace. Kleiber (1974) is somewhere between. Currentzis (2018) takes the buildup unusually fast and the eruption is immediate. Each pacing produces a different dramatic effect.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the Fifth Symphony is a _short_ work — _just_ the four-note opening expanded over a brisk thirty-minute span. It is short by 19th-century symphony standards (it is shorter than the _Eroica_ , half the length of Mahler's symphonies), but it is _unusually substantial_ compared to Haydn's and Mozart's symphonies (which typically run 25 minutes or less). The Fifth Symphony's thirty-two minutes establish a _new standard_ for what a major symphony should weigh. The Sixth (also thirty-two minutes) confirms it; the Seventh (forty minutes) extends it; the Ninth (sixty-five minutes) consolidates it. After Beethoven, the symphony as a genre would routinely exceed thirty minutes; before Beethoven, it rarely did.

The misconception matters because if you treat the Fifth as a _brief, punchy_ work, you miss the slow build of its dramatic argument. The four-movement structure with the long second movement, the attacca transition, and the extended coda is not a _brief_ statement; it is a sustained dramatic argument across thirty-two minutes. The compression comes from the _unity of material_ (the four-note motif threaded across), not from brevity.

### 9.4 The Stories

#### The December 22, 1808 four-hour concert

The Fifth Symphony's public premiere was at the Theater an der Wien on the evening of 22 December 1808, on a benefit concert that Beethoven himself had organized and was conducting (and performing in) himself. The program is in the _Wiener Zeitung_ announcement of 17 December 1808 and in subsequent reviews. The program ran approximately four hours and included:

* **Symphony No. 6 in F major ("Pastoral")** : world premiere
* **Aria "Ah! perfido," Op. 65** : concert aria, performed by soprano Josephine Killitschgy (substituting for the originally engaged soprano, who had withdrawn)
* **Gloria from the Mass in C** : world premiere of an excerpt from Op. 86
* **Piano Concerto No. 4 in G major** : world premiere of the public performance, with Beethoven as soloist
* **Intermission**
* **Symphony No. 5 in C minor** : world premiere
* **Sanctus from the Mass in C** : another excerpt
* **Improvised piano fantasy by Beethoven**
* **Choral Fantasy, Op. 80** : world premiere; composed in haste in the previous weeks for this concert, with the main theme that Beethoven would later transform into the Ode to Joy of Symphony 9

The concert was, by accounts, a difficult experience. The theater was unheated and the December cold was severe. The orchestra had not been adequately rehearsed; the complaints from contemporary observers include the lack of a single dress rehearsal of the full program. The _Choral Fantasy_ premiere broke down mid-performance and had to be restarted — the documented anecdote from Ignaz von Seyfried (who was at the concert) describes Beethoven himself stopping the performance, looking at the orchestra, and starting the work again from the beginning. The audience was small (the holiday season; the four-hour length; the cold hall).

The Fifth Symphony, in this concert, was first heard at the end of an exhausting program in a freezing hall by a small audience after the Sixth had already been performed. By most contemporary accounts, the work landed without immediate impact. Documented reviews of the concert mention the Fifth in passing among the various other works on the program.

The Fifth's canonization came later. By 1810, two years after the premiere, E. T. A. Hoffmann had reviewed the published score and declared the work one of the supreme statements of music. By 1820 the symphony was being performed regularly across Europe. By 1830 it was canonical. By 1900 it was the most-performed orchestral work in the standard repertoire, and the four-note opening was already on its way to becoming a global cultural icon.

The story of the premiere is a useful reminder. Even the most-canonical works can have inauspicious first performances. The work itself does its slow work over time; the premiere is just one event in the work's long life.

#### Hoffmann's 1810 review

E. T. A. Hoffmann (1776–1822) — best known today as the author of stories that Offenbach turned into _Tales of Hoffmann_ and Tchaikovsky into _The Nutcracker_ — was, in 1810, the music critic for the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_. His review of Beethoven's Fifth Symphony, published in the issue of 4–11 July 1810, is one of the founding documents of Romantic music criticism.

The review's central claim: instrumental music is the highest of the arts because it speaks directly to the listener's spirit without the mediation of words or images. Hoffmann had been arguing this thesis in earlier essays; the Beethoven Fifth was, for him, the _clearest possible demonstration_ of the thesis. The four-note motif's relentless development across thirty-two minutes, with the per aspera ad astra arc culminating in C-major triumph, was the case study for what instrumental music could do that no other art could.

Hoffmann's specific descriptions of the Fifth: _"the realm of the colossal and immeasurable opens to us"_ ; _"glowing rays shoot through the deep night of this realm, and we become aware of gigantic shadows surging back and forth"_ ; _"the listener experiences the abyss of the infinite."_ The language is the founding-Romantic vocabulary — _colossal_ , _immeasurable_ , _infinite_ , _abyss_ , _night_ , _gigantic shadows_. Music, for Hoffmann, became the medium of access to the _transcendent_.

The review had two consequences:

  1. **It re-canonized Beethoven.** Hoffmann's claim that the Fifth was the supreme work of instrumental music gave the work the cultural status it had not yet earned through its premiere. Reading Hoffmann was the start of how 19th-century Europeans were _taught to hear_ the Fifth.

  2. **It re-defined music criticism.** Before Hoffmann, music criticism was primarily descriptive (what happened, what the form was, what the technique was). After Hoffmann, music criticism became _interpretive_ in the Romantic sense — describing what the music _meant_ , what it _felt like_ , what _spiritual content_ it carried. The two centuries of music criticism that followed (through Liszt, Schumann, Wagner, Brahms's Eduard Hanslick, into the 20th century) all develop the Hoffmannian frame. We are still living with it.

The Hoffmann review is the documented act that made the Fifth Symphony into what it became. The Schindler "fate knocks" line gave the symphony a story; the Hoffmann review gave the symphony its meaning. The two are documented at different levels of reliability: Hoffmann is verifiable, Schindler is doubtful.

#### The 1813 Vienna performance and the canonical reception

The Fifth Symphony's reception consolidated in the years immediately after Hoffmann's review. Documented performances in Leipzig, Berlin, Frankfurt, Paris, and London between 1810 and 1820 established the work in the European concert circuit. By the late 1820s, the Beethoven _cult_ was beginning to form — the work was being performed with explicit invocations of Beethoven's now-late genius (Beethoven died in 1827). By the 1840s, the Fifth was being programmed on major occasions (anniversary concerts, memorial concerts). By the 1860s, the four-note opening was already entering popular consciousness as a quotation-able cultural reference.

The 20th-century reception added new dimensions. The Allies' use of the _V_ (for Victory) Morse code signal — _short-short-short-LONG_ , the same rhythm as the Fifth's opening — during World War II turned the symphony into an Allied wartime symbol. The BBC broadcast the Fifth Symphony's opening as the wartime call sign of the Free French Forces; the symbolism was widely recognized. After the war, the Fifth was, if anything, more famous than before. We will return to this thread in Chapter 24 (cultural reception).

#### Three readings of the Fifth

**Wilhelm Furtwängler / Vienna Philharmonic, 1947 broadcast (various labels).** Furtwängler's wartime and immediate postwar Fifth Symphony broadcasts are among the most-loved documents of the work. The 1947 Vienna broadcast (recorded by ORF, released variously by Deutsche Grammophon, Tahra, and other labels) is one of his slower, more weighted readings. The first movement runs about eight minutes; the attacca transition between movements three and four is extended; the C-major triumph in the finale is broad and patient. Furtwängler's reading is the model _metaphysical-Romantic_ approach — the work as cosmic drama, played with maximum gravitas.

**Herbert von Karajan / Berlin Philharmonic, 1963 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Karajan recorded the Beethoven symphonies four times across his career (1962–63, 1970s, 1980s, with the late readings being more variable). The 1962–63 cycle (his first complete) is the most-praised. The Fifth in this cycle is the model _polished-monumental_ reading — slick, beautifully proportioned, brass-forward, with the Berlin Philharmonic of the early 1960s at its peak. Karajan does not take the first-movement exposition repeat (which is part of why his recording is shorter). The reading is on the celebratory side: the C-major triumph is _unambiguous_.

**Carlos Kleiber / Vienna Philharmonic, 1974 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Kleiber's Fifth is the model _electric_ reading. Fast tempi, sharp articulation, exposition repeat included, the attacca taken with crackling urgency. The recording (paired with Symphony 7 on the original LP) is one of the most-loved Beethoven Fifth documents of the late 20th century. We have been using it as the primary recording throughout the chapter.

**Teodor Currentzis / MusicAeterna, 2018 (Sony Classical).** Currentzis's Fifth is the model _21st-century iconoclastic_ reading. Period-informed but on modern instruments, very fast tempi, dry orchestral sound, percussive timpani. The exposition repeat is taken. The attacca is taken at unusual speed. The C-major triumph is sharp rather than monumental. Some listeners find the reading thrilling; some find it manneristic. It is unambiguously different from the standard 20th-century approach. (Note: this is the chapter's one recording under the 10-year safeguard window — included on the strength of major reviews in Gramophone, BBC Music Magazine, and Fanfare.)

The four readings cover the central interpretive options for the Fifth. Furtwängler the metaphysical-romantic; Karajan the polished-monumental; Kleiber the electric; Currentzis the iconoclastic. We will return to these conductor lineages in Chapter 23 (recording history).

#### Cross-piece links

* Volume 1 Chapter 3 covered the Fifth's first movement in survey form. This chapter is the deeper pass on the same work.
* The four-note motif as cross-movement unifier connects to the **Ninth Symphony's recurring fourth-movement themes** (Chapter 18). The Ninth's finale brings back themes from the first three movements before settling on the Ode to Joy.
* The per aspera ad astra arc connects to the **Ninth Symphony's larger arc** (Chapter 18; D minor → D major triumph) and to many 19th-century descendants. We will return to the arc in Chapter 21 (signatures).
* The attacca transition device connects to the **Op. 27 No. 1 sonata's between-movement segues** (mentioned briefly in Chapter 4) and to **Op. 110's late between-movement transitions** (Chapter 16). The device is a Beethoven habit that varies by genre.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion to walk through the documented Schindler "fate knocks" controversy — what Schindler claimed, why modern scholarship has dismantled the claim, and how to listen to the four-note opening without the apocryphal story. The conversation should land on the Lockwood/Solomon position: the line is doubtful and should be flagged when used._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the attacca transition (between movements 3 and 4) felt most_ dramatic _to you on the listen, and ask her to compare Furtwängler 1947, Kleiber 1974, and Currentzis 2018 in pacing the buildup. Each conductor's choice has consequences._
* _Ask the companion about E. T. A. Hoffmann's 1810 review — what the actual text says, what claims it makes about instrumental music, and why it is the founding document of Romantic music criticism. The conversation can run from Hoffmann into the 19th-century critical tradition (Schumann, Liszt, Wagner, Hanslick)._
* _Bring up the trombones-and-piccolo-and-contrabassoon arrival at the start of movement 4 with the companion — why Beethoven added these instruments specifically for the finale, and what the orchestrational shift means structurally. The orchestral expansion is one of Beethoven's signatures._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The four-note rhythm across all four movements — try to spot it in the lower strings under the second movement's variation theme (around 11:30) and in the horns of the third movement (at 18:30).
* The C-major foreshadowing in movement 2 (around 9:00) — the moment the symphony briefly visits the home-key-of-triumph in the middle of the lyrical slow movement.
* The brief return of the third-movement scherzo material in the middle of the fourth movement (around 28:00) — the shadow of darkness that the finale banishes.
* The trombones and piccolo entering at 22:30 — listen specifically for the _change of sound_ at the moment of the C-major eruption.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Lewis Lockwood's chapter on Symphony 5 in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (W. W. Norton, 2003), chapter 11 "Heroic Symphonies (continued)."** Lockwood treats the symphony in the compositional and reception context, with careful attention to both the December 22, 1808 premiere and the Hoffmann 1810 review. Pair with Donald Tovey's essay on the Fifth in _Essays in Musical Analysis_ , vol. 1 (1935) — Tovey is older but his structural analysis is still useful, particularly on the attacca transition and the cross-movement motif.

### Chapter Summary

Symphony No. 5 in C minor, Op. 67 (composed 1804–1808, premiered 22 December 1808 at the Theater an der Wien) is the hinge work of Beethoven's heroic middle period. The four movements together — Allegro con brio (the four-note workshop, Volume 1 Chapter 3 covered), Andante con moto (lyrical variation form in A♭ major with C-major foreshadowing), Allegro (the spectral C-minor scherzo with horn-motif return), and Allegro (the C-major finale with trombones, piccolo, and contrabassoon entering for the first time) — form the per aspera ad astra arc that Beethoven made canonical with this work.

You met the documented Schindler "fate knocks at the door" controversy — the line is by general scholarly agreement a Schindler embroidery, not Beethoven's words. You met E. T. A. Hoffmann's 1810 review as the founding document of Romantic music criticism: the work that re-canonized Beethoven and re-defined music criticism in interpretive-Romantic terms.

You learned that the _attacca_ transition from movement 3 to movement 4 — the long quiet buildup followed by the full-orchestra C-major eruption with new instruments — is one of the most-discussed passages in the symphonic literature. Furtwängler 1947, Kleiber 1974, and Currentzis 2018 give different readings of the transition's pacing.

You met the **C-minor character** signature (Thread B return; first met in Chapter 3) at its symphonic apex. The Fifth is the major-mode-triumph case in Beethoven's catalogue. We will revisit the signature in Chapter 16 (Op. 111, the late counterpart).

You met the **long buildup-to-climax** signature (Thread B; first met in Chapter 6 for the _Eroica_ 's development) operating at the structural level of the attacca transition between movements.

You learned that the December 22, 1808 four-hour concert was an inauspicious premiere — under-rehearsed, in a freezing hall, with the _Choral Fantasy_ breaking down mid-performance — and that the work's canonization came later, primarily through the Hoffmann review of 1810.

You met four readings of the Fifth — Furtwängler 1947 (metaphysical-Romantic), Karajan 1963 (polished-monumental), Kleiber 1974 (electric), Currentzis 2018 (iconoclastic) — and the four-point work-vs-interpretation polygon they form.

The next chapter takes you to the Symphony 6 "Pastoral" — premiered on the same December 22, 1808 night as the Fifth, in many ways the Fifth's opposite. Where the Fifth is dramatic struggle and triumph, the Sixth is pastoral nature observation. The two symphonies, born together, are in conversation across the 1808 concert program.

* * *

---

## Chapter 10: Symphony No. 6 "Pastoral"

Premiered on the same December 22, 1808 four-hour Vienna concert as the Fifth Symphony, the Sixth Symphony is in nearly every respect the Fifth's opposite.

The Fifth is C minor; the Sixth is F major. The Fifth is dramatic struggle; the Sixth is pastoral observation. The Fifth's movements unfold a per aspera ad astra arc; the Sixth's movements depict five scenes from a country walk. The Fifth has four movements; the Sixth has five. The Fifth opens with a hammered four-note motif; the Sixth opens with a calm, song-like phrase in the strings. The Fifth's listener is asked to follow a dramatic argument; the Sixth's listener is asked to _be in the place_ the music depicts.

The two symphonies were composed in overlapping time periods (1804–1808 for both, with the Sixth more concentrated in 1808 itself), premiered on the same night by Beethoven himself conducting, and published within months of each other. They are twin works in Beethoven's middle period — twin not in similarity but in the sense of being conceived as a contrasting pair. The 1808 listener heard them in the same evening: first the Sixth (with its programmatic country-walk through five movements), then later in the evening the Fifth (with its dramatic argument).

The chapter's anchor moments are the opening of the first movement, the fourth-movement Thunderstorm, and the fifth-movement Shepherd's Song that closes the work.

### 10.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — "more an expression of feeling than painting"

Beethoven inscribed the title page of the Sixth Symphony with both a German title (_Pastoral-Sinfonie_) and a French title (_Sinfonie pastorale_), and gave each of the five movements a programmatic subtitle in German. The subtitles were Beethoven's own (unlike the _Moonlight_ and _Appassionata_ nicknames, which were later inventions):

  1. _Erwachen heiterer Empfindungen bei der Ankunft auf dem Lande_ — "Awakening of cheerful feelings on arrival in the countryside"
  2. _Szene am Bach_ — "Scene by the brook"
  3. _Lustiges Zusammensein der Landleute_ — "Merry gathering of country folk"
  4. _Gewitter, Sturm_ — "Thunderstorm"
  5. _Hirtengesang. Frohe und dankbare Gefühle nach dem Sturm_ — "Shepherd's song. Glad and grateful feelings after the storm"

The programmatic titles caused some unease for Beethoven about how the work would be received. On the autograph manuscript (and in subsequent letters to publishers) he added a clarifying inscription: _"Mehr Ausdruck der Empfindung als Mahlerey"_ — "More an expression of feeling than painting." The line is in multiple sources (the manuscript inscription itself; Beethoven's correspondence with Breitkopf & Härtel about the publication of the score, 1809). The phrase appears, in modified form, on the title page of the first edition.

What was Beethoven worried about? Program music, music that depicts specific external events or scenes, had a contested status in the early 19th century. The defenders argued that music could legitimately illustrate the world (Vivaldi's _Four Seasons_ of 1725 had been the canonical case). The skeptics argued that music was an _autonomous art_ that should refer primarily to itself; programmatic content was a betrayal of music's true nature. Beethoven's contemporaries included both positions: Hoffmann (whose 1810 Fifth Symphony review we discussed in Chapter 9) was at the autonomous-art end; many earlier 18th-century critics had been at the program-music-is-fine end.

By calling the work _more expression of feeling than painting_ , Beethoven was positioning the Sixth between the two positions. He was saying: _yes, the work has programmatic subtitles, but the music is not literally painting birds or thunderstorms; it is expressing the feeling of being in the country, encountering the brook, dancing with the country folk, weathering the storm, hearing the shepherd's song of gratitude_. The distinction was important. Beethoven did not want the Sixth to be heard as _sound-illustration_ of specific external events. He wanted it to be heard as _the inner experience of being in a particular place_.

The distinction is also slightly slippery. The fourth-movement Thunderstorm absolutely _paints_ a thunderstorm — there is no other way to hear the timpani-and-strings storm music, the rising winds, the lightning-flash dissonances, the eventual subsidence. The "feeling versus painting" distinction breaks down at the literal moment of the storm music. Beethoven's inscription was, in part, an _advance defense_ of a work that he knew would be heard as descriptive — saying, in effect, _please hear the descriptive moments as expressions of inner feeling, not as cheap musical photography_.

The whole question of _what Beethoven was up to in the Sixth_ has been debated ever since. Most modern scholars (Lockwood, Solomon, Swafford) treat the work as both descriptive _and_ expressive — depicting the country in literal terms in certain passages (the brook's flowing strings; the bird calls in the second movement; the village dance; the thunderstorm) while sustaining the _feeling_ of pastoral life as the overall expressive content. The work is not a contradiction but a layered statement.

#### What to notice

Three things across the five movements.

  1. **The opening pastoral phrase.** The first movement opens with a calm, song-like phrase in the strings — a four-bar melody in F major over a sustained dominant pedal. Listen for the _unhurriedness_ : the music does not announce, does not declare; it begins as if you have just walked into a landscape and the music has always been there. The opening is the entrance into the pastoral world.

  2. **The bird calls in movement 2.** The second movement (Scene by the brook) is one of the longest slow movements Beethoven wrote, in B♭ major. Near the end of the movement (around 11:00 in most recordings), Beethoven includes explicit bird calls in the woodwinds — the nightingale (flute), the quail (oboe), and the cuckoo (clarinet). The bird calls are labeled in the score. Listen for the _three named birds_ in the final pages of the movement; they are explicit and unambiguous.

  3. **The Thunderstorm and the Shepherd's Song.** The fourth movement (Thunderstorm) and the fifth movement (Shepherd's Song) run continuously — _attacca_ , no break between them. The Thunderstorm depicts the storm in clear musical terms (rising winds in the strings, timpani thunder, piccolo lightning); when the storm subsides, the Shepherd's Song begins in F major and continues until the symphony's quiet close. Listen for the _transition_ : the storm ends, the strings shift into the Shepherd's Song melody, and the gratitude after the storm is the closing emotional register.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed Symphony No. 6 in F major, Op. 68 in 1807–1808 and premiered it on 22 December 1808 at the Theater an der Wien on the same four-hour concert that included the premiere of the Fifth Symphony; the work was published in 1809, dedicated jointly to Prince Lobkowicz and Count Razumovsky (the same dedicatees as the Fifth).

### 10.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the symphony once. Second sustained listen, with timestamps into Böhm 1971.

#### Movement I: Allegro ma non troppo — "Awakening of cheerful feelings on arrival in the countryside" (0:00–11:30 in Böhm)

* **0:00–0:30.** The opening phrase. Strings playing a calm F-major melody over a sustained dominant pedal. Listen for the _unhurried_ character: Allegro ma non troppo (fast but not too fast), with the _non troppo_ very much present. Böhm takes the opening at a relaxed, ample pace.

* **0:30–2:00.** The first theme is extended. The melody passes between strings and woodwinds; the texture remains gentle; the harmonic motion is slow.

* **2:00–4:00.** The second theme arrives in C major. A more lyrical phrase in the upper strings. Listen for the _singing_ quality and the _open_ harmonic motion.

* **4:00–6:00.** Development of the themes. Beethoven uses _minimal_ development by Eroica or Fifth standards: the themes are simply extended, varied, set in new keys, without the dramatic working-through of the more heroic works. The development is _pastoral_ in the sense that it sustains the mood rather than challenging it.

* **6:00–9:00.** The exposition repeats (which Böhm takes) or the recapitulation begins (which it does after the repeat). The first theme returns in the home key.

* **9:00–11:30.** The closing material and coda. The movement ends quietly in F major, with the strings sustaining a final chord.

About eleven and a half minutes in Böhm.

#### Movement II: Andante molto moto — "Scene by the brook" (11:30–24:00 in Böhm)

* **11:30–13:00.** The opening. Listen for the _flowing strings_ : lower strings playing a _rocking_ sixteenth-note figure that depicts the running water of the brook. The figure continues across nearly the entire movement, sustaining the _sound of the brook_ as a textural underlay.

* **13:00–15:00.** The first melodic theme arrives in the upper strings. The brook continues underneath. The melody is lyrical, songful, unhurried.

* **15:00–18:00.** Development. The melody is taken through related keys; the brook texture sustains; the movement maintains its meditative character.

* **18:00–22:00.** Continued development and the approach to the coda. Beethoven introduces small woodwind figures that anticipate the bird calls.

* **22:00–23:00.** **The bird calls.** In the closing measures of the movement, Beethoven labels three birds explicitly in the score:

* The **nightingale** is the _flute_ with a sustained trill and warbling figure.

* The **quail** is the _oboe_ with a short call-figure (the quail rhythm in the score).

* The **cuckoo** is the _clarinet_ with a descending two-note figure (down a third, the cuckoo motif of European folk music).

The three bird calls are layered together in the woodwinds while the strings sustain a slow F-major chord underneath. Listen for the _three distinct woodwind voices_ ; each is the portrait of a specific bird.

* **23:00–24:00.** The closing measures. The brook flows once more; the movement ends quietly.

About twelve and a half minutes in Böhm.

#### Movement III: Allegro — "Merry gathering of country folk" (24:00–29:30 in Böhm)

* **24:00–25:00.** The opening of the scherzo. Listen for the _country-dance_ character: a rustic phrase in the strings, with a particular emphasis on heavy downbeats (the _dance pulse_ of a village dance).

* **25:00–27:00.** A trio section featuring solo oboe with a slightly _clumsy_ country-dance phrase (the "village oboist who can't quite count" passage; Beethoven has the oboe coming in slightly off the beat, depicting an amateur village musician).

* **27:00–28:30.** A second trio section, faster, more energetic. The country-folk gathering becomes more boisterous.

* **28:30–29:30.** The scherzo returns briefly, then segues directly into the fourth movement.

About five and a half minutes in Böhm. Notice that the third movement _does not end_ ; it segues attacca into the fourth.

#### Movement IV: Allegro — "Thunderstorm" (29:30–33:00 in Böhm)

* **29:30–30:00.** **The storm begins.** Listen for the _sudden_ change of texture from the country-dance ending: the strings begin a rapid agitated tremolo figure, the woodwinds enter with rising scale figures depicting the wind picking up, the timpani begins a soft growl.

* **30:00–31:30.** The storm intensifies. The full orchestra joins; the timpani thunder becomes loud; the strings continue their agitated tremolo; the piccolo enters with high lightning-flash figures (the piccolo's only appearance in the symphony is in the Thunderstorm, just as it appeared in the Fifth's finale).

* **31:30–32:30.** The storm reaches its peak. Beethoven uses extreme dynamics, sharp dissonances, and the full orchestra in fortissimo.

* **32:30–33:00.** **The storm subsides.** The dynamics drop; the timpani softens; the wind dies; the lightning ceases. The strings slow into a sustained chord. The transition into the Shepherd's Song begins.

About three and a half minutes in Böhm: the shortest movement of the symphony.

#### Movement V: Allegretto — "Shepherd's Song. Glad and grateful feelings after the storm" (33:00–43:00 in Böhm)

* **33:00–33:30.** **The Shepherd's Song begins.** The strings sustain a soft F-major chord. A clarinet enters with a _yodel-like_ call: the shepherd's call after the storm: answered by a horn. The "yodel-and-answer" exchange is the opening gesture of the finale.

* **33:30–35:30.** The first theme of the Shepherd's Song. A long, songful melody in F major in the strings, with the woodwinds providing countermelodies. Listen for the _gratitude_ in the music: the relief after the storm, the calm returning, the sun coming out.

* **35:30–38:00.** Development of the Shepherd's Song. The melody passes between sections; the harmonic motion is gentle.

* **38:00–41:00.** A more sustained passage with the full orchestra. The Shepherd's Song builds to its emotional climax around 39:00: a moment of full-orchestra F-major celebration that, like the Fifth's finale, sustains the emotional resolution at length.

* **41:00–43:00.** The coda. The Shepherd's Song quiets; the strings sustain; the symphony closes on a series of softening F-major chords. Böhm's final chord fades into silence.

About ten minutes in Böhm. The Shepherd's Song is the longest movement of the symphony.

Whole symphony in Böhm: about forty-three minutes. The longest of Beethoven's first six symphonies. The five-movement structure with the explicit programmatic titles is the innovation; the _attacca_ connection of the third-fourth-fifth movements is the structural device.

### 10.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The five-movement structure

Most Classical symphonies have four movements. The Beethoven symphonies (Nos. 1, 2, 3, 4, 5, 7, 8) all follow the four-movement convention. The Sixth is the exception — five movements, with the third, fourth, and fifth all connected by attacca transitions.

Why five movements? Beethoven's reason was the programmatic structure: the country walk has _five scenes_ (arrival in the countryside; scene by the brook; merry gathering of country folk; thunderstorm; shepherd's song after the storm), and each scene gets a movement. The structural device is _narrative_ rather than purely musical.

The result is a symphony that is _longer_ than the four-movement symphonies (forty-three minutes versus the Fifth's thirty-two) and that has a different dramatic shape. The Fifth is _vertical_ — struggle compressed and resolved within four movements. The Sixth is _horizontal_ — a sustained narrative spread across five movements, with the dramatic peak at the Thunderstorm and the closing relaxation at the Shepherd's Song.

The five-movement structure is not unique to the Sixth in the symphonic literature. Berlioz's _Symphonie fantastique_ (1830) has five movements (Reveries, Passions, A Ball, Scene in the Country, March to the Scaffold — Dream of a Witches' Sabbath). The Berlioz five-movement structure is the descendant of Beethoven's. Subsequent five-movement symphonies (Mahler's Fifth, Mahler's Seventh, Shostakovich's Eighth) all extend the tradition. The Sixth is the prototype.

#### The "more expression of feeling than painting" question

Beethoven's inscription, _more expression of feeling than painting_ , has been debated for two centuries. The question is whether the Sixth is, despite Beethoven's disclaimer, _fundamentally_ a piece of _Tonmalerei_ (tone-painting; sound-illustration of external events).

Modern scholars generally treat the work as _both_ — descriptive in certain passages (the brook, the bird calls, the storm) and expressive in the overall arc (the _feeling_ of pastoral life, the gratitude after the storm). The descriptive passages are unambiguous: the running-water figure in movement 2 is _meant_ to depict the brook; the bird calls in movement 2 are labeled with their species names; the Thunderstorm depicts a thunderstorm. The expressive arc is the _overall_ meaning: the symphony as a whole expresses the _experience_ of being in the country, which includes specific scenes but is more than the sum of them.

The two positions are not in conflict if you accept that music can simultaneously _describe_ and _express_. The descriptive content is the _material_ ; the expressive arc is the _form_. The Sixth uses descriptive material (bird calls, storm) within an expressive form (the pastoral feeling). Beethoven's inscription was a guide to how to hear the proportion of the two.

#### Definition: Program music versus absolute music

The distinction between _program music_ (music with explicit external references) and _absolute music_ (music referring primarily to itself) was articulated by 19th-century critics, with Eduard Hanslick's _On the Beautiful in Music_ (1854) being the classical defense of the absolute-music position. The distinction is partly anachronistic when applied to early 19th-century music — neither position had been fully theorized in 1808 — but the rough categories are useful.

The Sixth is generally classified as _program music_ in modern terms. The Fifth is generally classified as _absolute music_ (despite the cultural overlay of "fate knocks at the door"). The Sixth has explicit programmatic titles for each movement; the Fifth does not. The Sixth depicts external scenes; the Fifth depicts an inner dramatic argument.

Beethoven himself wrote both kinds. The _Eroica_ is _somewhat_ programmatic (the Napoleon dedication, the Funeral March's title) but mostly absolute. The _Pastoral_ is explicitly programmatic. _Wellington's Victory_ Op. 91 (1813) is overtly programmatic (it depicts an actual military battle and quotes British and French national anthems). _Christus am Ölberge_ (the oratorio) is programmatic in the religious-narrative sense. The Beethoven catalogue does not uniformly fall into one category.

The 19th century would inherit both traditions. Brahms, Schubert, Bruckner would write mostly absolute music. Berlioz, Liszt, Mahler would write mostly programmatic music. The two traditions co-existed and remain co-existing.

#### Definition: The bird-call tradition

The use of bird calls in instrumental music has a long history in European music. Renaissance composers (Clément Janequin's _Le Chant des oiseaux_ , 1530s) had used birdsong as material. Baroque composers (Vivaldi's _Four Seasons_ has bird calls in the spring concerto; François Couperin's harpsichord pieces include bird portraits) had developed the tradition. By 1808, the bird-call-in-music tradition was well-established.

Beethoven's three birds in the Sixth (nightingale, quail, cuckoo) are the inheritors of the tradition. The nightingale's flute trill follows the standard 18th-century nightingale convention (Vivaldi had used the trill earlier). The quail's oboe call follows the quail-rhythm of European folk music (the bird's call is approximately _quit-it!-quit-it!_ in short-short rhythm). The cuckoo's descending-third in the clarinet is the cuckoo motif that appears in many European composers' work (Mahler's First Symphony reuses it in the introduction).

What is _distinctive_ about Beethoven's use is the labeling in the score and the simultaneous layering of all three birds. Beethoven asks the listener to recognize each bird individually and to hear all three together. The moment is brief (about thirty seconds) but and famous.

The bird-call passage has predecessors in Beethoven's own work — the Op. 13 _Pathétique_ Adagio cantabile (Chapter 2) has some bird-like ornamental writing, the Op. 28 _Pastoral_ Sonata (1801; a different "Pastoral" than the Sixth Symphony) has some country-music writing — but the Sixth's bird passage is the first in his work where the birds are _explicitly labeled_ and where the depiction is unambiguous.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the Sixth Symphony's programmatic structure makes it a _lesser_ work than the Fifth. The misreading is understandable but wrong. The Sixth was Beethoven's own preferred symphony in some self-assessments (he reportedly said in 1817 that the Sixth was "the symphony I love best"). Many subsequent composers (Berlioz, Mahler) have considered the Sixth one of the great Beethoven symphonies. The work's structural innovations, the five-movement layout, the attacca transitions across multiple movements, the integrated programmatic narrative — are foundational for 19th-century programmatic symphonism.

The misconception comes partly from the cultural dominance of the Fifth Symphony's per-aspera-ad-astra narrative. The Fifth's structural argument is _more dramatic_ , but the Sixth's is _more sustained_. Both works are achievements of the same compositional moment, and Beethoven was at the height of his middle-period powers when he composed them. The 1808 concert audience heard them on the same evening, and the contemporary responses treat both works with equal seriousness.

### 10.4 The Stories

#### The composition history

The Sixth Symphony was composed mostly in 1807–1808, with some sketches dating to 1803–1804 (the early sketches for the pastoral-music idea appear in the same notebooks as the _Eroica_ sketches). Beethoven worked on the Fifth and the Sixth in parallel through 1807 and 1808, completing both for the December 22, 1808 premiere.

The Beethoven interest in nature and pastoral subjects is consistent across his life. He was a regular walker in the Vienna countryside; documented contemporary accounts (Wegeler-Ries, Schindler, conversation books) describe his summer residencies in Heiligenstadt, Mödling, and other rural-Vienna suburbs, where he composed and walked daily. The pastoral material in the Sixth Symphony is drawing on these direct nature observations — particular country dances he had heard, the sound of the brook at the Brühl stream, the bird calls he had identified in his summer walks. The work is not a fantasy of country life; it is a portrait of country life Beethoven had actually experienced.

This places the Sixth at an interesting place in Beethoven's biography. The Fifth Symphony's struggle-and-triumph arc fits the deafness-and-recovery narrative that has often been applied to the work (the Heiligenstadt Testament of 1802 → the heroic-period output → the Fifth's per aspera ad astra). The Sixth's pastoral repose fits a _different_ narrative — Beethoven the nature-lover, the walker, the country resident who found in pastoral observation a sustaining counterweight to the urban professional life of Vienna composer-virtuoso.

Both narratives are real. Beethoven was both the heroic struggling artist and the nature-lover. The 1808 productivity was the moment when both narratives produced major works simultaneously.

#### The December 22, 1808 concert (continued from Chapter 9)

The Sixth was the first symphony performed on the four-hour concert (the program order placed the Sixth before the Fifth, with the Piano Concerto No. 4 and other works in between). Beethoven himself conducted; the orchestra had been under-rehearsed; the contemporary reports include some criticism of the orchestral execution.

The Sixth's reception at the premiere was _more positive_ than the Fifth's. Some contemporary reviewers found the Sixth's pastoral material more immediately accessible than the Fifth's dramatic argument. The bird calls in the second movement were widely commented on (some critics found them too literal; others found them charming). The Thunderstorm was universally praised as one of the great pieces of orchestral storm-writing. The Shepherd's Song closing was praised for its emotional sustenance.

By 1820 the Sixth was being performed regularly across Europe. By 1830 it was canonical. By the late 19th century it had been overtaken in popularity by the Fifth (the Fifth's drama proved more durable in concert programs than the Sixth's pastoral length), but the Sixth has always remained one of the most-loved Beethoven symphonies, particularly among listeners who prefer the more contemplative side of the catalogue.

#### The Thunderstorm tradition

The Sixth's Thunderstorm has descendants in the 19th-century orchestral storm tradition. Rossini's _William Tell_ Overture (1829) has a famous storm passage that is borrowing from Beethoven's Thunderstorm. Verdi's _Otello_ (1887) has a storm opening. Strauss's _Alpine Symphony_ (1915) has an extended storm. The orchestral-storm tradition is one of the 19th-century inheritances from the Beethoven Sixth.

Beethoven's storm uses a particular set of devices that became the standard storm-music vocabulary: timpani-as-thunder; rapid string tremolos as wind and rain; piccolo flashes as lightning; rising chromatic figures as the storm's intensification; sudden dynamic shifts; the eventual subsidence into quietness as the storm passes. The vocabulary is Beethoven's invention (with some Baroque precedents — Vivaldi's _Four Seasons_ "Summer" has storm-music elements).

#### Three readings of the Pastoral

**Karl Böhm / Vienna Philharmonic, 1971 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Böhm was a Viennese conductor of the older generation (1894–1981), an Austrian, the conservator of the Viennese conducting tradition. His 1971 Pastoral with the Vienna Philharmonic is one of the most-loved postwar readings — relaxed tempi, beautiful Viennese strings (the Vienna Philharmonic at its 1970s peak), the bird calls and the Thunderstorm given their full character. The reading is the model _Viennese-traditional_ approach. Recording sound: rich, full DG analog.

**Bruno Walter / Columbia Symphony Orchestra, 1958 (Sony / Columbia).** Walter (1876–1962) was a Viennese-born conductor who had been Mahler's assistant and then a major figure across Europe and (after 1939) America. His 1958 Pastoral with the Columbia Symphony (a pickup orchestra of Los Angeles musicians assembled specifically for the recording) is the model _late-Romantic warm_ reading. Slightly slower than Böhm, with the lyrical movements taken at extreme tempo flexibility. The reading is one of the great Beethoven Pastoral documents of the recording era. Recording sound: warm, intimate, slightly hazy by modern standards but musically present.

**John Eliot Gardiner / Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique, 1994 (Archiv).** Gardiner's HIP Pastoral uses period instruments and faster tempi based on Beethoven's metronome markings. The first movement runs about ten minutes (versus Böhm's eleven and a half); the brook movement is sharper-articulated; the bird calls have more bite. The Thunderstorm is taken with period-instrument sharpness. The Shepherd's Song closes more briskly. The reading is the model HIP Pastoral.

#### Cross-piece links

* The Thunderstorm's storm-music vocabulary has descendants in **Rossini's _William Tell_ Overture** (1829), **Verdi's _Otello_ opening storm** (1887), and **Strauss's _Alpine Symphony_** (1915). All inherit Beethoven's vocabulary.
* The five-movement programmatic structure has descendants in **Berlioz's _Symphonie fantastique_** (1830) and **Mahler's Fifth, Seventh** symphonies.
* The Sixth's pastoral mood and Beethoven's nature-walking biography connect to the **Op. 28 "Pastoral" Piano Sonata** (1801) — a different work also called "Pastoral," shorter and earlier, but in the same expressive register. Briefly mentioned; rewards direct attention.
* The Sixth's _Allegretto_ third movement (the country-dance scherzo with the "village oboist who can't quite count") connects to **Symphony 8's witty second movement** (Chapter 11) and to the **Razumovsky Op. 59 No. 2 Russian-theme scherzo** (Chapter 12) — the playful-Beethoven thread that the topic prompt's Habit #1 was designed to unlearn.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about Beethoven's inscription "more expression of feeling than painting" — what it tells us about how he wanted the Sixth to be heard, and how modern scholars (Lockwood, Solomon) treat the inscription in light of the work's clearly descriptive passages (the brook, the bird calls, the storm)._
* _Tell the companion which of the three named bird calls in movement 2 (nightingale, quail, cuckoo) you found easiest to identify on the listen, and ask her about the bird-call-in-music tradition from Renaissance through Beethoven through Mahler._
* _Ask the companion to compare the Sixth and the Fifth as twin works, both premiered on December 22, 1808, and to walk through what each work does differently. The conversation should produce a clearer sense of why Beethoven composed both in the same productive period._
* _Bring up the influence of the Sixth's Thunderstorm on subsequent 19th-century orchestral storm music (Rossini's William Tell, Verdi's Otello, Strauss's Alpine Symphony) with the companion — and how the storm-music vocabulary became a standard._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The flowing-strings figure throughout the second movement — Beethoven sustains the brook texture across nearly twelve minutes without interruption. Try to follow it.
* The three labeled bird calls in the closing measures of movement 2 — nightingale (flute), quail (oboe), cuckoo (clarinet). All three play simultaneously in the final pages.
* The Thunderstorm at the start of movement 4 — listen for the rising-wind figure in the strings, the timpani thunder, the piccolo lightning. The vocabulary becomes the standard 19th-century orchestral storm.
* The attacca transition between movements 4 and 5 — the storm subsides into the Shepherd's Song clarinet-and-horn yodel exchange. The transition is one of the most-discussed in Beethoven's symphonic writing.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Donald Tovey's essay on the Sixth Symphony in _Essays in Musical Analysis_ , vol. 1 (1935).** Tovey is older but his analysis of the Sixth — particularly his treatment of the bird-call passage and the attacca transitions — is still useful, and his prose is more accessible than most modern academic writing. Pair with Lewis Lockwood's chapter on the Sixth in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (2003), which integrates the Fifth-and-Sixth twin-work relationship with the biographical context.

### Chapter Summary

Symphony No. 6 in F major, Op. 68 ("Pastoral"), composed 1807–1808 and premiered on the same December 22, 1808 four-hour Vienna concert as the Fifth Symphony, is Beethoven's contrasting twin to the Fifth. The five-movement structure with explicit programmatic titles ("Awakening of cheerful feelings on arrival in the countryside" / "Scene by the brook" / "Merry gathering of country folk" / "Thunderstorm" / "Shepherd's song. Glad and grateful feelings after the storm") is the structural innovation. Movements 3, 4, and 5 are connected by _attacca_ transitions, with the Thunderstorm at the dramatic peak and the Shepherd's Song providing the closing emotional resolution.

Beethoven's inscription, _more expression of feeling than painting_ , positioned the work between program music (which the work clearly is in places) and absolute music (which it also is in its overall expressive arc). The work depicts specific external scenes (the brook's flowing strings; three named bird calls in movement 2 — nightingale, quail, cuckoo; the thunderstorm) while sustaining the _feeling_ of pastoral life as the overall expressive content.

You met the bird-call tradition (Renaissance through Vivaldi through Beethoven to Mahler) and Beethoven's specific contribution: explicit labeling of three birds with their species names, layered simultaneously in the woodwinds.

You met the Thunderstorm vocabulary (timpani thunder, string tremolo wind, piccolo lightning) that became the standard 19th-century orchestral storm vocabulary (Rossini, Verdi, Strauss all inherit it).

You met three readings of the Pastoral — Böhm 1971 (Viennese-traditional), Walter 1958 (late-Romantic warm), Gardiner 1994 (HIP) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you to the Symphony 7 and Symphony 8 — Beethoven's two 1812 symphonies, the witty pair that follow the Fifth-Sixth twin works. The middle-period symphonic catalogue is nearing its close.

## Chapter 11: Symphonies 7 and 8 — the 1812 Pair

In a single fertile stretch of 1811–1812, mostly composed in the summer-and-autumn retreats of Teplitz and Karlsbad in Bohemia, Beethoven completed two symphonies. Symphony No. 7 in A major, Op. 92, finished by April 1812. Symphony No. 8 in F major, Op. 93, finished by October 1812. The two works were premiered on the same Vienna concert in December 1813, a benefit performance for Austrian soldiers wounded at the battle of Hanau. After Symphony 8, Beethoven would not complete another symphony for eleven years — Symphony 9 was finished in 1824, after the crisis years (Chapter 14) and the late-period inward turn.

This chapter's anchor is the Symphony 7's second movement Allegretto — by general agreement the most-famous individual movement in either of the 1812 symphonies, and one of the most-loved movements in any Beethoven symphony. Wagner famously called the Symphony 7 _the apotheosis of the dance_ ; the Allegretto is the slow, dance-pulsed center of the work. The movement was so popular at the 1813 premiere that the audience demanded an immediate encore, and the orchestra played it through a second time before continuing with the third movement.

We will also walk Symphony 8 in paragraph-level overview. The Eighth is Beethoven's _little symphony in F_ (his own self-acknowledged affection for the work, from the conversation books and from contemporary accounts) — wittier, shorter, sometimes underrated, but with several of the most-loved compositional jokes in Beethoven's catalogue.

### 11.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — the encore the audience demanded

The Symphony 7's premiere on 8 December 1813 was, by all accounts, a remarkable concert. The occasion was a benefit for Austrian and Bavarian soldiers wounded at the Battle of Hanau (30–31 October 1813, in which Austro-Bavarian forces had attempted to block Napoleon's retreat from the Battle of Leipzig). The program was organized partly by the inventor Johann Nepomuk Mälzel — the same Mälzel who had invented the _panharmonicon_ mechanical orchestra and who would invent the metronome — who had also recently commissioned from Beethoven the _Wellington's Victory_ Op. 91, a programmatic work depicting the British victory over the French at Vitoria in June 1813.

The 8 December 1813 program included:

* **Symphony No. 7 in A major** : world premiere
* **Two march movements by Pleyel and Dussek** : played on Mälzel's panharmonicon
* **Wellington's Victory, Op. 91** : world premiere

The concert was sold out. The hall was filled with the Vienna musical elite (Salieri was in the orchestra; Spohr was a violinist; Hummel was at the keyboard for one of the works; the contemporary accounts identify many other major figures present). Beethoven himself conducted; the orchestra was unusually full-rehearsed for once (the patriotic occasion had brought out extra commitment from the players).

The Symphony 7 was received with enthusiasm. The Allegretto second movement, in particular, was received with such immediate audience response that the orchestra played it twice — once in the original position in the work, and then a second time as an encore demanded by the audience. The encore is in contemporary reviews (the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_ of 23 February 1814, and other accounts). The work was repeated in concert several times in the following weeks, each time with the Allegretto encored.

The Symphony 7 became, within months of its premiere, one of the most-loved Beethoven works in Vienna. The Allegretto in particular entered the repertoire as a freestanding movement — played at memorial concerts, transcribed for piano, used as a quotation in subsequent composers' works (we will return to the Allegretto-quotations in §11.4). The premiere was one of the rare moments in Beethoven's compositional career when audience response immediately matched the work's eventual canonical status.

Wagner's famous line, _the apotheosis of the dance_ , came in 1849, in his essay _The Artwork of the Future_. Wagner argued that the entire Symphony 7 is built on dance rhythms — the rapid 6/8 of the first movement, the funeral-march pulse of the second-movement Allegretto, the rapid scherzo of the third movement, the wild celebratory rhythm of the finale. The work is, in Wagner's reading, _dance music elevated to symphonic argument_. The line has been quoted endlessly since; modern scholars (Lockwood, Solomon) treat it as a useful summary of one important dimension of the work.

#### What to notice

Three things in the Allegretto.

  1. **The dotted-rhythm theme.** The movement opens with a soft, ominous theme in A minor played by the lower strings (violas, cellos, second basses). The theme has a distinctive dotted rhythm: _short-short-long-short-long_ , repeated. Listen for the _funeral-march_ quality — the slow, weighted pulse that is the movement's foundation. The theme is not melodic in the singing sense; it is more like a _recitation_ or _intonation_ over a strict rhythmic ostinato.

  2. **The variation form and the counter-melody.** The Allegretto is in variation form. After the opening theme, a _counter-melody_ is added in the upper strings — a longer, more lyrical line that _sings_ over the strict dotted ostinato. The contrast between the dotted ostinato and the lyrical counter-melody is the expressive heart of the movement. Listen for the _two simultaneous textures_ : the ostinato pulse and the singing line.

  3. **The major-key trio.** The movement has a contrasting central section in A major, the relative major, where the ostinato is briefly replaced by a more flowing melodic phrase. Listen for the _brightening_ : after the dark A-minor ostinato, the A-major section provides a brief lift before the A-minor returns. The structural pattern, A-minor / A-major / A-minor, gives the movement its arc.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed Symphony No. 7 in A major, Op. 92 in 1811–1812, mostly during the summer residencies in Teplitz and Karlsbad, and premiered it on 8 December 1813 at a benefit concert in Vienna for soldiers wounded at the Battle of Hanau; the Allegretto second movement was so immediately popular that the audience demanded an encore at the premiere.
