### 11.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into Kleiber 1976.

* **0:00–0:15.** The opening A-minor chord, sustained, soft. Listen for the _opening unison_ : the wind chord at the top, the string chord below, both at quiet dynamic. The chord establishes the home key (A minor) and the meditative atmosphere.

* **0:15–0:45.** The dotted-rhythm theme enters in the lower strings (violas, cellos, basses). The rhythm is _short-short-long-short-long_ : repeated, ostinato-like. Listen for the _strictness_ of the rhythm: Beethoven holds the dotted figure absolutely strict across the entire movement; the rhythm is the foundation.

* **0:45–1:30.** The theme is taken up by other voices in the strings. The texture builds; the dotted rhythm is now in multiple sections. Notice the _cumulative_ quality: each new entry adds to the existing texture rather than replacing it.

* **1:30–2:30.** A _counter-melody_ arrives in the upper strings. Listen for the _singing_ quality of this new line: a long, lyrical melody that sings _above_ the strict dotted ostinato. The contrast between the singing counter-melody and the dotted ostinato is the expressive center of the movement.

* **2:30–3:30.** A second iteration of the texture, with the counter-melody now more developed and the dotted ostinato continued underneath. The dynamic builds slightly. The harmonic motion expands.

* **3:30–4:30.** Continued development. The full orchestra is now engaged; the brass enters; the dotted ostinato is taken into more registers. Listen for the _cumulative buildup_ : this is the long-buildup-to-climax signature (Thread B) applied in slow tempo.

* **4:30–5:30.** **The major-key trio section.** Listen for the _brightening_ : the ostinato is briefly replaced by a more flowing melodic phrase in the woodwinds; the harmony shifts to A major (the relative major); the dynamic becomes lighter. The trio is the contrast that lets the listener catch breath before the return.

* **5:30–6:30.** Transition back to the A-minor ostinato. The brightening fades; the dotted rhythm returns. Listen for the _return_ : the ostinato comes back with the gravity restored.

* **6:30–8:00.** The ostinato is now in full force again, with the counter-melody returning and the orchestra at fuller weight. The variation form's recapitulation is underway.

* **8:00–9:00.** **The closing measures.** The ostinato continues but slowly fades; the orchestra subsides; the movement ends quietly with the dotted rhythm in the lowest strings, returning to the opening A-minor chord. Kleiber holds the final chord and lets it fade.

About nine minutes in Kleiber. The dotted rhythm runs nearly continuously across the entire movement. The counter-melody sings above. The major-key trio provides the central contrast. The expressive arc is the funeral-march-elevated-to-symphonic-meditation.

### 11.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Allegretto's place in the variation-form tradition (Thread B return)

We have met variation form before. The second movement of the Fifth Symphony (Chapter 9) is in variation form. The finale of the _Eroica_ (the _Prometheus_ -theme variation set we will return to in Chapter 21) is in variation form. The Symphony 7's Allegretto is the variation movement that put the technique on a new expressive footing.

What is new in the Allegretto:

**The ostinato as the variation theme.** Most variation sets take a melodic theme and vary it. The Allegretto takes a _rhythmic ostinato_ : a strict dotted-rhythm pattern, and varies the _upper voices_ against the unchanging ostinato. The technique is sometimes called _passacaglia_ or _chaconne_ in the Baroque tradition (Bach's _Passacaglia and Fugue_ in C minor BWV 582 uses related technique); Beethoven applies the principle to a symphonic slow movement.

**The accumulation of voices.** The first iteration is the ostinato alone in the violas, cellos, and basses. The second iteration adds the second violins. The third adds the first violins. The fourth adds the woodwinds. The fifth adds the brass. The variation moves through _additive accumulation_ rather than through _melodic transformation_. The result is a slow buildup of texture from soft and thin to loud and full.

**The counter-melody as the singing voice.** Above the strict ostinato, Beethoven introduces a lyrical counter-melody that sings continuously. The counter-melody is itself varied across the movement (different keys, different orchestrations, different lengths) but always sings _above_ the ostinato pulse. The two-layer texture, strict pulse below, singing line above, is the expressive innovation.

The Allegretto's variation technique would influence subsequent composers. Brahms's First Symphony second movement (1876) uses related accumulation. Mahler's First Symphony third movement (1888) uses a related ostinato-with-singing-line texture. Shostakovich's Fifth Symphony second movement (1937) uses related ostinato writing. The Allegretto is the prototype.

#### Symphony 8 — the "little symphony in F"

Symphony No. 8 in F major, Op. 93, is one of Beethoven's most-loved and most-underrated symphonies. It is shorter than the others (about twenty-six minutes; the Fifth is thirty-two and the Sixth is forty-three); it is wittier; it returns explicitly to the Classical-Haydn model rather than continuing the heroic expansion of the _Eroica_ -Fifth-Seventh line.

Beethoven himself reportedly called the Eighth _his little symphony in F_ — a self-acknowledged affection. He said (in the conversation books and in remarks to friends) that the Eighth was "much better than the Seventh" — a judgment that has not been widely shared by subsequent listeners but reflects Beethoven's own attachment to the work.

The Eighth has four movements in the standard Classical layout, all relatively brief:

  1. **Allegro vivace e con brio** (~10 min) — sonata-form first movement at fast tempo with playful character.
  2. **Allegretto scherzando** (~4 min) — a famous joke movement; the "metronome" character (the _ticking_ sixteenth-note pulse depicting the regular click of Mälzel's invention).
  3. **Tempo di Menuetto** (~5 min) — Beethoven's only return to the _Menuetto_ label after Symphony 1; the dance-style genuine minuet in the older Haydn-Mozart tradition.
  4. **Allegro vivace** (~7 min) — a sonata-form finale with Haydn-like wit (especially the "wrong-key" moments where Beethoven jokes by suddenly modulating to a remote key for comic effect).

The recommended Eighth recording is **Carlos Kleiber / Bayerisches Staatsorchester, 1983 (Orfeo)** — Kleiber's live recording from a Munich performance, often paired with Symphony 4. The reading is electric and witty. Alternates: **Bruno Walter / Columbia Symphony, 1958 (Sony)** ; **John Eliot Gardiner / ORR, 1994 (Archiv)**.

The Eighth is Beethoven _retreating_ slightly from the heroic-expansion path of the _Eroica_ -Fifth-Seventh. The work is not a return to the early style (it is not in any sense like Symphony 1); it is more like an _interlude_ of compressed wit in the middle of the heroic-period output. Several scholars (notably Lockwood) treat the Eighth as the _end_ of the middle period — the last middle-period symphony, after which Beethoven enters the 1815–1820 crisis years (Chapter 14) and emerges with the very different late style.

#### Definition: The dramatic slow introduction at the Seventh (Thread B return)

The Symphony 7's first movement opens with a long slow introduction (Poco sostenuto) before the Allegro (Vivace). The introduction is about three minutes — one of the longest in Beethoven's symphonies, comparable to the Symphony 2 introduction (Chapter 5).

We have met the dramatic-slow-introduction signature in:

* The _Pathétique_ (Chapter 2) — short symphonic-style introduction to a piano sonata.
* Symphony 1 (Chapter 5) — short introduction with the wrong-chord opening.
* Symphony 2 (Chapter 5) — expanded introduction, three minutes.
* Symphony 7 (this chapter) — Poco sostenuto introduction, three minutes, harmonically very adventurous.

The Symphony 7's introduction is the harmonic peak of the slow-introduction tradition. Beethoven moves through several remote keys (C major, F major, briefly D major) before establishing the home key of A major. The harmonic adventure is more substantial than in any earlier slow introduction in the Beethoven catalogue.

After Symphony 7, Beethoven would use slow introductions only sparingly. The Eighth has no slow introduction (it opens at tempo). The Ninth has a famous opening that is _not_ a slow introduction but a _slow buildup from emptiness_ — open fifths, gradual accumulation of orchestral voices. The slow-introduction signature, having reached its peak in Symphony 7, recedes from the late catalogue.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the Symphony 7's Allegretto is the _slow movement_ of the symphony. It is not. The Allegretto is _not slow_ ; it is at _moderately-fast_ tempo (allegretto = "a little allegro" = a moderate fast pace). The slowness of the Allegretto comes from the _dotted-rhythm ostinato_ , which is in _slow note values_ (long-short-long), not from the actual tempo. The pulse is moderate-quick; the _gestures_ within the pulse are slow.

The misconception matters because if you wait for the _slow movement_ of Symphony 7, you will not find one. The Symphony 7 has no slow movement; it has four fast-or-moderate-tempo movements. The Allegretto is the _moderately-fast funeral-march-meditation movement_ , occupying the structural position where the slow movement would be in most symphonies but at a faster tempo. The hybrid is part of what Wagner was calling out when he said the whole symphony is dance: there is no rest from the dance pulse anywhere in the work.

### 11.4 The Stories

#### The Mälzel-Beethoven collaboration and the metronome

Johann Nepomuk Mälzel (1772–1838) was a German-Austrian inventor and showman who is one of Beethoven's most-discussed collaborators in this period. Mälzel had invented several musical mechanical devices — the panharmonicon (a mechanical orchestra), various automaton musical devices, ear-trumpets that Beethoven used to amplify his fading hearing. By 1813 Mälzel had developed an early version of the _metronome_ (an instrument for marking strict tempo by audible ticking), which he was demonstrating in Vienna. The instrument would be patented in 1815 and would gradually become the standard tempo-marking device for music.

The Beethoven-Mälzel collaboration produced two works:

**Wellington's Victory, Op. 91 (1813).** Originally composed for Mälzel's panharmonicon (which could play the work mechanically), then transcribed by Beethoven for orchestra. The work depicts the British victory over the French at Vitoria in June 1813. It quotes the British anthem ("Rule, Britannia") and the French revolutionary anthem ("Marlborough s'en va-t'en guerre"). It includes orchestral effects depicting cannons, muskets, and battlefield chaos. It was Beethoven's most-popular work in his own lifetime — by some contemporary accounts, more popular even than the Fifth Symphony. Beethoven himself, in later years, called it _eine Dummheit_ , "a stupid thing", and was embarrassed by its popularity.

**Symphony 8's second movement Allegretto scherzando.** The movement is described (in the _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_ and in subsequent commentary) as containing a musical _portrait of Mälzel's metronome_. The sixteenth-note pulse in the woodwinds and strings is the steady, mechanical tick. The movement's character is comic; Beethoven is gently mocking the metronome's regularity. A documented anecdote from Czerny describes a Vienna dinner party at which Beethoven extemporized the metronome theme as part of a comic catch (a round-song) called _Ta ta ta, lieber Mälzel_ , and that the theme of the Eighth's second movement is derived from this same comic catch. The story is plausible but not strictly verifiable (the catch survives only in later sources).

What is documented: Beethoven was thinking about metronomic tempo-marking in 1812–1813, was friends with Mälzel during this period (though the friendship would later sour, particularly over the _Wellington's Victory_ publication rights), and used the metronome's regular pulse as material in Symphony 8's second movement. The episode is one of the Beethoven engagements with mechanical/scientific innovations of his period.

#### Symphony 7's quotations in later composers

The Symphony 7's Allegretto has been quoted, paraphrased, or directly referenced by many subsequent composers. The list:

* **Schubert's Symphony 9 ("Great" C major, 1825)** , second movement: the dotted-rhythm ostinato derived from the Allegretto's pattern.
* **Brahms's German Requiem (1865–69)** : the third movement uses related dotted-rhythm meditative writing.
* **Mahler's Fifth Symphony (1902)** , third movement (Adagietto): though slower and more lyrical, the layered ostinato-and-singing-line texture is the Allegretto inheritance.
* **Shostakovich's Eighth Symphony (1943)** , fourth movement: the dotted-rhythm ostinato in remembrance of victims of war.

The Allegretto's expressive vocabulary — slow-pulsed funeral-march meditation, ostinato-with-singing-line, gradual additive buildup — became one of the core vocabularies of the symphonic slow-movement tradition.

#### Beethoven at Teplitz, 1812

The summer of 1812 was when Beethoven completed the Symphony 7 and began Symphony 8. He was at the Bohemian spa town of Teplitz, where he had gone for his hearing treatment and for the social-cultural milieu (Teplitz attracted European nobility and writers; Beethoven met Goethe there in July 1812, in one of the most-meetings of his life).

The Goethe meeting is in two letters — Goethe's letter to his wife Christiane (19 July 1812), Beethoven's letters to his publisher Breitkopf & Härtel and to others. The two great Germans walked together in Teplitz on multiple occasions over about three weeks. Goethe (then 63) was the dean of German letters; Beethoven (then 41) was the most-respected German composer. The relationship was professional and respectful but cool. Beethoven's account: Goethe was _too courtly_ — too willing to bow to passing nobility, too unwilling to speak his mind in society. Goethe's account: Beethoven was _unrestrained_ — uncomfortably direct in conversation, lacking the social graces Goethe expected of a major artist.

The most-famous incident, the _Teplitz incident_ , is the story of the two men walking on a road and encountering the Empress and her entourage. According to the documented Beethoven account (in his letter to Bettina von Arnim, 1812), Goethe stepped aside and bowed; Beethoven walked straight through the noble entourage with his hat on. The story is true in outline but the exact details have been embellished in later retellings (Bettina von Arnim's own writings are partly fictional; Beethoven's letter is more sober than the popular versions). What is documented: the two men did walk together in Teplitz, did encounter nobility on the roads (which was a daily occurrence in spa-town life), and did have different responses to the encounters. The general pattern (Beethoven uncourtly, Goethe deferential) is documented; the specific dramatic details are partly later embellishment.

What is also from Teplitz 1812: the famous _Immortal Beloved_ letter is dated 6 and 7 July 1812 ("K." — Karlsbad, where Beethoven would travel shortly), and Maynard Solomon's Antonie Brentano identification (Chapter 7) places Antonie in Karlsbad in July 1812. The summer of 1812 is the peak of the _Immortal Beloved_ episode.

#### Three readings of the Symphony 7

**Carlos Kleiber / Vienna Philharmonic, 1976 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Paired with Symphony 5 (1974) on the original LP — together one of the most-loved Beethoven symphonic recordings of all time. The Symphony 7 is electric, fast, with the Allegretto taken at a moderate pace (nine minutes; some conductors take it slower at eleven or twelve). The dance character is emphasized; Wagner's "apotheosis of the dance" reading fits Kleiber's approach perfectly. The recording sound is the late DG Vienna sound — warm, present, full.

**Wilhelm Furtwängler / Berlin Philharmonic, 1943 (various labels).** Furtwängler's wartime Berlin recording of Symphony 7 is one of the great wartime Beethoven recordings. The Allegretto runs about ten minutes — slower than Kleiber. The recording captures Furtwängler at his most metaphysical: the gradual buildup of the Allegretto is taken with extreme attention to dynamic shading and tempo flexibility. The sound is wartime broadcast quality (lower fidelity than studio recordings of the same period), but the playing has a intensity that Furtwängler attributed to the wartime atmosphere. The reading is a model of the _late-Romantic-German_ approach to Beethoven; pair with the 1947 Vienna Fifth for the Furtwängler perspective.

**John Eliot Gardiner / Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique, 1993 (Archiv).** Gardiner's HIP Symphony 7 takes the Allegretto fast (about eight minutes) on period instruments. The dotted rhythm is sharper-articulated on natural horns and gut strings; the accumulation of voices is more transparent. The reading is the model HIP Symphony 7. Pair with Gardiner's HIP Eroica (Chapter 6) and Pastoral (Chapter 10) for the complete HIP perspective.

#### Cross-piece links

* The Symphony 7's first movement, which we have not anchored in detail in this chapter, is one of Beethoven's most-loved Allegros — built on a rapid 6/8 rhythm that Wagner called "the dance of all dances." The first movement is a perpetual-motion fast-dance argument. Listen to it after the Allegretto.
* The Symphony 7's _finale_ (Allegro con brio) is the _wildest_ of Beethoven's symphonic finales — a rapid, almost manic celebration in A major with the contemporary description "a bacchanale." Wagner called it "the orgy."
* The Allegretto's variation technique connects to the **Diabelli Variations** (Op. 120, Chapter 17) — the variation-form summit of Beethoven's late period.
* The Symphony 8 has connections to the **Eighth Variations on Mozart's _Là ci darem_** (WoO 28, 1796) and to the **Op. 18 quartets** (Chapter 12) — the playful-Beethoven thread.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the 1813 premiere of Symphony 7, the benefit-concert occasion, the encore demand, the Vienna musical elite in attendance, and the immediate enthusiastic reception. The episode is one of the rare moments when audience response immediately matched eventual canonical status._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the Allegretto felt most_ funeral-march _to you on the second listen, and ask her about Wagner's "apotheosis of the dance" reading of the full Symphony 7. The Allegretto's slow pulse is in tension with Wagner's reading; the tension is itself interesting._
* _Ask the companion about the Goethe-Beethoven meeting at Teplitz in summer 1812 — what is documented, what is later embellishment (particularly in the Bettina von Arnim retellings), and how the two men differed in their social-aesthetic temperaments._
* _Bring up Symphony 8's "Mälzel metronome" second-movement reference with the companion — the story from Czerny, the comic catch_ Ta ta ta, lieber Mälzel, _and how the metronome theme became the symphonic Allegretto scherzando._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The dotted rhythm in the lower strings from 0:15 — try to hear it continuing across the entire movement. The rhythm never stops.
* The counter-melody arrival at 1:30 — listen for the moment a singing line first emerges above the ostinato.
* The major-key trio at 4:30 — listen for the brightening before the A-minor returns.
* The closing measures at 8:00–9:00 — listen for the way the ostinato gradually fades into the opening A-minor chord, closing the movement in the same harmonic place where it began.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further-listening recommendation, listen to **Symphony 4 in B♭ major, Op. 60** (1806) — the symphony between the _Eroica_ and the Fifth that we have not anchored in this volume. The recommended recording is **Carlos Kleiber / Bayerisches Staatsorchester, 1983 (Orfeo)**. The Fourth is the _slim Greek maiden between two Norse giants_ (Schumann's phrase) — a lighter, wittier symphony that complements the heroic-period heavy symphonies. Pair with Symphony 8 for the _witty-Beethoven_ thread.

### Chapter Summary

Symphony No. 7 in A major, Op. 92, composed 1811–1812 and premiered on 8 December 1813 at a Vienna benefit concert for soldiers wounded at the Battle of Hanau, was an immediate popular success. The Allegretto second movement was so well-received at the premiere that the audience demanded and got an immediate encore — one of the rare moments in Beethoven's compositional career when audience response immediately matched canonical status.

The Allegretto is a variation movement in A minor built on a strict dotted-rhythm ostinato (_short-short-long-short-long_), with a lyrical counter-melody that sings above the ostinato, and a major-key central trio that provides brightening before the return. The technique — ostinato-with-singing-line, additive accumulation of voices, slow buildup of texture — became the prototype for subsequent variation-form slow movements (Schubert, Brahms, Mahler, Shostakovich all extend it).

You met Symphony 8 (Op. 93, 1812) in paragraph-level overview — Beethoven's "little symphony in F," wittier and shorter than the heroic-period symphonies, with the famous metronome-portrait second movement and the Beethoven self-acknowledged affection. The recommended recording is **Carlos Kleiber / Bayerisches Staatsorchester, 1983 (Orfeo)**.

You met the Mälzel-Beethoven collaboration — _Wellington's Victory_ Op. 91 (the "stupid thing" that was Beethoven's most-popular work in his own lifetime) and the metronome-portrait second movement of Symphony 8.

You met the Goethe-Beethoven meeting at Teplitz in July 1812 — the two great Germans walking together, with different temperaments and different social-aesthetic positions, in the summer that also included the _Immortal Beloved_ letter and the completion of Symphony 7.

You met the _dramatic slow introduction_ signature (Thread B) at its harmonic peak in Symphony 7's first movement.

You met three readings of Symphony 7 — Kleiber 1976 (electric-Vienna), Furtwängler 1943 (wartime-metaphysical), Gardiner 1993 (HIP) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you out of the symphonic catalogue and into the chamber-music wealth of Beethoven's middle period — the Op. 18 and Razumovsky quartets, the _Archduke_ Trio, the _Kreutzer_ Sonata, and the rest of the chamber music we have not yet anchored.

* * *

## Chapter 12: Beethoven's Chamber Music Beyond the Piano Sonatas

We have covered Beethoven's piano sonatas (Chapters 2, 4, 7) and we will cover his late string quartets (Chapter 20). Between these two anchors lies the vast middle-period chamber music: the early string quartets Op. 18, the middle-period _Razumovsky_ string quartets Op. 59, the piano trios Op. 70 and Op. 97, the violin sonatas (Op. 47 _Kreutzer_ , Op. 96), the cello sonatas (Op. 69, Op. 102), the Triple Concerto Op. 56.

This chapter consolidates them. The anchor is the _Archduke_ Trio, Op. 97 — the last great piano-trio statement before Beethoven moved into the late style, completed in 1811 and dedicated to Archduke Rudolph (Beethoven's most-important patron from the middle period onward). The secondary anchor is the _Razumovsky_ Op. 59 No. 1 first movement — the apex of Beethoven's middle-period quartet writing.

The other works receive paragraph-level treatment. The aim is to make the middle-period chamber landscape legible, with recommended recordings for further listening, before we leave the middle period entirely in the next chapter (Fidelio).

### 12.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — Archduke Rudolph and a lifelong friendship

Archduke Rudolph of Austria (1788–1831) was the youngest brother of Emperor Franz I of Austria. He was Beethoven's pupil from 1803 or 1804 onward — a composition and piano student who became, by all accounts, one of the few people Beethoven was genuinely close to across his career. The relationship was unusual: Rudolph was the Imperial patron and Beethoven was the commoner-composer, but the personal correspondence shows mutual respect and affection across nearly thirty years.

Rudolph commissioned, dedicated to, or was the recipient of more than a dozen Beethoven works. The list includes:

* **Piano Concerto No. 4, Op. 58** (1806; dedicated to Rudolph): Chapter 8
* **Piano Concerto No. 5 "Emperor," Op. 73** (1809; dedicated to Rudolph)
* **Piano Sonata Op. 81a "Les Adieux"** (1809–10; dedicated to Rudolph; the programmatic farewell, absence, return narrative was Rudolph's 1809 departure from Vienna during the French occupation): Chapter 7
* **The _Archduke_ Trio, Op. 97** (1811; dedicated to Rudolph); this chapter's anchor
* **Piano Sonata Op. 96** (1812)
* **Piano Sonata Op. 106 "Hammerklavier"** (1818; dedicated to Rudolph): Chapter 15
* **Missa Solemnis, Op. 123** (1819–23; composed for Rudolph's 1820 Olmütz installation as Archbishop; Beethoven missed the deadline by three years): Chapter 19
* **Piano Sonata Op. 111** (1822; dedicated to Rudolph): Chapter 16
* **Grosse Fuge, Op. 133** (1825–26; dedicated to Rudolph): Chapter 20

Rudolph was, in effect, Beethoven's most-consistent patron from about 1804 until Beethoven's death in 1827. The financial arrangement: Rudolph contributed (along with Prince Lobkowicz and Prince Kinsky) to the 1809 annuity that provided Beethoven with 4,000 florins per year for life, conditional on Beethoven remaining in Vienna. The annuity was the financial foundation of Beethoven's middle-period and late-period productivity.

The _Archduke_ Trio Op. 97 is, in this context, the friendship-and-patronage gift. It was composed in 1810–11, premiered at Schuppanzigh-led private performances in 1814 (with Beethoven himself at the piano: the last public concert at which Beethoven appeared as pianist, the deafness having made further public appearances impossible), and published in 1816. The work has the expansive lyrical-and-architectural quality that Beethoven reserved for his closest associates and his most-ambitious patrons. The _Archduke_ Trio is the work where the middle-period chamber writing achieves its largest scale and its most-sustained warmth.

#### What to notice

Three things in the _Archduke_ first movement.

  1. **The opening lyrical theme.** The trio opens with the piano alone playing a long, lyrical, warm melody in B♭ major. The phrase is extended (it takes almost a minute to complete), unhurried, and _sings_ in the way that few Beethoven opening themes sing. Listen for the _generous_ quality of the opening: this is Beethoven in his most-lyrical register.

  2. **The piano-and-strings dialogue.** After the opening solo piano statement, the strings (violin and cello) enter with a varied response. The dialogue is _integrated_ rather than competitive — piano and strings sustain a common expressive line, sometimes leading, sometimes accompanying, but always in agreement. Listen for the _partnership_ of the three voices.

  3. **The structural expansion.** The first movement is about twelve minutes — long for a piano trio, comparable to the scale of the _Eroica_ 's first movement. The trio inhabits symphonic space within the chamber medium. Listen for the _sense of architectural breadth_ : this is a work that takes its time.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the Piano Trio in B♭ major, Op. 97 "Archduke" in 1810–1811, premiered it on 11 April 1814 at the Vienna Schuppanzigh quartet concerts with Beethoven himself at the piano, and published it in 1816 with the dedication to Archduke Rudolph.

### 12.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into Beaux Arts 1979.

* **0:00–0:45.** The opening lyrical theme in the piano alone. Listen for the _long phrase_ : the melody breathes across nearly a full minute without breaking into shorter cells. The harmony is gentle; the dynamic is soft-moderate; the character is _generous_. Beaux Arts's pianist Menahem Pressler plays the opening with patient warmth.

* **0:45–1:30.** The strings enter: violin first, then cello — with a varied response. The piano continues; the texture builds to a three-voice statement. Notice the _equality_ of the voices: no one instrument dominates; the three are in conversation.

* **1:30–2:45.** Development of the first theme. The melody passes between instruments; the harmonies expand; the dynamics build slightly. Listen for the _unhurriedness_ : this is not the heroic forward-drive of the _Eroica_ or _Appassionata_ ; this is the _expanded breathing_ of the Archduke's lyrical register.

* **2:45–3:45.** The second theme arrives. Listen for the _contrast_ : a slightly more agitated, more rhythmic phrase in the strings, with the piano providing accompaniment. The second theme has more _forward motion_ than the first.

* **3:45–5:00.** Development of the second theme; the texture thickens.

* **5:00–6:00.** Closing material of the exposition. The first theme returns briefly to close the exposition. About 6:00 the exposition ends. Beaux Arts does not take the standard repeat.

* **6:00–8:00.** **The development section.** Beethoven takes both themes through related keys; the dialogue between the three instruments continues; the texture remains warm. Listen for the _gentle_ developmental work: this is not the dramatic stress-testing of the symphonic developments; this is the _exploration_ of the material in the chamber register.

* **8:00–9:30.** Continued development; building toward the recapitulation.

* **9:30–11:00.** **The recapitulation.** The opening lyrical theme returns in the piano; the strings re-enter; the three-voice dialogue is restored. The second theme returns in the home key.

* **11:00–12:00.** The coda. The opening melody returns once more with the full three-voice texture; the movement closes in B♭ major with a long sustained final chord. Beaux Arts holds the final chord and lets it fade.

About twelve minutes total in Beaux Arts. The lyrical character is sustained throughout. The three-voice dialogue is the expressive structure. The work inhabits symphonic space within chamber medium.

### 12.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The middle-period chamber-music landscape

Beethoven's middle period produced an enormous quantity of chamber music. The major works:

**String quartets Op. 18 Nos. 1–6** (1798–1800). Six works that did for the string quartet what Op. 1 did for the piano trio. The Op. 18 No. 1 in F major opens with a motif that anticipates the Razumovsky-period writing. The Op. 18 No. 4 in C minor is one of Beethoven's C-minor-character works (Thread B). The Op. 18 No. 6 has a famous slow-introduction labeled _La Malinconia_ ("the melancholy"), one of the early harmonic experiments in Beethoven's quartet writing.

**Razumovsky string quartets Op. 59 Nos. 1–3** (1805–1806). Three works dedicated to Count Andreas Razumovsky, the Russian ambassador to Vienna and a major Beethoven patron. The works are at unprecedented scale for string quartets — Op. 59 No. 1's first movement is the longest individual quartet movement Beethoven had composed up to that point. Each work includes a _thème russe_ (a Russian theme) somewhere in its structure, as a courtesy to the Russian dedicatee. The Op. 59 No. 2's "false" Russian theme in the third movement is a Beethoven joke (the theme is _too sweet_ to be a real Russian folk melody, and Beethoven turns it upside down in the variations).

**String quartets Op. 74 "Harp" (1809) and Op. 95 "Serioso" (1810).** Two transitional works between the Razumovskys and the late quartets. The Op. 95 is short, intense, and proto-late-style — its slow movement already anticipates the introspective register of the late period.

**Piano trios Op. 70 Nos. 1 "Ghost" and 2 in E♭ (1808).** Two works composed concurrently with the Fifth and Sixth Symphonies. The "Ghost" nickname comes from the spectral slow-movement of Op. 70 No. 1 — long, mysterious, in D minor, with sketches that Beethoven also drew on for a planned (never-completed) opera on _Macbeth_. The slow movement is one of the eeriest passages in the chamber-music catalogue.

**Piano Trio Op. 97 "Archduke" (1810–11).** The trio we are anchoring on. The last great piano-trio statement before the late period.

**Violin sonatas Op. 47 "Kreutzer" (1803) and Op. 96 (1812).** The Op. 47 is one of the most-virtuosic violin sonatas in the repertoire, composed for the British violinist George Bridgetower (the first dedicatee, later revoked after a dispute over a woman; the work was then dedicated to Rodolphe Kreutzer, who never played it). The Tolstoy novella _The Kreutzer Sonata_ (1889) is the later cultural inheritance — using the work as material for a tale of jealousy and adultery. The Op. 96 is the last violin sonata, more introspective than the Kreutzer, composed for the violinist Pierre Rode.

**Cello sonatas Op. 69 (1808) and Op. 102 Nos. 1 and 2 (1815).** The Op. 69 is the great middle-period cello sonata, in A major, with the cello in confident lyrical voice. The Op. 102 sonatas, the bridge to the late style, are shorter, more compressed, more contrapuntally adventurous. Op. 102 No. 2 ends with a fugue (the first time Beethoven used a fugal finale in a chamber work; the late string quartets will continue the practice).

**Triple Concerto Op. 56 (1803–04).** Composed for violin, cello, and piano with orchestra, dedicated to Prince Lobkowicz. The work is the unusual middle-period concerto — three soloists rather than one, an experiment in chamber-concerto crossover. Less-loved than Beethoven's solo concertos but the slow movement (in A♭ major) is one of the lyrical high points.

This is the chamber-music wealth that this chapter cannot anchor individually. The recommended next-listening is one work from each category: a Razumovsky quartet (Op. 59 No. 1), the _Ghost_ Trio (Op. 70 No. 1), the _Kreutzer_ Sonata (Op. 47), the Op. 69 cello sonata. With these in the ear along with the _Archduke_ , the middle-period chamber-music landscape becomes legible.

#### The Razumovsky Op. 59 No. 1 — secondary anchor

The Op. 59 No. 1 in F major is the apex of middle-period quartet writing. The work was composed in 1805–1806 (concurrent with the _Eroica_ and the Violin Concerto) and is at unprecedented scale for a string quartet — about forty-five minutes total, with the first movement alone at about ten minutes.

The first movement (Allegro) opens with a cello solo: the cello plays the first theme alone for several measures before the upper strings enter. This was unusual for a quartet in 1806 — quartets typically opened with the first violin leading, with the lower strings providing accompaniment. Beethoven gives the _cello_ the opening melodic statement, in a inversion of the standard quartet hierarchy. The technique would be followed by subsequent quartet composers (Schubert's "Death and the Maiden" quartet of 1824 uses a related opening).

The recommended Op. 59 No. 1 recording is **Alban Berg Quartet, 1989 (EMI)** — the great Vienna quartet of the late 20th century, in a complete Beethoven cycle that is one of the references of the modern recording era. Alternates: **Quartetto Italiano, 1972 (Philips)** ; **Takács Quartet, 2002–2004 (Decca)**.

#### The Op. 47 "Kreutzer" Sonata — the violin-sonata milestone

The Op. 47 Sonata in A major for violin and piano is one of Beethoven's most-virtuosic works. It was composed in 1802–1803 — the same period as the _Eroica_ sketches and the Heiligenstadt Testament — and premiered in May 1803 at the Augarten in Vienna, with Beethoven at the piano and George Bridgetower (a British-Polish violinist) on the violin.

The Bridgetower episode is documented in Beethoven's letters and in Bridgetower's later memoirs. The two men had a falling-out shortly after the premiere — the cause was a dispute over a woman (the details are unclear; both men's accounts differ). Beethoven removed the dedication from Bridgetower and re-dedicated the work to Rodolphe Kreutzer, a French violinist whom Beethoven had met in Vienna in 1798. Kreutzer never played the work (he reportedly considered it unplayably difficult).

The work has features:

* **The first movement opens with a violin solo** : like the Op. 59 No. 1, the first violin gets the opening statement, atypical for the genre.
* **The first movement is in A minor** : the second and third movements are in F major and A major, but the opening is in the dramatic minor mode.
* **The work is technically demanding** : at the upper edge of what was playable in 1803 for both violin and piano.

The Tolstoy novella _The Kreutzer Sonata_ (1889) is one of the later cultural inheritances. Tolstoy used the work as the soundtrack to a tale of jealousy and murder. The novella's protagonist hears the sonata played by his wife and a violinist friend, becomes convinced they are having an affair, and eventually murders his wife. The Sonata-novella connection has been reproduced in subsequent literature, theater, and film. The cultural afterlife of the Kreutzer is one of the unusual cases where a piece of instrumental music acquires a sustained literary-cultural narrative.

The recommended Kreutzer recording is **David Oistrakh / Lev Oborin, 1962 (Philips)**.

#### The Op. 70 No. 1 "Ghost" Trio — paragraph

The Op. 70 No. 1 Trio in D major (with the famous D-minor slow movement) was composed in 1808, concurrent with the Fifth and Sixth Symphonies. The nickname _Ghost_ comes from the spectral slow movement (Largo assai ed espressivo), in which Beethoven uses extended pianissimo writing, eerie chromaticism, and unusual orchestral effects to create a atmosphere of unease. The compositional sketches for the slow movement are overlapping with sketches for a planned (never-completed) opera on _Macbeth_ , particularly the witches' scenes.

The slow movement of the Ghost is one of the great strange Beethoven moments. The recommended recording is again **Beaux Arts Trio, 1979 (Philips)**.

#### Definition: The variation-form finale (Thread B return)

The Archduke's finale (the fourth movement, not anchored in this chapter but worth knowing) is a variation set. Op. 70 No. 2 has a variation movement. Op. 96's finale is a variation set. The Razumovsky Op. 59 No. 1 ends with a variation movement on the Russian theme.

We have met the variation-form-finale signature in:

* Symphony 7's Allegretto (Chapter 11)
* _Eroica_ 's finale on the Prometheus theme (Chapter 6, mentioned)
* Symphony 5's second movement Andante (Chapter 9)

The variation-form finale is the Beethoven signature that culminates in:

* Op. 109's variation finale (Chapter 16)
* Op. 111's Arietta second movement (Chapter 16)
* The _Diabelli Variations_ (Chapter 17)
* Symphony 9's choral finale (which is, structurally, a giant variation set on the Ode to Joy theme — Chapter 18)

The signature is one of the most-consistent in Beethoven's compositional vocabulary. Chapter 21 will catalogue the full set.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume chamber music is _lower-stakes_ than orchestral music; that the symphonies are the "great" works and the chamber pieces are something like exercises. This is wrong for Beethoven specifically (and for several other composers). Beethoven's middle-period chamber works include the most-ambitious quartet writing of his time (Razumovsky), the most-virtuosic violin writing of his time (Kreutzer), and the most-sustained piano-trio writing of his time (Archduke). The chamber works are at the same artistic level as the symphonies and concertos.

The misconception comes partly from the _concert-hall_ dominance of the symphony in the 19th and 20th centuries. Symphonies are performed at major concerts with large audiences; chamber works are performed in smaller halls with smaller audiences. The concert-hall economics make symphonies more visible. But the _compositional ambition_ is equivalent. By Beethoven's late period (Chapter 20), the chamber writing actually _exceeds_ the orchestral writing in compositional adventure — the late quartets are the most-advanced Beethoven works, not the Ninth Symphony.

### 12.4 The Stories

#### The 1814 premiere of the Archduke and Beethoven's last public piano performance

The _Archduke_ Trio premiered on 11 April 1814 at the Schuppanzigh Quartet's chamber-music concerts in Vienna. Beethoven himself was at the piano; Ignaz Schuppanzigh (the leading Vienna violinist, founder of the eponymous quartet) was on violin; Joseph Linke was on cello. The performance was repeated several times in subsequent weeks.

The contemporary accounts of the 1814 _Archduke_ performances are extraordinary, and not in a good way. Beethoven's deafness, by 1814, was severe. He could no longer hear the high register of the piano accurately; he could no longer adjust his playing to the strings in real-time. The Louis Spohr account, written years later: _"the piano was wretchedly out of tune, which troubled Beethoven little, for he could not in any case hear it. There was scarcely anything left of the brilliance the great artist had once been famous for. In the forte passages the poor deaf man hammered so on the keys that whole groups of notes were inaudible; in the piano passages he played so softly that whole groups of notes were missing entirely. The whole effect would have been incomprehensible without a knowledge of the score. I was profoundly moved by the misfortune. To witness it is a sad thing."_

The 1814 _Archduke_ performances are the end of Beethoven's career as a public solo pianist. He never appeared as soloist in a public concert again. The 1808 four-hour concert (Chapter 9) had been his last satisfying solo performance; the 1814 _Archduke_ was the final attempt. After this, the deafness made it impossible.

The work itself, however, did not suffer for the awkwardness of the 1814 premieres. The score was published in 1816 and became immediately recognized as one of the great chamber works. Subsequent performers — Liszt's pupil Carl Tausig, Anton Rubinstein, Eugen d'Albert in the 19th century; Artur Schnabel, Pablo Casals (cello), Adolf Busch (violin) in early-20th-century chamber configurations; Beaux Arts, Trio Wanderer, others in the modern era — have made the _Archduke_ a central repertoire piece. The work's depth is independent of its imperfect premiere.

#### Schuppanzigh and the Vienna chamber-music tradition

Ignaz Schuppanzigh (1776–1830) was the leading Vienna violinist of his generation and the founder of the _Schuppanzigh Quartet_ , which premiered most of Beethoven's middle-period and late-period quartets. The Schuppanzigh Quartet (Schuppanzigh on first violin, with rotating personnel on other parts) was the core ensemble through which Beethoven's chamber music entered the world. The relationship with Beethoven was long, professional, and warm (with occasional exasperations on both sides — Beethoven joked about Schuppanzigh's increasing weight in his later years; Schuppanzigh joked about Beethoven's increasing difficulty as a composer for the strings).

The Schuppanzigh Quartet premiered:

* Op. 18 quartets (1798–1800)
* Op. 59 Razumovsky quartets (1806)
* Op. 74 _Harp_ (1809)
* Op. 95 _Serioso_ (1810)
* Op. 127 first late quartet (1825)
* Op. 132 (1825)
* Op. 130 in its original _Grosse Fuge_ finale (1826)

The Schuppanzigh-Beethoven collaboration is one of the most-sustained composer-performer relationships in chamber-music history. We will return to it in Chapter 20 (the late quartets).

#### Razumovsky and the Russian thread

Count Andreas Razumovsky (1752–1836) was the Russian ambassador to Vienna from 1792 to 1807, then again from 1809. He was an amateur violinist of considerable accomplishment (he played second violin in private quartet performances at his palace), a major Beethoven patron, and the dedicatee of the Op. 59 quartets.

The arrangement for Op. 59: Razumovsky commissioned the three quartets in 1805. He asked Beethoven to include a _thème russe_ (Russian theme) in each work as a gesture toward the dedicatee's nationality. Beethoven complied:

* **Op. 59 No. 1** : The Russian theme appears in the finale. It is a slow, sad Russian melody (folk-origin) that Beethoven transforms into a dancing variation set.
* **Op. 59 No. 2** : The Russian theme appears in the third movement as the trio of the scherzo. The theme is "Slava Bogu na nebe" ("Glory to God in heaven"): a Russian Orthodox hymn that Mussorgsky would later use in _Boris Godunov_. Beethoven's handling is to present the theme straight, then turn it upside down in the variations: a mock-Russian joke.
* **Op. 59 No. 3** : No explicit Russian theme. Beethoven explained in a letter that he had used the Russian-theme device twice already and felt the third work could stand without it.

The Razumovsky-Russian thread is one of Beethoven's few sustained engagements with non-German national-musical material. The other engagements are smaller: the _Folk Songs_ settings he made later for the publisher George Thomson of Edinburgh (Scottish, Irish, Welsh, English, and continental folk songs, 1809–20). The Russian-theme work in Op. 59 is the most-substantial use of foreign material in the Beethoven catalogue.

#### The Tolstoy _Kreutzer Sonata_ novella

Tolstoy's _Kreutzer Sonata_ (1889) is the most-sustained literary inheritance of a Beethoven work. The novella tells the story of Pozdnyshev, a Russian aristocrat who murders his wife in a fit of jealous rage after hearing her play the Beethoven Op. 47 Sonata with a violinist friend named Trukhachevsky. The novella is partly a moral argument (Tolstoy's late-life sexual asceticism), partly a piece of literary art, partly a meditation on the _power of music to provoke and destabilize_.

The passage that connects the novella to the Beethoven work:

> _"Music is a terrible thing! What is it? I don't know. What is music? What does it do? And why does it do what it does? They say music has an uplifting effect on the soul — rubbish, not true! It has an effect, an awful effect on me, that is, but not of an uplifting kind. It has an effect neither uplifting nor debasing, but irritating, exciting... How can I explain it? Music makes me forget myself, my real condition, transports me to some other condition not my own."_

Tolstoy's protagonist hears the Kreutzer's first movement and feels his moral defenses dissolving. The novella treats this dissolution as a danger of music itself, and Beethoven's Op. 47 specifically as the locus of the danger.

The novella was published in 1889 and immediately caused a scandal in Russia — the Tsarist censors briefly banned it. It was translated into multiple languages and produced subsequent works (Leoš Janáček's String Quartet No. 1 "The Kreutzer Sonata," 1923, is the musical response). The Beethoven-Tolstoy thread, extended by Janáček's quartet response, is one of the unusual cases where a piece of music acquired a sustained literary-cultural narrative that the music itself does not strictly require.

#### Three readings of the _Archduke_

**Beaux Arts Trio, 1979 (Philips).** Beaux Arts's second complete Beethoven trio cycle (the first was in 1964–65; the 1979 _Archduke_ is part of the second cycle). The reading is the model warm-classical interpretation — Pressler at the piano with unhurried lyricism, the strings (then Isidore Cohen on violin, Bernard Greenhouse on cello) providing equal weight in the three-voice dialogue. Recording sound is the late Philips analog — present, warm, ample.

**Trio Wanderer, 2012 (Harmonia Mundi).** Trio Wanderer's complete Beethoven trio cycle (2009–2012) is the modern reference. The _Archduke_ is the cycle's centerpiece. The reading is more _transparent_ than Beaux Arts — French chamber-music tradition applied to Beethoven, with the inner voices unusually audible. Recording sound: contemporary, with HIP-informed bowing and articulation.

**Daniel Barenboim / Pinchas Zukerman / Jacqueline du Pré, 1970 (EMI).** A exceptional configuration — three soloists each at the absolute peak of their careers, recorded in 1970 at the height of their collaboration. Du Pré was 25 and at the peak of her playing; Zukerman was 22; Barenboim was 28. The reading has intensity and rapid tempo (twelve and a half minutes for the first movement); the three-voice texture is more _competitive_ than in Beaux Arts (each soloist asserts), but the chemistry is remarkable. The reading is the most-loved configuration recording of the work.

#### Cross-piece links

* The Op. 70 No. 1 _Ghost_ Trio's spectral slow movement connects to the _Macbeth_ opera sketches Beethoven never completed — the eerie register reappears in the late quartets' slow movements (Chapter 20, particularly Op. 132's _Heiliger Dankgesang_).
* The Op. 59 No. 1's cello-opening innovation connects to **Schubert's "Death and the Maiden" Quartet** (1824) and subsequent quartet writing — the redistribution of opening-melodic-statement away from the first violin.
* The Op. 47 _Kreutzer_ connects to the Tolstoy novella (1889) and to **Janáček's String Quartet No. 1 "Kreutzer Sonata"** (1923) — the literary-musical inheritance chain.
* The _Archduke_ 's lyrical-warmth register connects to the **Op. 96 violin sonata** (1812) — the last violin sonata, in the same register of expanded lyrical writing.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the Archduke Rudolph-Beethoven friendship and patronage relationship — how it began (1803–04 lessons), what works it produced (the list is long; the Missa Solemnis is the culminating gift), and what the relationship tells us about Beethoven's social life beyond the heroic-Romantic-loner caricature._
* _Tell the companion which of the named secondary works in this chapter (Razumovsky Op. 59 No. 1, the Ghost Trio, the Kreutzer Sonata, the Op. 69 cello sonata) you'd like to sample next, and ask her to recommend a starting recording and a single thing to listen for._
* _Ask the companion about the Louis Spohr account of Beethoven's 1814 Archduke premiere performances — the "wretchedly out of tune" piano, the inaudible forte and missing piano passages, the end of Beethoven's public-solo career. The episode is one of the turning points in his late life._
* _Bring up the Tolstoy _Kreutzer Sonata_ novella (1889) and the Janáček response (1923) with the companion — what the literary-musical inheritance chain tells us about how an instrumental piece can acquire a sustained narrative not originally present in the music._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The opening solo-piano melody at 0:00–0:45 — try to follow the single phrase across the long breath. The melody is one of the lyrical peaks of Beethoven's middle-period writing.
* The string entrance around 0:45 — listen for the violin first, then the cello, as the three-voice texture builds.
* The development section from 6:00 — listen for the _gentleness_ of the developmental work compared with the dramatic symphonic developments.
* The closing coda from 11:00 — the opening melody returns with the full three-voice texture; the work closes in the same generous register in which it opened.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further-listening recommendation, listen to the **Op. 59 No. 1 string quartet** in **Alban Berg Quartet, 1989 (EMI)** — the apex of middle-period quartet writing, with the cello opening and the Russian-theme finale. Then sample the **Op. 70 No. 1 _Ghost_ Trio** slow movement (Beaux Arts 1979) for the spectral side of middle-period chamber writing. With _Archduke_ , _Razumovsky_ No. 1, and _Ghost_ , you have the middle-period chamber high points in the ear.

### Chapter Summary

The _Archduke_ Trio Op. 97 (1810–11) is the last great piano-trio statement of Beethoven's middle period, dedicated to Archduke Rudolph (Beethoven's most-consistent patron from 1804 onward). The first movement is a long lyrical Allegro moderato in B♭ major, with the expanded breathing and warm three-voice dialogue that mark Beethoven's most-generous chamber register.

The 1814 premiere was Beethoven's last public solo piano performance — the Spohr account describes the deafness's effect on the playing. The work itself survived the imperfect premiere and entered the chamber repertoire as one of the central works.

You met the middle-period chamber-music landscape in paragraph-level overview: Op. 18 quartets (1798–1800), Razumovsky Op. 59 quartets (1805–06), Op. 74 _Harp_ and Op. 95 _Serioso_ quartets (1809–10), Op. 70 trios with the _Ghost_ slow movement (1808), Op. 47 _Kreutzer_ Sonata (1803) and Op. 96 Violin Sonata (1812), Op. 69 and Op. 102 cello sonatas (1808 and 1815), Triple Concerto Op. 56 (1803–04).

You met the secondary anchor — Razumovsky Op. 59 No. 1 first movement, with the cello opening that inverts the standard quartet hierarchy. Recommended recording: **Alban Berg Quartet, 1989 (EMI)**.

You met the Razumovsky-Russian-theme connection: the three Op. 59 quartets each (in two cases) include a _thème russe_ as a courtesy to the Russian dedicatee, including the mock-Russian joke of Op. 59 No. 2's "Slava Bogu" turned upside down.

You met the Tolstoy _Kreutzer Sonata_ novella (1889) and Janáček's quartet response (1923) — the literary-musical inheritance chain that extends Beethoven's Op. 47.

You met the **variation-form finale** signature (Thread B return) at its middle-period apex.

You met three readings of the _Archduke_ — Beaux Arts 1979 (warm-classical), Trio Wanderer 2012 (modern-transparent), Barenboim/du Pré/Zukerman 1970 (intense-competitive) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you to _Fidelio_ — Beethoven's only opera, composed in three versions across nine years, with the Act II Prisoners' Chorus and the closing scene as the operatic high points. The middle-period catalogue's theatrical statement.

## Chapter 13: _Fidelio_ — Beethoven's Only Opera

Beethoven composed exactly one opera. Three versions of it, across nine years. _Fidelio_ opened in Vienna on 20 November 1805 in its first version (titled _Leonore_) before an audience of mostly French occupation officers (Napoleon's troops had taken Vienna a week earlier on 13 November; most of Beethoven's Viennese supporters had fled the city). The work failed. Beethoven revised it for an 1806 production. It failed again. Beethoven set the opera aside for eight years, returning to it in 1814 with a major revision that yielded the canonical final version. The 1814 _Fidelio_ opened on 23 May 1814 at the Kärntnertortheater, in a Vienna that was now post-Napoleonic and ready for the work's themes of political imprisonment and liberation. It succeeded.

Beethoven himself described _Fidelio_ as _the child that has cost me the most labor pains_ (a remark to Friedrich Treitschke, the librettist of the 1814 final version; reproduced in multiple memoirs). The twelve years of work — through three versions, four overtures (the three _Leonore_ overtures and the final _Fidelio_ overture), and uncountable revisions — produced one of the most-loved 19th-century operas. The work has a place in the German-opera tradition (between Mozart's _Magic Flute_ and Wagner's mature works) and in the political-humanism tradition (it inspired performances at moments of political crisis throughout the 19th and 20th centuries, including performances at the fall of the Berlin Wall in 1989).

This chapter's anchor is the Act I Prisoners' Chorus, Florestan's Act II Aria, and the closing scene "O namenlose Freude" — the three high points of the opera. We will not anchor every scene; the opera is over two hours long and our four-section listening loop is best applied to the most-loved passages.

### 13.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — the four overtures

Beethoven composed _four_ overtures for _Fidelio_ across the work's three versions.

**Leonore Overture No. 1** (Op. 138). Composed first, around 1805. Some scholars argue this was actually composed _for_ the 1805 premiere and rejected before being used; others argue it was composed in 1806–08 as a possible replacement for the No. 2. The documentation is unclear. The work is in C major, of moderate length, and is the least-monumental of the four overtures. It is rarely performed in modern productions.

**Leonore Overture No. 2** (Op. 72a, originally just "Overture"). Composed for and performed at the 1805 premiere. It is in C major, monumental in scale, with an extended slow introduction depicting Florestan's imprisonment. Contemporary critics found it dramatically powerful but unbalanced as a curtain-raiser for an opera.

**Leonore Overture No. 3** (Op. 72b). Composed for the 1806 revision. Also in C major, also monumental, derived from No. 2 with extensive rewriting. The No. 3 is Beethoven's most-celebrated symphonic overture — frequently performed as a stand-alone concert piece. Its dramatic intensity (the famous off-stage trumpet call that signals the arrival of the minister) is one of the great Beethoven orchestral moments. In modern productions, the No. 3 is sometimes inserted between the two scenes of Act II as a kind of orchestral interlude (a tradition starting with Mahler's productions in Vienna around 1904).

**Fidelio Overture** (Op. 72c). Composed for the 1814 final version. In E major (not C major, like the others), shorter, less monumental, more appropriate as a curtain-raiser. This is the overture used at most modern _Fidelio_ productions.

The four-overture history is one of the most-unusual cases in operatic history. Beethoven was not satisfied with any of the first three overtures and kept rewriting until he got something that worked dramatically for the opera's actual opening. The journey from _Leonore_ No. 2 (1805) through No. 3 (1806) to the final _Fidelio_ Overture (1814) reflects Beethoven's dissatisfaction with the work as a whole — not just the overtures but the libretto, the dramatic structure, the pacing, the characterizations. The 1814 final version is the work Beethoven was finally satisfied with; the prior versions are interesting historical documents but not the canonical _Fidelio_.

For modern listeners, the practice is: hear the _Fidelio_ Overture (Op. 72c) at the start of any modern production; hear _Leonore_ Overture No. 3 (Op. 72b) as a stand-alone concert piece or as an interlude in the second act; sample _Leonore_ No. 1 and No. 2 from recordings as historical curiosities. The work itself, in its 1814 final version, is the canonical _Fidelio_.

#### What to notice

Three things across the anchor passages.

  1. **The Act I Prisoners' Chorus.** Near the close of Act I (in the 1814 two-act version), the political prisoners — held in the dungeons of the prison Florestan is imprisoned in — are briefly allowed out into the courtyard for fresh air. The chorus they sing — _"O welche Lust, in freier Luft"_ ("Oh what joy, in the free air") — is one of the humanist landmarks of 19th-century opera. Listen for the _quietness_ of the chorus: the prisoners sing pianissimo, in awe and uncertainty, with the orchestra providing soft support. The chorus is the quiet revolutionary statement of the opera.

  2. **Florestan's Aria — "Gott! Welch Dunkel hier!"** ("God! What darkness here!"). Act II opens with the political prisoner Florestan alone in his cell, in chains, in near-darkness. He sings the great Beethoven scena: a slow recitative ("Gott! Welch Dunkel hier!") leading into an aria ("In des Lebens Frühlingstagen" — "In the spring days of my life"), with a closing vision of his wife Leonore. Listen for the _transition from despair to vision_ : the aria's two-part structure (despair, then vision) is the operatic emotional arc.

  3. **The closing "O namenlose Freude" duet.** Near the end of Act II, after Leonore (in disguise as the boy Fidelio) has revealed herself to Florestan and the minister has arrived to liberate the political prisoners, Leonore and Florestan sing a ecstatic duet of liberation and reunion — _"O namenlose Freude!"_ ("O nameless joy!"). The duet is the operatic climax of the work. Listen for the _embrace_ of the two voices: soprano and tenor in close interweaving, in F major, at full operatic celebration.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed three versions of _Fidelio_ — 1805 (titled _Leonore_ ; premiered 20 November 1805 at the Theater an der Wien before a Napoleon-occupation audience), 1806 (revised but still failed), and 1814 (the canonical final version, premiered 23 May 1814 at the Kärntnertortheater in post-Napoleonic Vienna) — with libretti by Joseph Sonnleithner (1805), Stephan von Breuning (1806), and Georg Friedrich Treitschke (1814), all derived from Jean-Nicolas Bouilly's French libretto _Léonore, ou L'amour conjugal_ (1798) for André Grétry's earlier opera setting.
