### 13.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the anchor passages once. Second listen, with timestamps into Klemperer 1962.

#### The Act I Prisoners' Chorus (Act I scene 7 in the 1814 version, "O welche Lust")

* **0:00–0:30.** The orchestral introduction. Listen for the _soft string opening_ : Klemperer takes the introduction at a slow, weighted tempo, with the strings sustaining the harmony at pianissimo. The atmosphere is established: the prisoners are emerging into light after long darkness.

* **0:30–1:30.** The chorus begins. The male voices (tenor and bass) enter at pianissimo, almost a whisper. The text — _"O welche Lust, in freier Luft den Atem leicht zu heben!"_ ("O what joy, in the free air to lift our breath lightly!") — is sung quietly, in awe, with the chorus's vocal ranges spread across a wide register (basses very low, tenors moderate-high).

* **1:30–3:00.** The chorus continues with the soft hymnal character. Beethoven uses choral writing that is more _spoken-prayer_ than _operatic display_. Listen for the _restraint_ : this is not the heroic-chorus tradition of Handel or even of Mozart's _Magic Flute_ ; this is the quiet revolutionary statement.

* **3:00–4:30.** A solo tenor voice rises out of the choral texture. Listen for the soloistic moment: a single prisoner steps forward and sings briefly above the chorus: the universal-particular gesture (the chorus is _all_ the prisoners; the soloist is _one_ prisoner).

* **4:30–6:30.** The chorus builds slightly. Listen for the _gradual emergence_ : the voices grow in dynamic, the orchestra adds weight, the chorus moves from awe toward a more sustained expression of relief.

* **6:30–8:30.** A second solo voice (bass) joins. The two soloists alternate with the chorus.

* **8:30–10:00.** The closing measures. The chorus and orchestra subside back into the opening atmosphere; the prisoners are called back to their cells; the chorus ends in uncertainty about the brief freedom and the expectation of return to darkness.

The Prisoners' Chorus runs about ten minutes in Klemperer. The quiet revolutionary character is sustained throughout. The chorus is one of the humanist-music landmarks; subsequent productions (Vienna 1955 reopening after WWII; Berlin Wall fall 1989; many others) have used the chorus as a political statement.

#### Florestan's Aria — "Gott! Welch Dunkel hier!" (Act II scene 1)

* **0:00–0:45.** The orchestral introduction. Klemperer takes the introduction at slow weighted tempo. Listen for the _dark woodwind harmonies_ : Beethoven uses unusual chord voicings (low oboe, low clarinet, sustained bassoon and contrabassoon) to depict the darkness of Florestan's cell.

* **0:45–2:00.** The recitative: Florestan begins to sing. _"Gott! Welch Dunkel hier!"_ : sung in declamatory style, with the orchestra providing soft chordal support. The recitative is the operatic expression of Florestan's despair.

* **2:00–3:30.** The slow aria begins — _"In des Lebens Frühlingstagen"_ ("In the spring days of my life"). Florestan reflects on his lost happiness, his imprisonment, his lost freedom. Listen for the _long lyrical line_ — Vickers in the Klemperer recording sings the line with tenor sustenance, building the long phrases of the aria.

* **3:30–5:00.** The aria continues. The orchestra builds. Florestan's despair reaches its emotional peak.

* **5:00–7:00.** **The vision section.** Florestan sees, in his despair, the vision of his wife Leonore — _"Und spür' ich nicht linde, sanft säuselnde Luft? Und ist nicht mein Grab mir erhellt?"_ ("And do I not feel a gentle, softly breezing air? And is not my grave illuminated for me?"). The orchestra shifts to a brighter register (oboe leading); the harmonies brighten; Florestan rises into a hallucinatory ecstasy.

* **7:00–8:00.** The aria closes. Florestan collapses; the orchestra subsides; the scene ends in the cell's darkness restored.

About eight minutes total for Florestan's scena in Klemperer. The two-part structure (despair → vision) is the operatic emotional arc.

#### The closing "O namenlose Freude!" duet (Act II scene 2, after the minister's arrival)

* **0:00–0:30.** The orchestral introduction in F major. Listen for the _triumphant character_ : full orchestra, brass-prominent, F-major celebration. The dungeon has been left behind; the duet takes place in the prison courtyard with the minister and the freed prisoners.

* **0:30–1:30.** The duet begins. Leonore (Ludwig in the Klemperer recording, a mezzo-soprano singing the soprano role) enters first with _"O namenlose Freude!"_. Florestan (Vickers) answers. The two voices interweave.

* **1:30–3:00.** The duet builds. The orchestra adds full force; the two voices sing in close intervals (often a third apart, the operatic intimacy interval). Listen for the _embrace_ of the two voices in musical terms: they are singing about reunion, and the vocal lines themselves enact the reunion through their interlocking.

* **3:00–4:00.** The closing measures. The chorus and the rest of the cast join briefly for the final celebration; the duet closes with a operatic-celebratory cadence in F major.

About four minutes in Klemperer. The embrace of two voices is the operatic high point.

### 13.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### _Fidelio_ 's place in the German-opera tradition

The German opera tradition of the late 18th and early 19th centuries contains several major works: Mozart's _Die Entführung aus dem Serail_ (1782) and _Die Zauberflöte_ (1791); Beethoven's _Fidelio_ (1805/06/14); Carl Maria von Weber's _Der Freischütz_ (1821); Wagner's _Der fliegende Holländer_ (1843) and the later works.

_Fidelio_ sits between Mozart's _Die Zauberflöte_ and Weber's _Der Freischütz_ — late-Classical to early-Romantic German opera, with the inheritance of the _Singspiel_ tradition (operas with spoken dialogue rather than recitative). _Fidelio_ uses spoken dialogue between musical numbers, not sung recitative; this is the German-opera convention of the period, distinct from the Italian-opera convention.

The work's themes — political imprisonment, the wife's heroic rescue of her husband, the noble minister who arrives to liberate the prisoners — fit the late-Enlightenment political-humanist tradition. The libretto is derived (through several intermediate adaptations) from a French original by Jean-Nicolas Bouilly (1798), based on a real story from the Reign of Terror in France. The work's sympathies are clear: political prisoners are sympathetic, tyranny is bad, the noble idealist's rescue of her unjustly-imprisoned husband is celebrated. The work is the operatic statement of liberal-humanist politics in the early 19th century.

#### The three versions

**1805 version.** Three acts. Premiered 20 November 1805 at the Theater an der Wien, a week after Napoleon's French troops had occupied Vienna on 13 November. The audience was largely French officers; most of Beethoven's Viennese supporters had fled the city. The opera was not well-received. The libretto was considered weak; the structure was considered unwieldy; the dramatic pacing was considered slow. The work closed after three performances.

**1806 version.** Two acts (consolidated from three). Premiered 29 March 1806. The libretto was revised by Stephan von Breuning. The structure was tightened. The work was received better than the 1805 version but still did not succeed. Beethoven, frustrated with the Vienna theater management's demands and his own dissatisfaction with the work, withdrew the opera and set it aside for eight years.

**1814 version.** Two acts. Premiered 23 May 1814. The libretto was extensively revised by Georg Friedrich Treitschke. Beethoven recomposed substantial portions of the music, removed some arias, added others, and replaced the overture with the final E-major _Fidelio_ Overture. The work was a success — Vienna in 1814 was post-Napoleonic, ready for the political-liberation themes, and the revised work was dramatically tighter. By 1820 it was being performed across the German-speaking world. By 1850 it was in the international operatic canon.

The three-version history shows Beethoven's persistence with a work he was not satisfied with but knew contained something essential. Most composers, after two failed productions, would have abandoned the work. Beethoven came back to it twelve years after the first attempt and got it right.

#### The humanist register

The _Fidelio_ score has passages of remarkable humanist statement. The Prisoners' Chorus is the quiet center. The closing scene's "Heil sei dem Tag!" ("Hail the day!") is the loud celebration. Florestan's Aria's vision section is the private prophecy of liberation. The Leonore-Florestan reunion duet is the embrace.

The work's theme: _fidelity_ (Leonore's fidelity to her imprisoned husband; the German word _Treue_) and _freedom_ (the prisoners' freedom; the noble minister's liberation; the broader Enlightenment-humanist freedom). The two themes are linked: fidelity sustains the love that motivates Leonore's actions; freedom is what those actions ultimately achieve. The work is the operatic-humanist statement.

The 1989 Berlin Wall fall produced a _Fidelio_ moment: at the Berlin State Opera's 21 November 1989 performance — the first opera performance after the Wall fell on 9 November — the audience reportedly stood and applauded for ten minutes after the Prisoners' Chorus. The connection between the opera's political-liberation themes and actual political-liberation moments has been repeated several times in the work's history.

#### Definition: The German operatic _Singspiel_ tradition

_Singspiel_ (literally "sing-play") is the German operatic genre of the 18th and early 19th centuries that uses _spoken dialogue_ between musical numbers, rather than sung recitative. The genre is the German-speaking world's reply to Italian opera (which used sung recitative throughout).

Documented Singspiele:

* Mozart's _Die Entführung aus dem Serail_ (1782) and _Die Zauberflöte_ (1791)
* Beethoven's _Fidelio_ (1805/06/14)
* Weber's _Der Freischütz_ (1821)
* Lortzing's various works
* Wagner's early _Der fliegende Holländer_ (1843) is in transition — partly Singspiel, partly through-composed

_Fidelio_ 's spoken dialogue (mostly between Marzelline, Jaquino, Rocco, and the other secondary characters) provides the dramatic-narrative function that Italian operas would handle in recitative. The musical numbers (arias, duets, choruses, the orchestral finales) are where the expressive intensity is concentrated.

The Singspiel tradition would be abandoned by mid-19th-century German opera. Wagner's mature operas (_Tannhäuser_ 1845, _Lohengrin_ 1850, the _Ring_ cycle 1853–74) are through-composed without spoken dialogue. The Singspiel as a genre effectively ended around 1850.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume _Fidelio_ is a _minor_ Beethoven work — a curiosity from the heroic period that did not match the symphonies. This is wrong. _Fidelio_ is one of the major Beethoven works and one of the great early-19th-century operas. The work has been performed continuously since 1814, over two hundred years of productions, and is in the standard operatic repertoire of every major opera house.

The misconception comes partly from the work's _single-opera_ status in Beethoven's catalogue. Wagner wrote ten operas; Verdi wrote about twenty-five; Mozart wrote twenty-two. Beethoven wrote one. The single work seems quantitatively minor against the opera catalogues of other composers. But _quality_ is substantial: _Fidelio_ is the equal of any single Mozart or Weber opera. The misconception flatters quantity over quality.

The other source of the misconception is the compositional difficulty of opera-writing for Beethoven. He never wrote another opera (he sketched several but completed none). The single completed opera was the great labor — twelve years of work, three versions, four overtures. The labor is not a sign of the work being minor; it is a sign of the work being hard-won.

### 13.4 The Stories

#### The 1805 premiere and the Napoleonic context

Vienna fell to Napoleon's army on 13 November 1805 (the Battle of Ulm of October 1805 had broken Austrian resistance). The French occupation lasted about a month before Napoleon's withdrawal after Austerlitz (2 December 1805). During the occupation, most of Vienna's Austrian aristocracy and bourgeoisie had fled the city for the safer countryside; the urban population was reduced; theaters operated on reduced schedules to whatever audience remained.

_Fidelio_ (then titled _Leonore_) premiered on 20 November 1805 — a week after the French occupation of Vienna began on 13 November. The audience was largely French officers and the small remaining Austrian population. Beethoven's Vienna supporters were not in the audience. The reception was lukewarm; one French officer in the audience reportedly remarked that the work was too long. The opera ran for three performances before being withdrawn.

The 1805 premiere is one of the most-difficult opening nights in operatic history. The work itself was not really at fault — the audience was wrong for it, the political circumstances were wrong, the opera-house management was distracted by the occupation. Beethoven himself blamed the Vienna theater management (in subsequent correspondence) for failing to support the work properly.

#### The 1814 revision and Treitschke

By 1814 the political context had changed completely. Napoleon's empire had collapsed (the 1812 Russian campaign disaster, the 1813 Leipzig defeat, the 1814 abdication). Vienna was the host of the Congress of Vienna, which would shortly redraw the European map. The post-Napoleonic Vienna was ready for an opera whose themes were political imprisonment and the eventual liberation of political prisoners.

Georg Friedrich Treitschke (1776–1842) was a German poet and theatrical writer who had been brought in by the Kärntnertortheater management to help revise the libretto for the 1814 revival. Treitschke made substantial changes:

* Replaced some weak arias and duets with stronger texts
* Tightened the dramatic pacing
* Removed the "graveyard digging" scene (in which Florestan and Rocco prepared the grave for Florestan's planned murder by Pizarro)
* Reshaped the final scene
* Replaced the libretto for several major numbers

Beethoven responded to Treitschke's revisions by recomposing substantial portions of the music. The chronology: in February–April 1814 Beethoven worked intensely on the revision, often in collaboration with Treitschke in Vienna. The new _Fidelio_ Overture was composed at the last minute (reportedly not finished by the dress rehearsal, with the orchestra playing the old _Leonore_ Overture No. 2 at the dress rehearsal).

The 23 May 1814 premiere was a success. The work was performed twenty-one times in its first season at the Kärntnertortheater. By 1820 it was in the major German-speaking opera houses. By 1850 it was international.

#### Florestan and the "political prisoner" type

The character of Florestan — a man imprisoned for his political conscience, held in a dungeon by a tyrannical governor, rescued by his wife — is one of the great political-prisoner figures in 19th-century opera. The character has historical roots in the French Revolution (the original Bouilly libretto of 1798 was based on a real episode from the Reign of Terror, in which a French aristocratic woman had rescued her unjustly-imprisoned husband by disguising herself as a man).

The type — the political prisoner whose ideals make him an enemy of the state — would be repeated throughout 19th-century opera. Verdi's _Don Carlos_ (1867) and _Otello_ (1887) have related types. Wagner's _Tannhäuser_ (1845) has a different but related type. The operatic-political tradition descends from _Fidelio_.

In the 20th century, _Fidelio_ became a vehicle for political-protest performances. The 1989 Berlin Wall fall is the most-famous example. The 1955 Vienna State Opera reopening after WWII (the first major operatic event in postwar Vienna) was a _Fidelio_ premiere with political-emotional weight. The 1989 Leipzig opera-house production was performed weekly through the late-1989 East German political upheaval. The work has political-emotional uses.

#### Three readings of _Fidelio_

**Otto Klemperer / Philharmonia / Christa Ludwig (Leonore) / Jon Vickers (Florestan) / Walter Berry (Pizarro), 1962 (EMI).** Klemperer's _Fidelio_ is the model classical-modernist reading. Slow tempi, weighted gravitas, Christa Ludwig as one of the great Leonores of the 20th century (she sang the role of Florestan's wife with the heroic-soprano character Beethoven had asked for). Jon Vickers's Florestan is one of the great post-war tenor performances. The recording sound is the late EMI Kingsway Hall sound — warm, full, with choral and orchestral presence.

**Leonard Bernstein / Vienna State Opera / Vienna Philharmonic / Gundula Janowitz (Leonore) / René Kollo (Florestan), 1978 (DG).** Bernstein's _Fidelio_ is the model expressive-Romantic reading. Faster tempi than Klemperer, with Bernstein flexibility of pulse and dynamic. Janowitz's Leonore is the lyric-soprano interpretation (Janowitz was lighter than Ludwig, more lyrical, less heroic). Kollo's Florestan is the modern-tenor interpretation.

**Nikolaus Harnoncourt / Chamber Orchestra of Europe / Charlotte Margiono (Leonore) / Peter Seiffert (Florestan), 1993 (Teldec).** Harnoncourt's HIP-influenced _Fidelio_ uses period-informed playing on modern instruments. Faster tempi than either Klemperer or Bernstein; sharper articulation; more transparent orchestral textures. The reading is one of the most-important late-20th-century _Fidelio_ recordings, bringing period-performance principles to a work that has traditionally been performed in the classical-modernist register.

#### Cross-piece links

* _Fidelio_ 's choral writing (Prisoners' Chorus, closing scene) connects directly to **Symphony 9's choral finale** (Chapter 18). The continuity between the two works is the vocal-orchestral expressive register that Beethoven was developing across this period.
* _Fidelio_ 's political-humanist themes connect to **Symphony 5's per aspera ad astra arc** (Chapter 9) — both are narratives of darkness-to-light, but _Fidelio_ makes the political content explicit while Symphony 5 keeps it abstract.
* Florestan's Aria's two-part recitative-and-aria structure connects to **the operatic scenas of Mozart and Beethoven's other concert arias** — _Ah, perfido!_ Op. 65 (1796) is the Beethoven precedent in the same vocal-orchestral register.
* The humanist-political register connects to **Schiller's _An die Freude_ text** (1785), which Beethoven would set in the Ninth Symphony finale (Chapter 18).

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the four-overture history of Fidelio — the Leonore Overtures Nos. 1, 2, 3 and the final Fidelio Overture — and the Mahler tradition of inserting the Leonore Overture No. 3 between scenes of Act II. The episode is one of the most-unusual operatic compositional histories._
* _Tell the companion which of the three anchor passages (Prisoners' Chorus, Florestan's Aria, closing duet) felt most revolutionary to you, and ask her about the post-WWII and post-Wall Fidelio performances (Vienna 1955, Berlin 1989) and how the work has functioned as a political-emotional vehicle._
* _Ask the companion about the 1805 premiere context — the French occupation, the small Vienna audience, the failed first run — and how the twelve-year journey to the 1814 final version is one of the most-persistent examples of compositional revision in operatic history._
* _Bring up the Beethoven attempts at other operas with the companion — the sketches for Macbeth (mentioned in Chapter 12 in connection with the Ghost Trio slow movement), the sketches for various other libretti — and why he never completed another opera after Fidelio. The compositional difficulty of opera-writing for Beethoven is one of the features of his catalogue._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The pianissimo opening of the Prisoners' Chorus — try to hear how soft Beethoven asks the chorus to begin. The quiet revolutionary character depends on the soft opening.
* The recitative-to-aria transition in Florestan's scena (around 2:00 in the Klemperer recording) — listen for the two-part structure (despair → vision) and the orchestral brightening at the vision section.
* The vocal interweaving in the closing duet — Leonore and Florestan singing in close intervals (often a third apart). The vocal-musical embrace is the operatic high point.
* The orchestral colors throughout — Beethoven uses unusual woodwind voicings (low oboe, contrabassoon) in the dungeon scenes; brass and full orchestra in the celebrations. The orchestrational range is one of the work's signatures.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further-listening recommendation, listen to **Beethoven's concert aria "Ah! perfido," Op. 65** (1796) — the Beethoven scena that anticipates _Fidelio_ 's operatic writing by ten years. The recommended recording is **Lorraine Hunt Lieberson / Boston Symphony Chamber Players, 2003 (Avie)**. The work is short (about thirteen minutes) and operates in the same recitative-and-aria vocal-orchestral register as Florestan's Aria.

### Chapter Summary

_Fidelio_ (Op. 72), composed in three versions across nine years (1805 / 1806 / 1814), is Beethoven's only completed opera and one of the great early-19th-century German operas. The work's themes, political imprisonment, fidelity (_Treue_), liberation, fit the late-Enlightenment humanist tradition.

The three anchor passages: the Act I Prisoners' Chorus _"O welche Lust, in freier Luft"_ (the finale of Act I in the 1814 two-act version — the quiet revolutionary statement); Florestan's Act II Aria _"Gott! Welch Dunkel hier!"_ with its two-part recitative-and-aria structure (despair → vision); and the closing duet _"O namenlose Freude!"_ (the operatic embrace of Leonore and Florestan).

You met the four-overture history (Leonore Nos. 1, 2, 3 and the final Fidelio Overture in E major) and the Mahler-tradition insertion of Leonore Overture No. 3 between scenes of Act II.

You met the three-version compositional history — the failed 1805 premiere during the Napoleonic occupation, the failed 1806 revision, the successful 1814 final version with Treitschke's libretto revision and Beethoven's substantial recomposition.

You met the German Singspiel tradition (spoken dialogue between musical numbers) and _Fidelio_ 's place in it.

You met the humanist-political afterlife — the 1955 Vienna State Opera reopening, the 1989 Berlin Wall fall performances, the work as vehicle for political-emotional response.

You met three readings — Klemperer 1962 (classical-modernist with Ludwig and Vickers), Bernstein 1978 (Romantic-expressive with Janowitz and Kollo), Harnoncourt 1993 (HIP-informed) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you out of the heroic middle period entirely and into the 1815–1820 crisis years. The productivity slump, the Karl-custody battle, the financial pressures, the deepening deafness, and the Op. 90 sonata that marks the transition into the late style.

## Chapter 14: 1815–1820 — The Crisis Years

Between the completion of Symphony 8 in October 1812 and the start of work on the Op. 101 piano sonata in late 1816, Beethoven's compositional productivity slowed substantially. After the 1815 completion of the cello sonatas Op. 102 — the transitional work, the slowdown deepened. The five years from 1815 to 1820 are in modern Beethoven scholarship as the _crisis years_. The label is in Lewis Lockwood's _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ and in Maynard Solomon's _Beethoven_ ; both biographers treat the period as a distinct phase between the heroic middle period and the late style proper.

Compositional output during 1815–1820 includes: the Op. 90 piano sonata in E minor (1814); the Op. 96 violin sonata (1812, already discussed in Chapter 12); the Op. 102 cello sonatas (1815, mentioned in Chapter 12); the song cycle _An die ferne Geliebte_ Op. 98 (1816, the first true song cycle in the German Lied tradition); the Op. 101 piano sonata in A major (1816, the threshold of the late style); a few smaller works (the WoO 137 Polonaise, a few canon-songs, the various Folk Song settings for George Thomson). The total output for these five years is substantially smaller than for the equivalent five-year stretches before and after.

The chapter's anchor is the Op. 90 piano sonata, the small, two-movement sonata that marks the transition. The Op. 90 is not the most-famous Beethoven sonata, nor the most-virtuosic, nor the longest. It is the quiet hinge work — written in 1814 as Beethoven was beginning to step away from the heroic-period public-performer career, and pointing forward to the introspective compositional style of the late period.

### 14.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — the Karl custody battle

The core of Beethoven's 1815–1820 difficulties was the Karl custody battle. His brother Caspar Carl van Beethoven died on 15 November 1815 of tuberculosis. Caspar Carl had a son, Karl van Beethoven, then nine years old. Caspar Carl's will named his wife Johanna and his brother Ludwig as joint guardians of Karl.

Beethoven's response: he was determined to remove Karl entirely from Johanna's custody and to raise the boy himself. Beethoven's case against Johanna was based on accusations of moral unfitness — accusations that were not entirely fair. Johanna had been convicted in 1811 of embezzlement (the case is real but the moral weight Beethoven attached to it was substantial). Beethoven petitioned the Vienna Imperial Court of Appeals, the _Landrechte_ , the court for nobility, to have sole guardianship.

The case lasted from 1815 to 1820. It moved through multiple jurisdictions. It involved a question about Beethoven's social rank (the Vienna court had initially treated the _van_ in his name as equivalent to the German _von_ , which would denote nobility; in fact the _van_ is just the Dutch preposition meaning _of_ , with no aristocratic implication. When this was discovered, the case was transferred from the noble court — the _Landrechte_ — to the commoner's court, the _Magistrat_). The case involved competing claims for Karl's welfare (Beethoven's competence as a guardian was repeatedly challenged by Johanna's lawyers; the testimony from Karl himself, as he grew older, was mixed). The case finally concluded in 1820 with Beethoven granted sole guardianship, but the victory came after five years of legal expense, public scandal, and intense emotional drain.

The Karl custody battle is the single largest external pressure on Beethoven's compositional life during these years. The correspondence from this period is full of references to lawyers, courts, financial difficulties from legal costs, and arguments with Johanna. The Beethoven biographer Maynard Solomon treats the custody battle as the central biographical fact of these years; Lewis Lockwood treats it as one of several factors.

Karl himself, at the center of this battle, became Beethoven's project. The boy was sent to various schools and tutors; Beethoven supervised his education obsessively; the relationship between uncle and nephew became, by the late 1810s, the central emotional relationship of Beethoven's life. The relationship would culminate in Karl's suicide attempt in July 1826 (an attempt that he survived but that Beethoven's last year was largely shaped by). The boy's later life was difficult; he ended up in modest military service and lived until 1858.

The Karl custody battle is not a story about a sonata. It is, however, the background against which the Op. 90 sonata was composed — in 1814, just before the custody battle began, with Caspar Carl already ill. The work was composed in a period when Beethoven knew large biographical pressures were coming. We listen to it in that light.

#### What to notice

Three things in the Op. 90 first movement.

  1. **The opening four-note phrase.** The movement opens with a short, somewhat tentative phrase in E minor — four notes in the right hand, with a brief left-hand response. Listen for the _fragmentation_ : this is not the long-breathed lyrical opening of the _Archduke_ or the dramatic chordal opening of the _Eroica_ ; this is a short, broken phrase that suggests _thinking aloud_ rather than _announcing_.

  2. **The two-movement structure.** The Op. 90 has only two movements (most Classical-period sonatas have three or four). The first movement is in E minor (the dramatic key); the second is in E major (the relaxed major-key counterpart). The two-movement structure is the structural innovation of the work — a streamlined sonata that anticipates the more flexible structures of the late style.

  3. **The introspective character.** The whole movement (about five minutes) maintains a introspective register. There are no large dramatic gestures, no virtuoso displays, no heroic climaxes. The dynamic stays mostly soft to moderate; the textures are economical; the harmonies move with restraint. Listen for the _quietness_ of the work's expressive register; this is a different Beethoven from the _Appassionata_ or even the _Archduke_.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the Piano Sonata No. 27 in E minor, Op. 90 in August 1814 (the compositional period was about four weeks), dedicated it to Count Moritz Lichnowsky (the younger brother of Beethoven's old patron Prince Karl Lichnowsky), and published it in 1815.

### 14.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into Kempff 1965.

* **0:00–0:15.** The opening four-note phrase. Listen for the _tentativeness_ : the phrase begins, pauses, continues, pauses. The character is _spoken_ rather than _declarative_. The marking is _Mit Lebhaftigkeit und durchaus mit Empfindung und Ausdruck_ — "with liveliness and throughout with feeling and expression," in German rather than the standard Italian tempo markings. The German markings are a Beethoven late-style preference; he started using German tempo markings here and would continue in Op. 101 and the late quartets.

* **0:15–0:45.** The phrase is extended. The harmonies move; the texture builds slightly. Notice the _economy_ : Beethoven uses few notes per measure, with the silence between notes contributing as much as the notes themselves.

* **0:45–1:30.** A more lyrical second phrase. The second theme arrives in B minor (the standard dominant-minor relationship for an E-minor first movement; Beethoven keeps the dramatic-minor register rather than relaxing into major). Listen for the _singing_ quality of the second theme: more lyrical than the first, but still in introspective register.

* **1:30–2:30.** Development of the themes. Brief; concise; the developmental work happens without the dramatic stress-testing of the heroic-period sonatas. Listen for the _quietness_ of the development.

* **2:30–3:30.** The recapitulation. The opening four-note phrase returns; the themes are walked through in the home key.

* **3:30–4:30.** Continued recapitulation; the closing material.

* **4:30–5:00.** The closing measures. The movement ends quietly in E minor, with a soft cadence and a sustained final chord.

About five minutes in Kempff. The introspective character is sustained throughout. The two-movement structure (we are listening only to movement 1; movement 2 in E major follows attacca) is the innovation.

For interest: the second movement (Nicht zu geschwind und sehr singbar vorzutragen — "Not too fast and very singing-style to be played"), about eight minutes long, is a long lyrical rondo in E major. Beethoven uses the German tempo marking again, and the singing quality of the second movement is anticipating the late-style melodic writing (particularly the lyrical themes of Op. 101 and the slow movements of the late quartets). Schindler's (now-discredited) story is that Beethoven told Count Moritz Lichnowsky that the second movement depicted "_conversation with the beloved_ " (referring to Lichnowsky's wife, whom he had recently married). The story is one of the documented Schindler embroideries that modern scholars treat with caution.

### 14.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### What characterizes the crisis years compositionally

The compositional features of Beethoven's 1815–1820 works include:

**Smaller scale.** The Op. 90 is two movements instead of three or four. The cello sonatas Op. 102 are shorter than the earlier Op. 69 cello sonata. The _An die ferne Geliebte_ song cycle Op. 98 (1816) is concise. The works are more compact than the heroic-period works.

**More introspective character.** The dramatic surface of the heroic-period works gives way to a more meditative register. The dynamics stay more often in the soft-to-moderate range. The textures are economical. The harmonic motion is restrained but more inventive.

**More contrapuntal interest.** Beethoven was deeply studying Bach and Handel during this period; the influence shows up as more counterpoint, more fugal writing (Op. 102 No. 2 ends with a fugue; Op. 101's finale is fugue-influenced; Op. 106's finale will be a full fugue). The contrapuntal interest is the late-style feature beginning to emerge.

**Use of German tempo markings.** The standard Italian tempo markings (Allegro, Andante, Vivace) are gradually replaced by German markings (_Mit Lebhaftigkeit_ , _Nicht zu geschwind_). The change is Beethoven's late-style preference and is one of the hallmarks of the late catalogue.

**Compositional pause for self-study.** The Beethoven correspondence and conversation books show extensive engagement with Bach, Handel, and Palestrina during this period. Beethoven was, in effect, retraining himself in the older counterpoint and modal traditions before producing the late-style works.

These five features together are the compositional signature of the crisis years. They are not signs of _decline_ (in the sense of failing powers) but of _transition_ (Beethoven shifting his compositional priorities).

#### The _An die ferne Geliebte_ — paragraph

The song cycle _An die ferne Geliebte_ ("To the Distant Beloved"), Op. 98, was composed in April 1816 — in the middle of the crisis years. The work is the first true song cycle in the German Lied tradition (preceding Schubert's _Die schöne Müllerin_ of 1823 by seven years).

The work sets six poems by Alois Jeitteles, a young Vienna doctor and amateur poet. The six songs are linked: each song flows into the next without break (the attacca structure), and the cycle ends with a return of the opening melody, framing the whole sequence. The form is anticipating Schubert's and Schumann's later song cycles.

The text is about a man's longing for a distant beloved — the lover separated from his beloved by physical distance, sending songs to her as a vehicle of his love. The connection to Beethoven's own biography (the _Immortal Beloved_ letter of 1812 is from four years earlier) has been argued by some scholars (Solomon's _Beethoven_) and treated cautiously by others (Lockwood). The poems are not directly autobiographical but the _register_ , longing for a distant beloved, fits Beethoven's romantic biography of this period.

The recommended recording is **Dietrich Fischer-Dieskau / Jörg Demus, 1966 (Deutsche Grammophon)** or **Matthias Goerne / Eric Schneider, 2010 (Harmonia Mundi)**. The cycle runs about fifteen minutes.

#### Wellington's Victory revisited

We mentioned _Wellington's Victory_ Op. 91 (1813) briefly in Chapter 11. The work was Beethoven's most-popular composition in his lifetime — composed for Mälzel's panharmonicon, then transcribed for orchestra, depicting the British victory over the French at Vitoria in June 1813. The premiere on 8 December 1813 (the same concert as the Symphony 7 premiere) was a enormous success.

Beethoven himself, in later years, called _Wellington's Victory_ _eine Dummheit_ — "a stupid thing." He was embarrassed by its popularity. The financial success of the work, however, was substantial — the published score sold widely in 1813–14, and the work was performed across Europe in the celebratory years after Napoleon's fall.

The work is now mostly performed as a historical curiosity. The orchestral effects (cannons, musket-fire, battlefield chaos) are interesting; the compositional quality is below Beethoven's standard. The work is a piece of _occasional music_ — composed for a specific political moment, useful while the moment lasted, less compelling once the moment passed. We do not anchor it.

But the work's massive popularity in 1813–14 is the context for the _Fidelio_ revival of 1814 (Chapter 13). Beethoven was, in the post-Napoleonic celebratory mood, suddenly very famous and very popular as a composer of political-celebratory works. _Fidelio_ fit this mood. _Wellington's Victory_ fit this mood. The 1814 success of both works helped finance the years immediately before the crisis years began.

#### The Maelzel collaboration revisited (and ended)

The Mälzel-Beethoven relationship had soured by 1814. The cause was a dispute over _Wellington's Victory_ — Mälzel claimed (in subsequent legal proceedings) that the work had been commissioned by him for his panharmonicon, that Beethoven had agreed to share the publishing rights with him, and that Beethoven had then violated the agreement by publishing the work himself. Beethoven claimed (in his own legal filings) that the original collaboration agreement did not extend to the orchestral version, that Mälzel was attempting to claim rights he had no legitimate basis for.

The dispute went to a Vienna court in 1814. The case was eventually settled out of court; both parties retained some rights. The friendship was damaged but not destroyed (the two men met again in later years and were on cordial terms by the 1820s). The Mälzel-Beethoven episode is the end of Beethoven's collaboration with mechanical-instrument inventors. After this, Beethoven's engagement with new musical technology was limited to the metronome (which he began using sporadically in his marking of tempos from 1817 onward).

#### Definition: The transitional sonata

The Op. 90 is a _transitional_ sonata in the conventional periodization of Beethoven's piano works. The early sonatas (Op. 2 through Op. 22, roughly 1795–1801) are in the early-Vienna register. The middle-period sonatas (Op. 26 through Op. 81a, roughly 1801–1810) are in the heroic-virtuoso register. The late sonatas (Op. 101 through Op. 111, 1816–1822) are in the introspective late-style register.

The Op. 90 (1814) sits between the heroic middle and the late style. It has features of both. The dramatic E-minor first movement still uses heroic-period harmonic and structural devices. The lyrical E-major second movement and the German tempo markings already point toward the late style. The work is the transitional sonata.

The transitional position is not just a chronological matter. The Op. 90 is shorter and more compact than the middle-period sonatas; it is also more _experimental_ in its two-movement structure. Beethoven was working out the late-style compositional priorities through small-scale experiments before committing to the larger late-style works (Op. 101, Op. 106). The Op. 90 is one of the experiments.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the compositional slowdown of 1815–1820 means Beethoven was _failing_ — losing his powers, no longer able to produce work at the heroic-period rate. This is wrong. The slowdown is a _strategic redirection_ : Beethoven was retraining himself in older counterpoint and modal traditions, was working out new compositional approaches, and was producing fewer but more-experimental works. The 1816 Op. 101 sonata is a major work; the _An die ferne Geliebte_ is a major work; the Op. 102 cello sonatas are major works. The total output is smaller, but the individual works are increasingly ambitious.

The misconception comes partly from biographers who treated the 1815–1820 period as a _crisis_ in the sense of _artistic decline_. Modern Beethoven scholars (Lockwood, Solomon) treat the period as a _crisis_ in the sense of _biographical pressure_ (Karl custody, financial difficulty, deafness deepening) but explicitly _not_ as artistic decline. The late style emerges from this period as one of the great late styles in compositional history.

### 14.4 The Stories

#### The 1815–1820 financial pressures

Beethoven's financial situation in the crisis years was complicated. The 1809 annuity from Lobkowicz, Kinsky, and Rudolph (4,000 florins per year for life) had been the financial foundation of his middle-period work. The annuity continued through the 1810s but its real value declined.

The reasons:

**Austrian currency collapse 1811.** The Austrian Empire declared a currency devaluation in February 1811, the _Finanzpatent_ of 20 February 1811, that effectively halved the value of the Austrian florin against the new _Wiener Währung_ paper currency. Beethoven's annuity, denominated in the old currency, was renegotiated downward.

**Patron deaths.** Prince Kinsky died in November 1812 from a riding accident. His estate was contested; payments to Beethoven were interrupted. Prince Lobkowicz had financial difficulties from 1813 onward (he had spent enormously on his musical establishment and his political activities; his estate was put in court-supervised receivership by 1814). Lobkowicz died in December 1816. The Kinsky and Lobkowicz contributions to the annuity were renegotiated with the heirs but were never as reliable as the original arrangement.

**Karl custody legal costs.** The legal costs of the Karl custody battle were substantial. Beethoven paid lawyers, court fees, and the expenses of Karl's various schools and tutors. The costs were a continuous drain on his finances throughout 1815–1820.

The combined effect: Beethoven's income, in real terms, was lower in the late 1810s than in the early 1810s, even though his annuity was nominally still in force. The response was to take on more commissions (the song-cycle for the publisher Steiner; the various Folk Song settings for Thomson; eventually the _Diabelli Variations_ and the late piano sonatas as commissioned works). The composer who had operated from a stable annuity-and-patronage base now had to take on more contract work.

#### Deafness deepening

The deafness's progression continued through the crisis years. By 1815, Beethoven had stopped public performance (the 1814 _Archduke_ premiere was the last). By 1818, the conversation books began — visitors to Beethoven would write their side of the conversation in a notebook, and Beethoven would speak or write his side. The conversation books have survived in substantial number (about 137 books, though some have been destroyed or forged by Schindler; the surviving books are the day-to-day record of Beethoven's late-period social life).

The 1818 onset of the conversation books is the marker that the deafness had become _substantially severe_ — no longer just difficult, but functionally impeding normal spoken communication. The Beethoven was still able to hear _some_ sound at this point (low registers were more accessible than high; loud sounds were more accessible than soft), but normal conversational hearing was no longer available.

The effect on composition: Beethoven worked increasingly from the _inner ear_ — composing from imagined sound rather than from playing-and-checking. The sketches from this period are more extensive and more carefully revised; Beethoven was using the compositional process itself to substitute for the playing-and-checking he could no longer reliably do.

#### Three readings of the Op. 90

**Wilhelm Kempff, 1965 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Kempff's 1964–65 complete sonata cycle includes the Op. 90 in his characteristic register: lyrical, structurally clear, with the German tempo markings respected (Kempff plays the first movement with feeling-and-expression rather than formal-fast tempo; he plays the second movement with singing-quality). The reading is the model classical-modernist Op. 90.

**Alfred Brendel, 1996 (Philips).** Brendel's 1990s complete sonata cycle is the late-Brendel reference. His Op. 90 takes the first movement slightly slower than Kempff's (about five and a half minutes), with more time given to the introspective character. The second movement is unhurried. Brendel's reading is the model late-20th-century interpretive Op. 90.

**Maurizio Pollini, 2007 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Pollini's late-period sonata cycle (recorded across the 2000s) includes the Op. 90 in the Pollini register: structurally clear, technically precise, with the developmental work taken at exact tempo. The reading is the model modern reference Op. 90.

#### Cross-piece links

* The Op. 90's two-movement structure is the experimental form that Beethoven would extend in **Op. 111** (the last sonata, also two movements; Chapter 16).
* The Op. 90's introspective register and German tempo markings connect to **Op. 101 (1816)** , the next sonata, also short, also experimental, the threshold of the late style proper. We will hear Op. 101 in paragraph form in Chapter 15.
* The _An die ferne Geliebte_ 's connected-song-cycle structure connects to **Schubert's _Die schöne Müllerin_ (1823)** and **Schumann's _Dichterliebe_ (1840)** — the descendants of Beethoven's Op. 98 in the Lied tradition.
* The crisis years' compositional features (smaller scale, introspective character, contrapuntal interest, German tempo markings) connect to the entire **late-style catalogue (Op. 101 onward; Chapters 15–20)**.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the Karl custody battle of 1815–1820 — what the legal facts were, why Beethoven pursued the case so persistently, and how the battle shaped his late-period biography. The conversation will run from the Caspar Carl death into the Karl suicide attempt of 1826._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the Op. 90 first movement felt most_ introspective _to you on the second listen, and ask her about the documented Schindler story of "conversation with the beloved" for the second movement, and how modern biographers treat the Schindler embroideries._
* _Ask the companion about the documented_ Wellington's Victory _episode and Beethoven's later disowning of the work, and how the massive popularity of an occasional work fits into the heroic-period catalogue._
* _Bring up the financial pressures of 1815–1820 with the companion — the Austrian currency devaluation of 1811, the Kinsky-and-Lobkowicz deaths, the legal costs of the custody battle — and how the combined pressures shaped the compositional slowdown._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The four-note opening at 0:00–0:15 — try to hear the _tentativeness_ of the phrase. Beethoven is thinking aloud rather than declaring.
* The German tempo marking — the score's instructions are in German rather than Italian. The late-style preference begins here.
* The transition between movements — the second movement follows attacca and provides the lyrical E-major counterpart to the first movement's introspective E minor.
* The economy of the texture — Beethoven uses few notes per measure compared with the heroic-period sonatas. The silences contribute as much as the notes.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Maynard Solomon,_Beethoven_ (Schirmer, 2nd ed 1998), the chapters on the years 1815–1820.** Solomon treats the period with the psychobiographical attention that Lockwood does not match. The Karl custody battle, the Beethoven-Johanna conflict, and the psychological texture of the crisis years are Solomon's specialty. Pair with Lewis Lockwood's chapters in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (2003) for the compositional-and-documentary context.

### Chapter Summary

The 1815–1820 crisis years were a period of substantial biographical pressure on Beethoven — the Karl custody battle (1815–1820), the financial pressures from the 1811 Austrian currency collapse and from patron deaths, the deepening deafness leading to the 1818 onset of the conversation books — and a corresponding compositional slowdown. The total compositional output for these five years is smaller than for the equivalent stretches before and after.

The Op. 90 piano sonata in E minor (composed August 1814, published 1815) is the transitional work that marks the shift. The two-movement structure, the introspective register, the German tempo markings, and the economical texture all anticipate the late style. The work was dedicated to Count Moritz Lichnowsky.

You met the features of the crisis-years compositional style: smaller scale, more introspective character, more contrapuntal interest, German tempo markings, compositional pause for self-study (Bach, Handel, Palestrina). These five features together are the foundation on which the late style proper (Op. 101 onward) would be built.

You met the _An die ferne Geliebte_ Op. 98 (1816) as the first true song cycle in the German Lied tradition, anticipating Schubert and Schumann.

You met the _Wellington's Victory_ Op. 91 (1813) — Beethoven's most-popular work in his lifetime, which Beethoven himself later disowned as _eine Dummheit_.

You met the Mälzel collaboration's end in the 1814 court dispute over _Wellington's Victory_ publishing rights.

You met three readings of the Op. 90 — Kempff 1965 (classical-modernist), Brendel 1996 (interpretive), Pollini 2007 (modern reference) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you into the late period proper. The Op. 101 (1816) is the threshold of the late style; the Op. 106 _Hammerklavier_ (1818) is the structural extreme. The crisis years have ended; the late style has begun.

* * *

## Chapter 15: Late Piano Sonatas, Part I — Op. 101 and Op. 106 "Hammerklavier"

After the quiet of the crisis years (Chapter 14), Beethoven re-emerged in 1816 with a new sonata: the Op. 101 in A major. The work was small in scale by middle-period standards (about twenty minutes total) but a major statement of new compositional direction. The Op. 101 opens with a slow, lyrical movement; concludes with a fugal finale; uses German tempo markings throughout; and operates in a register of _intimate seriousness_ very different from the _Appassionata_ or the _Waldstein_.

Two years later, in 1818, Beethoven completed the Op. 106 in B♭ major — the _Hammerklavier_ sonata. Where Op. 101 was small, Op. 106 was vast. The work runs about forty-five minutes — the longest piano sonata of its time. The first movement is a substantial Allegro; the second is a brief Scherzo; the third is an Adagio sostenuto of approximately eighteen minutes (one of the longest piano slow movements ever written); the fourth is a relentless three-voice fugue of unprecedented difficulty.

This chapter anchors on the Op. 106's third movement — the spiritual center of the work and, by general agreement, one of the great late-style Beethoven movements. We will also walk Op. 101 in paragraph-level overview. Charles Rosen's _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas: A Short Companion_ (2002) is the source-of-record voice for the late sonatas throughout.

### 15.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — "_Hammerklavier_ " the word

The word _Hammerklavier_ is German for "hammer-keyboard" — the German technical term for the piano (which has hammers that strike strings, as opposed to the older harpsichord, which has plucks that pluck strings). The word is Beethoven's own preferred term for the instrument; he used it on the title pages of both Op. 101 ("Sonate für das Hammer-Clavier") and Op. 106 ("Große Sonate für das Hammerklavier"). The word is therefore _not_ a nickname in the Cranz/Rellstab sense — it is Beethoven's own technical description.

The nickname _Hammerklavier_ attached specifically to Op. 106 (and not to Op. 101, despite Beethoven's identical use of the word) because of Op. 106's scale and difficulty. The work was so dramatically larger than other piano sonatas of its time that the word _Hammerklavier_ — which had been a generic term — became, by association, the specific identifier of Op. 106. The German-and-international musical world began calling Op. 106 _the Hammerklavier_ in the 1820s and 1830s.

The scale of Op. 106 has consequences. Carl Czerny — Beethoven's pupil from 1801 onward, by 1818 one of the leading Vienna pianists — reported in his memoirs that Beethoven himself said, when sending the Op. 106 manuscript to the publisher in 1819: _"Now you have a sonata that will keep pianists busy for the next fifty years."_ The quotation has been argued over (some scholars treat it as paraphrase rather than verbatim), but the prediction proved roughly accurate. Op. 106 was rarely performed in the first half of the 19th century. Liszt was one of the first major pianists to take it on (his performance of the work in Paris in 1836 was a major event). It entered the standard repertoire only gradually through the second half of the 19th century and into the 20th. Even today the work is one of the most-demanding piano sonatas in the canon, beyond the technical reach of most professional pianists.

The third-movement Adagio sostenuto is the spiritual center. The fourth-movement fugue is the technical and intellectual center. The two combined are the compositional summit of the middle-late style — what Beethoven could do at his most-ambitious in piano writing, in the same year he was beginning to sketch the Ninth Symphony and the _Diabelli Variations_.

#### What to notice

Three things in the Op. 106 Adagio sostenuto (third movement).

  1. **The opening F♯-minor descent.** The movement opens with a slow, dark F♯-minor descending figure in the right hand, with sustained chordal support in the left. Listen for the _darkness_ : the home key of Op. 106 is B♭ major, but the slow movement is in F♯ minor (the tritone away from the home key, the maximum harmonic distance), Beethoven is taking the listener into a different tonal world. The F♯-minor opening is the marker of the movement's spiritual register.

  2. **The long phrases.** The slow movement's phrases are _very long_ — sometimes ten or twelve measures of unbroken melodic line. Listen for the _breathing_ : the music sustains its lines beyond what is normal for piano writing, requiring the pianist to use the pedal-and-touch techniques to give the impression of sustained vocal singing. The long phrases are the spiritual register's structural feature.

  3. **The harmonic motion.** The Adagio sostenuto moves through remote keys with harmonic adventure. Beethoven uses chord progressions that are unusual by Classical standards — chromatic third-relationships, parallel keys, unprepared modulations. Listen for the _harmonic strangeness_ : the music does not follow standard tonic-dominant patterns. The harmonic motion is the late-style language emerging.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the Piano Sonata No. 29 in B♭ major, Op. 106 ("Hammerklavier") between November 1817 and April 1819, dedicated it to Archduke Rudolph for Rudolph's 1820 elevation to Archbishop of Olmütz (an event Beethoven missed by missing the composition deadline, but more on this in Chapter 19), and published it in September 1819.

### 15.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the movement once. Second listen, with timestamps into Pollini 1977.

* **0:00–0:30.** The opening F♯-minor descent. The right hand plays a slow descending figure (about six notes); the left hand sustains a chord underneath. Listen for the _darkness_ of the F♯-minor opening: the spiritual register is established immediately.

* **0:30–1:30.** The descending figure is extended into a longer phrase. The harmonies move; the texture remains thin (the economy of the late style). Pollini takes the opening slowly: the Pollini tempo is at the slow end of acceptable readings.

* **1:30–3:00.** The first theme proper. A more lyrical melodic line emerges in the right hand, with the chordal support continuing in the left. Listen for the _singing_ quality: the line sings even at slow tempo and low dynamic.

* **3:00–4:30.** Development of the first theme. The harmony moves through chromatic-third relationships; the texture thickens slightly; the dynamic builds.

* **4:30–6:00.** A more lyrical passage with a hymnal character. Listen for the _prayer-like_ register: the music sustains a meditative quality across long phrases.

* **6:00–8:00.** A second theme arrives. The texture changes: sometimes the right hand plays alone in a single line, sometimes with full chordal accompaniment. The dynamic varies more.

* **8:00–10:00.** Continued development. The music moves through remote keys. The slow tempo is maintained absolutely.

* **10:00–12:00.** A passage of harmonic adventure. Beethoven uses chord progressions that are unusual by Classical standards. Listen for the _harmonic strangeness_ : the music does not follow standard tonic-dominant patterns.

* **12:00–14:00.** The most-sustained lyrical passage of the movement. The first theme returns in a transformed register, with new countermelodies and new harmonic shadings.

* **14:00–16:00.** The slow recapitulation. The opening descending figure returns; the home key (F♯ minor) is restored. The themes are walked through in adapted form.

* **16:00–17:30.** The closing passage. The texture thins; the dynamic softens; the harmonies move toward closure.

* **17:30–18:00.** **The closing measures.** The final cadence is in F♯ major (rather than F♯ minor: the Picardy-third resolution to the major mode for the closing chord). The movement ends quietly with the major-mode chord sustained.

About eighteen minutes in Pollini. The slow movement is the longest piano slow movement Beethoven had composed. The spiritual register is sustained throughout. The harmonic adventure is the late-style language at substantial scale.

### 15.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Why the Op. 106 Adagio is the spiritual center

Charles Rosen's _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas: A Short Companion_ makes the case that the Op. 106 Adagio is the spiritual center of the work, and one of the great slow movements in Beethoven's catalogue. The argument rests on several features:

**Length.** The movement is approximately eighteen to twenty-one minutes in modern recordings (depending on the pianist's tempo choices). This is longer than any prior Beethoven slow movement, and longer than most slow movements in any classical work. The duration alone makes the movement function structurally as the _gravitational center_ of the four-movement work.

**Key relationship.** The movement is in F♯ minor — the tritone (six-half-step) interval away from the home key of B♭ major. This is the maximum harmonic distance from the home key. Beethoven places the slow movement in the most-remote key relationship available, then returns to the home key only in the fourth movement. The structural effect is a _spiritual journey_ from B♭ home territory (first movement) into F♯ remote territory (third movement) and back to B♭ (fourth movement).

**Harmonic adventure.** The movement uses chord progressions that are unusual by Classical standards. Beethoven uses chromatic-third relationships, parallel keys, unprepared modulations. The harmonic language is anticipating the late-Romantic vocabulary (Brahms, Wagner, Mahler).

**Spiritual register.** The dynamic stays soft for much of the movement (the _sempre pianissimo_ markings at several points). The textures are economical. The melodic lines are long-breathed. The character is _meditative_ , _prayer-like_ , _hymnal_. The movement is the prototype of Beethoven's late-style slow-movement writing — the same register that would appear in the Op. 132 _Heiliger Dankgesang_ and the Op. 130 _Cavatina_ (Chapter 20).

Rosen's reading is the modern critical consensus. The Op. 106 Adagio is the _spiritual center_ of the work in a way that goes beyond the work's mere internal architecture — the movement reaches for a kind of musical expression that the surrounding fast movements do not attempt.

#### Op. 101 — paragraph

The Op. 101 in A major, composed November 1816, is the threshold of the late style proper. The work has four movements:

  1. **Etwas lebhaft und mit der innigsten Empfindung** ("Somewhat lively and with the most heartfelt feeling") — a short opening movement (about four minutes) in slow-moderate tempo. The opening is lyrical, gentle, with German tempo marking.

  2. **Lebhaft, marschmäßig** ("Lively, march-like") — a march movement, about five minutes.

  3. **Langsam und sehnsuchtsvoll** ("Slow and full of longing") — a brief slow movement (about three minutes) that leads attacca into the finale.

  4. **Geschwinde, doch nicht zu sehr** ("Quickly, but not too much") — a fugue-influenced finale of about seven minutes.

The Op. 101 is the work where Beethoven's late style begins to crystallize. The contrapuntal interest (the finale is fugue-influenced; the third movement uses canon-like writing); the lyrical first movement; the German tempo markings; the introspective register — these are all the late-style features.

The recommended recording is **Maurizio Pollini, 1977 (Deutsche Grammophon)** or **Wilhelm Kempff, 1965 (Deutsche Grammophon)**.

#### The Op. 106 first-movement opening

The first movement of Op. 106 opens with two massive B♭-major chords in the left hand — like a smaller piano echo of the _Eroica_ 's two opening chords (Chapter 6). The two chords announce the home key with force. The first theme then arrives in the right hand: a striding, declamatory phrase that establishes the heroic-yet-late-style register.

The first movement (about eleven minutes) is one of the most-demanding piano movements in the repertoire. Beethoven uses a sonata-form structure but at the limits of what the form can hold — multiple themes, extensive development, a coda that functions as a second development. The work's compositional ambition is at the upper edge of what Beethoven attempted in his piano sonatas.

#### The Op. 106 finale — the fugue

The Op. 106's finale is a three-voice fugue in B♭ major. The fugue subject is long (about eight measures) and chromatically complex. Beethoven develops the subject through extensive contrapuntal techniques — inversion, augmentation, diminution, stretto. The movement runs about ten to twelve minutes and is among the most-difficult fugues ever written for piano.

The fugue is the technical and intellectual center of the work. It is the response to Beethoven's late-period Bach studies — the engagement with the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ that had begun in his Bonn years (Chapter 3) and was now at peak intensity. The Op. 106 fugue is the Beethoven-meets-Bach moment in the piano-sonata catalogue.

We will not anchor the fugue in this chapter; the Adagio is the chapter's anchor. But the fugue is the climax that the Adagio prepares — the late-style summit that the work as a whole reaches for.

#### Definition: The fugal finale (Thread B preview)

The fugal finale is a Beethoven late-style signature. Op. 110 (1821, Chapter 16) has a fugal finale. Op. 102 No. 2 (1815) had a fugal finale. The _Diabelli Variations_ (1819–23, Chapter 17) has a fugal Variation 32. The _Grosse Fuge_ Op. 133 (1825–26, Chapter 20) was the original fugal finale to Op. 130. The Op. 106 fugue is the first major fugal finale in the late-style catalogue.

The signature is part of Beethoven's late-period contrapuntal interest. Beethoven had studied Bach throughout his career (the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ references from Bonn through Vienna), but in the late period the contrapuntal interest intensified. The conversation books contain references to Beethoven's late-period studies of Palestrina (the 16th-century Italian master of vocal counterpoint), of Handel (whose oratorios Beethoven studied intensively), and of Bach again. The late-style counterpoint is the fruit of this intensified study.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the Op. 106's enormous scale and difficulty mean it is _unapproachable_ ; that the work is for specialist pianists and specialist listeners only. The misperception comes from the technical difficulty (which is real) and the total duration (about forty-five minutes, which is long). But the work's expressive content is approachable in the same way any major Beethoven work is approachable: the listener does not need to play the work to listen to it, and the Adagio sostenuto in particular is one of the most-sustained lyrical statements in any Beethoven slow movement.

The work _is_ difficult — for performers and for listeners new to late Beethoven. But difficult is not unapproachable. The Op. 106 Adagio rewards repeated listening at any level of musical knowledge; the spiritual register is available to any patient listener.

### 15.4 The Stories

#### The 1818 compositional context

Beethoven composed the Op. 106 between November 1817 and April 1819 — a eighteen-month period of intense compositional work. The work was composed at the end of the crisis years; the Karl custody battle was still ongoing (the case would conclude in 1820); the deafness was severe (the conversation books had just begun in 1818); the financial pressures continued.

During the same period, Beethoven was sketching the Ninth Symphony (the earliest Ninth sketches date from 1817–18) and the _Diabelli Variations_ (the invitation from Anton Diabelli had come in 1819). The Op. 106 was the major completed work of these eighteen months; the Ninth and _Diabelli_ would not be finished for another five and four years respectively. The compositional energy of the period was concentrated on Op. 106.

The dedication to Archduke Rudolph was for Rudolph's planned 1820 elevation to Archbishop of Olmütz. The sequence: Rudolph was named Archbishop in early 1820; the elevation ceremony was scheduled for 1820. Beethoven's Op. 106 was completed in time for the dedication (1819 publication); the _Missa Solemnis_ , also as composed for the elevation, was not finished by the 1820 deadline. The Missa Solemnis would not be completed until 1823, three years late, but Beethoven had at least managed to get the Op. 106 finished and dedicated to Rudolph by the appropriate date. The dedication to Rudolph is part of the Rudolph-Beethoven friendship-and-patronage relationship (Chapter 12).

#### The Op. 106 reception history

The Op. 106 was published in September 1819. The contemporary reception was _bewildered_. Reviewers found the work too long, too difficult, too harmonically adventurous. The _Allgemeine musikalische Zeitung_ review of 1820 described the work as _colossal_ but questioned whether it could be played and whether it should be listened to. Pianists in Vienna in 1819 reportedly considered the work unplayable.

The reception remained difficult through the 1820s and 1830s. Few pianists attempted the work. Beethoven himself did not publicly perform it (the deafness had ended his public-solo career by 1814; the work was beyond his physical capacity by 1819). The documented Czerny anecdote of "fifty years before pianists will catch up" was approximately accurate.

The turning point was **Liszt's 1836 Paris performance**. Liszt was twenty-four years old in 1836; he had been studying the Op. 106 for several years and had respect for the work as an intellectual and technical challenge. The Paris performance was a sensation. Liszt subsequently championed the work in performances across Europe through the 1840s and 1850s. By the late 19th century, Op. 106 had entered the standard repertoire — performed by Hans von Bülow, Eugen d'Albert, and other major late-19th-century pianists. By the 20th century the work was canonical: every major pianist's complete-sonata-cycle recording includes it.

The Liszt-revival pattern (Chapter 8 the Joachim-Mendelssohn revival of the Violin Concerto in 1844) is a common feature of Beethoven reception. Several major works (the Violin Concerto, the Op. 106, the late quartets, the _Grosse Fuge_ , the _Missa Solemnis_) had to be revived by major 19th-century performers a generation after their composition. The revivals are the moments when the works entered the canon.

#### Beethoven's Bach engagement

The Bach engagement that produced the Op. 106 fugue (and the other late fugues) has a long history in Beethoven's compositional biography. The early documentation: Christian Gottlob Neefe (Beethoven's Bonn teacher) introduced him to the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ in the 1780s. Beethoven reportedly memorized large portions of the WTC in his early teens. Throughout his Vienna career, Beethoven kept the WTC at his keyboard.

By the late 1810s, the Bach engagement intensified. The conversation books contain references to Beethoven's late-period purchases of Bach editions, to late-period discussions of Bach's harmonization techniques, to Bach-based exercises Beethoven was setting for his nephew Karl (in the brief period when Beethoven was teaching Karl piano himself). The late-period engagement was substantial.

The Op. 106 fugue is one fruit. The Op. 110 finale fugue is another. The _Grosse Fuge_ is another. The _Hammerklavier_ fugue specifically is drawing on Bach's _Art of Fugue_ (BWV 1080) and on the late-Bach contrapuntal techniques. The relationship is one of _engagement_ rather than _quotation_ : Beethoven does not quote Bach directly, but the contrapuntal vocabulary and the seriousness of the fugue's treatment derive from Beethoven's Bach studies.

#### Three readings of Op. 106

**Maurizio Pollini, 1977 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Pollini's 1977 _Hammerklavier_ is the model modern reading. Technically impeccable; structurally clear; the Adagio sostenuto at the slow end of acceptable tempi (about eighteen minutes); the fugue at extreme but controlled tempo. The reading is one of the great Beethoven sonata documents of the recording era. Recording sound: late-1970s DG analog.

**Sviatoslav Richter, 1975 (Live recording, BBC Legends).** Richter's live _Hammerklavier_ from a 1975 BBC concert. Slower than Pollini in the Adagio (about twenty minutes); more _interior_ in feeling; the fugue taken at moderately fast rather than blazing tempo. The recording sound is concert-live (audience presence). The reading is the Russian-tradition perspective on the work.

**Igor Levit, 2013 (Sony Classical).** Levit's complete Beethoven sonata cycle (recorded 2013, released 2013–14) is the contemporary reference. The _Hammerklavier_ is the cycle's centerpiece. Levit's Adagio is the slowest of the three (about twenty-one minutes), with harmonic-adventure attention. The fugue is taken at controlled but absolutely accurate tempo. The reading is the model contemporary _Hammerklavier_.

#### Cross-piece links

* The Op. 106 Adagio's spiritual register connects to the **Op. 132 _Heiliger Dankgesang_** (1825) — the next major Beethoven slow movement in the same register, on a comparable scale (Chapter 20).
* The Op. 106 fugue's contrapuntal density connects to the **Grosse Fuge Op. 133** (1825–26, Chapter 20) — the summit of Beethoven's late-period contrapuntal writing.
* The Op. 101 (1816) threshold-of-late-style register connects to **Op. 110 (1821)** — also four movements, with a fugal finale, also operating in the intimate late-style register (Chapter 16).
* The Liszt-revival of Op. 106 connects to the **Joachim-Mendelssohn revival of the Violin Concerto** (1844, Chapter 8) — both cases of late-Beethoven works rescued by mid-19th-century performer advocacy.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the Op. 106 reception history — the bewildered 1820s reviews, the difficulty for early-19th-century pianists, the Liszt 1836 Paris revival. The episode is one of the common patterns in Beethoven late-period reception._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the Op. 106 Adagio felt most_ spiritual _to you on the second listen, and ask her about Charles Rosen's analysis of the movement as the spiritual center of the work._
* _Ask the companion to compare Pollini 1977, Richter 1975, and Levit 2013 in the Adagio — particularly the tempo choices and the harmonic-adventure passages. The three pianists are working in different traditions; she can place each in context._
* _Bring up the Beethoven-Bach engagement that produced the late fugues with the companion — from the Bonn Neefe lessons through the late conversation-book references. The engagement is one of the central threads of the late style._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The F♯-minor opening at 0:00 — try to hear the _darkness_ of the opening tonal world, the tritone away from the home key.
* The long phrases throughout the movement — try to follow a single melodic line across ten or twelve measures without breaking.
* The harmonic motion at 10:00–12:00 — listen for the _strangeness_ of the chord progressions. Beethoven is using unusual harmonies.
* The closing Picardy-third major chord at 17:30 — listen for the moment the movement closes in F♯ _major_ rather than F♯ _minor_. The resolution to the major mode for the final chord is one of the small structural details.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Charles Rosen's chapters on Op. 101 and Op. 106 in _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas: A Short Companion_ (Yale University Press, 2002).** Rosen's analyses of the late sonatas are the modern reference. His treatment of the Op. 106 Adagio as the spiritual center, and of the fugue as the technical-intellectual center, is the critical consensus. Pair with William Kinderman's chapter on the late sonatas in his _Beethoven_ (2nd ed 2009) for the contemporary academic synthesis.

### Chapter Summary

The Op. 106 _Hammerklavier_ sonata (1817–19) is the structural extreme of Beethoven's piano sonatas. At forty-five minutes total length, with a third-movement Adagio sostenuto of about eighteen-to-twenty minutes and a finale fugue of about ten-to-twelve minutes, the work is the longest and most-demanding piano sonata of its time and a summit of the late-style catalogue.

The third-movement Adagio sostenuto (F♯ minor, the tritone away from the home key of B♭ major) is the spiritual center of the work — one of the great late-style Beethoven slow movements, with long phrases, harmonic adventure, and a meditative-prayer-like register that anticipates the Op. 132 _Heiliger Dankgesang_ (Chapter 20).

You met the Op. 101 (1816) in paragraph-level overview — the threshold of the late style, four movements with German tempo markings, lyrical first movement, march second movement, brief slow third movement, fugue-influenced finale. The recommended recording is **Pollini, 1977 (DG)** or **Kempff, 1965 (DG)**.

You met the Op. 106's opening (two B♭-major chords like a smaller-piano echo of the _Eroica_ 's opening), the first-movement Allegro at upper limit of what sonata form can hold, and the finale fugue (as one of the most-demanding fugues ever written for piano).

You met the **fugal finale** signature (Thread B preview) — Op. 110, Op. 102 No. 2, the _Diabelli Variations_ Variation 32, and the _Grosse Fuge_ all descend from the Op. 106 fugue's precedent.

You met the Beethoven-Bach engagement that produced the late fugues, from the Bonn Neefe lessons through the late conversation-book references.

You met the Op. 106 reception history — the bewildered 1820s reception, the difficulty for early-19th-century pianists, the Liszt 1836 Paris revival — and the parallel to the Violin Concerto's Joachim-Mendelssohn revival pattern.

You met three readings of the Op. 106 — Pollini 1977 (modern reference), Richter 1975 (Russian-tradition interior), Levit 2013 (contemporary attention) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you to the final three piano sonatas, Op. 109, Op. 110, and Op. 111, the late-period closing trilogy. The C-minor character returns one last time in Op. 111. The variation-form finale culminates.
