## Chapter 16: Late Piano Sonatas, Part II — Op. 109, Op. 110, Op. 111

In a three-year stretch from 1820 through 1822, Beethoven completed his final three piano sonatas. Op. 109 in E major, finished in summer 1820. Op. 110 in A♭ major, finished in late 1821. Op. 111 in C minor, finished in early 1822. The three works are a closing trilogy of the late-piano-sonata style, with each sonata exploring a different compositional priority: Op. 109 the variation form, Op. 110 the fugue-and-arioso closing, Op. 111 the two-movement architecture and the Arietta variation finale.

This chapter anchors on Op. 111 — Beethoven's last piano sonata, in C minor (the late counterpart to Op. 1 No. 3 and Symphony 5, completing the C-minor character thread that Chapter 21 collects and reads as a single signature). The work has only two movements: a dramatic Allegro con brio in C minor that revisits the C-minor character at maturity, and an Arietta in C major that consists of a theme and four variations leading to a quiet close. The Arietta is one of the most-loved Beethoven movements; it includes the famous _boogie-woogie_ passage in Variation 3 (the Rosen reading) and concludes in a register of _transcendence_ that Beethoven never wrote again.

We will also walk Op. 109 and Op. 110 in paragraph-level overview. The documented Schindler unreliability discipline (Chapter 9, §9.3) is applied formally to a late-period quotation in this chapter.

### 16.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — Variation 3 and the _boogie-woogie_ passage

The Arietta of Op. 111 consists of a theme (a calm, hymn-like phrase in C major) and four variations. Variation 1 is a lyrical decoration of the theme. Variation 2 doubles the rhythmic activity of Variation 1, with sixteenth notes flowing across the texture. Variation 3 doubles again, to thirty-second notes, and produces what Charles Rosen, in _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas_ (2002), called _the boogie-woogie passage_.

Rosen's argument: Variation 3 features a rhythmic pattern of nearly continuous thirty-second notes in the right hand with a marked downbeat-and-offbeat figure in the left hand. The result, in Rosen's reading, has the _swing_ feel of mid-20th-century American boogie-woogie blues piano — a coincidence that Rosen treats not as Beethoven anticipating jazz (which would be an anachronism) but as Beethoven and the boogie-woogie tradition independently discovering similar rhythmic possibilities at the piano.

The Rosen reading has become widely cited. Modern performances often draw the listener's attention to Variation 3 with the boogie-woogie framing in mind. Some pianists (notably Igor Levit and András Schiff) have discussed the passage explicitly in their interviews and writings. Other pianists (Mitsuko Uchida, Maurizio Pollini) have treated the passage without the boogie-woogie framing, emphasizing instead its place in the variation's overall arc.

The truth is probably that Rosen's reading is illuminating without being the _only_ reading. Variation 3 does have a distinctive rhythmic feel that points forward toward jazz; the connection is real if anachronistic. The variation is also a serious compositional statement within the Arietta architecture, with the rhythmic intensification serving the variation-set's overall acceleration before Variation 4 returns to a more contemplative register.

For listeners new to the work, the boogie-woogie framing is a useful way into Variation 3; it makes the rhythmic profile audible immediately. After hearing the variation a few times, listeners can move past the framing into hearing the variation in its own terms. The framing is a temporary scaffold, not the final reading.

#### What to notice

Three things across Op. 111's two movements.

  1. **The dramatic C-minor opening of Movement I.** The first movement opens with a slow dramatic introduction (Maestoso) in C minor, similar in spirit to the _Pathétique_ 's opening Grave but more extended and harmonically adventurous. Listen for the _return of the C-minor character_ — the dramatic register that Beethoven had used in Op. 1 No. 3, the _Pathétique_ , Symphony 5, and (briefly) the Piano Concerto No. 3. This is the C-minor character at late-period maturity.

  2. **The transition from movement to movement.** Op. 111 has only two movements (most Classical sonatas have three or four; the two-movement structure of Op. 90, Chapter 14, was the precedent). The first movement (the dramatic C-minor Allegro) and the second movement (the C-major Arietta) are structurally and emotionally opposite. The transition from one to the other is one of Beethoven's most-discussed structural decisions.

  3. **The Arietta's variations 1-4.** The Arietta theme is followed by four variations, each at a different rhythmic and expressive level. Listen for the _rhythmic acceleration_ across Variations 1, 2, and 3, and the return to a contemplative register in Variation 4. The variations reach the spiritual peak of the work — the _transcendent_ register that Op. 111 closes in.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the Piano Sonata No. 32 in C minor, Op. 111 between 1820 and January 1822, dedicated it to Archduke Rudolph (his last published Rudolph dedication for a piano sonata), and published it in 1823.

### 16.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the sonata once. Second listen, with timestamps into Pollini 1977.

#### Movement I: Maestoso – Allegro con brio ed appassionato (0:00–8:50 in Pollini)

* **0:00–0:30.** The opening Maestoso (slow introduction) in C minor. Heavy descending chords; dramatic register. Listen for the _similarity_ to the _Pathétique_ 's Grave (Chapter 2): the same kind of slow, weighted opening, but on a larger scale.

* **0:30–1:30.** The Maestoso continues. The harmonies become more adventurous than the _Pathétique_ 's; the dramatic tension is sustained.

* **1:30–2:30.** The transition to the Allegro. Listen for the _acceleration_ : the slow introduction breaks into the fast movement.

* **2:30–4:30.** The Allegro con brio ed appassionato (fast, with brilliance and passion). Listen for the _return of the C-minor character_ : the dense piano writing, the dramatic forward drive, the heroic-period gestures returning at late-period maturity. The first theme has a angular profile.

* **4:30–6:00.** Development of the first theme. Beethoven works the material through related keys at high pressure. The fugal writing emerges in the contrapuntal episodes (a late-style feature).

* **6:00–7:30.** The recapitulation. The C-minor character is sustained.

* **7:30–8:50.** The coda. The dramatic argument reaches its close. The movement ends in C minor: not the major-mode resolution. The decision to end in the minor is one of the work's structural features.

#### Movement II: Arietta – Adagio molto semplice e cantabile (8:50–25:30 in Pollini)

* **8:50–10:30.** **The Arietta theme.** A calm, hymn-like phrase in C major. Listen for the _contrast_ with the just-ended first movement: where movement I was dramatic and minor-key, the Arietta is contemplative and major-key. The transition between movements is the structural shift.

* **10:30–13:00.** **Variation 1.** A lyrical decoration of the theme. The right hand provides ornamental figures; the texture is light.

* **13:00–15:30.** **Variation 2.** A rhythmic intensification. Sixteenth notes flow across the texture; the dynamic builds.

* **15:30–18:00.** **Variation 3: the "boogie-woogie" passage.** Listen for the _thirty-second notes in the right hand_ with the _swinging_ dotted figures in the left. The Rosen reading frames this anticipating jazz; whether or not you accept that framing, the rhythmic profile is unmistakable. Pollini takes the variation at fast tempo and rhythmic precision.

* **18:00–21:00.** **Variation 4.** A return to contemplative register. The rhythmic intensification reaches its peak in Variation 3; Variation 4 reverses course and returns to a slow, prayer-like register. Listen for the _spiritual quietness_ : the music is now at the late-style summit.

* **21:00–23:00.** A _coda_ on the Arietta theme. The opening theme returns in transformed form. Listen for the _trill_ in the right hand (an extended trill that runs across many measures): the sustained trill is one of the late-style signatures (we will hear it again in the late quartets, Chapter 20).

* **23:00–25:30.** The closing measures. The sustained trill continues; the harmony moves toward closure in C major; the dynamic softens to pianissimo. The final cadence is in C major: quiet, sustained, transcendent.

About twenty-five minutes total for the work in Pollini. The first movement is dramatic; the second is transcendent; together they form the late-period closing statement.

### 16.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Op. 111 as the closing of the C-minor character thread

We have been tracing the C-minor character throughout this volume. The thread:

* **Op. 1 No. 3** (1795, Chapter 3): the launch
* **Pathétique Op. 13** (1798, Chapter 2): the early dramatic statement
* **Piano Concerto No. 3 Op. 37** (1800, mentioned in Chapter 5)
* **Coriolan Overture Op. 62** (1807, briefly mentioned)
* **Symphony 5 Op. 67** (1808, Chapter 9): the heroic-period summit
* **Op. 111** (1822, this chapter): the closing statement

(Chapter 21 §21.1 maps this same thread on a single page, and shows which works take the per-aspera-ad-astra arc into C major triumph and which end in the minor mode — Op. 111 is the unique case of _both moves in a single work_.)

Op. 111 is the final stop. The work returns to the dramatic C-minor first-movement register that Op. 1 No. 3 had launched twenty-seven years earlier. The opening Maestoso is parallel to the _Pathétique_ 's Grave. The C-minor argument is sustained across the first movement.

But Op. 111 then _moves beyond_ the C-minor character. The second movement is in C major (the major-mode counterpart) and operates in a register, _contemplative_ , _transcendent_ , _prayer-like_ , that the earlier C-minor works did not reach for. Symphony 5's C-major triumph was _celebratory_ ; Op. 111's C-major Arietta is _meditative_. The Arietta is Beethoven's reading of _what comes after the C-minor struggle has been completed_.

The two-movement structure is the expression of this idea. The first movement closes the C-minor argument; the second movement opens a different territory entirely. There is no third movement because the second movement does not need to be followed by anything. The work is structurally complete after the Arietta.

#### Op. 109 in E major (1820) — paragraph

Op. 109 is the first sonata of the closing trilogy. Three movements:

  1. **Vivace, ma non troppo — Adagio espressivo** , a short dual-tempo first movement (about four minutes). The Vivace material and the Adagio espressivo material alternate; the compositional design is unusual (most sonatas have a single tempo for each movement).

  2. **Prestissimo** , a brief furious second movement (about three minutes) in E minor.

  3. **Andante molto cantabile ed espressivo — Variations** , a seven-minute variation set on a hymn-like theme. The variations build to a spiritual peak (similar in register to Op. 111's Arietta but on smaller scale) before returning to the theme at the close.

The Op. 109 is the work where Beethoven first uses the variation form as the _closing movement_ of a piano sonata at substantial scale. The signature would culminate in Op. 111's Arietta and in the _Diabelli Variations_ (Chapter 17). The recommended Op. 109 recording is **Maurizio Pollini, 1977 (DG)** or **Wilhelm Kempff, 1965 (DG)**.

#### Op. 110 in A♭ major (1821) — paragraph

Op. 110 is the middle work of the closing trilogy. Four movements:

  1. **Moderato cantabile molto espressivo** , the lyrical first movement (about six minutes).

  2. **Allegro molto** , a brief sturdy scherzo (about two minutes).

  3. **Adagio ma non troppo, Recitativo, Arioso dolente, Fuga, Arioso (return) — Fuga (inverted, return)** , a complex final movement (about ten minutes) that combines arioso (operatic-recitative-like vocal writing) and fugue. The structural design is the late-style architectural experiment.

The third movement of Op. 110 is one of the most-discussed structural designs in the late catalogue. The work opens with a slow Adagio; transitions into a recitative (the operatic-vocal style applied to the piano); then into an _arioso dolente_ (sorrowful arioso) — a melancholy slow song; then a fugue; then the arioso returns; then the fugue returns in inverted form (upside down); then the work concludes with a triumphant cadence.

The Op. 110 third movement is Beethoven's most-experimental piano-sonata structural design. The combination of arioso and fugue, the recitative writing, the inverted fugue — these are innovations. The recommended recording is **Maurizio Pollini, 1977 (DG)** or **Stephen Kovacevich, 1996 (EMI)**.

#### Definition: The trill as late-style signature

The Op. 111 Arietta's closing trill — a sustained right-hand trill across many measures, accompanying the closing harmonic resolution — is one of the late-style trill devices. The trill is _not_ a Classical-period ornament (a brief decoration of a single note); it is an _extended sustained device_ that becomes part of the texture.

The extended sustained trill recurs in Beethoven's late works:

* **Op. 109's variation finale** : sustained trills in the closing variations
* **Op. 111's Arietta coda** : the famous sustained trill
* **The _Diabelli Variations_ Variation 33** — sustained trills throughout
* **The late string quartets** — Op. 132's _Heiliger Dankgesang_ has sustained quartet-trill writing; Op. 131's slow movement has extended trill passages

The trill becomes, in the late style, a _texture_ rather than an _ornament_. The effect is of a continuous shimmer over the harmonic motion, suggesting a kind of perpetual motion or eternal presence. The device is one of the late-style features that subsequent composers (Schubert, Schumann, Chopin, Liszt) would inherit and extend.

#### The Schindler unreliability flag applied

Anton Schindler's 1840 _Biographie von Ludwig van Beethoven_ contains a quotation about Op. 111 that has been widely cited and is, by modern critical consensus, doubtful.

The Schindler version: Schindler claims that Beethoven, asked why Op. 111 has only two movements, replied that _the third movement was simply not necessary_ ; that the Arietta was complete in itself and no further movement was required.

The claim is doubtful for several reasons:

  1. **Single-source.** Schindler is the only source for the quotation. No other contemporary record confirms it.
  2. **Schindler's documented unreliability.** Schindler forged conversation-book entries; invented incidents; supplied quotations Beethoven had not delivered. Modern scholars (Lewis Lockwood's _Beethoven and His Conversation Books_ , 2018) treat Schindler quotations as default unreliable.
  3. **The quotation is too perfectly thematic.** The Schindler quotation fits the _received reading_ of Op. 111 as a work whose two-movement architecture is the structural point. The fit may indicate Schindler invented the quotation to support a reading he wanted readers to take from the work.

What is documented and reliable: Op. 111 has two movements; the work is the closing piano sonata in Beethoven's catalogue; the two-movement structure is the compositional decision Beethoven made. Whether Beethoven articulated the _reason_ for the two-movement structure to anyone is uncertain. The Schindler quotation may be true in spirit (it may reflect what Beethoven actually thought) but is unreliable as direct testimony.

Modern Beethoven scholarship treats the quotation as a _Schindler embroidery_ — useful for understanding how the 19th century received the work, not reliable as testimony from Beethoven himself. The volume's discipline (Chapter 1, §9.3 of the topic prompt) requires that Schindler quotations be flagged. This is the formal application.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume Op. 111 is _unfinished_ because it has only two movements. This is wrong. The two-movement structure is the compositional design. Beethoven completed the work in January 1822 and published it as a complete sonata in 1823. The work is _not_ unfinished; it has _two movements_ by Beethoven's choice. The misperception comes from the expectation that a sonata "should" have three or four movements, but Op. 90 (Chapter 14) had already shown that Beethoven could write two-movement sonatas as complete artistic statements.

The two-movement structure of Op. 111 is the late-style architectural innovation. The work's place as Beethoven's last piano sonata makes the structural decision particularly meaningful: Beethoven's _last word_ in the piano-sonata genre is a two-movement work whose second movement is a slow Arietta variation set. This is the compositional statement of the late style at piano-sonata scale.

### 16.4 The Stories

#### The Op. 111 composition history

Beethoven began the Op. 111 sketches in late 1820, immediately after completing Op. 109. The work was completed in January 1822. The compositional period overlapped with the final years of the Karl custody battle (which concluded in 1820), with the continued _Missa Solemnis_ work (Beethoven was now four years into the _Missa Solemnis_ composition; Chapter 19), and with the start of the _Diabelli Variations_ (Chapter 17).

The dedication to Archduke Rudolph is the last Beethoven dedication of a piano sonata to Rudolph. The relationship (Chapter 12) continued, but the dedicated works shifted to other categories (Missa Solemnis 1823, Grosse Fuge 1825–26). Op. 111 was the closing of the Rudolph-Beethoven piano-sonata dedication thread.

The work's 1823 publication caused some confusion. The publisher initially feared that the two-movement structure indicated an unfinished work — Beethoven had to write to the publisher to confirm that the work was complete as published. The documented correspondence (preserved in the Schindler-Beethoven correspondence, with some Schindler caveats) shows Beethoven's insistence on the two-movement structure.

#### The Vienna reception 1823–1828

The Op. 111 was received with caution by the 1823 Vienna pianistic community. Like the Op. 106 (Chapter 15), the work was considered difficult, harmonically adventurous, and structurally unusual. Documented reviews from 1823 onward describe the work as _colossal_ but _strange_ ; the Arietta's spiritual register was admired but found difficult to integrate into the standard sonata expectations.

The work entered the standard repertoire gradually through the 1830s and 1840s. By Liszt's generation (Liszt was twelve in 1823) the work was being studied by serious pianists; by the 1850s it was being performed in major recitals. By the late 19th century it was canonical — every major pianist's Beethoven cycle includes the work.

Twentieth-century philosophical engagement with the work has been substantial. Thomas Mann's novel _Doctor Faustus_ (1947) has a chapter in which a German music teacher, Wendell Kretzschmar, gives a lecture on Op. 111 — analyzing the work's two-movement structure and its closing in C major as the modern philosophical-musical statement. Theodor W. Adorno's late essays on Beethoven (the unfinished _Beethoven: Philosophy of Music_ project, posthumously edited by Rolf Tiedemann in 1993) treat Op. 111 as the summit of Beethoven's late style and of Western musical modernism more broadly.

The Mann-Adorno engagement has made Op. 111 the most-philosophically-engaged Beethoven work of the 20th century. Whether or not the philosophical engagement is sound (Mann's lecture is fictional; Adorno's text is incomplete and difficult), the engagement has shaped how readers in the second half of the 20th century approached the work. We will return to Adorno briefly in Chapter 22.

#### Three readings of Op. 111

**Maurizio Pollini, 1977 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Pollini's 1977 Op. 111 is the model modern reading. Structurally clear; technically impeccable; the Arietta at moderate tempo (about seventeen minutes); Variation 3 taken at the Rosen reading's rhythmic precision. The recording is one of the central documents of modern Beethoven sonata interpretation.

**Wilhelm Kempff, 1965 (Deutsche Grammophon).** Kempff's 1965 Op. 111 is the postwar German classical reading. Faster than Pollini (Arietta about sixteen minutes); more lyrical; the trill in the coda is voiced with Kempff sensitivity. The reading is one of the great Beethoven sonata documents of the postwar era.

**Mitsuko Uchida, 2007 (Decca).** Uchida's late-recorded Op. 111 (she has recorded the work several times; the 2007 reading is the major statement) takes the Arietta at slow tempo (about nineteen minutes). The interior quality of Uchida's playing — the close attention to harmonic shading, the contemplative pace — produces a reading that is one of the most-loved contemporary Op. 111 documents.

**Igor Levit, 2013 (Sony Classical).** Levit's Op. 111 (part of his complete cycle) takes the Arietta at the slowest tempo of the four readings (about twenty minutes). The harmonic-adventure attention reaches its peak here; the Variation 3 boogie-woogie passage is taken with precise rhythmic profile.

#### Cross-piece links

* The Op. 111 Arietta's spiritual register connects to **the late quartets' slow movements** (Op. 132, Op. 131, Op. 135 — Chapter 20).
* The variation-form finale (Op. 109, Op. 111, Diabelli, Symphony 9 finale) connects to **the _Diabelli Variations_** (Chapter 17) as the next major statement.
* The C-minor character thread closes with Op. 111. Earlier stops: **Op. 1 No. 3** (Chapter 3), **Pathétique** (Chapter 2), **Symphony 5** (Chapter 9).
* The Op. 110's arioso-and-fugue closing connects to **Op. 132's slow movement (_Heiliger Dankgesang_)** — the hymnal-and-contrapuntal register continues into the late quartets.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the closing of the C-minor character thread — from Op. 1 No. 3 in 1795 through Op. 111 in 1822, twenty-seven years of recurring compositional preoccupation. The conversation should produce a sense of how a single tonal-expressive concern can recur across an entire career._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the Arietta felt most_ transcendent _to you on the second listen, and ask her about Charles Rosen's "boogie-woogie" reading of Variation 3, and about the Mann-Adorno philosophical engagement with the work in the 20th century._
* _Ask the companion about the documented Schindler quotation regarding Op. 111's two-movement structure — what Schindler claimed, why modern scholars treat the quotation as doubtful, and what we can reliably say about Beethoven's decision to write a two-movement sonata._
* _Have the companion compare Pollini 1977, Kempff 1965, Uchida 2007, and Levit 2013 in the Arietta — particularly the tempo choices and the Variation 3 rhythmic profile. The four pianists are working with different traditions; she can place each in context._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The Maestoso opening at 0:00 — try to hear the _similarity_ to the _Pathétique_ 's Grave (Chapter 2). The two openings are parallels at twenty-four-year remove.
* The transition between movements at 8:50 — the dramatic C-minor close gives way directly to the contemplative C-major opening of the Arietta. The transition is one of the structural shifts.
* Variation 3 from about 15:30 — listen for the _thirty-second notes_ and the _swing-like_ dotted figures in the left hand. The Rosen "boogie-woogie" framing makes the rhythmic profile audible.
* The closing trill from about 21:00 — the sustained right-hand trill across many measures, the late-style trill device.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further-listening recommendation, listen to **the Mitsuko Uchida 2007 Decca recording of Op. 111 alongside Pollini 1977 DG** — the two readings give different approaches to the Arietta, with Uchida slower and more contemplative, Pollini brisker and more structural. The contrast is one of the productive ways to hear how interpretive choices can shape a single movement. Then read **Thomas Mann's _Doctor Faustus_ (1947), the Kretzschmar lecture chapter on Op. 111** — the fictional musical analysis is one of the great 20th-century literary engagements with a single Beethoven work.

### Chapter Summary

The Op. 111 piano sonata in C minor (composed 1820–22) is Beethoven's last piano sonata. The two-movement structure (Maestoso–Allegro con brio in C minor; Arietta with variations in C major) is the compositional design. The first movement closes the C-minor character thread that Op. 1 No. 3 had launched in 1795. The second movement opens the transcendent register that the late style had been moving toward.

The Arietta's four variations build through rhythmic intensification (Variation 1 ornamental; Variation 2 sixteenth notes; Variation 3 the "boogie-woogie" thirty-second-note passage in Rosen's reading; Variation 4 the return to contemplative register) before the coda with the sustained trill closes the work.

You met Op. 109 (1820) in paragraph-level overview — three movements with the variation finale that opens the closing-trilogy variation-form discipline. The recommended recording is **Pollini, 1977 (DG)**.

You met Op. 110 (1821) in paragraph-level overview — four movements with the arioso-and-fugue third-movement architectural experiment. The recommended recording is **Pollini, 1977 (DG)** or **Kovacevich, 1996 (EMI)**.

You met the _trill as late-style signature_ — the sustained right-hand trill that becomes a texture rather than an ornament, appearing across Op. 109, Op. 111, the _Diabelli Variations_ , and the late string quartets.

You met the documented Schindler quotation about Op. 111's two-movement structure and the formal application of the Schindler unreliability flag — the quotation is doubtful and modern scholars treat it as a Schindler embroidery.

You met the Mann-Adorno 20th-century philosophical engagement with Op. 111 — Mann's _Doctor Faustus_ Kretzschmar lecture, Adorno's late essays on Beethoven.

You met four readings of Op. 111 — Pollini 1977 (modern reference), Kempff 1965 (postwar German classical), Uchida 2007 (contemporary interior), Levit 2013 (contemporary harmonic-adventure attention) — and the work-vs-interpretation tetrahedron they form.

The next chapter takes you to the _Diabelli Variations_ — Beethoven's variation-form summit, 33 variations on Anton Diabelli's waltz theme, composed in the same 1819–1823 stretch as Op. 111 and the _Missa Solemnis_.

## Chapter 17: _Diabelli Variations_ , Op. 120

In late 1819, the Vienna music publisher Anton Diabelli sent a brief waltz theme — about thirty-two measures, in C major, composed by Diabelli himself — to a long list of composers, with an invitation. Each composer was asked to write one variation on the theme. Diabelli would publish the resulting collection as a single volume, with each composer's name attached to his variation, as a anthology of contemporary Vienna composition. About fifty composers eventually contributed; the resulting volume (published in 1824 as _Vaterländischer Künstlerverein_ — "Patriotic Artists' Association") includes variations by Schubert, Liszt (then twelve years old, with the youngest contribution), Hummel, Czerny, Moscheles, and many others.

Beethoven received the invitation, looked at the theme, called it (in his response to friends) a _Schusterfleck_ — a "cobbler's patch" or "shoemaker's patch," a derogatory term for a primitive musical sequence — and declined to participate. Or so the story went. Within a few months, Beethoven had begun composing variations on the theme privately. By 1820 he had written about twenty variations. By 1823 he had written thirty-three. The completed work was published in 1823 as a stand-alone Op. 120 — not as part of Diabelli's anthology, but as a separate volume titled _Thirty-Three Variations on a Waltz by Anton Diabelli_.

The work is one of the great architectural achievements of the late period. Hans von Bülow called it _the microcosm of Beethoven's art_ ; Alfred Brendel has called it the _greatest of all piano works_. The thirty-three variations together run about fifty to sixty minutes — making the work, along with the _Hammerklavier_ (Chapter 15), one of the longest individual works in the piano literature.

This chapter does not attempt to walk all thirty-three variations. It anchors on five key variations: the theme itself, Variation 1 (the march transformation), Variation 14 (the slow grandeur), Variation 20 (the still center), Variation 32 (the fugue), and Variation 33 (the closing minuet). These five give the architectural shape of the work; the others fill in the journey between.

### 17.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — the "cobbler's patch" and the four-year composition

The story of Beethoven's initial refusal comes from multiple sources — Anton Schindler's later biography, Carl Czerny's memoirs, and (in modified form) from contemporaries who knew Beethoven in 1819–20. The exact words of Beethoven's documented rejection are uncertain (the Schindler version uses the _Schusterfleck_ word, which may or may not be exact), but the substance is consistent: Beethoven initially found Diabelli's waltz theme primitive, sequential, derivative, and unworthy of serious compositional treatment.

The Diabelli theme, viewed objectively, is indeed somewhat simple. It is in C major. It is thirty-two measures long. It uses a sequential pattern, a phrase that repeats at different pitches, that was a common (and somewhat clichéd) compositional device in early-19th-century Vienna popular music. The harmonic motion is straightforward. The melodic ideas are not striking.

What was Beethoven's response? Compose thirty-three variations.

The compositional history is in detail. The first burst of work was in 1819 — Beethoven composed approximately twenty variations between roughly summer 1819 and early 1820. He then set the work aside for nearly three years to work on the Op. 111 sonata (Chapter 16) and the _Missa Solemnis_ (Chapter 19). He returned to the _Diabelli_ in early 1823 and completed the remaining thirteen variations in a few weeks. The full work was finished by April 1823 and published in June 1823 as _33 Veränderungen über einen Walzer von A. Diabelli_.

The four-year compositional history is one of the longest for any single Beethoven work. It reflects the late-period pattern: Beethoven worked on multiple major projects simultaneously, picking up and setting down each as compositional inspiration directed. The _Diabelli_ , the late piano sonatas (Op. 109, Op. 110, Op. 111), the _Missa Solemnis_ , and the Ninth Symphony were all in compositional progress simultaneously from 1819 through 1824. The interweaving of these projects is the late-period's compositional pattern.

What did Beethoven find in Diabelli's "cobbler's patch" that turned thirty-three variations? The answer is that the theme's _simplicity_ was, paradoxically, productive. A simple theme has many possible variations because each compositional decision opens new territory; a more complex theme constrains the variations more tightly. Beethoven took Diabelli's simple theme and treated it as a _prompt_ — a starting point for thirty-three different compositional explorations, only some of which directly engage the theme's melodic material.

Some variations treat the theme literally (Variations 1, 6, 9, 10, 11, 30 retain the melodic outline). Many variations treat the theme only in terms of its harmonic structure (Variations 5, 8, 12, 14, 16, 17, 18 retain the harmonic skeleton but transform the melody and texture). Some variations treat the theme as a launching point and develop in directions the theme itself does not suggest (Variations 19, 20, 28, 31, 32 are more independent compositional statements). The cumulative effect is a microcosm of Beethoven's late-style compositional thinking.

#### What to notice

Three things across the five anchor variations (theme + Variations 1, 14, 20, 32, 33).

  1. **The transformation of the simple theme.** The theme itself is a 32-bar C-major waltz with a sequential phrase structure. Listen for the _transformation_ in Variation 1: Beethoven turns the waltz into a _march_ in C major, with the same harmonic skeleton but completely different rhythmic-and-textural character. The variation immediately announces the work's compositional ambition.

  2. **The slow grandeur of Variation 14.** Variation 14 is one of the spiritual peaks. It is in a slow tempo (Grave e maestoso) and a thicker chordal texture; it sounds _like a hymn_ or _like a religious oratorio_ dropped into the middle of a waltz-variation set. Listen for the _register-shift_ : the variation is taking the work into a different expressive territory.

  3. **The fugue of Variation 32 and the closing of Variation 33.** Variation 32 is a fully-developed fugue — comparable in compositional ambition to the _Hammerklavier_ finale (Chapter 15). Variation 33 is a surprise — instead of closing with another high-pressure variation, Beethoven ends the work with a _minuet_ (Tempo di minuetto moderato), the gentle final statement. Listen for the _closing surprise_ : the work concludes with quiet grace rather than triumphant celebration.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the _Thirty-Three Variations on a Waltz by Anton Diabelli_ , Op. 120 in two phases — about twenty variations in 1819 and the remaining thirteen in early 1823 — and published the work in June 1823, dedicated to Antonie Brentano (the Solomon-identified _Immortal Beloved_ candidate from Chapter 7).

### 17.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the complete work once. Second listen, with timestamps for the five anchor moments, in Kovacevich 1968.

#### Theme (0:00–1:00 in Kovacevich)

Diabelli's waltz. Listen for the _simplicity_ : a 32-bar C-major waltz, with a sequential phrase structure (the opening four-bar phrase is repeated at a different pitch in measures 5-8). The theme is light, dance-like, somewhat unremarkable. The harmonic motion is straightforward.

This is the _raw material_. Everything that follows is what Beethoven made of it.

#### Variation 1: Alla marcia maestoso (1:00–2:30 in Kovacevich)

**The march transformation.** Listen for the _rhythmic shift_ : where the theme was a light waltz (3/4 time, dance-pulse), Variation 1 is a march (4/4 time, walking-pulse) at moderate tempo. The melodic outline of the theme is retained but the character is completely altered. Beethoven is showing immediately that the variations will not be a sequence of small decorations; they will be major compositional re-imaginings of the theme's possibilities.

The variation has a declarative quality. Hans von Bülow's description: _"Beethoven establishes immediately that this work will not be a Diabelli anthology piece. This is going to be a major Beethoven composition."_

#### Variation 14: Grave e maestoso (about 20:00 in Kovacevich)

**The slow grandeur.** Variation 14 is one of the spiritual peaks of the work. The tempo is _Grave e maestoso_ (slow and majestic). The texture is thick — full chordal writing across the keyboard. The harmony is adventurous. The duration of the variation is unusually long (about three minutes; most of the variations are one to two minutes).

Listen for the _register-shift_ : the variation sounds _like a hymn_ or _like a religious oratorio_ dropped into the middle of a waltz-variation set. The C-major key is retained but the character is devotional. The variation is the spiritual marker that _the work is reaching for something beyond a waltz-variations set_.

#### Variation 20: Andante (about 30:00 in Kovacevich)

**The still center.** Variation 20 is one of the strangest of the thirty-three. The tempo is _Andante_ (moderate-slow) but the character is unlike anything else in the work — _static_ , _mysterious_ , _suspended in time_. The texture is thin; the harmony is adventurous; the dynamic stays soft throughout.

Listen for the _stillness_ : the variation does not develop; it sustains a single meditative atmosphere across its duration (about two minutes). William Kinderman's _Beethoven_ (2nd ed 2009) compares the variation to the Bach _Goldberg Variations_ Variation 25 — the "black pearl" of the _Goldbergs_ , also strange, static, meditative. Whether Beethoven was consciously responding to the Goldberg precedent is debated (Kinderman argues yes; other scholars are more cautious). What is documented: the variation has the _Goldberg Variation 25 atmosphere_ applied to the _Diabelli_ context.

#### Variation 32: Fuga (about 47:00 in Kovacevich)

**The fugue.** Variation 32 is a fully-developed three-voice fugue — comparable to the _Hammerklavier_ finale fugue (Chapter 15). The fugue subject is derived from the Diabelli theme but transformed into a contrapuntally workable form. The fugue runs about four minutes and includes extensive contrapuntal techniques — inversion, augmentation, stretto.

Listen for the _intellectual density_ : this is Beethoven the contrapuntist at full powers, working with the same Bach-influenced vocabulary that produced the _Hammerklavier_ fugue, the Op. 110 finale fugue, and the _Grosse Fuge_ (Chapter 20). The fugue is the compositional climax of the _Diabelli Variations_.

#### Variation 33: Tempo di minuetto moderato (about 51:00 in Kovacevich)

**The closing surprise.** Variation 33 is _not_ another high-pressure variation; it is _not_ a triumphant celebration; it is _not_ a return to the original waltz. It is a _minuet_ (Tempo di minuetto moderato) — a gentle dance-rhythm closing in C major. The character is light, almost playful, with the variation's thematic material drawn obliquely from the theme.

Listen for the _quietness_ of the close. The thirty-three variations have built through extensive compositional development, including the spiritual peak of Variation 14, the still center of Variation 20, and the contrapuntal summit of Variation 32. The work could close with a triumphant explosion. Instead, Beethoven closes with the gentle minuet — the work _steps back_ into a small-scale dance form, completing the variation set with the closing grace.

The final cadence is in C major, quiet, sustained. The work ends in the same key it began but on a different emotional register — what was a primitive waltz at the opening is now, after thirty-three variations of compositional exploration, the closing minuet of the late style.

### 17.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Diabelli architecture

The thirty-three variations have a structural architecture; they are not a random sequence. The architecture has been analyzed by William Kinderman, Alfred Brendel (in his essays on the work), and Charles Rosen.

The Brendel reading: the variations divide into approximately three large groups:

* **Variations 1–10** : the "exploration" group. Beethoven works through different basic possibilities: the march of Variation 1, the variations 2-3 that develop melodic decorations, Variation 7 that develops rhythmic alternatives, etc.
* **Variations 11–22** : the "expansion" group. The compositional ambition increases. Variation 14 (the spiritual peak), Variation 16 and 17 (the rapid Allegro variations), Variation 20 (the still center), Variation 21 (a homage to Bach), Variation 22 (a quotation of Mozart's Don Giovanni's "Notte e giorno faticar" aria — Beethoven directly quotes the Mozart in the variation, as a joke about the Mozart-Diabelli connection).
* **Variations 23–33** : the "summit" group. The most-ambitious variations come at the end. Variation 24 is a Fughetta (small fugue) that prepares for Variation 32. Variation 31 is a Largo that reaches another spiritual peak. Variation 32 is the fugue. Variation 33 is the closing minuet.

The Kinderman reading: the architecture has parallels to Bach's _Goldberg Variations_ , with both works structured around variations of varying types (canon variations, dance variations, character variations) building toward a culminating fugue and a closing return.

Charles Rosen's reading is more cautious about strict architectural claims, treating the variations as a succession of compositional ideas without a single rigorous structural plan.

What is documented: the variations have _some_ structural design (the climaxes are placed; the fugue is near the end; the closing minuet is the quiet end), but the design is _looser_ than a sonata-form work. The _Diabelli_ is in the variation tradition, where the architectural rules are less strict than in sonata form.

#### The Mozart quotation in Variation 22

Variation 22 quotes the opening of _Don Giovanni_ 's _"Notte e giorno faticar"_ — the bass aria with which Leporello opens the Mozart opera, complaining about having to work day and night for his master.

The Beethoven joke: Diabelli, the publisher, had been Beethoven's regular publisher and was the dedicatee of several works. The relationship was professional and occasionally exasperating (Diabelli had requests for fast composition that Beethoven sometimes resented). The Leporello aria, Leporello complaining about working for Don Giovanni, is Beethoven's joke about working for Diabelli on these variations. The Beethoven is, in Variation 22, _Leporello complaining about being made to vary Diabelli's primitive theme_.

The Mozart quotation has been in modern scholarship (it appears in Kinderman's book, in Brendel's essays, in Rosen's writings). It is one of the small jokes that Beethoven embedded in the work as in-jokes between the composer and the Vienna musical community.

#### The Bach precedent

The Beethoven engagement with Bach (Chapter 15, Chapter 16) reaches its peak in the _Diabelli Variations_. Beethoven had owned and studied the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ throughout his career; the engagement intensified through the late years. The conversation books contain references to Beethoven's purchases of Bach editions, to late-period discussions of Bach's variation techniques.

The connection to Bach's _Goldberg Variations_ (BWV 988, 1741) is argued by Kinderman and others. The similarities:

* Both works are large-scale variation sets on a relatively simple theme.
* Both works include canon variations interspersed with character variations.
* Both works culminate in a fugue (the _Diabelli_ 's Variation 32; the _Goldberg_ 's Quodlibet at Variation 30, which is a variation that combines two folk melodies in counterpoint with the Aria's bass line).
* Both works end with a quiet closing return (the _Diabelli_ 's minuet; the _Goldberg_ 's Aria da capo).

Whether the _Goldberg_ was a direct model for Beethoven in 1819–23 is debated. The Bach engagement is documented; the _Goldberg_ connection is plausible but not strictly attested as Beethoven's stated intent. The chapter's discipline (per §1.2 Ch 17 of the topic prompt) is to _cite Kinderman where the Goldberg-precedent argument is invoked_ , and to note that Beethoven's Bach engagement at large is the more reliable precedent than the specific _Goldberg_ connection.

For the listener, the practical guidance: if you have heard Bach's _Goldbergs_ , you will hear parallels with the _Diabelli_. If you have not, you do not need to hear the _Goldbergs_ first to understand the _Diabelli_. The Beethoven work stands on its own.

#### Definition: The variation-form summit (Thread B return)

The variation-form-finale signature (Thread B, introduced in Chapter 12) reaches its summit with the _Diabelli Variations_. The _Diabelli_ is, structurally, an _entire work_ in variation form — not a finale-variation-set but a complete composition.

The thread:

* **Symphony 7's Allegretto** (Chapter 11): variation slow movement
* **Op. 109's variation finale** (Chapter 16)
* **Op. 111's Arietta** (Chapter 16): variation second-movement-as-whole
* **Diabelli Variations** (this chapter): complete-work variation
* **Symphony 9's choral finale** (Chapter 18): variation finale on the Ode to Joy theme

The _Diabelli_ is the largest variation-set Beethoven composed. The Op. 111 Arietta was a variation set, but on smaller scale (about twenty minutes). The Symphony 9 finale would be a variation set, but with additional structural elements (choral writing, multiple themes). The _Diabelli_ is the pure variation-set in its largest form — fifty-plus minutes on a single theme, with thirty-three variations.

Chapter 21 will summarize the full set of signatures. The variation-form discipline is one of the central ones.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the _Diabelli Variations_ is a _novelty work_ — Beethoven amusing himself with variations on a trivial theme. The misperception comes from the "cobbler's patch" origin and the apparent triviality of Diabelli's waltz. The work is, in fact, the Beethoven late-style summit in the variation form — one of his most-ambitious works, in some respects more compositionally adventurous than the _Hammerklavier_ sonata. Hans von Bülow's "microcosm of Beethoven's art" is the modern critical consensus.

The misperception also fails to account for what Beethoven _did_ with the trivial theme. The transformation of a primitive waltz into thirty-three variations spanning the full range of his late-style compositional thinking is the work's artistic statement: _any_ theme, no matter how simple, can be the seed of major composition if the composer brings sufficient skill and ambition. The work is Beethoven's statement of late-period compositional confidence.

### 17.4 The Stories

#### Anton Diabelli and the Vienna publishing context

Anton Diabelli (1781–1858) was a Vienna music publisher who had founded the firm Cappi & Diabelli in 1818. He had been a composer himself (mostly minor piano pieces and church music) before turning to publishing. By 1819 his firm was one of the leading Vienna music publishers, with relationships with Schubert (Diabelli published Schubert's early works), Beethoven (Diabelli published Op. 90, Op. 91 _Wellington's Victory_ , and others), and many other Vienna composers.

The 1819 invitation to write variations was Diabelli's commercial scheme. The plan: a collection of variations from many famous and minor composers, published as a single volume, with the variations functioning as both a anthology of contemporary Vienna composition and as a showcase of Diabelli's publishing firm. The plan was sound business and was to produce a sellable volume.

Diabelli was, by accounts, a businessman first and a composer second. The waltz theme he composed for the project was functional rather than artistic — designed to be variable, not to be artistic in itself. Beethoven's characterization of the theme as a "cobbler's patch" was, from a strictly artistic perspective, accurate.

What Diabelli received from Beethoven was not the single variation he had requested but a thirty-three-variation magnum opus that the publisher had to publish as a stand-alone work (Op. 120, 1823) separate from the anthology. The Beethoven dedication of the work to Antonie Brentano (rather than to Diabelli himself) was, by Vienna commercial standards, somewhat snubbing — Beethoven was dedicating the work that Diabelli had commissioned to someone other than Diabelli. The relationship between the two men was professional but not warm in this period.

#### The Antonie Brentano dedication

The _Diabelli Variations_ are dedicated to **Antonie Brentano** (1780–1869). She had been the Solomon-identified _Immortal Beloved_ candidate from the July 1812 letter (Chapter 7). By 1823, Antonie was living in Frankfurt with her husband Franz Brentano and her four children. She had not seen Beethoven since 1812. The dedication is one of the lingering Beethoven-Antonie connections, suggesting that Beethoven maintained warm feelings for her across the decade.

The dedication is one of the strongest pieces of indirect evidence for Solomon's _Immortal Beloved_ identification. If Antonie was the _Immortal Beloved_ , then the 1823 dedication of one of Beethoven's most-ambitious works to her makes sense as a gesture across the years. The dedication does not _prove_ the identification (it could have other explanations — Antonie's Vienna social circle, the Brentano family musical connections, the Beethoven-Brentano friendship), but it is consistent with the identification.

We will not resolve the _Immortal Beloved_ question here. The chapter's commitment is to note the dedication and the connection to the _Immortal Beloved_ hypothesis, without adjudicating the identification.

#### The 19th and 20th-century reception

The _Diabelli Variations_ were received with bewilderment in 1823, similar to the Op. 106 and Op. 111 receptions. The work was considered too long, too varied, too compositionally adventurous for the typical concert program. Liszt was one of the early advocates; he performed the work and discussed it in his writings. Hans von Bülow was the major late-19th-century champion (his "microcosm" line is documented).

Twentieth-century reception expanded the engagement. Alfred Brendel performed the work many times and wrote extensive essays about it (collected in his _Music Sounded Out_ and other essay volumes). Andras Schiff has performed and discussed the work extensively. Igor Levit has recorded the work as part of his Beethoven sonata cycle. The work is now as one of the standard repertory pieces for serious pianists.

The Diabelli Variations have inspired later compositional responses. Hans Werner Henze composed _Royal Winter Music_ with Diabelli echoes. Frederic Rzewski's _The People United Will Never Be Defeated!_ (1975) is a 36-variation set on a Chilean protest song, as inspired in part by the _Diabelli_ model. Beethoven's variation set has become the model for substantial variation works in subsequent music.

#### Three readings of the _Diabelli Variations_

**Stephen Kovacevich, 1968 (Philips).** Kovacevich (then publishing as Stephen Bishop) recorded the _Diabelli_ as his major Beethoven statement. The reading is exceptionally clear architecturally — the variations' architectural shape is communicated through Kovacevich's attention to tempo, dynamic, and structural articulation. The recording sound is late-1960s Philips analog. The reading remains one of the references.

**Alfred Brendel, 1988 (Philips).** Brendel's late-Brendel _Diabelli_ (he has recorded the work multiple times; the 1988 reading is the major statement). The interpretation is more interpretive than Kovacevich's — Brendel takes more freedom with tempo, more attention to expressive nuance. The reading is one of the great recorded _Diabelli_ readings.

**Andreas Staier, 2012 (Harmonia Mundi, fortepiano).** Staier's fortepiano reading uses a period instrument (a copy of an early-19th-century Vienna fortepiano). The dynamic range is narrower than on a modern piano; the timbral colors are different; the rhythmic articulation is sharper. The recording allows the listener to hear what the work might have sounded like at its 1823 publication. The reading is one of the references for HIP-informed _Diabelli_ performance.

**Igor Levit, 2015 (Sony Classical).** Levit's _Diabelli_ (recorded as part of his complete Beethoven variations and sonatas project) takes a modern reference-level approach. The reading is technically impeccable, architecturally clear, with contemporary attention to the harmonic adventure of the variations.

#### Cross-piece links

* The _Diabelli_ 's Variation 32 fugue connects to the **Op. 106 fugue** (Chapter 15), the **Op. 110 finale fugue** (Chapter 16), and the **Grosse Fuge** (Chapter 20).
* The variation-form summit connects to the **Op. 111 Arietta** (Chapter 16), the **Symphony 9 finale variations** (Chapter 18), and the **Bach Goldberg Variations** as the precedent.
* The Antonie Brentano dedication connects to the **Solomon Immortal Beloved identification** (Chapter 7) and the **1812 Immortal Beloved letter**.
* The Mozart _Don Giovanni_ quotation in Variation 22 connects to the Beethoven-Mozart relationship (the 1787 Vienna trip in Chapter 3, the Mozart influence throughout Beethoven's catalogue).

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion to walk through the architectural shape of the Diabelli Variations — the Brendel three-group reading (variations 1-10, 11-22, 23-33), the climaxes (Variation 14, Variation 20, Variation 32, Variation 33), and how the architecture supports the work's overall arc._
* _Tell the companion which of the five anchor variations (theme, 1, 14, 20, 32, 33) felt most_ unforgettable _to you on the second listen, and ask her about the Bach precedents (the Goldberg Variations) and the Mozart joke in Variation 22 (the Leporello quotation)._
* _Ask the companion about the Antonie Brentano dedication — what we know about the Beethoven-Antonie relationship, how the 1823 dedication fits with the 1812 Immortal Beloved letter, and how Solomon's identification has held up under modern scholarly scrutiny._
* _Have the companion compare Kovacevich 1968, Brendel 1988, and Staier 2012 (fortepiano) — the three approaches give different perspectives on the work, with the fortepiano reading particularly illuminating the 1823 sound world._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The Diabelli theme itself at 0:00 — try to hear the _sequential phrase structure_ that Beethoven called primitive. The simplicity is the raw material.
* The march transformation in Variation 1 — listen for the _rhythmic shift_ from waltz (3/4) to march (4/4) and for the declarative quality.
* The hymnal grandeur of Variation 14 — listen for the _register-shift_ into devotional-prayer territory.
* The static stillness of Variation 20 — listen for the _suspended-in-time_ quality and try to compare it (if you know the work) with Bach's Goldberg Variation 25.
* The fugue of Variation 32 — listen for the contrapuntal density and try to follow the three voices independently.
* The closing minuet of Variation 33 — listen for the _quiet grace_ of the close, the surprise that Beethoven does not end with a triumphant variation.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Alfred Brendel's essay "Beethoven's _Diabelli Variations_ " in his _Music Sounded Out_ (Farrar, Straus & Giroux, 1990).** Brendel performed the work many times and his essay is the great pianist-and-essayist statement on the work — architecturally aware, technically informed, lyrically written. Pair with **William Kinderman's _Beethoven's Diabelli Variations_ (1987)** for the major academic study (Kinderman's book is the most-comprehensive scholarly treatment).

### Chapter Summary

The _Diabelli Variations_ (Op. 120), composed in two phases between 1819 and April 1823 and published in June 1823, is the variation-form summit of Beethoven's late period. The work takes a primitive waltz theme by the publisher Anton Diabelli, which Beethoven characterized as a "cobbler's patch", and develops thirty-three variations across about fifty to sixty minutes of music. The compositional history (the refusal, then the composition over four years) is one of the unusual cases in Beethoven's catalogue.

You met five anchor moments: the theme (the raw material), Variation 1 (the march transformation), Variation 14 (the spiritual peak with hymnal grandeur), Variation 20 (the still center with Goldberg-like suspension), Variation 32 (the fugue), and Variation 33 (the closing minuet that quietly closes the work rather than triumphantly).

You met the architectural shape of the work, the Brendel three-group reading (1-10, 11-22, 23-33), and the climaxes at Variations 14, 20, 32, and the quiet closing.

You met the Beethoven-Bach engagement at its peak in the _Diabelli_ and the connection (per Kinderman) to Bach's _Goldberg Variations_. The connection is plausible but not strictly documented; Beethoven's Bach engagement at large is the more reliable precedent.

You met the Mozart _Don Giovanni_ "Notte e giorno faticar" quotation in Variation 22 — Beethoven's joke about working for Diabelli the publisher.

You met the Antonie Brentano dedication and the connection to Solomon's _Immortal Beloved_ identification.

You met the variation-form summit signature (Thread B return) — the _Diabelli_ completes the variation thread that will continue into Symphony 9's choral finale (Chapter 18).

You met four readings of the _Diabelli Variations_ — Kovacevich 1968 (architectural clarity), Brendel 1988 (interpretive nuance), Staier 2012 (fortepiano HIP), Levit 2015 (modern reference) — and the work-vs-interpretation tetrahedron they form.

The next chapter takes you to the Ninth Symphony — all four movements, with the 1817–1824 gestation, the Kärntnertortheater premiere of 7 May 1824, and the choral finale. The largest single work of Beethoven's catalogue.

## Chapter 18: Symphony No. 9 "Choral" — All Four Movements

In Volume 1 of _Close Listening_ , you met the Ninth Symphony in Chapter 14 — focused on the choral finale's Ode to Joy and the work's place as the symphonic summit of Beethoven's catalogue. This chapter returns to the work with all four movements in view, from the 1817 sketches through the 7 May 1824 Kärntnertortheater premiere.

The Ninth runs about sixty-five to seventy minutes — Beethoven's largest single work, and the largest symphony in the standard 19th-century repertoire until Mahler's symphonies thirty years later. The four movements: a substantial sonata-form Allegro in D minor (about fifteen minutes); a Molto vivace Scherzo with the famous timpani-octave intervention (about twelve minutes); a slow third-movement Adagio molto e cantabile in B♭ major (about fifteen minutes), in variation form, one of the spiritual peaks of the catalogue; a choral finale of about twenty-five minutes that introduces vocal soloists and chorus to the symphonic medium for the first time in major repertoire, setting Friedrich Schiller's _An die Freude_ ("Ode to Joy") in a variation structure.

The chapter anchors on the choral finale (Movement IV) since the prior three movements are best heard in sequence with the finale's architectural payoff. We will sketch all four movements but the listening-window timestamps focus on the finale.

### 18.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — the 7 May 1824 premiere and Caroline Unger

The Ninth Symphony's premiere was at the Kärntnertortheater in Vienna on 7 May 1824. The orchestra and chorus had been under-rehearsed (Beethoven's standard problem at his premieres). The conductor was Michael Umlauf (Beethoven was nominally the conductor, but his severe deafness by this point made it impossible for him to coordinate with the orchestra in real time — Umlauf, the Kärntnertortheater's resident conductor, gave the actual beat, and Beethoven stood beside him conducting the work as he heard it in his inner ear).

The vocal soloists were Henriette Sontag (soprano, then 18 years old), Caroline Unger (contralto, then 21), Anton Haitzinger (tenor, then 28), and Joseph Seipelt (bass). The chorus and orchestra were members of the Theater an der Wien establishment, supplemented by amateur singers.

The story is that, at the end of the performance, the audience erupted in applause. Beethoven, facing the orchestra (or by some accounts, looking at the score on his music stand), could not hear the applause behind him. Caroline Unger, the contralto, walked over to him on the stage and turned him around, physically rotated him to face the audience, so he could see the standing ovation.

The story is famous. It is also a story with a complicated provenance. The earliest documented written sources are from years after the premiere — Anton Schindler's biography (1840), various memoirs published in the 1830s and 1840s, and a Unger letter (date uncertain) that some scholars consider authentic and others doubt. Modern Beethoven scholarship treats the story with caution: Lewis Lockwood writes in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ that the gesture _probably did occur_ in some form, but the details (who exactly turned Beethoven, how exactly the gesture happened) are uncertain. The standard canonical attribution to Caroline Unger comes from documented later memoirs and the Unger letter.

What is documented and reliable: the premiere occurred on 7 May 1824; Beethoven was substantially deaf by then; he could not have heard the applause normally; some gesture (by some singer) probably brought the audience's response to his attention. The dramatic-emotional weight of the story has carried it through the decades since, even as the strict documentary basis is partial.

The story's place in Beethoven's biographical narrative is the closing image of his public-performance life. The deaf composer, hearing the masterpiece he can no longer hear, turned by a young contralto to see the audience standing in tribute; this is the iconic image of the late Beethoven, often used as the visual-emotional summary of his last years.

#### What to notice

Three things across the four movements.

  1. **The opening of Movement I.** The first movement opens with emptiness — open fifths in the strings (just two notes, not a complete chord), with the orchestra gradually filling in the harmony. Listen for the _cosmic empty opening_ : this is not the dramatic chordal announcement of the _Eroica_ or the four-note hammer of the Fifth; this is something stranger, more _generative_ , as if the symphony is being assembled out of nothing.

  2. **The _Schreckensfanfare_ opening of Movement IV.** The fourth movement opens with a dissonant fanfare — _fortissimo_ full orchestra, with the woodwinds shrieking and the brass blaring, in a chord that Beethoven himself called _the fanfare of terror_ (_Schreckensfanfare_). After this, the cellos and basses play a _recitative_ , wordless instrumental speech, that rejects the themes of the first three movements before introducing the Ode to Joy melody. Listen for the _vocal-like_ quality of the instrumental recitative; Beethoven is preparing for the vocal entries that will come.

  3. **The Ode to Joy melody.** The cellos and basses introduce the melody in D major, played softly — the gentle entry of the great theme. The melody is then built up in successive variations: lower strings alone; lower strings with violas; full orchestra; eventually the chorus enters. Listen for the _gradual unfolding_ of the melody across the orchestral-variation introduction before the chorus enters.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed Symphony No. 9 in D minor "Choral," Op. 125 between 1817 (the earliest sketches) and February 1824 (the completion), premiered it on 7 May 1824 at the Kärntnertortheater in Vienna with Michael Umlauf conducting (Beethoven on stage as nominal conductor), and published it in 1826 with the dedication to King Friedrich Wilhelm III of Prussia.
