### 18.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the symphony once. Second sustained listen, with the timestamps focused on Movement IV. The earlier movements get briefer signposts.

#### Movement I: Allegro ma non troppo, un poco maestoso (0:00–17:00 in Furtwängler)

The first movement is a sonata-form Allegro in D minor at unprecedented scale. The opening:

* **0:00–0:30.** **The empty opening.** Open fifths in the strings (A-E), no third (so neither major nor minor at first). The harmony gradually fills in. Listen for the _generative_ quality: the symphony is being assembled out of emptiness.

* **0:30–1:30.** The first theme arrives: a descending figure in D minor, with the angular profile that anticipates the rest of the work.

* **1:30–onward.** The exposition continues. The first movement runs about seventeen minutes in Furtwängler. Major landmarks: the second-theme group around 4:00; the development section around 9:00; the recapitulation around 12:00; the coda from about 15:00.

The first movement is the dark argument of the work — D minor throughout, with harmonic adventure and structural ambition. It is one of the longest individual sonata-form first movements Beethoven composed.

#### Movement II: Molto vivace (17:00–28:00 in Furtwängler)

The Scherzo. Listen for:

* **17:00–17:30.** The opening: timpani-octave intervention: the timpani strikes an F (the opening pitch) with rhythmic force, interrupting the strings' opening phrase. The intervention is one of Beethoven's most-celebrated orchestrational moments — the timpani is a _thematic instrument_ rather than just a rhythmic-accompanying instrument.

* **17:30–25:00.** The fast scherzo argument. Beethoven uses complex rhythmic-metric patterns including _ritmo di tre battute_ (three-bar groupings) alongside the standard four-bar phrasing. The result is rhythmic disorientation that adds to the scherzo's wild character.

* **25:00–28:00.** The Trio section (in D major rather than D minor). Listen for the _brightening_ and the contrasting register.

About twelve minutes for the Scherzo in Furtwängler.

#### Movement III: Adagio molto e cantabile (28:00–45:00 in Furtwängler)

The slow movement. The spiritual peak of the work's middle movements. Listen for:

* **28:00–30:00.** The opening: a long lyrical melody in B♭ major in the strings, with the woodwinds providing soft countermelodies. The serenity is established immediately.

* **30:00–34:00.** A second theme in D major (a different key than expected). The two themes alternate across the variation form of the movement.

* **34:00–42:00.** Variations of the two themes. The melodies are taken through elaborations, with horn calls and woodwind solos providing contrasting timbres.

* **42:00–45:00.** The closing measures: the opening theme returns once more; the movement ends quietly in B♭ major.

About seventeen minutes for the third movement in Furtwängler. The variation form, the two-theme structure, the lyrical serenity are all the structural features.

#### Movement IV: Presto – Allegro assai – choral variations (45:00–70:00 in Furtwängler)

**The choral finale** — the chapter's anchor.

* **45:00–45:15.** **The _Schreckensfanfare_ (fanfare of terror).** Listen for the _dissonance_ : the full orchestra plays a fortissimo chord that includes unusually harsh dissonances. Beethoven called this chord the _Schreckensfanfare_. The fanfare is the announcement that _something new is required_ : the symphony cannot continue in the previous registers.

* **45:15–46:30.** **The instrumental recitative.** The cellos and basses play a slow, declamatory phrase — _wordless instrumental speech_. Listen for the _vocal-like_ quality. Beethoven is using the cellos-and-basses to _speak_ as a chorus or soloist would speak in opera.

* **46:30–48:00.** **The rejection of earlier themes.** Brief reminiscences of the first three movements appear in the orchestra: a passage from Movement I, then from Movement II, then from Movement III. Each is _rejected_ by the cellos-and-basses recitative: the _No, not this_ gesture, played out across the instrumental dialogue.

* **48:00–49:00.** **The introduction of the Ode to Joy melody.** The cellos and basses begin to play, very quietly, a new melody: the Ode to Joy. Listen for the _gentle entry_ of the great theme.

* **49:00–52:00.** **The orchestral variations on the Ode to Joy.** The melody is built up: first cellos and basses alone; then violas joining; then second violins; then full strings; then woodwinds; then full orchestra. The variations are Beethoven introducing the theme to the orchestra before introducing it to the chorus.

* **52:00–52:30.** **The Schreckensfanfare returns — its second appearance.** This time the dissonant chord is _answered directly_ by the **baritone soloist** , whose first words _address the fanfare itself_ : _"O Freunde, nicht diese Töne!"_ ("O friends, not these sounds! — rather let us tune our voices to something more agreeable and joyful"). The baritone is, in effect, rejecting the _Schreckensfanfare_ on behalf of the listener. Listen for this _first vocal entry_ in a symphonic work — Beethoven is bringing the human voice into the symphony for the first time in major repertoire, and the voice's first job is to _answer back_ to the orchestra. The baritone's text (which Beethoven wrote himself, not Schiller) introduces what is to come.

* **52:30–55:00.** **The first verse of the Ode to Joy** : the baritone soloist with chorus. _"Freude, schöner Götterfunken!"_ ("Joy, beautiful spark of the gods!"). Listen for the _human voice carrying the theme_ that the orchestra has just introduced.

* **55:00–58:00.** **The Turkish-march variation.** The Ode to Joy theme is now in a _military-band style_ : triangle, cymbals, bass drum entering for the first time in the symphony, with the tenor soloist singing _"Froh, wie seine Sonnen fliegen"_ ("Joyful as His suns fly"). The variation is one of the work's strangest moments — a Turkish-style march suddenly inserted into the choral-symphonic framework.

* **58:00–62:00.** **The instrumental fugue.** After the Turkish march, the orchestra plays a double-fugue on the Ode to Joy theme and a new countersubject. The fugue is the contrapuntal climax of the choral finale.

* **62:00–66:00.** **The chorus returns** with the main statement: _"Seid umschlungen, Millionen!"_ ("Be embraced, you millions!"). The text is the Schiller passage that Beethoven selected as the central humanist statement.

* **66:00–69:00.** **The four soloists' quartet entry.** All four vocal soloists sing together in close interweaving: the operatic-quartet moment within the symphonic-choral framework.

* **69:00–70:00.** **The closing prestissimo.** Full orchestra and chorus together in fast tempo. The work closes with the orchestral-choral celebration in D major.

About twenty-five minutes for Movement IV in Furtwängler. The choral-symphonic architecture, the vocal-orchestral integration, the variation form applied to a symphonic finale — these are the structural features.

### 18.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The choral-symphony innovation

The Ninth Symphony is the first major symphony to use voices in a structural sense. Beethoven had used voices in symphonic-scale works before (the _Choral Fantasy_ Op. 80 of 1808, where the theme that would become the Ode to Joy first appeared; the _Mass in C_ Op. 86 of 1807), but those were not symphonies. The Ninth is a symphony, in four movements, in the standard symphonic framework, that brings voices in for the fourth movement.

The innovation has many consequences. Subsequent composers, Berlioz, Mendelssohn, Liszt, Mahler, all wrote choral symphonies in the Ninth's wake. The genre of the _Choral Symphony_ (or _Symphony with chorus_) is Beethoven's invention; Mahler's Second ("Resurrection"), Third, Fourth, and Eighth all derive from the Ninth's precedent. Berlioz's _Symphonie funèbre et triomphale_ (1840), Liszt's _Faust_ Symphony (1857), and many others extend the choral-symphonic tradition.

The structural design of the Ninth's finale is also as innovative. The movement begins with instrumental music (recitatives, the Ode to Joy orchestral variations), then brings in voices, then alternates between instrumental and choral sections, with the variation form running throughout. The integration of choral and orchestral writing at this scale was unprecedented.

#### Schiller's _An die Freude_ — the text

Friedrich Schiller (1759–1805) wrote _An die Freude_ ("Ode to Joy") in 1785, when he was 26. The poem was a expression of late-Enlightenment universalist humanism — all humans as brothers under the universal Father, joy as the fundamental human emotion that unites all people regardless of nationality or class. The poem is in the stanzaic structure of late-18th-century German verse, with the main stanza ("Freude, schöner Götterfunken...") and a refrain ("Seid umschlungen, Millionen!").

Beethoven had contact with the poem from his Bonn years onward. He attempted to set the text as early as 1793 — the planning for a Schiller-Ode setting is in early-Bonn-period sketches. He returned to the project several times across his career. The 1808 _Choral Fantasy_ 's theme is the early version of what would become the Ode to Joy melody. The full setting in the Ninth is Beethoven's culminating engagement with the text after roughly thirty years of intermittent work.

Beethoven did not set the entire Schiller poem. He selected approximately one-third of the original text, in a arrangement that emphasizes the universalist-humanist passages and de-emphasizes some of the more abstract philosophical sections. The Beethoven editorial work is one of the lesser-known but important contributions to the symphony's text.

The text Beethoven did set:

* **Stanza 1** : "Freude, schöner Götterfunken..." (Joy, beautiful spark of the gods)
* **Refrain** : "Seid umschlungen, Millionen!" (Be embraced, you millions!)
* **Selected later stanzas** dealing with universal brotherhood and the cosmic-Father

The selection is the humanist statement that the Ninth has come to represent.

#### The Ode to Joy melody's cultural afterlife

The Ode to Joy melody has had a extensive cultural afterlife. The 1972 Council of Europe adoption of the melody as its anthem; the 1985 EEC (European Economic Community) adoption at the Milan Summit; the use of the melody in many subsequent contexts (the World War II broadcasts of the rhythm as "V for Victory" Morse code — though this was Symphony 5's opening, not Symphony 9's; the use in _Die Hard_ — actually a Vivaldi piece, not Beethoven; the use in many other media).

The 1972 Council of Europe adoption is the major institutional moment. The melody became the official anthem of the Council of Europe (then a political-cultural organization of European democracies); after the EEC's expansion into the European Union, the Ode to Joy became the official anthem of the EU as well. The 1985 Milan Summit adoption was the EEC's formal vote. The anthem is performed instrumentally (no text, no chorus) — a deliberate decision to avoid privileging any one European language by setting Schiller's German text.

The choice of the Ode to Joy as the European anthem reflects the work's humanist-universalist register. The melody was selected (over many other candidate works) because the Schiller text, universalist brotherhood, transcending national boundaries, fit the European-integration political-cultural project.

#### Definition: The choral symphony (Thread B return)

The choral symphony is the genre Beethoven invented with the Ninth. The genre's central feature: a symphonic work in the standard four-movement framework that incorporates choral writing as a structural component.

Documented descendants:

* **Berlioz's _Symphonie funèbre et triomphale_** (1840)
* **Mendelssohn's _Lobgesang_ Symphony 2** (1840)
* **Liszt's _Faust Symphony_** (1857)
* **Mahler's symphonies (Nos. 2 "Resurrection," 3, 4, 8)** (1894 onward)

The choral symphony is one of the major 19th-century symphonic developments. The genre's invention is the Ninth's most-influential structural contribution.

#### Definition: The variation form at choral-symphonic scale (Thread B return)

The Ninth's finale is a variation set on the Ode to Joy theme. The structural design: theme (gentle orchestral introduction); variations (orchestral, then choral, then Turkish-march variation, then fugue variation, then choral closing). The variation form is the structural foundation of the entire fourth movement.

The variation-form summit we have been tracing (Op. 109's variation finale, Op. 111's Arietta, the _Diabelli Variations_ , this Ninth finale) reaches its largest scale here — about twenty-five minutes of variations on a single melody, with orchestral and choral resources.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the Ninth is _primarily_ the Ode to Joy finale; that the first three movements are preparation for the choral finale. The misperception is partly true but misleading. The first three movements are not _only_ preparation; they are major movements in their own right. The Movement I sonata-form Allegro is one of Beethoven's most-ambitious first movements (rivaling the _Eroica_ and the Fifth). The Movement II Scherzo is innovative in its rhythmic-metric experiments. The Movement III Adagio is one of the great Beethoven slow movements, comparable to the Op. 132 _Heiliger Dankgesang_.

The choral finale is the work's culmination, but the work is _the four movements together_ — not a collection of preparatory movements leading to one famous finale. The proper way to hear the Ninth is to listen to all four movements in sequence, with the finale's arrival as the culminating moment of the whole symphonic argument.

### 18.4 The Stories

#### The 1817–1824 compositional history

The Ninth's compositional history spans roughly seven years. The earliest sketches date from 1817. Beethoven worked on the symphony intermittently from 1817 through 1822 while completing other major works (the late piano sonatas; the _Diabelli Variations_ ; the _Missa Solemnis_). The full composition was concentrated in the period from late 1822 through February 1824.

The turning point in the compositional process was Beethoven's decision (sometime in 1822–23) to include a choral finale. Documented sketches show that Beethoven considered multiple possible finales before settling on the choral setting of Schiller's text. One alternative was a purely instrumental finale (which Beethoven eventually used as the basis for the Op. 132 String Quartet's finale, 1825). The choice of the choral finale was a major compositional decision that committed the symphony to unprecedented scale.

The dedication to King Friedrich Wilhelm III of Prussia is Beethoven's response to Prussian patronage requests. The King was an amateur cellist and a Beethoven admirer; he had requested a major Beethoven work in the early 1820s. Beethoven dedicated the Ninth to him in part because of the Prussian patronage but also (per Lockwood) because the King's liberal-humanist political stance fit the Schiller text's universalist message. The King reportedly gave Beethoven a diamond ring in acknowledgment of the dedication; the documented ring's later history is uncertain.

#### The 1824 premiere context

The 7 May 1824 premiere was at the Kärntnertortheater in Vienna. The occasion was a benefit concert organized by Beethoven, with the proceeds going to him directly. The program included three works:

* **Overture _Die Weihe des Hauses_** (Op. 124, composed 1822 for the dedication of the Josefstadt-Theater)
* **Three movements from the _Missa Solemnis_** (the Kyrie, Credo, and Agnus Dei) — billed cautiously as _Three Grand Hymns_ because the Vienna church-music regulations did not permit full Mass settings outside religious contexts.
* **Symphony No. 9** : world premiere

The event was Vienna's first major Beethoven premiere in some years. The audience included the Vienna musical and political elite. The performance was a qualified success — the orchestra and chorus were under-rehearsed, the vocal quartet had difficulty with the high tessitura (the soprano part in particular reaches notes that are at the upper edge of soprano range), the Beethoven inability to coordinate with the orchestra in real time made the Umlauf conducting essential.

But the audience response was strong. The Caroline Unger turning gesture (whatever its exact form) is one of the work's founding stories. The work was repeated twice more in May 1824 at the Vienna Redoutensaal, with increasing success.

#### The 19th-century reception

The Ninth was received with mixed responses in the 1820s. Some critics found the work too long, too innovative, too demanding for orchestra and chorus. Others (notably the German Romantics) recognized the work as a major statement. The choral finale was particularly debated — some critics found the introduction of voices into the symphony a breaking of the genre; others found it a opening of new possibilities.

By the 1830s, the Ninth was being performed across Europe. The Paris premiere (under François-Antoine Habeneck, in 1831) was a major event that established the work in the French repertoire. The London premiere (under the Philharmonic Society, in 1825) was earlier but less successful. By the 1840s, the Ninth was in the major-symphonic repertoire of major European orchestras.

Wagner's engagement with the Ninth was substantial. He conducted the work many times (notably in Dresden in 1846); he wrote extensively about it (his 1849 _The Artwork of the Future_ devotes substantial space to the Ninth as the prototype of the _Gesamtkunstwerk_ — the total artwork that combines music, poetry, and drama); his own subsequent operatic-musical work is drawing on the Ninth's choral-symphonic-orchestral integration.

By the late 19th century, the Ninth was canonical. By the 20th century, it had become the symphonic statement of universal humanism — performed at moments of political-emotional significance (the 1945 Bayreuth performance celebrating the end of the war, the 1989 Berlin Wall fall performance under Leonard Bernstein with chorus singing "_Freiheit, schöner Götterfunken_ " — _Freedom_ substituted for _Joy_ in the Bernstein conductor's intervention). The work's cultural-political afterlife is unmatched.

#### Three readings of the Ninth

**Wilhelm Furtwängler / Bayreuth Festival Orchestra and Chorus, 29 July 1951 (EMI Bayreuth).** Furtwängler's Bayreuth performance is one of the great Ninth Symphony recordings. The occasion was the reopening of the Bayreuth Festival after WWII; the post-war emotional weight of the Ninth was at its peak. The performance is slow (about seventy-five minutes total), with extreme tempo flexibility, intense dynamic range, and a finale that builds to overwhelming climax. The recording sound is EMI live broadcast (lower fidelity than studio recordings, but musically present). The reading is the model metaphysical-Romantic Ninth.

**John Eliot Gardiner / Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique / Monteverdi Choir, 1992 (Archiv).** Gardiner's HIP reading uses period instruments (gut strings, natural horns, period winds) and faster tempi based on Beethoven's metronome markings. The whole work runs about sixty-five minutes — substantially faster than Furtwängler. The sharper articulation of the period winds and natural horns gives the work a clarity that the modern-orchestra readings do not have. The reading is the model HIP Ninth.

**Herbert Blomstedt / Leipzig Gewandhausorchester / GewandhausChor / MDR Rundfunkchor, 2017 (Accentus).** Blomstedt's late-career Ninth (he was 90 at the recording) is the model contemporary classical Ninth. The reading takes moderate tempi (about seventy minutes total), uses modern instruments but with HIP-informed articulation. The recording sound is contemporary high-fidelity. The reading is the late-Blomstedt statement of the work. (Note: this recording is under the 10-year safeguard window — included on the strength of substantial reviews in Gramophone, Fono Forum, Rondo, and the international classical press.)

#### Cross-piece links

* The Ninth's variation-form finale is the largest variation set in Beethoven's catalogue, connecting to **Op. 109** , **Op. 111** , **Diabelli Variations** (Chapter 17), and the **Symphony 7 Allegretto** (Chapter 11).
* The Ninth's choral writing connects to **Fidelio's Prisoners' Chorus** (Chapter 13) and the **Missa Solemnis** (Chapter 19) — Beethoven's three major vocal-choral statements.
* The Ninth's per aspera ad astra arc (D minor → D major triumph) connects to **Symphony 5's C minor → C major arc** (Chapter 9). The two works are the major Beethoven symphonic narratives in this arc.
* The Ninth's inheritance is to extend through Berlioz, Liszt, Mahler — the entire choral-symphony tradition of the 19th century.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the 7 May 1824 premiere — the Caroline Unger turning gesture, what is reliably documented and what depends on later memoirs. The episode is the iconic image of the deaf Beethoven hearing his masterpiece._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the choral finale felt most_ universal _to you on the second listen — the_ Schreckensfanfare, _the gentle introduction of the Ode to Joy in the cellos and basses, the Turkish-march variation, the closing prestissimo. The fourth movement has multiple peaks._
* _Ask the companion about the Wagner engagement with the Ninth (his 1849 essay treats the work as the Gesamtkunstwerk precedent), and about the Mahler engagement (his choral symphonies all descend from the Ninth's model)._
* _Bring up the Ode to Joy cultural afterlife with the companion — the 1972 Council of Europe adoption, the 1985 EEC adoption, the 1989 Berlin Bernstein "Freiheit" performance — and how the work has functioned as a universal-humanist statement._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The empty fifths opening at 0:00 — listen for the _generative_ quality of the symphonic opening, the assembly from nothing.
* The timpani-octave intervention at 17:00–17:30 — listen for the moment the timpani interrupts the strings as a _thematic instrument_.
* The _Schreckensfanfare_ at 45:00 — listen for the _dissonance_ , the "fanfare of terror" that announces the choral finale.
* The cellos-and-basses recitative starting at 45:15 — listen for the _vocal-like_ instrumental writing, the preparation for the vocal entries.
* The Ode to Joy melody from 48:00 — try to follow the _gradual unfolding_ from quiet cello-and-bass statement through orchestral variations to the choral entry at 55:00.
* The Turkish-march variation at 55:00 — listen for the _military-band style_ with triangle, cymbals, and bass drum.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Lewis Lockwood's chapter on the Ninth Symphony in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (W. W. Norton, 2003).** Lockwood treats the work with compositional and reception care. Pair with **Esteban Buch's _Beethoven's Ninth: A Political History_ (University of Chicago Press, 2003)** for the cultural-political afterlife — the use of the work in various political contexts from the 1820s through the late 20th century.

### Chapter Summary

Symphony No. 9 in D minor "Choral," Op. 125 (composed 1817–1824, premiered 7 May 1824 at the Kärntnertortheater in Vienna), is Beethoven's largest single work and the symphonic summit of his catalogue. The four movements together run about sixty-five to seventy minutes, with the choral finale of about twenty-five minutes setting Friedrich Schiller's _An die Freude_ (1785) in variation form.

You met the compositional history (1817 sketches; 1822–24 concentrated composition; the choice to use a choral finale rather than an instrumental one) and the 7 May 1824 premiere (the Umlauf-led conducting with Beethoven on stage as nominal conductor; the Caroline Unger turning gesture, treated with appropriate caution as a later-memoir story).

You met the four-movement structure: first movement empty-fifths opening and sonata-form D-minor argument; second movement Molto vivace Scherzo with timpani-octave intervention; third movement Adagio molto e cantabile slow movement in variation form; fourth movement choral finale with _Schreckensfanfare_ , instrumental recitative, Ode to Joy variations, Turkish march, fugue, and closing prestissimo.

You met the choral-symphony genre invention — Beethoven's innovation that became the foundation for Berlioz, Mendelssohn, Liszt, Mahler choral-symphonic writing.

You met the Ode to Joy cultural afterlife — the 1972 Council of Europe adoption, the 1985 EEC anthem adoption, the 1945 Bayreuth post-war performance, the 1989 Berlin Wall fall performance with Bernstein's "Freiheit" substitution.

You met the variation form at its largest scale (Thread B return) — the choral finale as a variation set on the Ode to Joy theme, with orchestral and choral variations.

You met three readings — Furtwängler 1951 Bayreuth (metaphysical-Romantic with post-war weight), Gardiner 1992 (HIP), Blomstedt 2017 (contemporary classical) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you to the _Missa Solemnis_ — the great sacred work composed in the same 1819–1823 period as the Diabelli Variations and the Ninth.

## Chapter 19: _Missa Solemnis_ , Op. 123

In 1819 Archduke Rudolph (Chapter 12, Chapter 15, Chapter 16) was elevated to Archbishop of Olmütz (now Olomouc in the Czech Republic). The Imperial archbishopric was one of the most-prestigious ecclesiastical positions in the Austrian Empire. The installation ceremony was scheduled for 9 March 1820. Beethoven, hearing the news in summer 1819, began work on a Mass for Rudolph's installation.

He missed the deadline by three years.

The _Missa Solemnis in D major_ , Op. 123, was completed in 1823. By that date, Rudolph had been Archbishop of Olmütz for three years. The work had grown so much larger than a standard Mass setting that it could not be completed within the original timeline; Beethoven had underestimated the compositional ambition required by his own vision. The work was eventually premiered, partially, on 7 May 1824 — the same Vienna concert that premiered the Ninth Symphony (Chapter 18), where three of the five movements (Kyrie, Credo, Agnus Dei) were performed under the cautious title _Three Grand Hymns_ to avoid Vienna church-music regulations.

The full _Missa Solemnis_ was first performed in St. Petersburg on 7 April 1824 (as Beethoven sent the score to Prince Galitzin, the Russian patron, who arranged the premiere there). The Vienna full premiere was much later. The work is one of the most-demanding choral-orchestral works in the repertoire — its technical difficulty, its length (about ninety minutes for the complete work), and its theological-musical ambition all combine to make the _Missa Solemnis_ a compositional summit.

This chapter anchors on two of the five movements: the **Kyrie** (the opening; about ten minutes) and the **Benedictus** (the spiritual peak with the famous solo-violin obbligato; about sixteen minutes).

### 19.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote, _"Von Herzen, möge es wieder zu Herzen gehen"_

Beethoven inscribed the autograph score of the _Missa Solemnis_ with a epigraph at the head of the Kyrie:

> _"Von Herzen — möge es wieder zu Herzen gehen."_ _"From the heart — may it go again to the heart."_

The inscription is in Beethoven's own hand on the autograph and is reproduced in modern critical editions of the score. The phrase has become the most-famous Beethoven aesthetic-spiritual statement — the declaration that the _Missa Solemnis_ is composed _from the heart_ and is intended to reach _to the heart_ of the listener.

The inscription is Beethoven's response to the question of what kind of object the _Missa Solemnis_ is. The work is, technically, a Mass setting — a setting of the Latin liturgy used in Catholic worship since the medieval period. Beethoven was a Catholic but was, by all accounts of his late life, not an observant one. The _Missa Solemnis_ is therefore not, in any straightforward sense, a _liturgical_ work composed for use in a specific liturgical context. Nor is it, however, a _secular_ work without religious content. The "from the heart" inscription positions the work in a third category: a spiritual-musical statement that uses the Mass form as a _vehicle_ for universal humanist-religious expression, without committing to confessional Catholicism.

The Beethoven engagement with the religious-musical tradition in the late period is substantial. The studies of Palestrina (the 16th-century Italian master of vocal polyphony), the studies of Handel (whose oratorios Beethoven studied intensively), the Bach engagement (the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ , the keyboard variations, the oratorios) — these are the background to the _Missa Solemnis_. Beethoven was studying the great tradition of religious-musical composition and was producing his own contribution to it. The "from the heart" inscription is the signal that the work is offered in this tradition while not being strictly liturgical.

What kind of listener is the _Missa Solemnis_ for? The answer is: anyone willing to engage with a serious, large-scale, technically demanding, spiritually ambitious work that uses the Catholic Mass form as its frame. The work is not for confessional Catholics only; it is not for agnostic listeners only. It is for the serious music listener, regardless of religious position, willing to give about ninety minutes to the spiritual-musical encounter.

#### What to notice

Three things across the Kyrie and Benedictus.

  1. **The Kyrie's opening D-major statement.** The work opens with a massive D-major orchestral chord, followed by the chorus and soloists singing "Kyrie eleison" (Lord have mercy). The opening establishes the work's monumental scale immediately. Listen for the _gravity_ of the opening; this is not a quiet liturgical entry but a major declarative statement.

  2. **The Benedictus's solo-violin obbligato.** Near the start of the Benedictus, after a slow orchestral introduction, a _solo violin_ enters with a long, lyrical melody that ascends into the violin's upper register. The solo violin sustains a prominent role across the entire Benedictus. Listen for the _singing violin_ : this is one of Beethoven's most-loved instrumental writings, set within the choral-orchestral framework of the Mass.

  3. **The choral-orchestral integration.** Throughout both movements, listen for the dialogue between chorus, vocal soloists, and orchestra. Beethoven's choral writing is technically demanding (the soprano part is at the extreme upper register; the tessitura is at the limit of what amateur choirs can sing); his orchestral writing is at full late-style power; the integration of the two is the compositional achievement.

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the _Missa Solemnis in D major_ , Op. 123 between summer 1819 and early 1823, originally intended for Archduke Rudolph's 9 March 1820 installation as Archbishop of Olmütz (which Beethoven missed by three years), and partial-premiered three movements (Kyrie, Credo, Agnus Dei) on 7 May 1824 at the same Vienna concert that premiered the Ninth Symphony.

### 19.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the two anchor movements once. Second listen, with timestamps into Klemperer 1965.

#### Kyrie (0:00–10:00 in Klemperer)

* **0:00–0:15.** **The opening D-major chord.** Massive orchestral fortissimo. The chorus then enters with the first "Kyrie": sustained, declamatory. Klemperer takes the opening at slow weighted tempo.

* **0:15–1:30.** The chorus continues with "Kyrie eleison" (Lord, have mercy). The polyphonic texture is established: multiple choral voices in independent lines, with the orchestra providing both support and independent counterpoint. Listen for the _complexity_ : this is not the simple homophonic choral writing of the Classical Mass tradition; this is the polyphonic complexity of Beethoven's late-period choral writing.

* **1:30–3:00.** The "Christe eleison" central section. The four soloists enter; the texture changes to soloist-with-chorus dialogue. The harmony shifts to B minor (the modulation that marks the Mass's middle Christe section).

* **3:00–4:30.** Continued Christe development. The texture builds; the contrapuntal interest intensifies.

* **4:30–7:00.** Return of the Kyrie: the closing section that mirrors the opening. The chorus returns to the home key of D major. The dynamic builds; the declarative quality returns.

* **7:00–9:00.** The Kyrie's closing buildup. The orchestra adds full weight; the chorus reaches climactic dynamic; the _Kyrie eleison_ is sustained at full force.

* **9:00–10:00.** The closing measures. The dynamic softens; the harmony moves toward closure in D major; the movement ends quietly with a sustained final chord.

About ten minutes for the Kyrie in Klemperer. The monumental opening, the polyphonic complexity, the A-B-A architectural design (Kyrie-Christe-Kyrie) are all the structural features.

#### Benedictus (within Sanctus, about 60:00–75:00 in Klemperer)

The Benedictus is the second half of the Sanctus movement of the Mass. (The full structure of the Mass: Kyrie, Gloria, Credo, Sanctus + Benedictus, Agnus Dei.) Beethoven sets the Sanctus and Benedictus as a single large movement, with the Benedictus as the second half.

* **0:00–1:30.** **The orchestral introduction to the Benedictus.** A slow, lyrical orchestral passage: strings, woodwinds, with the characteristic late-style harmonic adventure. The dynamic stays soft; the spiritual register is being established.

* **1:30–2:00.** **The solo violin enters.** Listen for the moment the solo violin begins its long ascending melody. The solo-violin obbligato is one of Beethoven's most-celebrated instrumental writings.

* **2:00–4:00.** The violin's continuing melody. The melody ascends into the upper register, sustained, lyrical, prayer-like. The chorus enters quietly with the "Benedictus" text — _"Blessed is he who comes in the name of the Lord"_.

* **4:00–8:00.** The Benedictus's main section. The solo violin continues with continuous lyrical writing; the chorus and soloists sing the text in soft and reverent dynamic; the orchestra provides gentle harmonic support. Listen for the _integration_ : the solo violin, the chorus, and the orchestra are woven into a single textural continuum.

* **8:00–12:00.** The "Hosanna in excelsis" central section. The dynamic builds; the chorus enters with more declarative writing; the solo violin sustains its presence above. The "Hosanna" is the moment of vocal celebration within the Benedictus.

* **12:00–14:00.** Return to the Benedictus material. The solo violin returns to its lyrical writing; the chorus quiets; the dynamic softens.

* **14:00–16:00.** The closing measures. The Benedictus ends quietly with the solo violin sustaining its lyrical line over the chorus's final cadence.

About sixteen minutes for the Benedictus in Klemperer. The solo-violin obbligato, the integration of chorus and orchestra, the "Hosanna" central section, the spiritual register are all the structural features.

### 19.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### The Mass form

The Catholic Mass setting is the major sacred genre of Western music since the medieval period. The Mass consists of five Ordinary movements (movements whose text is the same at every Mass, regardless of liturgical season):

  1. **Kyrie** — "Lord, have mercy" — the opening petition
  2. **Gloria** , "Glory to God in the highest", the hymn of praise
  3. **Credo** , "I believe in one God", the statement of faith
  4. **Sanctus** \+ **Benedictus** — "Holy, holy, holy" and "Blessed is he who comes" — the twin movements
  5. **Agnus Dei** , "Lamb of God", the closing petition

The Mass settings of the 18th and early 19th centuries (Haydn's many Masses, Mozart's Masses including the Requiem, early Schubert Masses) established the Classical-Mass-setting tradition. Beethoven's _Missa Solemnis_ is the largest and most-ambitious of the early-19th-century Mass settings, exceeding all his predecessors in scale and compositional difficulty.

Beethoven's innovations in the _Missa Solemnis_ :

* **Scale.** About ninety minutes for the complete Mass: longer than any Haydn or Mozart Mass.
* **Choral difficulty.** The tessitura (vocal range required) of the soprano and tenor parts is at the upper limit, beyond what most amateur choirs can sing. The professional-choir requirement was one of the work's obstacles to early performance.
* **Orchestral complexity.** Full late-style Beethoven orchestral writing, with contrapuntal and harmonic adventure throughout.
* **Solo writing.** The solo-violin obbligato in the Benedictus is unprecedented in the Mass tradition. Beethoven uses instrumental solo writing as a structural element of the Mass.

The _Missa Solemnis_ relationship to liturgical use is complicated. The work is technically a Mass setting in the standard five-movement form. It is, however, too long and too musically demanding for use in actual liturgical services (where the Mass setting must fit within the timing constraints of the actual liturgy). The _Missa Solemnis_ is, in practice, a concert work rather than a liturgical work — performed in concert halls and major-occasion contexts rather than in regular Sunday services. This separation from liturgical use is one of the work's characteristics; it is a Mass setting that has _outgrown_ the liturgical context.

#### The Benedictus solo violin

The solo-violin obbligato in the Benedictus is one of the most-loved Beethoven instrumental writings. The compositional design: the solo violin enters at the start of the Benedictus and sustains a continuous lyrical line through the entire movement, ascending into the violin's upper register and descending back, sometimes leading, sometimes accompanying the chorus.

The effect: the solo violin functions as a _transcendent voice_ above the chorus's terrestrial voice. The chorus sings the Benedictus text in human voice; the solo violin floats above in a voiceless register, providing what some commentators have described as the "angel's voice" or "spirit's voice" — a descent from above that complements the human chorus singing from below. Whether or not the listener takes the theological framing, the musical effect is clear: the solo violin and the chorus are at different _levels_ of the musical space.

Beethoven had used instrumental obbligato writing before — the Op. 58 Piano Concerto's slow movement (Chapter 8) uses the piano as a soloist against the orchestral strings — but the Benedictus's solo-violin treatment is an innovation in the choral-orchestral context. The technique would influence subsequent composers. Verdi's Requiem (1874) uses similar solo instrumental writing in choral-orchestral contexts. Brahms's _German Requiem_ (1865–69) uses related techniques.

#### Definition: The Mass setting at concert scale

The Mass-setting-at-concert-scale genre is the invention of late Beethoven and Berlioz (whose _Grande Messe des morts_ of 1837 and _Te Deum_ of 1849 are the descendants). The genre's central feature: a Mass setting too large, too compositionally demanding, and too musically ambitious for liturgical use — composed as a concert work in the Mass framework.

Documented descendants of the genre:

* **Berlioz's _Grande Messe des morts_** (Requiem, 1837)
* **Verdi's Requiem** (1874)
* **Brahms's _German Requiem_** (1865–69; not strictly a Mass setting but in the same "religious-concert" tradition)
* **Bruckner's Masses and _Te Deum_** (1860s–80s)
* **Stravinsky's _Symphony of Psalms_** (1930; the modernist successor)

The genre is one of Beethoven's most-influential late-period legacies. The "religious-concert" tradition that the _Missa Solemnis_ opened became one of the major 19th-century compositional categories.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the _Missa Solemnis_ is a less-loved work than the Ninth Symphony — overshadowed by the more-famous symphony composed in the same years. The misperception comes partly from the practical difficulty of performing the _Missa Solemnis_ (the choir difficulty, the orchestral complexity, the its length) and partly from the popular-cultural saturation of the Ninth (the Ode to Joy as European anthem, the many cultural appearances).

The _Missa Solemnis_ is one of the absolute great Beethoven works — Beethoven himself called it "my greatest work" in correspondence with publishers (the Beethoven evaluation has been variously interpreted but the statement is documented). Lewis Lockwood and Charles Rosen both treat the work as a major achievement, comparable to the Ninth and the late quartets. The work's relative absence from popular awareness is a function of its performance difficulty, not its compositional quality.

For a listener encountering the _Missa Solemnis_ for the first time, the best approach is to start with the Kyrie and the Benedictus (the chapter's anchor movements). After these, the Gloria and Credo (each about sixteen minutes) and the Agnus Dei (about fifteen minutes) can be sampled. The full work, in its complete ninety-minute form, rewards repeated listening over time.

### 19.4 The Stories

#### The 1819–1823 compositional history

Beethoven began work on the _Missa Solemnis_ in summer 1819, with the expectation of completion by the 9 March 1820 ceremony. The compositional ambition rapidly outgrew the timeline. The chronology:

* **Summer 1819 – early 1820:** Beethoven works on the Kyrie and Gloria. He misses the installation deadline.
* **1820 – early 1821:** The Credo. Beethoven works on the Mass throughout 1820–21 while also working on Op. 109 and (later) Op. 110.
* **1821 – 1822:** The Sanctus and Benedictus.
* **1822 – 1823:** The Agnus Dei. The Mass is completed in early 1823.
* **1823:** Beethoven offers the work to multiple potential subscribers (Cardinal Rudolph, the King of Prussia, the Tsar of Russia, the King of France, the King of Sweden, the King of Denmark, and others). The subscription scheme produced about ten purchasers at 50 ducats each: providing Beethoven with significant income from the work.
* **7 April 1824:** First full premiere in St. Petersburg (under Prince Galitzin's arrangement).
* **7 May 1824:** Partial Vienna premiere (Kyrie, Credo, Agnus Dei only).

The compositional history reveals a pattern: Beethoven began the work intending a commemorative gift to Rudolph, then expanded the work beyond its original scope, then eventually completed it as a major compositional statement in its own right. The Rudolph dedication was retained throughout, despite Beethoven's missing the original deadline.

#### Rudolph and the installation

Archduke Rudolph's elevation to Archbishop of Olmütz was the culminating step in the Imperial-family religious career path. The installation ceremony on 9 March 1820 was a major court event, with Imperial family attendance and full ecclesiastical pomp. Rudolph was 32 years old at the time of installation; he had been a amateur composer and serious musician for many years (he had studied composition with Beethoven from 1804).

Rudolph's response to Beethoven missing the deadline is not extensively but appears to have been gracious — the correspondence between Rudolph and Beethoven from 1820–23 shows continued warmth, with Rudolph receiving updates on the Mass's progress and the eventual completed work. The Rudolph-Beethoven friendship-and-patronage relationship (Chapter 12) was robust enough to survive Beethoven's delay.

Rudolph died in 1831, at age 43, of tuberculosis. The _Missa Solemnis_ dedication remained with him to the end. Beethoven's other Rudolph-dedicated works include Piano Concerto No. 4, _Les Adieux_ sonata, _Archduke_ Trio, Piano Concerto No. 5 (_Emperor_), _Hammerklavier_ sonata, Op. 111, and the _Grosse Fuge_ — a body of work that represents the central output of Beethoven's middle and late periods.

#### The Beethoven late-style religious engagement

The Beethoven engagement with religious-musical sources in the late period was substantial. Documented engagements include:

* **Palestrina studies.** Beethoven studied 16th-century vocal polyphony, particularly Palestrina's Masses. The late-style choral writing in the _Missa Solemnis_ draws on these studies.
* **Handel oratorios.** Beethoven studied Handel's oratorios (particularly the _Messiah_ and the other major works). The late-style choral-orchestral writing draws on these studies.
* **Bach engagement.** The Bach engagement (Chapter 15, Chapter 16, Chapter 17) included Bach Masses (the B-minor Mass was less in Vienna at this period, but Beethoven was familiar with Bach's sacred works through his Bonn-period studies and beyond).
* **Gregorian chant and modal materials.** Beethoven's late-style modal writing (the _Heiliger Dankgesang_ in the Lydian mode, in the next chapter) draws on late-medieval and Renaissance modal traditions.

These engagements together form the background for the _Missa Solemnis_. The work is not a isolated religious-musical statement; it is the fruit of years of Beethoven's study of the major religious-musical traditions.

#### The "From the heart" inscription

The inscription at the head of the Kyrie, _"Von Herzen, möge es wieder zu Herzen gehen"_ — has become the most-famous Beethoven aesthetic statement. It has been variously interpreted:

**Romantic-aesthetic reading.** The Romantic reading treats the inscription as the declaration of music's communicative function — music as the direct transmission of one heart's experience to another, bypassing words and reason. This reading is in 19th-century critical writing.

**Religious reading.** The religious reading treats the inscription as the Catholic-mystical statement — the heart that _gives_ the work is the heart of the worshipper offering praise to God; the heart that _receives_ the work is the heart of the listener as worshipper. This reading is in more religiously-oriented scholarship.

**Universalist-humanist reading.** The universalist reading treats the inscription as the statement that the _Missa Solemnis_ speaks not only to Catholics but to all humans of good will — the "heart" is the universal human heart, not the confessional Catholic heart. This reading is in 20th-century scholarship (particularly Lewis Lockwood's).

The three readings are not mutually exclusive. The inscription is broad enough to support all three. What is documented and reliable: Beethoven wrote the inscription; he intended the work to communicate heart-to-heart; the register is the universal-spiritual one that the _Missa Solemnis_ sustains throughout.

For this book — without dismissing the other two — the universalist-humanist reading sits most easily with the volume's overall stance. It connects the _Missa Solemnis_ 's heart-to-heart inscription to the Ninth Symphony's _Seid umschlungen, Millionen!_ and to Schiller's late-Enlightenment universalism, both of which are at work in the same 1819–1824 stretch. Beethoven's own religious life was not strictly confessional; his late-period engagement with Palestrina, Handel, and Bach was musical-spiritual rather than narrowly liturgical; and the _Von Herzen_ inscription's address to "the heart" is broad enough that it lands the same way on a Catholic listener, a Lutheran listener, an agnostic listener. The Romantic and religious readings remain available; the universalist-humanist reading is the one this volume has been quietly assuming throughout.

#### Three readings of the Missa Solemnis

**Otto Klemperer / New Philharmonia Orchestra and Chorus / Elisabeth Söderström / Marga Höffgen / Waldemar Kmentt / Martti Talvela, 1965 (EMI).** Klemperer's Missa Solemnis is one of the great recordings of the work. The reading is slow, weighted, dignified — Klemperer takes the Kyrie at full-tempo weight; the Benedictus at sustained lyrical pace. The soloist quartet is distinguished — Söderström was a major soprano; Kmentt and Talvela were major Austrian-German singers. The recording sound is the late 1960s EMI Kingsway Hall sound — warm, present, with choral and orchestral presence.

**John Eliot Gardiner / Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique / Monteverdi Choir, 1990 (Archiv).** Gardiner's HIP-informed Missa Solemnis uses period instruments and faster tempi based on Beethoven's metronome markings. The Monteverdi Choir is one of the finest period-style choirs. The reading is the model HIP Missa Solemnis. The recording sound is contemporary Archiv — clean, articulate.

**Nikolaus Harnoncourt / Concentus Musicus Wien, 2015 (Sony Classical).** Harnoncourt's late-career Missa Solemnis (he died in 2016, the year after this recording) is one of his great late recordings. The Concentus Musicus Wien is one of the early period-instrument ensembles. The reading is the model contemporary HIP Missa Solemnis.

#### Cross-piece links

* The Missa Solemnis's choral-orchestral integration connects to the **Ninth Symphony's choral finale** (Chapter 18). The two works share compositional resources from the same 1819–1824 period.
* The Beethoven engagement with religious-musical sources (Palestrina, Handel, Bach) connects to the **Op. 132's Heiliger Dankgesang** (Chapter 20) — the Lydian-mode slow movement that uses similar modal-religious materials.
* The "Mass at concert scale" genre Beethoven opened connects to **Berlioz's Requiem and Te Deum** , **Verdi's Requiem** , **Brahms's German Requiem** , **Bruckner's Masses** , **Stravinsky's Symphony of Psalms** — the 19th-and-20th-century descendants.
* The "From the heart" inscription connects to the late-style universalist register that runs through the Ninth, the late quartets, and the final works.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the "From the heart — may it go again to the heart" inscription — what the inscription says, how it has been interpreted (Romantic-aesthetic / religious / universalist-humanist readings), and how it positions the Missa Solemnis a religious-and-universal work._
* _Tell the companion which moment in the Benedictus felt most_ otherworldly _to you on the second listen, and ask her about the solo-violin obbligato and how Beethoven uses the violin as a "transcendent voice" above the chorus._
* _Ask the companion about the compositional history — why Beethoven missed the 9 March 1820 deadline for Rudolph's installation, how the work grew beyond its original scope, and how the dedication remained with Rudolph despite the three-year delay._
* _Bring up the Mass-at-concert-scale genre that Beethoven opened with the Missa Solemnis with the companion — Berlioz's Requiem, Verdi's Requiem, Brahms's German Requiem, Bruckner's Masses — and how the genre evolved through the 19th century._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The opening D-major chord at 0:00 — try to hear the _gravity_ of the orchestral opening before the chorus enters.
* The Christe central section of the Kyrie (1:30–4:30) — listen for the _modulation_ to B minor and the soloist-with-chorus texture.
* The solo violin's first entry in the Benedictus (around 1:30 in the Benedictus's local timing) — listen for the _moment_ the violin begins its long ascending melody.
* The "Hosanna" central section of the Benedictus — listen for the _vocal celebration_ within the otherwise quiet movement.
* The integration of chorus, soloists, and orchestra throughout — try to follow how Beethoven moves between different vocal-orchestral textures.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Lewis Lockwood's chapter on the _Missa Solemnis_ in _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (W. W. Norton, 2003).** Lockwood treats the work with compositional and biographical care, with attention to the Rudolph dedication and the compositional history. Pair with **Joseph Kerman's chapter on the _Missa Solemnis_ in his _Beethoven: The Mass and the Symphony_** (collected essays) for the detailed musical analysis.

### Chapter Summary

The _Missa Solemnis in D major_ , Op. 123 (composed 1819–1823, partial-premiered 7 May 1824 in Vienna with the Ninth Symphony), is Beethoven's great sacred work and one of the largest and most-compositionally-ambitious Mass settings in the 19th-century repertoire. The work was originally intended for Archduke Rudolph's 9 March 1820 installation as Archbishop of Olmütz; Beethoven missed the deadline by three years.

You met the "Von Herzen, möge es wieder zu Herzen gehen" ("From the heart, may it go again to the heart") inscription at the head of the Kyrie — Beethoven's aesthetic statement about the work's communicative function, variously interpreted as Romantic-aesthetic, religious, or universalist-humanist.

You met the Kyrie's opening D-major orchestral statement and the Kyrie-Christe-Kyrie A-B-A architectural design.

You met the Benedictus solo-violin obbligato — one of the most-loved Beethoven instrumental writings, with the solo violin functioning as a "transcendent voice" above the terrestrial chorus.

You met the Mass-at-concert-scale genre that the _Missa Solemnis_ opened — Berlioz's Requiem and Te Deum, Verdi's Requiem, Brahms's German Requiem, Bruckner's Masses are descendants.

You met the Beethoven late-style religious engagement — Palestrina studies, Handel oratorios, Bach engagement, Gregorian chant and modal materials — that forms the background of the Missa Solemnis.

You met the 1819–1823 compositional history — the expansion beyond original scope, the missed installation deadline, the subscription scheme to multiple royal courts.

You met three readings — Klemperer 1965 (slow-weighted Romantic), Gardiner 1990 (HIP), Harnoncourt 2015 (contemporary HIP) — and the work-vs-interpretation triangle they form.

The next chapter takes you to the late string quartets — Beethoven's chamber summit, composed in the same 1825–1826 period as the final years of his life.

* * *

---

## Chapter 20: The Late String Quartets — Op. 127, Op. 130, Op. 131, Op. 132, Op. 135, _Grosse Fuge_ Op. 133

In the final two years of his life, between November 1824 and October 1826, Beethoven completed five string quartets and one stand-alone movement (the _Grosse Fuge_) — the late string quartets that are by general critical consensus the summit of his catalogue and one of the great compositional achievements in Western music. The works:

* **Op. 127 in E♭ major** (1822–25; the first of the late quartets)
* **Op. 132 in A minor** (1825; with the famous _Heiliger Dankgesang_ slow movement)
* **Op. 130 in B♭ major** (1825–26; in its original form with the _Grosse Fuge_ as finale)
* **Op. 133 _Grosse Fuge_** (1825–26; the original Op. 130 finale, later published separately)
* **Op. 131 in C♯ minor** (1826; seven movements played without pause)
* **Op. 135 in F major** (1826; the final completed work)

These six works constitute the late-quartet cycle. Joseph Kerman's _The Beethoven Quartets_ (1967) is the source-of-record for the late-quartet analysis throughout this chapter; the Kerman framework has been extended by William Kinderman, Daniel Chua, and others, but Kerman remains the foundation.

This chapter's anchor is **Op. 131 in C♯ minor** — by general critical agreement the summit of the late quartets and one of the great Beethoven works. The work is in seven movements played without pause across about forty minutes. It is the work Karl Holz arranged to be performed for the dying Schubert in November 1828 (the Schubert-after-Op. 131 anecdote we will revisit in §20.4). The work's Movement IV, a long Andante set of variations, is the spiritual center.

We will also walk the other five late quartets in paragraph-level overview. The Volume 1 Chapter 14 covered Op. 132's _Heiliger Dankgesang_ and Op. 130's Cavatina; this chapter widens the lens to the full cycle.

### 20.1 Before You Listen

#### Hook anecdote — the Galitzin commission and the Schuppanzigh premiere

In 1822 Prince Nikolaus Galitzin (a Russian nobleman and amateur cellist living in St. Petersburg) wrote to Beethoven asking him to compose two or three string quartets. Galitzin was a serious amateur musician and a major Beethoven admirer; he had previously arranged the St. Petersburg premiere of the _Missa Solemnis_ (Chapter 19). The commission terms were generous — Galitzin offered Beethoven 50 ducats per quartet.

Beethoven accepted. He had not composed a string quartet since the Op. 95 _Serioso_ in 1810 — twelve years earlier. The Galitzin commission therefore brought Beethoven back to the string-quartet medium after a long absence. The composition began in 1822 with what would become Op. 127.

The compositional ambition outgrew the commission. Beethoven completed Op. 127 in 1825, then immediately began Op. 132 (1825), then Op. 130 (1825–26), then the _Grosse Fuge_ (1825–26), then Op. 131 (1826), then Op. 135 (1826). Five complete quartets and one substantial stand-alone movement in roughly two years. Only the first three (Op. 127, Op. 132, Op. 130) were strictly part of the Galitzin commission; the others were Beethoven's own continuation of the quartet project.

The Ignaz Schuppanzigh (Chapter 12) was the violinist for whom the late quartets were composed. Schuppanzigh's Schuppanzigh Quartet premiered most of the late quartets, with Schuppanzigh on first violin (his health and weight had declined by this period; the Beethoven joked about Schuppanzigh's increasing girth, but the professional collaboration continued). The premieres:

* **Op. 127:** 6 March 1825 (Schuppanzigh Quartet, Vienna)
* **Op. 132:** 6 November 1825 (Schuppanzigh, Vienna)
* **Op. 130 with _Grosse Fuge_ finale:** 21 March 1826 (Schuppanzigh, Vienna)
* **Op. 131:** Not premiered in Beethoven's lifetime; first performances in 1828
* **Op. 135:** Not premiered in Beethoven's lifetime; first performance in 1828
* **Op. 130 with new finale (the Allegro replacement):** April 1827, shortly after Beethoven's death

The premiere receptions were mixed. Op. 127's premiere was reportedly under-rehearsed; subsequent performances in spring 1825 were more successful. Op. 132's premiere was received with greater appreciation. The Op. 130 premiere with the _Grosse Fuge_ finale was bewildering to the audience — the contemporary reports describe listeners finding the _Grosse Fuge_ unplayable and ugly. This response led to the replacement-finale story (§20.4).

#### What to notice

Three things in Op. 131.

  1. **The opening fugue.** Op. 131 opens with a slow fugue in C♯ minor — Adagio ma non troppo e molto espressivo. Listen for the _contrapuntal opening_ : the fugue subject is a four-note phrase that descends from the first violin through the second violin, viola, and cello in turn. The fugue is one of the most-celebrated openings in the quartet literature. Slow, contemplative, prayer-like.

  2. **The seven movements without pause.** The work is in seven movements played continuously without break (the attacca structure). Movement I is the slow fugue (about seven minutes). Movement II is a fast Allegro (about two minutes). Movement III is a brief recitative-like passage. Movement IV is the long Andante variations (about thirteen minutes — the spiritual center). Movement V is a Presto scherzo (about five minutes). Movement VI is a brief slow passage. Movement VII is the fast finale (about six minutes). Listen for the _continuity_ : the work is a single sustained meditation across forty minutes, not as a sequence of separate movements.

  3. **The slow movement (Movement IV) variations.** The Andante is the spiritual center. It is a variation set on a long, lyrical, hymn-like theme in A major (the relative major of the home key). The variations build through expressive transformations across about thirteen minutes. Listen for the _spiritual register_ ; this is one of the great late-style slow movements, comparable to the Op. 132 _Heiliger Dankgesang_ and the Op. 106 Adagio (Chapter 15).

#### One-sentence background

Beethoven composed the String Quartet No. 14 in C♯ minor, Op. 131 between November 1825 and July 1826, dedicated it to Baron Joseph von Stutterheim (an army officer who had given Beethoven's nephew Karl a place in his regiment after Karl's July 1826 suicide attempt), and the work was not premiered in Beethoven's lifetime; the first performance was in 1828, after Beethoven's death.
