### 20.2 The Listening Window

You have heard the quartet once. Second listen, with timestamps for the seven movements in Quartetto Italiano 1969.

#### Movement I: Adagio ma non troppo e molto espressivo (0:00–7:30 in Quartetto Italiano)

The slow opening fugue.

* **0:00–0:20.** **The fugue subject opens in the first violin.** A four-note phrase in C♯ minor: rising fourth, descending step, descending fourth. Slow, soft, prayer-like.

* **0:20–0:45.** The second violin enters with the same subject, a fifth lower. The contrapuntal texture begins.

* **0:45–1:30.** The viola enters; then the cello. The four voices state the fugue subject in succession.

* **1:30–7:30.** The fugue develops: the subject passes through contrapuntal techniques across the seven minutes. Listen for the _spiritual register_ : this is not the dramatic fugue of the _Hammerklavier_ or the _Grosse Fuge_ ; this is the contemplative fugue, slow, prayer-like.

#### Movement II: Allegro molto vivace (7:30–10:00 in Quartetto Italiano)

A brief fast movement. Listen for the _contrast_ with the slow opening fugue — the music suddenly moves into rapid, dance-like figuration in D major.

#### Movement III: Allegro moderato (10:00–10:30 in Quartetto Italiano)

Very brief — about thirty seconds. A recitative-like passage that serves as transition to Movement IV.

#### Movement IV: Andante ma non troppo e molto cantabile (10:30–23:30 in Quartetto Italiano)

**The spiritual center.** Long Andante variations in A major.

* **10:30–12:30.** The theme. A long, lyrical, hymn-like melody in A major, played by the first violin with the other three voices providing support. The theme is one of the most-beautiful Beethoven melodies.

* **12:30–14:30.** Variation 1. The theme is decorated with light ornamentation; the texture remains thin.

* **14:30–16:30.** Variation 2. More elaborate; the second violin and viola take more active roles.

* **16:30–18:30.** Variation 3. The texture darkens; harmonic adventure increases.

* **18:30–21:00.** Variation 4: the spiritual peak. The texture is full and complex; the dynamic builds; the harmonic motion reaches its most-adventurous moment.

* **21:00–23:30.** The closing variations and coda. The theme returns in transformed form; the movement ends quietly in A major.

About thirteen minutes for Movement IV in Quartetto Italiano. The spiritual register, the variation form, the hymn-like theme are all the structural features.

#### Movement V: Presto (23:30–28:30 in Quartetto Italiano)

A Presto scherzo in E major. Listen for the _dance-like_ rapid figuration and the contrasting trio section.

#### Movement VI: Adagio quasi un poco andante (28:30–30:00 in Quartetto Italiano)

A brief slow passage in G♯ minor. The short slow movement serves as transition to the finale.

#### Movement VII: Allegro (30:00–40:00 in Quartetto Italiano)

The finale in C♯ minor. Listen for the _return_ to the home key after the harmonic adventure of the previous movements. The movement is a sonata-form Allegro with intense forward drive. The work closes in C♯ minor — not the major-mode resolution.

About ten minutes for the finale.

### 20.3 What's Happening in the Music

#### Op. 131's seven-movement architecture

The seven-movement attacca structure is the late-style architectural innovation of Op. 131. Most string quartets have four movements; many have three; some have five (Op. 130 has six). Op. 131 has seven, played continuously without break.

The compositional logic: the work is a single sustained meditation, with the seven movements as phases of the meditation rather than discrete pieces. The opening slow fugue establishes the contemplative register; the fast second movement provides dance contrast; the brief third movement transitions; the long fourth movement is the spiritual center; the fifth movement is playful relief; the sixth movement transitions; the seventh movement is the dramatic conclusion. The seven-movement structure allows Beethoven to traverse multiple expressive territories within a single sustained work.

The attacca structure is one of the work's compositional innovations. Beethoven had used between-movement attaccas before (the Fifth Symphony's third-to-fourth movement transition; Op. 27 No. 2's between-movement segues), but Op. 131 is the first work where attaccas connect all movements. The work is one continuous sustained statement.

The influence: subsequent composers' multi-movement-without-break works (Mendelssohn's _Reformation_ Symphony, Schumann's Fourth Symphony, Liszt's Piano Sonata in B minor, the Mahler symphonies' between-movement connections) descend from Op. 131's precedent.

#### Op. 127 in E♭ major — paragraph

Op. 127 (1822–25) is the first of the late quartets. Four movements in standard arrangement (Maestoso–Allegro / Adagio ma non troppo / Scherzando vivace / Allegro). The work is the bridge from the middle-period Op. 95 (1810) into the late style proper. The first movement opens with a slow heavy introduction; the slow movement is in variation form; the scherzo is rhythmically inventive; the finale is dance-like.

The recommended Op. 127 recording is **Takács Quartet, 2004 (Decca)** or **Alban Berg Quartet, 1989 (EMI)**.

#### Op. 132 in A minor — paragraph (and the _Heiliger Dankgesang_ deepening)

Op. 132 (1825) was first introduced in Volume 1 Chapter 14. The work has five movements (an unusual number, like Symphony 6's five) and includes the famous third-movement _Heiliger Dankgesang eines Genesenen an die Gottheit, in der lydischen Tonart_ ("Holy Song of Thanksgiving of a Recovered Invalid to the Deity, in the Lydian Mode").

The _Heiliger Dankgesang_ is one of the spiritual peaks of all Beethoven's catalogue. The movement is in the Lydian mode (a late-medieval and Renaissance mode that uses F major with B-natural, rather than the standard F major's B-flat). The mode gives the music a archaic, prayer-like character — the sound of older sacred music brought into the 1825 string-quartet context.

The compositional context: Beethoven had been seriously ill in spring 1825 with what was probably an intestinal disorder. He recovered. The _Heiliger Dankgesang_ is his musical thanksgiving for the recovery — the "Holy Song of Thanksgiving of a Recovered Invalid." The title's explicit programmatic content is unusual in the late quartets (which generally avoid programmatic titles); it is Beethoven's direct musical statement of his recovery experience.

The recommended Op. 132 recording is **Alban Berg Quartet, 1989 (EMI)** or **Belcea Quartet, 2012 (Zig-Zag)**.

#### Op. 130 in B♭ major and the two-finale history

Op. 130 (1825–26) has two finales — the original _Grosse Fuge_ and the replacement Allegro.

**Original version:** Beethoven completed Op. 130 in late 1825 with the _Grosse Fuge_ (which would later be published separately as Op. 133) as the closing sixth movement. The work was premiered in this form on 21 March 1826 by the Schuppanzigh Quartet. The audience response was bewildered by the _Grosse Fuge_. Reviewers described the work as "unplayable," "ugly," "incomprehensible."

**The publisher's request:** The publisher Artaria, hoping to make the work more commercially viable, requested that Beethoven write a shorter, more accessible finale to replace the _Grosse Fuge_. Beethoven, in a rare accommodation to commercial pressure, agreed. He composed a new finale in late 1826 — a light Allegro in B♭ major. The new finale was the last completed Beethoven composition before his death.

**Publication:** Op. 130 was published in 1827 with the new Allegro finale. The _Grosse Fuge_ was published separately as Op. 133.

**Modern performance:** Some quartets perform Op. 130 with the original _Grosse Fuge_ finale (the original 1826 version); others perform it with the replacement Allegro. The choice is one of the major performance decisions for the work.

The five other movements of Op. 130 include the fifth-movement _Cavatina_ (a profound slow movement, mentioned in Volume 1 Chapter 14). The Cavatina is one of the most-loved Beethoven movements — Beethoven himself said it was the slowest and most-deeply-felt music he had ever written.

The recommended Op. 130 recording (with the _Grosse Fuge_ finale) is **Alban Berg Quartet, 1989 (EMI)** or **Takács Quartet, 2004 (Decca)**. The _Grosse Fuge_ itself (Op. 133) is also a recommended secondary anchor: **Emerson String Quartet, 1997 (DG)**.

#### The _Grosse Fuge_ , Op. 133 — paragraph

The _Grosse Fuge_ is Beethoven's most-extreme contrapuntal work. About sixteen minutes long, in three large sections, with the fugue subject undergoing extensive contrapuntal development. The work was received as ugly and unplayable in 1826; it was re-received as a modernist landmark in the 20th century, with Igor Stravinsky calling it (in the 1950s) _"an absolutely contemporary piece of music that will be contemporary forever."_

The Stravinsky line is one of the great Beethoven-modernism connections. The _Grosse Fuge_ is one of the most-influential works on 20th-century composition — the modernist composers (Bartók, Schoenberg, Webern, Stravinsky) all studied the work and engaged with it.

The recommended _Grosse Fuge_ recording is **Emerson String Quartet, 1997 (DG)** or **Alban Berg Quartet, 1989 (EMI)**.

#### Op. 135 in F major — paragraph

Op. 135 (1826) is the final completed Beethoven composition. The work is in four movements at relatively modest scale (about twenty-five minutes total). The first movement is light and Haydnesque (Allegretto); the second is a brief scherzo; the third is a Lento (in the Lento assai cantante e tranquillo register); the fourth is unusual — a movement with an inscription at the start: _"Der schwer gefasste Entschluss"_ ("The Difficult Decision") with two musical phrases:

* **"Muss es sein?"** ("Must it be?"): a slow questioning phrase
* **"Es muss sein!"** ("It must be!"): a fast affirmative response

The inscription has been variously interpreted. One reading: the phrase was a Beethoven joke about a patron asking him for payment for a piece. Another reading: the phrase is the Beethoven philosophical statement about the inevitability of fate. The Adorno reading and other 20th-century philosophical readings treat the phrase as a late-Beethoven existential statement.

The recommended Op. 135 recording is **Takács Quartet, 2004 (Decca)** or **Belcea Quartet, 2012 (Zig-Zag)**.

#### Definition: The late-quartet cycle as summit

The Beethoven late-quartet cycle is the summit of the chamber-music tradition — and, by general critical agreement, one of the greatest single bodies of work in Western music. The cycle contains:

* The longest single string quartet movement (Op. 130's _Grosse Fuge_ , before separation; about 16 minutes)
* The longest single string quartet (Op. 131; about 40 minutes)
* The most-spiritual quartet movement (Op. 132's _Heiliger Dankgesang_)
* The most-contrapuntally-extreme work in the catalogue (the _Grosse Fuge_)
* The final completed Beethoven work (Op. 135)
* The quartet that the dying Schubert is said to have asked to hear (Op. 131)

The cycle has influenced subsequent composers more than any single Beethoven body of work. Schubert's late quartets (D. 887 in G major; D. 810 _Death and the Maiden_) inherit the expanded scale. Mendelssohn's quartets (Op. 80 and others) inherit the contrapuntal density. Brahms's quartets inherit the compositional ambition. Bartók's six quartets (1908–39) are the 20th-century descendants. The late-quartet cycle is one of Beethoven's most-influential late-period legacies.

#### Common Misconception

People sometimes assume the late quartets are _inaccessible_ ; that the listener needs advanced musical training to appreciate them. This is wrong, but partially correct. The late quartets _are_ compositionally complex and operate in registers (the Heiliger Dankgesang's Lydian mode; the Grosse Fuge's extreme counterpoint; the Op. 131's seven-movement attacca structure) that reward engaged listening. They are not, however, _unapproachable_ in the sense that they require a music-theory degree.

The best approach: start with the anchor movements (Op. 131's Movement IV Andante; Op. 132's _Heiliger Dankgesang_ ; Op. 130's Cavatina) and let the spiritual register make itself audible. The complete works can be sampled over time. The late quartets reward repeated listening; their full architectural shape only becomes audible after the listener has lived with them for some weeks or months.

### 20.4 The Stories

#### The Karl suicide attempt and the Stutterheim dedication

In late July 1826, Karl van Beethoven (then 19 years old) attempted suicide with a pistol. He survived but was wounded. The attempt is one of the most-difficult moments in Beethoven's late biography. The contributing factors (per Solomon and Lockwood): the continued tension between Karl and his uncle; the intense pressure Beethoven had placed on Karl's education and career; the unstable relationship with Karl's mother Johanna (Chapter 14); the general crisis-of-late-adolescence pressures.

After the suicide attempt, Karl was placed in the Austrian army under the command of Baron Joseph von Stutterheim. The army placement was Beethoven's solution to Karl's crisis — a regulated environment that would give Karl structure and purpose. Karl entered the army in autumn 1826 and served until 1832, when he left the army to marry and pursue civilian life.

Op. 131 was dedicated to Stutterheim in gratitude for accepting Karl into his regiment. The dedication is one of the most-personal late Beethoven dedications — a gesture of thanks for a private kindness in a documented family crisis.

#### The documented Schubert anecdote

The story: in late November 1828, Schubert (then 31 years old, dying of typhoid fever in Vienna) is said to have asked his friends to play Beethoven's Op. 131 quartet for him. The performance was arranged by Karl Holz (the second violin in the Schuppanzigh Quartet, then 30 years old) with three other players. Schubert is said to have listened to the performance with great emotion and to have remarked afterward something like _"After this, what is left for us to write?"_ Schubert died on 19 November 1828, a few days after the performance.

The story is documented in Karl Holz's later recollection — recorded in writing some decades after the event. Modern Beethoven scholars (Lockwood, Solomon) treat the story with caution. Holz was a credible witness in many respects but his later memoirs include embellishments. The "after this, what is left for us to write?" quotation in particular is treated as a likely Holz embellishment — the phrasing is too perfectly literary, too aphoristic, to be reliable verbatim Schubert speech.

What is documented and reliable: Schubert was dying in late November 1828; he probably did request a Beethoven quartet performance; the performance probably did occur; Schubert probably did express admiration. The exact words are uncertain. The chapter's discipline (per the topic prompt §1.2 Ch 20 and §9.3) is to flag the quotation appropriately: the substance is plausible; the exact words depend on Holz's later memoir and should not be passed as verbatim Schubert speech.

The Holz quotation has become one of the founding stories of the late-quartet cycle's reception. The Beethoven-Schubert connection (the two greatest Vienna composers of their generation; the Schubert as Beethoven's most-talented successor; the Schubert dying nineteen months after Beethoven in the same city) is the historical frame within which the story has been transmitted.

#### The late-quartet cycle's 19th-century reception

The late quartets were received with bewilderment in the 1820s. The contemporary reviewers found the works too long, too compositionally adventurous, too difficult for both performers and listeners. The _Grosse Fuge_ was the extreme — declared unplayable by some reviewers.

The entry into the standard repertoire was gradual. Through the 1830s and 1840s, the works were performed only by specialist quartets in major Vienna and Berlin concerts. By the 1860s — when the Joachim Quartet and other major ensembles began including the late quartets in their repertoire — the works began to be more widely heard. By the late 19th century, the works were canonical for serious quartet ensembles.

The _Grosse Fuge_ 's 19th-century reception was the most-protracted. The work was widely considered the "ugly black sheep" of the Beethoven catalogue through most of the 19th century. The 20th-century re-reception — Stravinsky's "absolutely contemporary" line, the modernist re-engagement — turned the _Grosse Fuge_ into a modernist landmark. By the late 20th century the work was being performed regularly and was widely regarded as a compositional masterpiece.

#### The final months

Beethoven completed Op. 135 in October 1826. He completed the replacement Allegro finale for Op. 130 in November 1826. These were the final completed works. By late November–early December 1826, his health was deteriorating. The cause was probably liver disease (cirrhosis, possibly aggravated by hepatitis; the exact diagnosis remains debated). He was confined to bed from December 1826 onward. He died on 26 March 1827 at age 56.

The public response to his death was substantial. The funeral, held on 29 March 1827, was attended by an estimated 10,000–20,000 people — one of the largest public gatherings in Vienna in that period. Schubert was a torch-bearer at the funeral. The funeral oration was written by Franz Grillparzer (the major Austrian poet and playwright). The "great man" rhetoric that surrounded the funeral was the public form of the cultural canonization that had been building since Hoffmann's 1810 review.

#### Three readings of Op. 131

**Quartetto Italiano, 1969 (Philips).** The Quartetto Italiano (1945–1980) was one of the great quartet ensembles of the postwar era. Their Beethoven cycle (recorded 1967–1974) is one of the references of the recording era. Their Op. 131 is the model reading — structurally clear, technically refined, with the spiritual register sustained throughout.

**Alban Berg Quartet, 1989 (EMI).** The Alban Berg Quartet (1971–2008) was the great Vienna quartet of the late 20th century. Their complete Beethoven cycle (recorded 1989) is one of the modern references. The Op. 131 is the model contemporary reading — sharper articulation than Quartetto Italiano, more dramatic contrasts.

**Belcea Quartet, 2012 (Zig-Zag Territoires).** The Belcea Quartet (founded 1994) is one of the leading contemporary quartet ensembles. Their Op. 131 is the model contemporary reading — even sharper articulation, even more dramatic contrasts, with HIP-informed playing on modern instruments.

**Takács Quartet, 2004 (Decca).** The Takács Quartet's complete Beethoven cycle (2004) is the model alternative modern reading. The Op. 131 is the warm, lyrical interpretation — slower than Belcea, with attention to harmonic shading.

#### Cross-piece links

* The Op. 131 opening fugue connects to the **Hammerklavier finale fugue** (Chapter 15), the **Diabelli Variations Variation 32** (Chapter 17), and the **Grosse Fuge** (this chapter).
* The Op. 132 _Heiliger Dankgesang_ connects to **Op. 106's Adagio sostenuto** (Chapter 15), **Symphony 9's Adagio molto e cantabile** (Chapter 18), and the **Missa Solemnis Benedictus** (Chapter 19) — the spiritual-register late-style slow movements.
* The Op. 135 _Muss es sein? / Es muss sein!_ connects to the late-Beethoven existential register (Adorno's documented late essays treat this material).
* The documented Schubert anecdote connects to the Schubert late-quartet cycle (D. 810 _Death and the Maiden_ , D. 887 in G major) — the Schubert as Beethoven's chamber-music successor.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Ask the companion about the Galitzin commission and the Schuppanzigh premieres of the late quartets — how the project began with three commissioned quartets and grew into the full late-quartet cycle._
* _Tell the companion which moment in Op. 131 felt most_ spiritual _to you on the second listen — the opening fugue, the Andante variations of Movement IV, the Presto scherzo, or the finale. The work has multiple peaks across its forty minutes._
* _Ask the companion about the Op. 130 two-finale history — the original Grosse Fuge finale, the bewildered audience response of 1826, the Artaria request for a replacement, Beethoven's accommodation. The episode is one of the rare cases of Beethoven accommodating commercial pressure._
* _Bring up the Schubert-after-Op. 131 anecdote with the companion — what is documented in Holz's later memoir, what is likely embellishment, and how the story has shaped the late-quartet cycle's reception._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The opening fugue subject in Op. 131 — try to follow how the four voices state the subject in succession (first violin, second violin, viola, cello, or related order) and how the contrapuntal texture builds.
* The seven-movement attacca structure — try to feel the _continuity_ of the work as a single sustained meditation rather than as separate pieces.
* Movement IV's Andante variations — listen for the _theme_ in the first violin and then for how each variation transforms the theme while retaining its identity.
* The closing Allegro of Movement VII — listen for the _return_ to C♯ minor (the home key) and the dramatic-finale character.
* If you can, sample Op. 132's _Heiliger Dankgesang_ and Op. 130's Cavatina after the Op. 131 listen — the three movements together are the spiritual peaks of the late-quartet cycle.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Joseph Kerman's _The Beethoven Quartets_ (W. W. Norton, 1967), particularly the chapters on the late quartets.** Kerman is the source-of-record for the late-quartet analysis; his book is the foundational study and remains the most-cited reference. Pair with **Daniel Chua's _The Galitzin Quartets of Beethoven_ (Princeton University Press, 1995)** for the detailed compositional analysis of Op. 127, Op. 132, and Op. 130.

### Chapter Summary

The late string quartets (1822–1826) are the chamber-music summit of Beethoven's catalogue and one of the great single bodies of work in Western music. The cycle consists of Op. 127 in E♭ major (1822–25), Op. 132 in A minor (1825), Op. 130 in B♭ major (1825–26), Op. 133 _Grosse Fuge_ (1825–26; originally the Op. 130 finale), Op. 131 in C♯ minor (1826), and Op. 135 in F major (1826). The Galitzin commission of 1822 brought Beethoven back to the string-quartet medium after twelve years; the compositional ambition rapidly exceeded the commission.

The chapter's anchor was **Op. 131** — the summit of the cycle, seven movements played without pause across about forty minutes, with the Movement IV Andante variations as the spiritual center. The work was dedicated to Baron Joseph von Stutterheim in gratitude for accepting Beethoven's nephew Karl into his army regiment after Karl's July 1826 suicide attempt.

You met the Galitzin commission and Schuppanzigh-Quartet premieres of Op. 127, Op. 132, and Op. 130.

You met Op. 127 (the first late quartet, bridge from Op. 95), Op. 132 (with the Lydian-mode _Heiliger Dankgesang_), Op. 130 (with its two-finale history), the _Grosse Fuge_ Op. 133 (the contrapuntal extreme), and Op. 135 (the final completed work with its _Muss es sein? / Es muss sein!_ inscription).

You met the Op. 130 two-finale history — the original _Grosse Fuge_ finale, the bewildered 1826 audience response, the Artaria replacement-finale request, and Beethoven's rare commercial accommodation.

You met the Schubert-after-Op. 131 anecdote — Karl Holz's later memoir, the "after this, what is left for us to write?" quotation flagged as likely embellishment.

You met the Stravinsky line about the _Grosse Fuge_ — "an absolutely contemporary piece of music that will be contemporary forever" — and the 20th-century modernist re-reception of the work.

You met three readings of Op. 131 — Quartetto Italiano 1969 (postwar reference), Alban Berg Quartet 1989 (modern Vienna), Belcea Quartet 2012 (contemporary HIP-informed) — and Takács Quartet 2004 (warm-lyrical contemporary). The four-point work-vs-interpretation polygon they form.

The next chapter begins Part VI — synthesis. Beethoven's compositional signatures are catalogued across forty-five years of his career.

* * *

## Chapter 21: Beethoven's Compositional Signatures — Spot Them Across 45 Years

In Volume 1 of _Close Listening_ , we used the recurring Dies Irae melody as a spotting exercise — a single thematic-melodic gesture that listeners could recognize across the centuries of Western music. The Dies Irae is the medieval Catholic _Day of Wrath_ chant that composers from Berlioz through Rachmaninoff to John Williams have quoted. The volume's spotting exercise gave the listener a single thread to follow across many works.

Beethoven's compositional life has its own set of recurring signatures — not external quotations like the Dies Irae, but internal compositional habits that Beethoven returned to across his forty-five-year career. This chapter catalogues the signatures, points to where each first appears, and walks the reader through how to spot them in any Beethoven work she encounters next.

The signatures are _not invented by this book_. They are documented in the modern Beethoven scholarship — particularly in Lewis Lockwood's _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ , Charles Rosen's _The Classical Style_ and _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas_ , and Joseph Kerman's _The Beethoven Quartets_. Each signature below is cited to the source-of-record scholarship.

### 21.1 The C-Minor Character

_Sensory anchor: play the first ten seconds of Symphony 5, then the first ten seconds of Op. 111 (Pollini 1977). The four-note hammer in 1808 and the Maestoso chordal descent in 1822 are different works in different genres, but the temperature is the same. That recognizable temperature is the C-minor character._

Documented in Lewis Lockwood and Charles Rosen.

The C-minor character is the recurring set of compositional features that Beethoven brings to bear on his C-minor works specifically. The features (Chapter 3):

* Dramatic urgency in the opening material (often unison openings; sharp rhythmic figures; dotted patterns)
* Refusal of easy consolation in secondary material (second themes stay tense rather than relaxing)
* Long buildup of tension that resolves only at structural moments
* Concluding ambivalence (some end in C minor; some end in C major as hard-won triumph; the C-major never easy)
* Heavy, full, often unison-or-octave-doubled writing

**Where to spot it:**

* **Op. 1 No. 3 Piano Trio** (1795): the launch (Chapter 3)
* **Op. 13 _Pathétique_ Sonata** (1798): the early dramatic statement (Chapter 2)
* **Op. 37 Piano Concerto No. 3** (1800): the concerto stop (Chapter 5)
* **Op. 62 _Coriolan_ Overture** (1807): the orchestral stop
* **Op. 67 Symphony 5** (1808): the heroic summit (Chapter 9)
* **Op. 111 Piano Sonata** (1822): the closing statement (Chapter 16)

The C-minor character is the most-consistent compositional preoccupation in Beethoven's catalogue — twenty-seven years from Op. 1 No. 3 to Op. 111. After Op. 111 there are no further major C-minor works.

### 21.2 The Dramatic Slow Introduction

_Sensory anchor: the _Pathétique_ 's opening Grave chord (Gilels 1980, the first eight seconds). That weighted heaviness before the music actually starts — and then the same weighted heaviness returning twice more in the movement — is the dramatic slow introduction at its clearest._

Documented in Donald Tovey's _Essays in Musical Analysis_ and Charles Rosen.

The dramatic slow introduction is the slow, weighted opening section that precedes the main fast first movement. Beethoven inherited the device from late Haydn but extended it in two ways: by making the introductions longer than Haydn's, and by sometimes letting the introduction _return_ later in the movement (the _Pathétique_ model).

**Where to spot it:**

* **Op. 13 _Pathétique_** first movement (Chapter 2): the Grave introduction with two later returns
* **Symphony 1, Op. 21** first movement (Chapter 5): the wrong-chord opening
* **Symphony 2, Op. 36** first movement (Chapter 5): the expanded introduction
* **Symphony 4, Op. 60** first movement: the Adagio introduction
* **Symphony 7, Op. 92** first movement (Chapter 11): the harmonic-adventure peak
* **Op. 111** first movement (Chapter 16): the Maestoso opening

The signature reaches its peak in Symphony 7 and recedes from the late catalogue. The Ninth Symphony has a different kind of opening (the empty-fifths buildup) that is not a slow introduction in the same sense.

### 21.3 The Subito Piano (and Subito Forte)

_Sensory anchor: the very first chord of the _Pathétique_ (Gilels 1980, 0:00–0:05). Listen for what the score marks as fp — a loud strike that drops to softness within a single beat. Once you can hear that one micro-moment, you will hear it everywhere in Beethoven._

Documented in Lockwood and Rosen.

The _subito piano_ (sudden quiet) and its inverse _subito forte_ (sudden loud) are Beethoven signatures. The technique was unusual in the Classical-era repertoire — Haydn and Mozart used it occasionally; Beethoven used it as a constitutive feature of certain movements.

**Where to spot it:**

* **Op. 13 _Pathétique_** opening (the _fortissimo piano_ : Chapter 2)
* **Op. 57 _Appassionata_** (Chapter 7): the showpiece for the technique
* **Symphony 5, Op. 67** (Chapter 9): particularly in the first movement and the third-to-fourth movement transition
* **Op. 132** quartet (Chapter 20): the late-style use
* **Op. 131** quartet (Chapter 20): particularly in Movement V and Movement VII

The signature is most-frequent in works where dramatic dynamic contrast is the compositional point. Heroic-period works use it more than late-period works (which generally have a more sustained dynamic register).

### 21.4 The Variation-Form Finale

_Sensory anchor: Op. 111's Arietta (Pollini 1977, second movement). Beethoven announces a hymn-like theme, then takes it through four variations of increasing rhythmic intensity, and ends with a sustained trill in C major. That whole arc is what the variation-form finale signature does, distilled into seventeen minutes. The same arc plays out across twenty-five minutes in the Ninth Symphony's choral finale and fifty minutes in the Diabelli Variations._

Documented in Rosen and Kinderman.

The variation-form finale — using a theme-and-variations design as the closing movement of a larger work — is one of Beethoven's most-consistent compositional signatures.

**Where to spot it:**

* **Symphony 3 _Eroica_** finale (Chapter 6): the _Prometheus_ theme variations
* **Symphony 5** second movement (Chapter 9): variation set within the symphony
* **Symphony 7** second movement Allegretto (Chapter 11): the variation slow movement
* **Op. 109** variation finale (Chapter 16)
* **Op. 111** Arietta second movement (Chapter 16): variation second-movement-as-whole
* **Diabelli Variations Op. 120** (Chapter 17): complete-work variation
* **Symphony 9** choral finale (Chapter 18): variation finale on the Ode to Joy
* **Op. 131** Movement IV (Chapter 20): variation movement within the seven-movement structure

The signature culminates in the late period (Op. 109, Op. 111, Diabelli, Ninth, Op. 131). The Diabelli is the largest variation set; the Ninth finale is the largest variation-as-finale; Op. 111's Arietta is the spiritual peak.

### 21.5 The Long Buildup-to-Climax

_Sensory anchor: Symphony 5's third-to-fourth-movement transition (Kleiber 1974, 21:30 onward). Ninety seconds of sustained quiet on a single C, timpani rolling underneath, voices gradually accumulating, and then the C-major fanfare erupts. That extended-tension-then-release is the long buildup, at its most-celebrated._

Documented in Lockwood and Tovey.

The long buildup-to-climax — extended preparation for a structural moment, sustained tension across an unusually long span — is one of Beethoven's compositional habits at structural junctures.

**Where to spot it:**

* **Symphony 3 _Eroica_** first-movement development (Chapter 6): the six-minute development preparing the recapitulation
* **Symphony 5** third-to-fourth-movement attacca transition (Chapter 9): the 90-second quiet buildup
* **Symphony 7** Allegretto (Chapter 11): the additive accumulation of voices
* **Symphony 9** finale's choral entry (Chapter 18): the orchestral introduction before the chorus enters
* **Op. 106 _Hammerklavier_** finale fugue (Chapter 15): the contrapuntal extension toward its close
* **Grosse Fuge Op. 133** (Chapter 20): the long contrapuntal development

The signature is one of the marks of Beethoven's architectural ambition. The technique requires the listener to _follow_ the buildup — to recognize that something is being prepared and to wait for the arrival.

### 21.6 Unison-Orchestra Openings

_Sensory anchor: Symphony 5's first four notes (Kleiber 1974, 0:00). The entire string section plus clarinets striking the same three Gs and one E♭ together, no harmony — pure rhythmic unison. The Ninth Symphony's Schreckensfanfare does the same thing 80 minutes later in the catalogue, on a much harsher chord. The device is the same: orchestra-as-single-voice._

Documented in Lockwood.

The unison-orchestra opening — the full orchestra striking the same pitches together with no harmonic accompaniment — is one of Beethoven's orchestrational devices.

**Where to spot it:**

* **Op. 1 No. 3 Piano Trio** (Chapter 3): early unison opening
* **Symphony 5** opening (Chapter 9, Volume 1 Chapter 3): the famous four-note hammer in strings and clarinets
* **Symphony 9** fourth-movement _Schreckensfanfare_ (Chapter 18): the dissonant fanfare
* **Op. 111** first-movement Maestoso (Chapter 16): the heavy chordal opening

The signature is one of Beethoven's dramatic-announcement devices. The technique establishes the work's key, dynamic, and dramatic register in a single gesture.

### 21.7 The Heroic Key-Change at the Climax

_Sensory anchor: the very moment in Symphony 5 when the C-minor scherzo dies into the sustained quiet, and then the C-major finale erupts (Kleiber 1974, 22:30). The per aspera ad astra arc is built into that single transition. Brahms's First Symphony, Mahler's Second, Tchaikovsky's Fifth, Sibelius's Fifth all aim at this moment, ninety years after Beethoven first made it canonical._

Documented in Lockwood.

The heroic key-change at the climax — the modulation from minor to major (the per aspera ad astra arc) at the structural climax of a work — is Beethoven's narrative signature.

**Where to spot it:**

* **Symphony 5** third-to-fourth-movement transition (Chapter 9): C minor to C major
* **Symphony 9** finale (Chapter 18): D minor first movement to D major finale
* **Op. 111** first-to-second movement (Chapter 16): C minor to C major
* **Egmont Overture, Op. 84** (1810; not anchored in this volume): F minor to F major triumphant close

The signature is one of the most-influential structural designs in 19th-century symphonic music. The arc became the canonical narrative for Brahms's First, Mahler's Second, Tchaikovsky's Fifth, Sibelius's Fifth, and many others.

### 21.8 The Fugal Finale (Late-Style Signature)

_Sensory anchor: the _Grosse Fuge_ 's opening twenty seconds (Emerson String Quartet 1997). Stravinsky in the 1950s called it "an absolutely contemporary piece of music that will be contemporary forever." Listen to the opening: contrapuntal density beyond what the Classical language could support, sustained for sixteen minutes. The fugue is no longer a finishing-school exercise; it is the late-Beethoven structural extreme._

Documented in Rosen and Kerman.

The fugal finale, a fugue as the closing movement of a large work, is a late-period signature, emerging from Beethoven's late-period Bach studies.

**Where to spot it:**

* **Op. 102 No. 2 Cello Sonata** (1815): the documented first fugal finale
* **Op. 106 _Hammerklavier_** finale (Chapter 15): the massive fugue
* **Op. 110** Piano Sonata finale (Chapter 16): the arioso-and-fugue closing
* **Diabelli Variations** Variation 32 fugue (Chapter 17)
* **Op. 131** opening fugue (Chapter 20): the opening fugue (not the finale)
* **Grosse Fuge Op. 133** (Chapter 20): the contrapuntal extreme

The signature is the mark of Beethoven's late-period compositional ambition. The fugue is one of the most technically and intellectually demanding compositional forms; Beethoven's late fugues are among the most-demanding in the Western tradition.

### 21.9 The Extended Sustained Trill

_Sensory anchor: the closing minutes of Op. 111's Arietta (Pollini 1977, last three minutes). A single trill in the right hand that goes on, and on, and on, across many bars, while the harmonies move underneath. That sustained shimmer is no longer a Classical ornament; it is texture. By the time the work ends, the trill itself feels like a voice._

Documented in Rosen.

The extended sustained trill — a trill that runs across many measures rather than being a brief ornament — is a late-style signature.

**Where to spot it:**

* **Op. 109** variation finale (Chapter 16)
* **Op. 111** Arietta coda (Chapter 16): the sustained trill closing the work
* **Diabelli Variations** Variation 33 (Chapter 17)
* **Op. 132** _Heiliger Dankgesang_ (Chapter 20; Volume 1 Chapter 14): the sustained quartet-trill writing
* **Op. 131** Movement IV (Chapter 20): extended trill passages

The signature appears almost exclusively in the late period. The trill becomes a _texture_ rather than an _ornament_ — a late-style innovation.

### 21.10 The Three Periods Framework — A Caveat (Preview of Chapter 22)

The signatures above run across the three periods (early / middle / late). Some signatures (the C-minor character, the dramatic slow introduction, the subito piano) appear across all three periods. Others (the fugal finale, the extended trill) are late-period signatures. The variation-form finale is documented as straddling middle and late.

This is a tension worth naming before the next chapter takes it up in full.

The three-period framework — early (1782–1802), middle (1803–1814), late (1815–1827) — is the standard organizing scheme of modern Beethoven scholarship. Wilhelm von Lenz introduced it in 1852; Lockwood, Solomon, Swafford, and Kinderman all use some version of it; this volume has used it throughout (Parts II / III / V of the table of contents are early / middle / late). The framework is genuinely useful: the _Eroica_ (1804) is unmistakably a different kind of composition from Op. 1 No. 3 (1795), and Op. 132 (1825) is unmistakably a different kind of composition from the _Eroica_. The shifts are real.

But the signatures don't respect the boundaries. The C-minor character runs from Op. 1 No. 3 (early) through Op. 111 (late) — twenty-seven years and three "periods" — and the _compositional_ register stays remarkably continuous. The dramatic slow introduction first appears in 1798 in the _Pathétique_ and reaches its harmonic peak in 1812 in Symphony 7's _Poco sostenuto_. The variation-form finale is in _Eroica_ (middle), Op. 109 (late), and the _Diabelli_ (late) — same signature, three different periods. The framework's vertical lines (1802, 1815) suggest sharp breaks, but the signatures cut horizontally across the framework, showing a composer working continuously on a vocabulary that was his from the beginning.

So which view is right? Both are. The framework is right that Beethoven's compositional _priorities_ shifted at certain hinges (Heiligenstadt 1802; the crisis years 1815–1820). The signatures are right that his compositional _vocabulary_ was more continuous than the framework suggests. The framework is a useful organizing scheme; it is not a description of compositional rupture.

The next chapter (Chapter 22) takes up the three-period framework directly — its 1852 origin in Wilhelm von Lenz, its modern defenders (Lockwood, Solomon, Swafford), its critics (Kinderman for an earlier-starting late period; Kerman for cross-period continuity; Burnham for the heroic-middle as central with the late as continuation), the transitional works that resist clean placement (Op. 90, Op. 95, Op. 96, Op. 97, the Op. 70 trios), and what the framework illuminates versus what it hides. After Chapter 21's signature catalogue and Chapter 22's framework critique, the reader has both tools: the chronological scaffold and the cross-period thread.

### 21.11 How to Use This Catalogue

The catalogue is a spotting guide. The recommended use:

  1. **Listen to a Beethoven work** — any work, from any period.
  2. **Listen for the signatures** — try to spot one or two of the signatures above.
  3. **Identify which signature you hear** , and trace where it came from in the Beethoven catalogue.
  4. **Compare with other works** — note how the signature is being used in this work compared to its other appearances.

The catalogue gives the listener a way to _hear across_ the catalogue rather than hearing each work in isolation. Beethoven's compositional vocabulary is remarkably consistent across his career; once the listener has the signatures in her ear, she can hear connections between works that might not be obvious from genre or chronological position alone.

### 21.12 Thread C revisited — the Six Listening Habits, two hundred chapters later

Chapter 1 launched **Thread C** : six listening habits to unlearn for Beethoven specifically. Now is the moment to circle back, because some of those habits are easier to unlearn after twenty chapters than they were at the start.

**Habit 1: "Beethoven is grim."** Recall Symphony 8's second movement, the metronome joke (Chapter 11). Recall the _Razumovsky_ Op. 59 No. 2's mock-Russian theme that Beethoven turned upside down (Chapter 12). Recall the _Diabelli_ Variation 22, where Beethoven quotes Leporello's _"Notte e giorno faticar"_ from Mozart's _Don Giovanni_ — Beethoven the composer complaining about working for Diabelli the publisher (Chapter 17). The playful Beethoven is documented in twenty different places across the catalogue. If you still hear only the grim Beethoven, you have not been listening.

**Habit 2: "Famous works first, rare works later."** By Chapter 20 you walked into the late quartets without the prerequisite-checking that the famous-works-first habit requires. Op. 131 is now available to you. So is the _Grosse Fuge_. If you wait for permission to listen to those works until you have "earned them" through the famous repertoire, you will never get there.

**Habit 3: "Beethoven invented everything."** Recall Haydn-the-teacher in 1795 (Chapter 3); Bach as the through-line of the _Well-Tempered Clavier_ in Beethoven's keyboard from Bonn onward (Chapter 3, Chapter 15, Chapter 17); Palestrina and Handel in the late-period studies (Chapter 19). Beethoven extended; he did not invent ex nihilo. The "lone genius" myth is over.

**Habit 4: "The deafness is the story."** The Heiligenstadt Testament (Chapter 5) is the most-cited piece of evidence for the deafness-is-the-story reading. But Chapter 5 also showed that the Symphony 2 Larghetto was composed during the same months as the Testament — a movement of serene lyricism written in the depths of the Testament's despair. The late style (Chapter 15, Chapter 20) is creative invention, not pathology. The deafness shaped the _circumstances_ ; it did not produce the work.

**Habit 5: "The early works are warm-ups."** Op. 1 No. 3 (Chapter 3) is a major work. The _Pathétique_ (Chapter 2) is a major work. The _Moonlight_ (Chapter 4) is a major work. The Op. 18 quartets (Chapter 12) are major works. The "early" tag captures chronological position, not stature. If you still treat the first-Vienna decade as preparation-for-Eroica, you have missed what 1795–1802 actually achieved.

**Habit 6: "I don't get the late quartets."** Op. 131 in Chapter 20 was reached not as graduation reward but as the structural endpoint of a sustained twenty-chapter buildup. By the time you arrived at Op. 131's seven-movement attacca structure, you had the C-minor character (Chapter 3), the dramatic slow introduction (Chapter 2), the variation form (Chapters 11, 16, 17), the unison opening (Chapter 9), the fugal finale (Chapter 15), and the sustained trill (Chapter 16) in your ear. The vocabulary was in place. Op. 131 was available.

Six habits down. If even three or four of them have shifted in your listening across this volume, the book has done what Chapter 1 said it would do.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Walk the companion through the documented Lockwood treatment of the C-minor character — what features it identifies, which works it discusses, and how it traces the thread from Op. 1 No. 3 through Op. 111._
* _Tell the companion which signature you found most immediately spottable on the listens through this volume, and ask her about other Beethoven works where the same signature appears that we have not anchored._
* _Share with the companion the late-style signatures (the fugal finale, the extended trill, the harmonic adventure of the late slow movements) and ask how they emerged from the crisis-years compositional pause._
* _Bring up the "spotting practice" idea: ask the companion to recommend a Beethoven work you have not heard yet where one or two of the signatures appear in particularly clear form._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The C-minor character in Op. 111 first movement: try to hear how Beethoven returns to the C-minor language of Op. 1 No. 3 across twenty-seven years.
* The unison-orchestra opening of Symphony 5's first movement: try to hear how the signature appears in the first measures.
* The variation-form finale of the Ninth Symphony: try to follow how the Ode to Joy theme is built up through orchestral variations before the chorus enters.
* The extended trill at the end of Op. 111's Arietta: try to hear how the trill becomes a texture rather than an ornament.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Lewis Lockwood's _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (W. W. Norton, 2003), particularly the index entries on the recurring signatures (C-minor character, dramatic slow introduction, variation finale, etc.).** Lockwood's index is the practical entry point into the signature catalogue. Pair with **Charles Rosen's _The Classical Style_ (W. W. Norton, 1971, revised 1997)** for the compositional-language context.

### Chapter Summary

Beethoven's compositional life has recurring signatures — compositional habits that Beethoven returned to across his forty-five-year career. The chapter catalogues nine signatures:

  1. The C-minor character (Op. 1 No. 3 → Op. 111)
  2. The dramatic slow introduction (_Pathétique_ → Symphony 7 → Op. 111)
  3. The subito piano and forte (_Appassionata_ → late quartets)
  4. The variation-form finale (Op. 109, Op. 111, Diabelli, Symphony 9)
  5. The long buildup-to-climax (_Eroica_ → Ninth → _Grosse Fuge_)
  6. Unison-orchestra openings (Symphony 5 → Symphony 9 _Schreckensfanfare_)
  7. The heroic key-change at the climax (Symphony 5 → Symphony 9)
  8. The fugal finale (Op. 102 No. 2 → _Hammerklavier_ → _Grosse Fuge_)
  9. The extended sustained trill (Op. 109 → Op. 111 → late quartets)

Each signature is documented in modern Beethoven scholarship (Lockwood, Rosen, Kerman, Tovey, Kinderman). The signatures cross the three-period framework boundaries, suggesting compositional continuity across Beethoven's career.

You also met **Thread C revisited (§21.12)** — the Six Listening Habits from Chapter 1 reviewed in light of the twenty-chapter journey. The habits are easier to unlearn after the volume than at the start.

The catalogue is a spotting guide for future Beethoven listening. The listener who has the signatures in her ear can hear connections between works that might not be obvious from genre or chronological position alone.

The next chapter takes up the three-period framework — its origin, its defenders and critics, and what it hides.

* * *

---

## Chapter 22: The Three-Period Framework — Defenders, Critics, and What It Hides

The three-period framework — Beethoven's compositional life as early (1782–1802), middle (1803–1814), and late (1815–1827) — has been the organizing scheme of this volume. We have used it throughout. The chapters are structured by period: Part II (early), Part III (heroic middle), Part IV (crisis years transition), Part V (late). The framework is the standard scaffolding of modern Beethoven scholarship.

It is also imperfect. The boundaries are porous; the transitional works don't fit cleanly; the late-style features can be seen emerging earlier than the framework allows; the early works contain late-period premonitions. The framework illuminates, but it also hides.

This chapter takes up the framework directly. The history of the framework, the defenders, the critics, the transitional works that resist clean placement, and an honest assessment of what the framework does and does not capture.

### 22.1 The Origin: Wilhelm von Lenz, 1852

Wilhelm von Lenz (1809–1883) was a Baltic-German lawyer (born in Saint Petersburg, of German descent, working in Russian Imperial service) and amateur musicologist who, in 1852, published _Beethoven et ses trois styles_ ("Beethoven and his three styles"). The book established the three-period framework as the organizing scheme for Beethoven's career.

Lenz's three styles:

* **First style** (early): from the Bonn beginnings through about 1800 or 1802. Haydn-Mozart-influenced.
* **Second style** (middle): from about 1800 through about 1815. The heroic-individual-Beethoven period.
* **Third style** (late): from about 1815 through 1827. The introspective-experimental period.

Lenz's book was not the first to identify three Beethoven periods (other writers had proposed related schemes earlier), but it was the first to _establish_ the framework as the standard organizing scheme. The book was widely read in the mid-19th century and became the standard reference.

Subsequent modern Beethoven biographers — Lockwood (2003), Solomon (1998 2nd ed), Swafford (2014), Kinderman (2009 2nd ed) — all use some version of Lenz's framework. The period boundaries vary slightly across the modern biographers, but the basic structure (early / middle / late) is shared.

### 22.2 The Modern Consensus

The modern Beethoven scholarship generally treats the framework as useful but imperfect. The consensus:

**Useful for organizing the catalogue.** The three periods correspond to identifiable compositional shifts. The early-Vienna style (Op. 1 through Op. 22) is different from the heroic middle-period style (Op. 36 through Op. 92), which is different from the late style (Op. 101 through Op. 135). The shifts are real; the framework captures them.

**Useful for biographical narrative.** The Heiligenstadt Testament (1802) marks the hinge between early and middle. The 1815–1820 crisis years mark the hinge between middle and late. The biographical narrative tracks the compositional periodization.

**Imperfect at the boundaries.** The transitional works (Op. 90, Op. 96, Op. 101, the Op. 70 trios, the Op. 95 quartet) resist clean placement. Op. 90 (1814) is either late-middle or early-late depending on the critic. Op. 95 (1810) is middle-period in date but late-style in compositional features.

**Imperfect at the internal divisions.** The middle period contains heterogeneous sub-stretches. The _Eroica_ (1803–04) is different from the _Pastoral_ (1808), which is different from Symphony 7 (1812). Treating all three as a uniform "heroic middle" is somewhat reductive.

**Imperfect at the late style.** Some scholars (notably William Kinderman) have argued that the late style starts earlier than 1815 — pointing to the Op. 95 quartet (1810), the Op. 90 sonata (1814), and the Op. 96 violin sonata (1812) as proto-late-style works. Other scholars retain the 1815 boundary.

### 22.3 The Transitional Works

Several works resist clean period placement:

**Op. 90 in E minor (1814).** Composed in the year before the crisis years began. Has heroic-middle features (the dramatic E-minor first movement) and late-style features (the German tempo markings, the two-movement structure, the introspective register). Lockwood treats Op. 90 as the bridge between middle and late.

**Op. 95 in F minor "Serioso" (1810).** Composed in the middle of the heroic period. But has late-style features (the compressed scale, the harmonic adventure, the introspective slow movement). Joseph Kerman in _The Beethoven Quartets_ treats Op. 95 as proto-late-style.

**Op. 96 Violin Sonata in G (1812).** Composed at the end of the productive middle period. Has lyrical register and contrapuntal interest that anticipate the late style. Placed in the middle period chronologically but late-style stylistically.

**Op. 97 _Archduke_ Trio (1810–11).** Composed in the middle period. The expanded lyrical register and the chamber-symphonic ambition are middle-period features. But the warmth and the structural breadth point forward toward the late style.

**The Op. 70 piano trios (1808).** Composed in the middle period. The Op. 70 No. 1 _Ghost_ trio's spectral slow movement is proto-late-style in its harmonic adventure and introspective register.

These transitional works are the evidence that the framework's boundaries are porous. The works do not fit cleanly into either period; they have features of both.

### 22.4 The Critics of the Framework

Several modern scholars have argued for modifications or replacements of the three-period framework:

**Maynard Solomon (1998 2nd ed)** uses the standard three-period framework but adds psychoanalytic-biographical dimensions. Solomon's sub-periods within each period (early-early, late-early, etc.) suggest finer subdivisions than the three-period framework allows.

**William Kinderman (2009 2nd ed)** has argued for an earlier start to the late period (around 1810–1814 rather than 1815). The Op. 95 quartet, Op. 90 sonata, Op. 96 violin sonata, and _An die ferne Geliebte_ song cycle are, on Kinderman's reading, late-period works rather than late-middle-period works.

**Joseph Kerman (1967)** in _The Beethoven Quartets_ treats the late quartets as stylistically connected to earlier Beethoven (especially the Op. 18 quartets' counterpoint and the Op. 59 Razumovsky quartets' ambition). Kerman's reading suggests continuity across periods rather than sharp breaks.

**Scott Burnham** in _Beethoven Hero_ (1995) treats the heroic middle period as the central compositional achievement and the late period as continuation rather than break.

These critics are not rejecting the three-period framework entirely but acknowledging its limits. The framework is useful at large scale but limiting at small scale.

### 22.5 What the Framework Hides

The three-period framework emphasizes certain aspects of Beethoven's career while hiding others:

**The compositional continuities.** The signatures catalogued in Chapter 21 cross the period boundaries. The C-minor character thread runs from Op. 1 No. 3 (1795, early) through Op. 111 (1822, late). The dramatic slow introduction runs from the _Pathétique_ (1798, early) through Symphony 7 (1812, middle). The framework presents these as separate-period works, but they are compositionally continuous.

**The genre-specific development.** Beethoven's compositional development was different in different genres. The piano sonatas show one trajectory; the symphonies show another; the string quartets show another. The three-period framework treats Beethoven's development as uniform across genres, when in fact each genre had its own developmental arc.

**The experimental side-paths.** Beethoven composed many works that don't fit any standard period. _Wellington's Victory_ (1813) is occasional music. The Folk Song settings (1809–20) are popular-music transcriptions. The various canons and bagatelles are small-scale experiments. These works exist outside the three-period framework.

**The influence of biography.** The framework places biographical hinges (Heiligenstadt 1802, crisis years 1815–1820) at the period boundaries. But Beethoven's biographical and compositional rhythms don't always align — Symphony 2 was composed _during_ Heiligenstadt; Op. 101 was composed _during_ the crisis years. The biographical-compositional alignment is less neat than the framework suggests.

**The late-style's internal variety.** The late style is not uniform. The _Hammerklavier_ (Chapter 15) is different from the _Diabelli Variations_ (Chapter 17), which are different from the late string quartets (Chapter 20). Treating these as all "late period" is somewhat reductive.

### 22.6 What the Framework Captures

Despite the limitations, the framework captures real compositional shifts:

**The Heiligenstadt hinge.** Beethoven's work before and after 1802 is genuinely different. The early-style Vienna composer in 1800 is different from the heroic-middle-period composer in 1804. The _Eroica_ 's compositional ambitions are different in kind from the Op. 1 piano trios.

**The late-style emergence.** Beethoven's work after the 1815–1820 crisis years is genuinely different from the heroic middle. Op. 101 (1816) opens compositional territory that the _Eroica_ did not. The late style is genuinely a new compositional voice.

**The genre developments.** Within each genre, the three-period framework captures real developmental arcs. The piano sonatas show clear early-middle-late arcs. The string quartets show clear early-middle-late arcs. The genre-by-genre analyses confirm the framework's utility.

### 22.7 How to Use the Framework

The practical recommendation: use the framework as an organizing scheme while being aware of its limitations.

**Use it for orientation.** When encountering a new Beethoven work, the period placement gives the listener a frame for what to expect. Op. 27 No. 2 (1801) is early-Vienna; the compositional language is the early-Vienna language. Op. 132 (1825) is late-period; the compositional language is the late-period language.

**Use it for historical-biographical context.** The period placement connects the work to the biographical context. Op. 90 (1814) was composed at the start of the crisis years; the compositional features reflect that context.

**Don't use it as the final word.** The framework is useful but imperfect. Listening to the actual music always reveals features that don't fit cleanly. The framework is a starting point, not the endpoint.

**Be alert to the transitional works.** Op. 90, Op. 95, Op. 96, the Op. 70 trios, _An die ferne Geliebte_ are works that don't fit cleanly. Listen to them as works that straddle the framework boundaries.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Walk the companion through the Wilhelm von Lenz origin of the three-period framework in 1852 — what Lenz argued, how the framework became standard, and how modern scholars treat Lenz's original formulation._
* _Tell the companion which of the transitional works (Op. 90, Op. 95, Op. 96, Op. 70 trios) you found most genre-bending, and ask her about the compositional features that make the work resist clean period placement._
* _Bring up William Kinderman's argument for an earlier start to the late period (around 1810–14 rather than 1815) with the companion, and ask how the argument changes the framework's boundaries._
* _Share with the companion how the signature catalogue (Chapter 21) interacts with the three-period framework — how the signatures cross period boundaries and what that suggests about compositional continuity._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The transitional features of Op. 90's first movement (Chapter 14): try to hear both heroic-middle-period elements and late-style elements.
* The C-minor character thread across periods: listen to Op. 1 No. 3 (early), Symphony 5 (middle), Op. 111 (late) and try to hear the compositional continuity.
* The variation-form finale signature across periods: listen to the _Eroica_ finale (middle), Op. 111 Arietta (late), Diabelli (late), Symphony 9 finale (late) and try to hear the signature's evolution.
* The Op. 95 quartet (1810, briefly mentioned in Chapter 12) if you sample it: try to hear late-style features in a middle-period work.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Wilhelm von Lenz's _Beethoven et ses trois styles_ (1852) in its original or in modern translations.** The book is the foundation of the three-period framework. Pair with **William Kinderman's _Beethoven_ (2nd ed Oxford, 2009)** for the contemporary academic synthesis and the arguments for finer subdivisions.

### Chapter Summary

The three-period framework — Beethoven's compositional life as early (1782–1802), middle (1803–1814), and late (1815–1827) — was introduced by Wilhelm von Lenz in 1852 and has been the organizing scheme of modern Beethoven scholarship since.

The modern consensus treats the framework as useful but imperfect:

* **Useful** for organizing the catalogue, for biographical narrative, and for period-by-period genre development.
* **Imperfect** at the boundaries (the transitional works resist clean placement), at the internal divisions (each period contains heterogeneous sub-stretches), and at the late style (some scholars argue for an earlier start to the late period).

You met the transitional works that resist clean placement: Op. 90 (1814), Op. 95 (1810), Op. 96 (1812), Op. 97 _Archduke_ Trio (1810–11), Op. 70 trios (1808). Each straddles the framework boundaries.

You met the critics of the framework: Solomon (sub-periods within each period), Kinderman (earlier start to the late period), Kerman (compositional continuity across periods), Burnham (heroic-middle as central with late as continuation).

You met what the framework hides: the compositional continuities across periods (the signatures of Chapter 21), the genre-specific developmental arcs, the experimental side-paths, the late-style's internal variety.

You met what the framework captures: the Heiligenstadt hinge (1802), the late-style emergence (post-1815–1820 crisis years), and the genre-by-genre developmental arcs.

You met the practical recommendation: use the framework as an organizing scheme while remaining aware of its limitations. Listen to the actual music as the authoritative source; the framework is a starting frame, not the endpoint.

The next chapter takes you to the Beethoven recording history — from the 1900 early acoustic recordings through the 2026 contemporary performances.
