## Chapter 23: Recording History — Beethoven on Disc 1900–2026

Beethoven died in 1827. The earliest sound recordings (Edison's phonograph patent) come from 1877. The earliest commercial Beethoven recordings are from the early 1900s. In between — across the seventy-year gap between Beethoven's death and the earliest recordings — the Beethoven performance tradition was transmitted through direct teaching (the pupils of Beethoven's pupils, the continuing performance practices, the printed editions). What we have on disc starts in 1900.

The century-plus of Beethoven recordings since then is one of the most-extensive in the recording era. Every major Beethoven work has been recorded dozens of times — Symphony 5 alone has more than 300 commercial recordings. Every major conductor has recorded the Beethoven symphonies; every major pianist has recorded the Beethoven sonatas (or substantial portions); every major string quartet has recorded the Beethoven quartets.

This chapter maps the major conducting and pianistic lineages of the recording era. The aim is to give the listener a framework for recognizing that _"Beethoven 5"_ (or any major Beethoven work) is many works, one per major interpreter, and to provide recommended starting points for exploring the recorded tradition.

### 23.1 Conducting Lineages

#### Scene-setting: four temperaments, one symphony

Before the lineages-by-list, a scene. It is March 1942, Berlin. The Philharmonic has been ordered to play Beethoven's Ninth for Hitler's birthday week. **Wilhelm Furtwängler** conducts. The orchestra is depleted by conscription; the hall is half-full of Party officials. The broadcast that survives is one of the most extreme readings of the Ninth in the recording era — the tempi heavy and elastic, the _molto vivace_ taken at near-rage, the _Adagio_ stretched until time seems to dissolve, the _Freude_ finale crying out from within a state collapsing around it. After the final chord, Goebbels walks onto the platform to shake Furtwängler's hand. Furtwängler, the surviving photographs show, immediately pulls a handkerchief from his pocket and wipes his palm.

Across the Atlantic, **Arturo Toscanini** has been refusing to conduct in fascist Italy since 1931 (when Blackshirts beat him outside the Teatro Comunale in Bologna in May 1931 for refusing to play the Fascist anthem _Giovinezza_ before the concert) and in Nazi Germany since 1933 (when he cancelled Bayreuth after Hitler took power). His NBC Symphony broadcasts during the war are deliberately the _opposite_ of Furtwängler's Berlin Beethoven: brisk, structurally argued, free of cosmic-Romantic weight. The 1939 Toscanini Symphony 5 takes the first movement at metronome ♩=108 (close to Beethoven's score marking); Furtwängler in the same period takes it at ♩=88. The numerical gap is the political-aesthetic gap.

After the war, **Herbert von Karajan** — who had joined the Nazi Party in 1933 and was de-Nazified by the Allies in 1947 — rebuilds his career through London and Vienna, takes the Berlin Philharmonic in 1955 (succeeding Furtwängler, who had died the year before), and constructs a new model: Beethoven not as wartime cry or postwar argument, but as _polished monument_ , recorded in the studio, balanced for the LP and (from the early 1980s) the compact disc — formats Karajan embraced earlier and more wholeheartedly than most of his peers, treating each new medium as an opportunity to re-record the symphonies. The 1962 Berlin Beethoven cycle — Karajan's first — is what Beethoven sounded like in the West for a generation.

And then, in 1974, **Carlos Kleiber** records Symphony 5 with the Vienna Philharmonic in two sessions. He cancels the second sessions repeatedly; he revises his approach between takes; he insists on tempi faster than the orchestra wants and on rhythmic precision that the orchestra delivers only under pressure. The disc that results is, by general critical agreement, one of the great Beethoven recordings of the LP era. Kleiber goes on to make only a handful of further recordings in his life. He withdraws from the concert circuit for years at a stretch. When he died in 2004, he had left behind perhaps fifteen studio recordings and a reputation as the most-electrifying conductor of the second half of the 20th century.

Four temperaments. Furtwängler the metaphysician; Toscanini the structuralist; Karajan the polisher; Kleiber the electrician. Every recording-era Beethoven conductor sits somewhere on the lines between them, and the four lineages below trace the descendants of each.

#### The Furtwängler Lineage — Metaphysical-Romantic

**Wilhelm Furtwängler** (1886–1954) was the founder of the modern German conducting tradition. His Beethoven cycle (the 1947 Vienna Symphony 5 broadcast we used as a primary alternate in Chapter 9; the 1951 Bayreuth Ninth that was our Chapter 18 primary; numerous other broadcasts and studio recordings) established the metaphysical-Romantic approach.

The Furtwängler approach: slow weighted tempi; extreme tempo flexibility within movements; emphasis on long-line architectural shape; cosmic-dramatic interpretation. The "metaphysical" register is one of dramatic struggle and transcendent resolution — the per aspera ad astra arc taken at full Romantic register.

Descendants: **Wilhelm Furtwängler's direct successors** at Berlin and Vienna (Wilhelm Kempff in piano; Karl Böhm in opera; subsequent figures); **Otto Klemperer** (1885–1973) — closer to Furtwängler than to Toscanini, though different in important ways; **Eugen Jochum** (1902–1987), Bavarian Furtwängler-influenced; **Günter Wand** (1912–2002), late-20th-century Furtwängler-tradition.

#### The Toscanini Lineage — Structurally Rigorous

**Arturo Toscanini** (1867–1957) was the Italian conductor whose American career established the structurally-rigorous approach. His NBC Symphony Orchestra Beethoven cycle (1949–1953 recordings) is the reference for this tradition.

The Toscanini approach: fast tempi; strict pulse with minimal flexibility; emphasis on clear orchestral textures; refusal of Romantic-Furtwängler extravagances. The Toscanini Beethoven is brisk, structurally clear, sometimes sharp.

Descendants: **George Szell** (1897–1970) at Cleveland; **Fritz Reiner** (1888–1963) at Chicago; **Erich Leinsdorf** (1912–1993) at various orchestras; the American mainstream conducting tradition through the mid-20th century.

#### The Karajan Lineage — Polished-Monumental

**Herbert von Karajan** (1908–1989) was the Berlin-Vienna conductor whose long tenures at the Berlin Philharmonic (1955–1989) and the Vienna State Opera (1956–1964) established the polished-monumental approach. His four complete Beethoven symphony cycles (1962–63, 1976–77, 1982–84, the digital cycle of the early 1980s) are the reference points.

The Karajan approach: smooth proportions; brass-forward orchestral balance; sustained legato playing; absolute precision of ensemble. The Karajan Beethoven is polished, monumental, beautifully proportioned, sometimes over-polished by critics.

Descendants: **Riccardo Muti** (born 1941); **Lorin Maazel** (1930–2014); the major studio-recording conductors of the late 20th century.

#### The Kleiber Lineage — Electric

**Carlos Kleiber** (1930–2004) was the Argentine-Austrian-German conductor whose few recordings (he made very few; he was a perfectionist and unreliable in terms of scheduling) established the electric approach. His Symphony 5/7 disc (1974/1976), Symphony 4/7 disc (1980s), and Symphony 6/8 disc (1980s) are the references.

The Kleiber approach: fast tempi; crackling rhythmic energy; attention to inner-voice detail; theatrical phrasing. The Kleiber Beethoven is electric, urgent, theatrically alive.

Kleiber's influence is felt in spirit more than in lineage. His few recordings have shaped how subsequent conductors approach these works, but no direct school followed.

#### The HIP (Historically-Informed Performance) Revolution

The HIP movement emerged in the 1970s and 1980s. The principles:

* **Period instruments.** Use the instruments of Beethoven's time (natural horns; valveless trumpets; period winds; gut-string violins; fortepiano rather than modern concert grand) rather than modern instruments.
* **Period performance practice.** Use the bowing techniques, articulation, ornamentation, and tempo choices of Beethoven's time rather than modern conventions.
* **Beethoven's metronome markings.** Take the metronome markings Beethoven wrote in the score (the markings are controversial; some are considered impossibly fast on modern instruments) as authoritative.
* **Smaller forces.** Use the smaller orchestra sizes of Beethoven's time rather than the larger modern concert orchestras.

Founding figures: **Roger Norrington** (born 1934) with the London Classical Players (later the Stuttgart Radio Symphony) — his complete Beethoven cycle (1986–87, EMI) was a landmark; **John Eliot Gardiner** (born 1943) with the Orchestre Révolutionnaire et Romantique and the Monteverdi Choir — his complete Beethoven cycle (1991–94, Archiv) is the reference; **Frans Brüggen** (1934–2014) with the Orchestra of the 18th Century — complete Beethoven cycle (1990s, Philips).

Contemporary HIP-tradition continuators: **Philippe Herreweghe** (born 1947); **René Jacobs** (born 1946); **Marc Minkowski** (born 1962); **Andrew Manze** (born 1965); the period-instrument scene of the contemporary era.

#### The Contemporary Continuators

**Teodor Currentzis** (born 1972) with MusicAeterna, the contemporary iconoclast. His Symphony 5 (2018, Sony) and Symphony 7 (2018, Sony) are the references for his approach: very fast tempi, dry orchestral sound, period-inflected timpani, theatrical extremes. The 2018 recordings are under the 10-year safeguard window but well-reviewed in Gramophone, BBC Music Magazine, and Fanfare.

**Andris Nelsons** (born 1978) with the Boston Symphony, the contemporary mainstream. His Beethoven cycle (in progress as of 2026) takes a middle-ground approach.

**Riccardo Chailly** (born 1953) with the Leipzig Gewandhausorchester — Beethoven cycle (Decca, 2009–11). A contemporary approach with HIP-informed tempi but on modern instruments.

**Herbert Blomstedt** (born 1927) — our Chapter 18 alternate Ninth (Accentus, 2017). The elder statesman of the contemporary mainstream. The 2017 recording is also inside the 10-year window but received substantial press coverage in Gramophone, Fono Forum, Rondo, and the international classical press.

### 23.2 Pianistic Lineages

#### The Schnabel Lineage — First Complete Cycle

**Artur Schnabel** (1882–1951) was the Austrian-Polish-American pianist who, between 1932 and 1935, recorded the first complete Beethoven sonata cycle (HMV, later Pearl). The cycle established Schnabel as the great Beethoven interpreter of the early recording era.

The Schnabel approach: intellectual rigor; attention to structural detail; tempo flexibility within strict overall pulse; sometimes-rough technical execution (Schnabel was known for "playing the notes as a vehicle for the music" rather than for technical perfection).

Descendants: **Solomon (Cutner)** (1902–1988); **Clifford Curzon** (1907–1982); **Leon Fleisher** (1928–2020); the mid-20th-century structural-classical school.

#### The Kempff Lineage — Lyrical

**Wilhelm Kempff** (1895–1991) was the German pianist whose two complete Beethoven sonata cycles (1951–56 mono, 1964–65 stereo, both Deutsche Grammophon) established the lyrical-singing approach.

The Kempff approach: lyrical singing-line; attention to voicing; use of subtle tempo flexibility; German-tradition warmth. The Kempff Beethoven is lyrical, songful, occasionally received as too gentle by critics who prefer dramatic readings.

Descendants: **Daniel Barenboim** (born 1942) — Kempff-influenced in his earlier readings; three complete Beethoven cycles (1966 EMI, 1980s EMI, 2003–05 DG); **Stephen Kovacevich** (born 1940); the lyrical-classical school.

#### The Richter-Gilels Lineage — Russian-Soviet

**Sviatoslav Richter** (1915–1997) and **Emil Gilels** (1916–1985) were the great Soviet pianists of their generation. Both were pupils of Heinrich Neuhaus at the Moscow Conservatory; both established Beethoven interpretations that have become references.

Richter's Beethoven: dramatic; incandescent live performances (the 1960 Carnegie Hall recital we used for the _Appassionata_ in Chapter 7); intensity at extreme. Richter recorded the Beethoven sonatas sporadically (no complete cycle).

Gilels's Beethoven: structurally clear; attention to harmonic motion; warm German sound. Gilels recorded an incomplete Beethoven cycle (DG, 1972–1984); the cycle was incomplete at his death.

Descendants: **Maurizio Pollini** (born 1942) — Italian pianist who has absorbed both Richter's intensity and Gilels's clarity; complete Beethoven cycle (DG, 1970s–2000s); **Mitsuko Uchida** (born 1948); the modern reference school.

#### The Modern References

**Maurizio Pollini.** The modern reference for many late Beethoven sonatas (Op. 106 Chapter 15, Op. 111 Chapter 16). Technically impeccable, structurally clear.

**Mitsuko Uchida.** The contemporary interior approach. Her Op. 111 (2007 Decca) we discussed in Chapter 16.

**Alfred Brendel** (born 1931). One of the great late-20th-century Beethoven interpreters. Complete Beethoven cycle (Philips, 1992–96; the late Brendel approach).

**András Schiff** (born 1953). The contemporary structural-analytical approach. Complete Beethoven cycle (ECM, 2004–08) and lecture-recital performances of the sonatas.

**Igor Levit** (born 1987). The contemporary younger-generation reference. Complete Beethoven cycle (Sony, 2013).

#### The Specialists

Some pianists are references for specific Beethoven repertoire:

* **Glenn Gould** (1932–1982): the late sonatas Op. 109, Op. 110, Op. 111 (Columbia, 1956); his Op. 57 _Appassionata_ (Columbia, 1960); not a reference for the heroic-middle sonatas.
* **Friedrich Gulda** (1930–2000): the complete Beethoven cycle (Amadeo, 1968).
* **Annie Fischer** (1914–1995): the complete Beethoven cycle (Hungaroton, late career).
* **Stephen Kovacevich** : the Diabelli Variations (Philips 1968, the primary recording in Chapter 17).
* **Andreas Staier** (born 1955): fortepiano specialist; Diabelli Variations on fortepiano (Harmonia Mundi, 2012).

### 23.3 String Quartet Lineages

The string-quartet tradition has several major ensembles whose Beethoven cycles are references:

**Quartetto Italiano** (1945–1980) — postwar Italian ensemble; complete Beethoven cycle (Philips, 1967–74). The reference for the mature-classical approach.

**Alban Berg Quartet** (1971–2008) — the great Vienna quartet of the late 20th century; complete Beethoven cycle (EMI, 1989). The reference for the modern Vienna approach.

**Takács Quartet** (founded 1975) — Hungarian-American quartet; complete Beethoven cycle (Decca, 2002–04). The reference for the contemporary warm-lyrical approach.

**Belcea Quartet** (founded 1994) — contemporary European quartet; complete Beethoven cycle (Zig-Zag, 2012). The reference for the contemporary HIP-informed approach.

**Emerson String Quartet** (1976–2023) — American quartet; complete Beethoven cycle (DG, 1997). The reference for the modern American approach.

### 23.4 What This Means for the Listener

The practical implications:

**"Beethoven 5" is many works.** The Furtwängler reading, the Karajan reading, the Kleiber reading, the Gardiner reading, the Currentzis reading are different interpretations of the same score. Each is internally consistent. The listener can develop preferences by comparing readings.

**Start with one canonical reading.** For each major work, the chapter's primary recording is the starting point. After internalizing the primary, sample the alternates for the interpretive contrasts.

**Don't try to hear everything.** The Beethoven discography is vast. Listening to every recording of a single work is impossible and unnecessary. The strategy is: pick two or three different readings of each major work and live with them.

**Develop preferences.** The preferences are the evidence of the listener's growing musical opinion. Liking Kleiber's Symphony 5 more than Karajan's, or Pollini's Op. 111 more than Kempff's, is the mark of an engaged listener. The book is not in the business of telling you which to prefer.

**Follow the lineages.** Once the major lineages are in mind (Furtwängler / Toscanini / Karajan / Kleiber for conductors; Schnabel / Kempff / Richter-Gilels / Pollini for pianists), new recordings can be placed in context. The "is this Furtwängler-influenced or Toscanini-influenced?" question is useful for understanding contemporary readings.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Tell the companion which lineage (Furtwängler / Toscanini / Karajan / Kleiber for conductors; Schnabel / Kempff / Richter-Gilels / Pollini for pianists) most appeals to you as a starting point, and ask her about recordings to sample within that lineage._
* _Bring up a recording you have heard in this volume and ask the companion to compare it with a different reading by a different conductor or pianist — for example: Kleiber's Symphony 5 versus Furtwängler's 1947, or Pollini's Op. 111 versus Uchida's 2007. The comparison should produce articulated preferences._
* _Ask the companion about the HIP movement: what its principles are, what the advantages of period-instrument readings are, what the disadvantages are, and how to evaluate contemporary HIP readings._
* _Share with the companion the Stravinsky line on the Grosse Fuge (Chapter 20) and ask about the 20th-century reception history of the late quartets and the Beethoven catalogue more broadly._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* A work where you have only heard one reading (perhaps the _Hammerklavier_ in Pollini 1977 from Chapter 15): sample a different reading (Richter 1975 live, or Levit 2013) and try to identify the interpretive differences.
* The HIP versus modern-instrument contrast: sample Gardiner's Beethoven Symphony 9 (Chapter 18) and Furtwängler 1951 Bayreuth; the contrast is one of the major interpretive divides of the recording era.
* The Russian-tradition versus German-tradition piano contrast: sample Richter's _Appassionata_ (Chapter 7) and Kempff's _Pathétique_ (Chapter 2); the traditions are audibly different.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Norman Lebrecht's _The Maestro Myth_ (Simon & Schuster, 1991)** — a critical history of the conducting tradition, with chapters on Furtwängler, Toscanini, Karajan, and others. Pair with **David Hurwitz's _Classics Today_ reviews** (online) for the contemporary recording-comparison perspective. Hurwitz's writing is sharp, opinionated, and useful for orienting yourself in the recorded tradition.

### Chapter Summary

The Beethoven recording history runs from 1900 to 2026 — over 125 years of recordings, with every major work recorded dozens of times. The major lineages:

**Conductors:**

* **Furtwängler** lineage: metaphysical-Romantic; descendants Klemperer, Jochum, Wand
* **Toscanini** lineage: structurally-rigorous; descendants Szell, Reiner
* **Karajan** lineage: polished-monumental; descendants Muti, Maazel
* **Kleiber** lineage: electric; influential more in spirit than direct school
* **HIP revolution** — Norrington, Gardiner, Brüggen; contemporary continuators Herreweghe, Manze, Minkowski
* **Contemporary continuators** — Currentzis, Nelsons, Chailly, Blomstedt

**Pianists:**

* **Schnabel** lineage: first complete cycle 1932–35; descendants Solomon, Curzon, Fleisher
* **Kempff** lineage: lyrical; descendants Barenboim, Kovacevich
* **Richter-Gilels** lineage: Russian-Soviet; descendants Pollini, Uchida
* **Modern references** — Pollini, Uchida, Brendel, Schiff, Levit
* **Specialists** — Gould, Gulda, Annie Fischer, Kovacevich, Staier

**String Quartets:**

* Quartetto Italiano, Alban Berg, Takács, Belcea, Emerson — references for the modern quartet tradition.

You met the practical implications: "Beethoven 5" (or any major Beethoven work) is many works; the listener can develop preferences by comparing readings; the major lineages give the listener orientation in the vast discography.

The next chapter takes you to Beethoven beyond the concert hall — the cultural reception of the work from 1827 through 2026, the Ode to Joy as European anthem, and the contemporary debates about Beethoven in the canon.

* * *

---

## Chapter 24: Beethoven Beyond the Concert Hall — Cultural Reception 1827–2026

Beethoven died in 1827. He was, by that date, the most-famous living composer in Europe and one of the most-famous artists across all the arts. His funeral on 29 March 1827 drew an estimated 10,000–20,000 people in Vienna — one of the largest public gatherings in the city in that decade.

The cultural afterlife since then has been extensive. The Beethoven catalogue has been quoted, paraphrased, referenced, parodied, and politically deployed across two centuries. The Ode to Joy is the European Union anthem; the Symphony 5 opening was used as the Allied wartime "V for Victory" signal; the Beethoven name has become synonymous with _serious music_ in popular consciousness. The cultural reception is its own subject of study.

This chapter takes up the cultural afterlife observationally. The book describes the patterns of reception; it does not adjudicate the contemporary debates about Beethoven's place in the canon. The observational frame is the chapter's discipline.

### 24.1 The Ode to Joy as European Anthem

The Ode to Joy melody (from Symphony 9's finale, Chapter 18) has had a sustained institutional life:

**1972: Council of Europe adoption.** The Council of Europe (a political-cultural organization of European democracies, founded 1949) adopted the Ode to Joy melody as its anthem in 1972. The adoption was at the Council level, not yet at the European Economic Community level.

**1985: EEC adoption.** At the Milan Summit of the European Economic Community (the predecessor to the European Union), the Ode to Joy was formally adopted as the EEC anthem. The anthem is performed instrumentally, no text, no chorus, to avoid privileging any one European language.

**1993 onward: EU continuation.** When the EEC became the European Union in 1993, the Ode to Joy continued as the EU anthem. It remains so as of 2026.

The selection process produced commentary at the time. Some advocates argued that the Ode to Joy was the natural choice: a universal humanist anthem from a German composer expressing universal brotherhood. Some critics argued that the work's German origin (and Schiller's German text) made it less neutral than its supporters claimed.

The compromise, instrumental performance without text, was the resolution. The melody is heard as universal; the text is not performed institutionally.

### 24.2 The Symphony 5 as Wartime Signal

The Symphony 5 opening (the four-note _short-short-short-LONG_ rhythm) was used as a wartime signal during World War II.

**The Morse code "V" for Victory.** The Morse code symbol for the letter V is _dot-dot-dot-dash_ , short-short-short-long, which is the same rhythm as the Symphony 5 opening. BBC broadcasts during the German occupation of Europe used the Symphony 5 opening (or its Morse-code equivalent) as the call sign for the Free French Forces and other Allied broadcasts.

The connection between the German composer Beethoven and the Allied resistance to Nazi Germany was widely noted at the time. The use was an act of cultural reclaiming — the great German cultural figure (Beethoven) was invoked against the Nazi German political regime. The Symphony 5 was played in many Allied wartime contexts.

The "V for Victory" association has persisted. The Symphony 5 opening continues to function in popular culture as the quotation-able sign of _resolve_ or _destiny_.

### 24.3 Beethoven in Popular Culture

The Beethoven catalogue has extensive appearances in popular culture across two centuries:

**Stanley Kubrick's _A Clockwork Orange_ (1971).** The 1971 film uses Beethoven's Symphony 9 (particularly the finale) as the soundtrack to the protagonist Alex's violent fantasies. The film raised the question of whether high-art music could be redemptive or whether it could be co-opted for violent purposes. The film became part of the Beethoven cultural reception.

**Bernard Rose's _Immortal Beloved_ (1994).** The biographical film of Beethoven, starring Gary Oldman. The film proposed (fictionally) that the Immortal Beloved was Beethoven's sister-in-law Johanna — a hypothesis not held by any serious modern scholar (Solomon's Antonie Brentano identification is the modern reference). The film is dramatically engaging but historically unreliable.

**Schroeder in _Peanuts_ comic strip.** Charles Schulz's long-running _Peanuts_ strip features the character Schroeder, a small boy with an intense attachment to Beethoven, who plays Beethoven on a toy piano. The strip ran from 1950 through 2000 and became one of the most-widely-circulated Beethoven cultural references in 20th-century American popular culture. Schroeder's hero-worship of Beethoven helped maintain popular awareness of the composer across generations.

**The Disney animated film _Fantasia_ (1940) and _Fantasia 2000_ (1999).** The Symphony 6 _Pastoral_ is animated in the first _Fantasia_ (set to Roman mythological scenes); the Symphony 5 finale is animated in _Fantasia 2000_. Both Disney films have been major popular-culture introductions to Beethoven for multiple generations of American children.

**Television and film soundtracks.** The Beethoven catalogue has been quoted in many television and film soundtracks. The Symphony 9 finale appears in many contexts — political documentaries, romantic dramas, advertising campaigns. The Symphony 5 opening appears in many cartoon and comedy contexts.

### 24.4 The Critical Reception Arc

The critical reception of Beethoven across two centuries has evolved in distinct phases. The shifts in how Europeans (and later, the world) _talked about_ Beethoven are themselves a record of how Western musical culture has thought about what music is for.

**19th-century deification (1827–1900).** The 19th century treated Beethoven as the supreme composer — the "Great Master" whose works were the summit of Western music. Hoffmann's 1810 review (Chapter 9) was the founding document, declaring instrumental music the highest of the arts and the Fifth Symphony its supreme example. Subsequent Romantic critics extended the deification. Schumann's writings in the _Neue Zeitschrift für Musik_ (founded 1834) treated Beethoven as the standard against which contemporary composers were measured; the Schumann reviews of Schubert's "Great" C-major Symphony (1840) and the Brahms First Symphony (1853, before Brahms had composed it) both invoke the Beethoven inheritance explicitly. Wagner's 1849 essay _Das Kunstwerk der Zukunft_ ("The Artwork of the Future") treated the Ninth Symphony as the prototype of the _Gesamtkunstwerk_ — the moment when music broke out of its purely-instrumental shell and reached toward dramatic-vocal totality, prefiguring Wagner's own mature operas. By the late 19th century the Beethoven cult was institutional: major orchestras programmed Beethoven cycles as the core of their seasons; the Wagner Bayreuth Festspielhaus opened in 1876 with the Ninth Symphony; Beethoven busts appeared in concert halls across Europe and America. The 1870 centenary of Beethoven's birth produced festivals in dozens of cities. The composer had become, by 1900, the cultural property of educated Western Europe.

**Early-20th-century modernist questioning (1900–1950).** The modernist generation's relationship to Beethoven was complicated and varied. **Arnold Schoenberg** (1874–1951), in his writings on composition (collected in _Style and Idea_ , 1950) and in his teaching, traced his own twelve-tone system explicitly back to Beethoven's _developing variation_ — the technique by which a single motivic cell could generate an extended work through transformation. Schoenberg considered himself a Beethoven descendant in the structural-developmental sense, even as the surface harmonic language broke radically with the tonal tradition. His Op. 31 _Variations for Orchestra_ (1928) is, in his own characterization, an Eroica-style development of a single twelve-tone row. **Igor Stravinsky** (1882–1971), often pitched as Schoenberg's polar opposite, made the _Grosse Fuge_ his late-style touchstone — calling it in the 1950s _"an absolutely contemporary piece of music that will be contemporary forever"_ (Chapter 20). The Stravinsky reading positions Beethoven's last instrumental utterances as the door into modernism. **Béla Bartók** (1881–1945) studied the late Beethoven quartets intensively; his own six string quartets (1908–1939) extend the late-Beethoven quartet vocabulary into the 20th-century modernist register.

Not all modernist responses were reverent. The Italian **Futurists** (Marinetti's 1909 manifesto and the subsequent musical movements) rejected the entire 19th-century cult of the "Great Composer" as a sentimental obstacle to a new music. Some Parisian modernists (Erik Satie's circle, the group around Jean Cocteau and Les Six in the 1920s) treated Beethoven as a Germanic-Romantic monument to be sidestepped rather than engaged. The modernist period thus produced two parallel responses: the Schoenberg-Stravinsky-Bartók engagement (where Beethoven was the late-style ancestor) and the Futurist-Cocteau dismissal (where Beethoven was the cult to be discarded).

**Mid-20th-century academic re-engagement (1950–1990).** The postwar academic Beethoven scholarship — the **Lockwood-Solomon-Kerman-Rosen generation** — turned away from both Romantic worship and modernist polemic, toward sustained critical attention based on archival sources, sketch studies, and close compositional analysis. **Lewis Lockwood** 's sketch studies (_Beethoven: Studies in the Creative Process_ , 1992; _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ , 2003) reconstructed how Beethoven's works actually came into being — bar by bar, revision by revision — from the surviving sketchbooks. **Maynard Solomon** 's _Beethoven_ (1977, 2nd ed 1998) applied psychobiographical attention to the documented Beethoven life, producing the modern Antonie Brentano identification of the _Immortal Beloved_ (Chapter 7). **Joseph Kerman** 's _The Beethoven Quartets_ (1967) gave the late quartets the analytical close-reading they had not previously received in English. **Charles Rosen** 's _The Classical Style_ (1971) and _Beethoven's Piano Sonatas_ (2002) placed Beethoven inside the compositional-language history of the Classical-Romantic transition. The generation produced scholarship that was neither hagiographic nor dismissive — it took Beethoven seriously enough to argue with him, to revise traditional readings, to debunk Schindler embroideries (Chapter 9), and to leave the Romantic-deification rhetoric behind.

**21st-century re-engagement and challenge (1990–2026).** The contemporary period has produced both continued Beethoven engagement and critical challenges. The continued engagement: **William Kinderman** 's _Beethoven_ (2nd ed 2009) and _Beethoven's Diabelli Variations_ (1987) extend the Lockwood tradition; **Scott Burnham** 's _Beethoven Hero_ (1995) re-examines the heroic-period rhetoric and its 19th-century reception; **Daniel Chua** 's _The Galitzin Quartets of Beethoven_ (1995) gives the late quartets the dedicated treatment Kerman could only sketch. The **Beethoven 250 anniversary** in December 2020 produced a wave of new books, documentaries, recordings, and broadcast cycles — Andris Nelsons and the Boston Symphony, Daniel Barenboim and the West-Eastern Divan Orchestra, and many others reissued or completed cycles for the anniversary year. The critical challenges (the canon-questioning discussions of §24.5) are not arguments against listening to Beethoven; they are arguments about how the _cultural status_ of the Western classical canon was constructed historically. The debate is ongoing, and the next several decades will continue to reshape how Beethoven is heard.

### 24.5 The Contemporary Canon Debate

The contemporary period has produced critical discussions about the place of Beethoven (and the Western classical canon more broadly) in contemporary musical culture. The book's discipline (per the topic prompt §1.2 Ch 24) is to _describe the debate; not adjudicate it_. We present the three-column observational frame.

The **2020 Symphony No. 5 debate** is the major contemporary episode. In September 2020, _Vox_ published an article by Nate Sloan and Charlie Harding (hosts of the _Switched on Pop_ podcast) arguing that the Symphony 5 should be heard _as music of a specific historical moment_ rather than as universal "great music." The article was part of the _Switched on Pop_ series engaging with the Western classical canon critically.

The article produced responses across three positions:

**Position A: Defenders of the traditional canon.** Commentators arguing that the Symphony 5 _is_ universal great music; that the work transcends its historical context, that the compositional achievement is inherent in the music itself, and that the attempt to treat the work as historical artifact is reductive.

**Position B: Critics of the traditional canon.** Commentators arguing that the Symphony 5 reflects specific cultural assumptions (German Romantic individualism, Western European aesthetic priorities, gendered and racialized assumptions about who counts as a "great composer") that should be examined rather than taken as universal. The critics are not arguing against listening to Beethoven; they are arguing against treating the canon as self-evident.

**Position C: Middle-ground synthesis.** Commentators arguing that both positions have merit. The Symphony 5 _is_ great music _and_ a historical artifact; the compositional achievement is real _and_ the cultural-historical context shapes how the work has been received. The canon is both a record of compositional achievement _and_ a record of which compositional achievements institutional culture has recognized.

The debate is ongoing. The Beethoven 250 anniversary in 2020 produced multiple essays, podcasts, and books engaging with these questions. The book takes no position; it describes the three positions and notes that they are all currently held by serious commentators.

#### A scene from this afterlife: 9 November 1989, Berlin

The book ends this chapter with one specific scene, because abstract reception history runs thin without a moment to anchor it.

On 9 November 1989, the Berlin Wall opened. Within days, Leonard Bernstein was on the phone arranging a concert. On Christmas Day, in the Schauspielhaus on the eastern side of Berlin (newly accessible to a Western conductor), Bernstein conducted the Ninth Symphony with an orchestra and chorus assembled from East and West: musicians from the Bavarian Radio Symphony, the New York Philharmonic, the Kirov, the Orchestre de Paris, the Dresden Staatskapelle, the London Symphony, plus children's choirs from East and West Berlin. In the choral finale he changed Schiller's word _Freude_ (Joy) to _Freiheit_ (Freedom). _"Freiheit, schöner Götterfunken!"_ The chorus sang it that way. The broadcast went out across Europe on Christmas evening. _Freiheit_ is not what Schiller wrote and not what Beethoven set, but Bernstein's argument — made on the spot, made in the moment — was that _if any work was ever going to be re-edited at a moment of political-emotional history, it was this one, and the moment was now_. The substitution has been argued ever since (some scholars accept it as Bernstein's right as performer; others treat it as defacing). What is undisputed is the scene: a divided city briefly undivided, an aging American conductor with a German-Jewish heritage standing in East Berlin, the chorus from both sides of the Wall, the Ninth Symphony rebuilt one syllable to fit a political moment. This is what cultural-reception history actually looks like at its peak — a specific decision in a specific room on a specific evening — and it shows what the Ninth Symphony's afterlife has been: not a fixed text but a vessel that has been reused, again and again, by people who wanted to say something the moment required.

### 24.6 What the Reception History Tells Us

Several observations from the two-century reception:

**The works survive.** The Symphony 5, the Ninth Symphony, the late quartets, the _Missa Solemnis_ are all central to the Western classical repertoire as of 2026. The two-century reception has sustained their canonical status.

**The context changes.** What the Symphony 5 _meant_ in 1810 (Hoffmann's "abyss of the infinite") is different from what it meant in 1944 (Allied wartime resolve), which is different from what it meant in 2020 (the canon-debate question mark). The works are stable; the contexts in which they are heard are variable.

**The audience varies.** The 1810 audience for Beethoven was small and educated. The 2026 audience is far larger and includes many cultural backgrounds. The works have absorbed multiple cultural contexts.

**The compositional achievements persist.** The contemporary canon debates do not dispute the compositional achievements of the works. The Symphony 5 _is_ a compositional masterpiece; the question is what _cultural status_ the work should hold. The compositional question and the cultural-status question are separate.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Share with the companion the 1989 Bernstein "Freiheit" performance and ask whether you would have done the same in his place, and what the case is for and against changing Schiller's word in performance._
* _Tell the companion which moment from the popular-culture references (A Clockwork Orange, Immortal Beloved, Schroeder in Peanuts, Fantasia) feels most familiar to you, and ask how those appearances shaped general awareness of Beethoven._
* _Bring up the 2020 Vox / Switched on Pop Symphony 5 debate and ask the companion to walk through the three positions in the chapter without taking sides, and then, only after she has presented all three fairly, say which one you find most persuasive._
* _Ask the companion about the 1972 Council of Europe adoption of the Ode to Joy and the 1985 Milan Summit EEC formal adoption — what the selection process was, what the arguments for and against were, and how the anthem has functioned institutionally._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

* The Symphony 9 finale Ode to Joy theme — try to hear it as the European anthem, then as the Schiller-Beethoven universalist statement, then as the compositional achievement. The different framings produce different listening experiences.
* The Symphony 5 opening (the four-note hammer) — try to hear it as the compositional cell (Volume 1 Chapter 3, this volume Chapter 9), then as the Morse-code "V" wartime signal, then as the popular-culture quotation. The different framings produce different listening experiences.
* A Beethoven work that has not been heavily quoted or referenced in popular culture (perhaps the Op. 132 _Heiliger Dankgesang_ from Chapter 20) — try to hear it without the popular-culture overlay.

### Going Deeper

If you want one further reading, read **Esteban Buch's _Beethoven's Ninth: A Political History_ (University of Chicago Press, 2003)** — the authoritative cultural-political history of the Ninth Symphony and its uses across two centuries. Pair with **the _Switched on Pop_ podcast Beethoven episodes (2020)** for the contemporary popular-culture critical engagement.

### Chapter Summary

Beethoven's cultural afterlife has been extensive across two centuries. The chapter mapped major moments:

**The Ode to Joy as European anthem** — 1972 Council of Europe adoption, 1985 EEC Milan Summit adoption, continued EU usage. The anthem is performed instrumentally to avoid privileging any one European language.

**The Symphony 5 as wartime signal** — the Morse code "V" for Victory (short-short-short-long) used by the BBC during WWII Allied broadcasts. The connection between the German composer and Allied resistance to Nazi Germany was an act of cultural reclaiming.

**Beethoven in popular culture** — Kubrick's _A Clockwork Orange_ (1971), Rose's _Immortal Beloved_ (1994), Schroeder in _Peanuts_ , Disney's _Fantasia_ (1940) and _Fantasia 2000_ (1999), and many television and film soundtracks.

**The critical reception arc** — 19th-century deification, early-20th-century modernist questioning, mid-20th-century academic re-engagement, 21st-century re-engagement and challenge.

**The contemporary canon debate** — the 2020 _Vox_ /_Switched on Pop_ Symphony 5 episode and the subsequent discussions. Three positions:

* **Position A** : defenders of the traditional canon
* **Position B** : critics of the traditional canon
* **Position C** : middle-ground synthesis

The chapter's discipline was observational rather than adjudicating — the book describes the positions without taking sides.

You met the 25 December 1989 Bernstein "Freiheit" performance — Schiller's _Freude_ changed to _Freiheit_ in a city briefly undivided after the Wall opened, with chorus and orchestra assembled from both sides.

You met observations from the reception history: the works survive; the context changes; the audience varies; the compositional achievements persist as separate from the cultural-status questions.

The next chapter is the final one — the closing chapter that points you toward the reading list, the recordings to live with, and the next single-composer volumes in the Close Listening series.

## Chapter 25: Where to Go Next, and the Beethoven Recordings to Live With

You have walked through fifty-six years of Ludwig van Beethoven's creative life. Twenty-four chapters of close listening, with about fifty anchored works and another forty or so secondary works mentioned. Three threads, the biographical arc, the compositional signatures, the listening habits to unlearn — have run across the volume. The late quartets are in your ear. The Ninth's choral finale is in your ear. The _Pathétique_ Grave's three returns are in your ear. Op. 111's Arietta closing trill is in your ear.

This is the last chapter. The aim is to point you toward where to go next, the reading list, the recordings to live with, the documentaries, concert advice, and the next single-composer volumes in the Close Listening series.

### 25.1 The Reading List

The modern Beethoven scholarship is extensive. The following list consolidates the references that have been consistently cited across this volume.

**Lewis Lockwood,_Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (W. W. Norton, 2003).** The single most-recommended starting volume. Balanced biographical-and-musical voice. Careful with sources. Accessible to the amateur reader. Every chapter of this volume has drawn on Lockwood. If you read one Beethoven book alongside this volume, read Lockwood.

**Maynard Solomon,_Beethoven_ (Schirmer, 2nd ed 1998).** The most-cited Beethoven biography. Psychobiographical attention; the Immortal Beloved = Antonie Brentano identification (Chapter 7); the Heiligenstadt-as-hinge reading. Solomon's voice is more interpretive than Lockwood's. Pair with Lockwood for the complete biographical-and-analytical perspective.

**Jan Swafford,_Beethoven: Anguish and Triumph_ (Houghton Mifflin Harcourt, 2014).** The narrative-voice biographer. Gripping prose. Bonn-period detail. If you want an engaging single-volume biography that reads like good narrative non-fiction, read Swafford.

**Charles Rosen,_Beethoven's Piano Sonatas: A Short Companion_ (Yale University Press, 2002).** The source-of-record for piano-sonata analysis. Structural attention; late-sonata expertise. If you read one analytical book on Beethoven, read Rosen on the sonatas. Pair with Rosen's earlier _The Classical Style_ (1971) for the compositional-language context.

**Joseph Kerman,_The Beethoven Quartets_ (W. W. Norton, 1967).** The source-of-record for quartet analysis. Late-quartet analytical detail. If you want to go deeper on the quartets, read Kerman.

**William Kinderman,_Beethoven_ (Oxford University Press, 2nd ed 2009).** The contemporary academic synthesis. Critical attention to the three-period framework (Kinderman argues for an earlier start to the late period). Pair with Lockwood for the contemporary scholarly perspective.

**Donald Tovey,_Essays in Musical Analysis_ , vols. 1–6 (Oxford University Press, 1935–39).** The classic amateur-friendly analytical voice. Essays on each Beethoven symphony, on the concertos, on many of the chamber works. Older language and some dated assumptions, but still useful and engagingly written.

**Theodor W. Adorno,_Beethoven: Philosophy of Music_ (posthumously edited by Rolf Tiedemann, 1993; English translation 1998).** The philosophical-critical voice. Attention to the late style's philosophical implications. Difficult reading; an incomplete project (Beethoven never finished it). For advanced readers willing to engage with dense philosophical-critical writing.

**Anton Schindler,_Biographie von Ludwig van Beethoven_ (1840; multiple modern editions).** The unreliable but historically significant first major Beethoven biography. Read it critically — Schindler's embroideries and invented quotations are well-catalogued by modern scholars. Read with Lockwood's _Beethoven and His Conversation Books_ (2018) as the corrective companion.

**Lewis Lockwood,_Beethoven and His Conversation Books_ (Boydell Press, 2018).** The authoritative study of the conversation books (1818–1827) and the Schindler forgeries. For the reader who wants to understand which quotations are reliable and which are Schindler embroideries.

### 25.2 The Recordings to Live With

The recommendations across this volume form a starter collection. The following list consolidates the primary recordings for the major works:

**Symphonies:**

* **Symphony 5** : Carlos Kleiber / Vienna Philharmonic 1974 (DG)
* **Symphony 6** : Karl Böhm / Vienna Philharmonic 1971 (DG)
* **Symphony 7** : Carlos Kleiber / Vienna Philharmonic 1976 (DG)
* **Symphony 9** : Furtwängler / Bayreuth 1951 (EMI) or John Eliot Gardiner / ORR 1992 (Archiv)
* **Symphony 3 _Eroica_** : Otto Klemperer / Philharmonia 1959 (EMI)

**Piano sonatas:**

* **Pathétique Op. 13** : Emil Gilels 1980 (DG)
* **Moonlight Op. 27 No. 2** : Wilhelm Kempff 1965 (DG)
* **Appassionata Op. 57** : Sviatoslav Richter Live Carnegie Hall 1960 (Sony/RCA)
* **Waldstein Op. 53** : Maurizio Pollini 1997 (DG)
* **Op. 90** : Wilhelm Kempff 1965 (DG)
* **Hammerklavier Op. 106** : Maurizio Pollini 1977 (DG)
* **Op. 111** : Maurizio Pollini 1977 (DG) or Mitsuko Uchida 2007 (Decca)

**Concertos:**

* **Violin Concerto Op. 61** : Anne-Sophie Mutter / Karajan / Berlin Philharmonic 1979 (DG)
* **Piano Concerto No. 4** : Wilhelm Kempff / Leitner / Berlin Philharmonic 1962 (DG)

**Chamber works:**

* **Op. 1 No. 3 Trio** : Beaux Arts Trio 1965 (Philips)
* **Op. 97 Archduke Trio** : Beaux Arts Trio 1979 (Philips)
* **Op. 59 No. 1 Razumovsky Quartet** : Alban Berg Quartet 1989 (EMI)

**Late quartets:**

* **Op. 131** : Quartetto Italiano 1969 (Philips) or Alban Berg Quartet 1989 (EMI)
* **Op. 132** : Alban Berg Quartet 1989 (EMI) or Belcea Quartet 2012 (Zig-Zag)
* **Op. 130 (with _Grosse Fuge_)**: Alban Berg Quartet 1989 (EMI)
* **Grosse Fuge Op. 133** : Emerson String Quartet 1997 (DG)

**Operatic and choral:**

* **Fidelio** : Klemperer / Philharmonia 1962 (EMI)
* **Missa Solemnis** : Klemperer / New Philharmonia 1965 (EMI)

**Variations:**

* **Diabelli Variations Op. 120** : Stephen Kovacevich 1968 (Philips)

This is a starter list of about twenty recordings. The complete Beethoven collection runs into hundreds of recordings; a starter list gives the core. After internalizing these, the alternates mentioned in each chapter can be sampled.

### 25.3 Your First Ten Beethoven Listens

If you have just completed this volume and want a starter sequence for sustained listening over the next year, here is the recommendation:

  1. **Symphony 5, all four movements** — Kleiber 1974 (Chapter 9)
  2. **Symphony 9, all four movements** — Furtwängler 1951 Bayreuth (Chapter 18)
  3. **Pathétique Sonata, first movement** — Gilels 1980 (Chapter 2)
  4. **Moonlight Sonata, first movement** — Kempff 1965 (Chapter 4)
  5. **Appassionata Sonata, first movement** — Richter 1960 (Chapter 7)
  6. **Op. 111, both movements** — Pollini 1977 (Chapter 16)
  7. **Op. 131 String Quartet, all seven movements** — Quartetto Italiano 1969 (Chapter 20)
  8. **Op. 132 String Quartet, third movement _Heiliger Dankgesang_** — Alban Berg Quartet 1989 (Chapter 20)
  9. **Diabelli Variations** — Kovacevich 1968 (Chapter 17)
  10. **Missa Solemnis, Kyrie and Benedictus** — Klemperer 1965 (Chapter 19)

These ten anchor the Beethoven catalogue at its peaks. After these, the other major works (Symphony 3, Symphony 6, Symphony 7, Violin Concerto, Archduke Trio, Hammerklavier, Op. 110, Fidelio, the other late quartets) can be sampled. The full Beethoven listening life extends well beyond this volume.

### 25.4 Documentaries and Visual Resources

The Beethoven documentaries and visual resources include:

**The BBC _Beethoven_ series** (2003 documentary series, various directors). An overview of the life and works, with professional performances. Accessible to amateur viewers; useful but not essential.

**Various conductor-led commentaries.** Daniel Barenboim's Beethoven lectures (available on Barenboim's YouTube channel and other platforms). John Eliot Gardiner's Beethoven discussions. Andris Nelsons's Boston Symphony commentaries on the Beethoven cycle.

**András Schiff's lecture-recital videos** on the Beethoven piano sonatas (Wigmore Hall and DG channels). Schiff plays and discusses each sonata; the videos are one of the best resources for the sonata cycle.

**Verify availability in 2026.** The streaming and online-video landscape changes rapidly. The recommendations above were available as of recent years; specific availability should be checked when this volume is read.

### 25.5 Concert Advice

The Beethoven cycle is the gold-standard concert experience. Major orchestras typically perform complete cycles every five to ten years.

**Hear a complete cycle if possible.** Hearing all nine symphonies (and ideally the Missa Solemnis as a culminating event) over the course of a single concert season is one of the major Western-musical experiences. Major orchestras worldwide do cycles; check the seasonal programming of your local orchestra (or visit one of the major Beethoven-cycle centers — Berlin, Vienna, London, New York, Boston, Chicago, Cleveland).

**Hear the late quartets in concert if possible.** The late quartets are intimate works that reward concert-hall listening with serious quartet ensembles. Most major chamber-music series program the late quartets; check Lincoln Center, Wigmore Hall, the Concertgebouw, and similar venues.

**The Ninth is special.** The Ninth Symphony in concert with chorus and soloists is one of the great concert experiences. The work is typically performed only once per season by major orchestras; check seasonal programming.

**The Missa Solemnis is rarer.** The Missa Solemnis is less-frequently programmed than the symphonies because of its technical difficulty. When it is programmed (typically by major orchestras with professional choruses), it is worth traveling for.

### 25.6 Closing — Live With Him

This volume completes the second installment of _Close Listening_. Volume 1 was _Classical_ — the survey of Western classical music in twenty-seven chapters. Volume 2 is _Beethoven_ — the single-composer deep dive into fifty-six years.

The next single-composer volumes are planned: **Mozart** , **Chopin** , **Bach** , **Schubert** , **Brahms** , **Tchaikovsky**. Each volume will follow the same four-section listening loop and the same fifty-anchor depth. The next composer up is Mozart — Volume 3 of _Close Listening_.

But before you go to Mozart, live with Beethoven for a year.

That is the central recommendation of this closing chapter. Take the ten works in §25.3. Put the recordings on your shelf. Listen to each anchor twice — once for the prose in the chapter, once for the music. Then, every month or so, return to one of them with the chapter reopened next to you, and notice what you hear now that you did not hear before.

Live with Lockwood next to those recordings. When you encounter something in a chapter you want to chase — Beethoven and Haydn's documented counterpoint lessons, the Heiligenstadt Testament's full text, Rosen on the late sonatas, Kerman on the late quartets — go to Lockwood first, then to the more specialized scholars. The book is in your library; the chapters are spaced for a reader who reads them slowly.

Hear the Beethoven cycle in concert if a major orchestra in your reach is programming it. Take the late quartets to a chamber-music series with a serious ensemble — Alban Berg's heirs, Takács, Belcea. If you have one opportunity in a lifetime to hear the Missa Solemnis in concert, take it.

You will form opinions. You will end up preferring one _Eroica_ recording to another. You will decide whether Op. 131 or the _Heiliger Dankgesang_ is the higher Beethoven, even though the question is, strictly, not answerable. You will become slightly evangelical about one piece you used to underestimate — perhaps Symphony 8, perhaps the Op. 110 sonata, perhaps the Op. 96 violin sonata that this volume only mentioned in passing. You will recommend specific recordings to friends. You will, at concerts, lean over and tell the person next to you that the second-movement Allegretto of Symphony 7 was so popular at its 1813 premiere that the orchestra had to play it twice. You will become, in a small, specific, useful way, a person who knows Beethoven.

That is the work. The book has tried to make the work specific. You took the time. The composer is now yours.

Beethoven was, after all, a human being. Difficult, brilliant, deaf, persistent, productive, occasionally generous, occasionally infuriating, capable of profound moments of compositional ambition and profound moments of personal failure. He composed for forty-five years. He left a catalogue that has been heard for two centuries and will be heard for many more.

You have now met him. You know about fifty of his works. You can spot the C-minor character (Op. 1 No. 3 → Op. 111), the dramatic slow introduction (Pathétique → Symphony 7), the variation-form finale (Op. 111 → Diabelli → Ninth), the unison openings (Symphony 5 → Schreckensfanfare), the heroic key-change (Symphony 5 third-to-fourth movement, Symphony 9 first-to-fourth). You know the Heiligenstadt Testament, the Karl custody battle, the Antonie Brentano dedication of the _Diabelli_ , the December 22, 1808 four-hour concert in the freezing Theater an der Wien, the Caroline Unger turning gesture, the Schubert-after-Op. 131 anecdote. You know which Schindler quotations to trust and which to flag.

You know enough to walk into any Beethoven program in a concert hall with confident anticipation rather than reverent caution.

That is the goal the volume has been aiming at all along.

Go listen.

### Conversation Seeds

* _Tell the companion which of the ten works in §25.3 you want to return to first, and ask her to suggest a second-listen strategy: which moment in the work you should focus on this time, given what you absorbed across the volume._
* _Bring up one Beethoven work this volume mentioned only in passing — the Op. 18 No. 4 quartet (C minor, Chapter 12), the Triple Concerto (Op. 56, Chapter 12), the Egmont Overture (Op. 84), or the Bagatelles Op. 126 (the last Beethoven piano work) — and ask the companion to walk you through what makes it worth direct attention._
* _Tell the companion which moment from this volume you most expect to remember in five years, and ask her why specific moments lodge in listening lives in a way that whole works often do not._
* _Ask the companion to read your listening preferences from this volume, which works moved you most, and recommend which of the next single-composer volumes (Mozart, Chopin, Bach, Schubert, Brahms, Tchaikovsky) might appeal to you first when they become available._

### Things to Listen For on Re-Listen

This is the closing chapter; there is no single anchor work. Instead, the recommendation: pick a work from the earlier part of the volume — perhaps the _Pathétique_ (Chapter 2), the Op. 1 No. 3 trio (Chapter 3), or the _Eroica_ first movement (Chapter 6) — and listen again with everything you have absorbed across the twenty-four chapters. The earlier work, heard now with the full Beethoven catalogue in your ear, will be different from how it was when you first encountered it.

That is the point of the volume.

### Going Deeper

If you read one further work for the long-term Beethoven listening life, read **Lewis Lockwood's _Beethoven: The Music and the Life_ (W. W. Norton, 2003)** — the single most-recommended starting volume, cited consistently across this book. After Lockwood, the other entries in the reading list (Solomon, Swafford, Rosen, Kerman, Kinderman, Tovey) sit in your library to be sampled as your interests direct.

### Chapter Summary

This second volume of _Close Listening_ — focusing on Beethoven — completes here. You have walked through fifty-six years of Ludwig van Beethoven's creative life across twenty-four chapters, with about fifty anchored works and another forty or so secondary works mentioned.

The reading list: Lockwood (2003) as start; Solomon (2nd ed 1998); Swafford (2014); Rosen on the sonatas (2002); Kerman on the quartets (1967); Kinderman (2nd ed 2009); Tovey _Essays_ (1935–39); Adorno _Beethoven: Philosophy of Music_ (1993 posthumous).

The recordings to live with: a starter list of about twenty primary recordings — Kleiber's Symphony 5, Furtwängler's Symphony 9, Pollini's Hammerklavier and Op. 111, Quartetto Italiano's Op. 131, Klemperer's Fidelio and Missa Solemnis, Kovacevich's Diabelli, and others as listed in each chapter.

The first-ten Beethoven listens: a sequenced list for the next year of sustained listening.

The concert advice: hear a complete symphonic cycle if possible; hear the late quartets in concert with serious quartet ensembles; the Ninth is special; the Missa Solemnis is rarer.

The next single-composer volumes in _Close Listening_ : Mozart (Volume 3, planned), Chopin, Bach, Schubert, Brahms, Tchaikovsky.

In closing: Beethoven is one of the great creative figures of Western music; the volume has tried to give you the achievement without the mystification; you now know about fifty of his works and can walk into any Beethoven program with confident anticipation. The listening life from here is yours.
